The Forty-Day Clause, Part One

The Proclamation and the Flight

Chapter 1. Reading Aloud

The Scales calculates the sentence. But it takes effect only when a person reads it aloud.

I asked why, once. I was eleven. The proclamation supervisor looked at me for a moment, then went back to sorting the papers in his hands and answered.

"That's the rule."

"But why?"

"Because that's the rule."

I didn't like that answer. I didn't know yet that it wasn't an answer at all.

Herald training begins with the voice.

We read four hours a day. First short sentences, then long ones, then sentences with other people's names in them. A sentence with someone else's name in it is the hardest thing to read.

The training hall had a high ceiling and no windows. Sound struck the walls and came back. Read for four hours in a room where your own voice returns to you, and before long you can't tell whether it's your voice or the wall's.

The supervisor said he'd chosen that room on purpose.

"Out there, when you read, nobody answers." he said. "So get used to it in here. Your own voice, coming back to you."

What he taught wasn't pronunciation. By the third day everyone's pronunciation sounded more or less the same. What he really taught was how not to stop.

"Most people trip on the name." he said. "A name is just letters. Why hold your breath to read letters?"

We practiced reading names. Names we didn't know first, then names we did. In the last week we read fake sentence documents with our own names on them.

When my turn came, I read straight through without stopping once. The supervisor nodded.

"Pheugon. Well done."

The boy sitting beside me looked at me. He had stopped three times on his own name.

I took that look for admiration, back then. I know now it wasn't. He wasn't envying me. He was afraid of me.

That boy's name was Had. When training ended, Had didn't become a herald. He never broke the habit of stopping on names.

I said nothing the day Had washed out. Had said nothing to me either. But as he carried his things out, he turned once.

"Why don't you ever stop?"

I couldn't answer.

I carried that question for three years. By the time I could answer it, Had was gone.

What we read is not the verdict. The verdict has already been handed down by the Scales. What we read is the procedure that makes that verdict audible to the world.

The Scales knows everything. It calculates, without error, the weight of every act committed inside a city. It knows exactly what one stolen loaf of bread is worth, what one lie is worth, what one blow of a hand is worth. People adjust their lives by the figures the Scales gives them.

But the Scales does not speak its own verdicts.

This is the first strangeness of this world. The Scales weighs, the Scales sentences, the Scales executes — and yet reading the verdict aloud, that alone, is left to a person. And not just any person. Someone trained specifically for that one task.

Unread, it has no effect. However precisely the Scales calculates, if no one reads it aloud, the verdict stays a number written on paper.

I used to think this rule was a flaw in the Scales. One old procedure left standing in an otherwise perfect system. Vestigial. Something that would be done away with, eventually.

I doubt I was the only one who thought so. No one asked why the procedure existed. Everyone was satisfied with "because that's the rule."

But that procedure wasn't vestigial. It was the one thing, across every one of these cities, that the Scales could not do — and someone had placed a person in that spot. Who did that, and why, I wouldn't think to ask until much, much later.

The year I turned fourteen, I became a herald in full.

My first proclamation was small. A merchant in the market who'd tampered with his own scale weights. His weight hadn't crossed the limit, so he wasn't pushed out of the city — only barred from the market for six months.

I stood before him and read the sentence. He watched me the whole time. A man of about fifty, looking up at a fourteen-year-old.

I read without stopping. Name, charge, disposition, effective date.

I folded the paper when I finished. The merchant said something; I didn't hear it. Not hearing was part of the training too.

I went home and washed my hands for a long time. I washed them without knowing why.

That night I tried to remember what the merchant had said. I'd seen the shape of his mouth, so I could guess. Two words, maybe.

I couldn't remember.

I thought that was the training taking hold. That not-listening had settled into my body.

It hadn't. I had heard him. And that same night, I had already erased it. Only the one who erases something knows what was erased. After that I erased a hundred and eleven more times.

Over the three years that followed, I read a hundred and twelve cases.

I kept count, not to boast. I counted because I couldn't have borne it otherwise. While I was counting, it wasn't a person. It was a number.

Of those hundred and twelve, not one was a misjudgment. Not a single one. I checked. For days after each reading I went through the records, wanting to know whether the Scales had ever been wrong. If it had been wrong even once, my reading would have meant something different.

It never had.

I think I felt reassured, then. If the Scales was never wrong, every sentence I read was a correct sentence. And a person who reads correct sentences has done nothing wrong.

I leaned on that logic for a long time. It took forty days to bring it down.

I don't do wrong.

My weight is the lowest in my cohort. The supervisor checked it once, and then checked it again, as if to say, is this right.

I don't lie, I don't touch what belongs to others, I don't get angry. Not that anger doesn't come — I don't let it out. I know those are two different things.

Everyone assumed it was because I was conscientious.

It wasn't. I wasn't afraid of weight. I believed there was a sentence I would one day have to read, and I didn't want to lose the right to read it.

What that sentence was, I had known since I was twelve.

There is only me in my family now.

I don't intend to tell it all here. I'm not able to tell it all even to myself yet. I'll write down only this much — a few years ago, someone came to stay in our house, and after that person left, our family came apart.

That person came from the Reckoning.

The Scales calculated it. Calculated it precisely. A weight came out, and a disposition came down. I have no complaint about that disposition. It was precise.

Precise, and nothing came back. I thought for a long time about how those two sentences could stand together, and in the end I stopped thinking about it. Instead I decided to do something else.

I decided to become a herald.

Around the start of autumn, the supervisor called me in alone.

His room was always cool. The window faced north, and a list of heralds hung on the wall. A number beside each name — the count of cases that person had read. Beside my name it said 112.

The supervisor set a document on the desk.

"This one's big."

I looked at the document. In the first line was a city's name.

The Reckoning.

I looked at those letters. For a long time. The supervisor was explaining something. I couldn't hear it.

"…Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"Are you listening."

"…Yes."

The supervisor looked at me for a moment. Then he tapped the document again.

"This isn't an individual case. It's the whole city. Accumulated weight crossed the limit. A case like this comes once a decade, if that."

"…The whole city?"

"Sentence of demolition." the supervisor said. "Forty-day grace period. Meaning: get your affairs in order and get out, within that time."

"…And the people?"

"They scatter." the supervisor said. "Some go to cities that'll take them in. Some don't get taken in, and live outside."

"…How many people."

The supervisor turned a page.

"A hundred and twenty thousand."

I heard that number. A hundred and twenty thousand. A number I'd never counted to. Counting a hundred and twelve, I had thought that was a lot.

I had no sense, then, that the number was people. I still don't think that was my fault. No one can feel a hundred and twenty thousand as people. I only think, now, that if even one face had been in it, something might have been different — and forty days later I would learn how wrong that thought was.

Even after a face appeared, I still wanted that city to fall.

I noticed, in that moment, that my hands weren't shaking. They should have been. Through a hundred and twelve readings my hands had never once shaken, but this time, they should have. They didn't.

Because they didn't shake, I knew I had been waiting for this.

"The reason I'm assigning you is simple." the supervisor said. "You don't stop."

"…Yes."

"A whole-city sentence draws a crowd. People cry, they shout, they grab at you. Stop partway through, and you have to start over from the beginning. There have been heralds who stopped more than three times, reading in front of a crowd like that."

The supervisor looked at me.

"You won't stop."

"…Yes."

"That's not a compliment." the supervisor said. "And it doesn't mean this is good for you, either."

I didn't answer.

The supervisor slid the document toward me. He kept his hand on it a moment before he let go.

"Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"Do you have some connection to the Reckoning."

I looked at him. He was looking at the document.

"…No, sir."

The supervisor took his hand off the paper.

"All right."

I told exactly one lie in that room that day. Weighed out, it would have been a very light lie. The Scales would have calculated it, and that figure is probably sitting in my record somewhere, even now.

The Scales never knew that lie was the heaviest one of my life.

I didn't know, then, that the supervisor was worried about me. In that room, that day, he was the only one who was.

I walked out of the room with the sentence document in my hand.

The corridor was long. Windows at regular intervals let light and shadow cross my face in turns as I walked.

At the end of the corridor stood a terminal of the Scales. Put a hand on it, and your weight comes up. People at the proclamation office rest a hand on it several times a day. Habit.

I passed it and stopped.

I almost raised my hand, then didn't. I'd just told a lie; the figure would be up a little. Checking how much might have settled my mind. It would only be a small rise, after all.

I didn't raise that hand that day. And for the forty days after, not once. I understood why only later. It wasn't that I was afraid the number would be high. I was afraid it would be low.

I already knew, somewhere in me, that what I was about to do would barely register on the Scales at all.

I walked on, unfolding the paper. I didn't mean to read it through. I only wanted to see the city's name once more.

The Reckoning.

I had known that name for a very long time. I had never once said it aloud. Not since I was twelve.

And now I had become the person who had to read that name aloud. And not just any line of it — the city's last line.

I stopped in the middle of the corridor.

And I laughed. Silently, for just a moment.

I remember that laugh. I couldn't have explained, then, what kind of laugh it was. I can now.

It was relief.

At home I laid the sentence document on my desk.

I didn't read it. A herald isn't permitted to read a sentence aloud before proclamation day. That's a rule too. The moment it's read, it takes effect.

I only skimmed it with my eyes. City name, accumulated weight, basis of calculation, grace period, method of execution. The sentences were short and exact. The Scales always writes that way.

And at the very bottom of the page.

The type was one size smaller than the rest, set close to the margin. The first time I saw it, I took it for a printing error.

Article 4, Clause 11.

I stopped on that line.

It was one sentence.

Should this city, within forty days, be confirmed to have turned, this sentence shall be void.

I read that sentence, and read it again, and a third time.

Turned.

Not a word the Scales uses. The Scales writes "weight decreases," or "the figure falls below the limit." It doesn't use words that name a direction. The Scales has no category called direction.

But this sentence had one.

In a hundred and twelve sentence documents, I had never once seen a sentence like it. This clause doesn't attach to individual cases. Only to whole-city cases. And a whole-city case comes once a decade, if that.

I didn't understand, that night, exactly what the sentence meant. I never asked who "confirmed" meant — confirmed by whom. I assumed the Scales would confirm it.

It would take twenty more days from that night before I learned the Scales cannot confirm it. And standing in the place where I learned that, I would, for the first time, understand precisely why I had run.

I traced the sentence with my fingertip. The paper was thin. The small type set into the margin sat slightly out of line with the body text, as though someone had added it in later.

I don't know how many times I looked at that line again that night. I didn't count. It was the first time in my life I'd forgotten to count.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. Weight

_Previously_ — Pheugon was the trainee who best mastered reading without
feeling. He became a full herald at fourteen and read a hundred and twelve
cases over three years, not one of them a misjudgment. As autumn begins,
the supervisor assigns him a whole-city sentence of demolition — the
Reckoning. Hearing the name, Pheugon is startled that his hands do not
shake.

The Reckoning isn't the only city that uses the Scales.

Every city in this region uses it. They differ only in size and precision. Ours was a small one. Three terminals in the square, two at the market's edge, one in the proclamation office corridor. Six in all, measuring the weight of the whole city's people, in real time.

I'd heard that the limit differs from city to city. Some cities have high limits, tolerant of most wrongs. Some have low ones, where a handful of small lies is enough to push a person out. I didn't know then which the Reckoning's limit was. I learned later — the Reckoning had a high limit. And still it was crossed.

People put a hand up to the terminal. A number appears on the screen. That's all. The Scales doesn't explain. It doesn't tell you why this number, why it rose or fell since yesterday. It only gives the number.

As a child I thought that was unkind. I think differently now. The Scales doesn't explain because it isn't unkind. It has nothing to explain. A number is a number. A number has no reason.

No one knows exactly how weight is assigned. The calculation happens inside the Scales, and there's no way to bring that process out in a form a person can see. We can only guess from the result.

One lie is worth roughly this much. Theft, heavier. Striking someone, heavier still. Weight has units, though no one bothers with what to call them. It's just a number. Low is good, high is bad. That's all, and that's all this city knows.

When accumulated weight crosses an individual's limit, that person is pushed out of the city. Pushed out isn't quite the right word. No one lays a hand on them. Everything that person used — the house, the job, the market pass, the ration — simply stops, one thing at a time. I learned very young that the Scales can push a person out of a city without ever touching them.

When a whole city's accumulated weight crosses the city's limit, a sentence of demolition falls on the city itself. That's rare. Individual cases come out every month, but a whole-city case, as the supervisor said, comes once a decade, if that.

The Reckoning was that rare case. The day I was assigned it, I didn't grasp how rare it was. I understood only after I stood in that city myself.

I remember the day school first taught us about weight. I was seven.

The teacher lined us up in front of a terminal. Had us put our hands up one at a time. When the number came up, the other children all saw it. Some laughed. Some cried. It wasn't that the crying children's numbers were especially high. They were just afraid of having their number shown in front of everyone.

"This isn't a punishment." the teacher said. "It's just knowing. Knowing where you stand right now."

I believed her. For a long time.

I know now. It was a punishment. In a world with no line between knowing and being punished, "this isn't a punishment" wasn't comfort — it was already, in itself, a piece of the punishment.

The teacher taught us how to keep our numbers low. Don't lie. Don't touch what isn't yours. Don't get angry. That was how the school told us to be good. Not goodness. A figure.

I was the child who learned it best.

From around twelve, I checked my weight every day.

If you asked why, I had no real answer. I called it habit. It wasn't habit. I checked every day because I wanted to see the number hadn't dropped — no, not dropped, hadn't risen. I wanted to confirm it was staying low.

I tried not to lie. In ambiguous situations, I just said nothing at all. I never touched anyone's belongings. If my hand brushed something, I pulled it back. When something made me angry, I kept my mouth shut. Keeping it shut enough, the occasions for anger themselves grew fewer. No — not fewer. I simply stopped calling it anger.

My weight was the lowest in my cohort. Low enough that the supervisor checked it, and checked it again.

The other trainees thought it was just my nature. That I was good, and that was why. Whenever I heard that, I said nothing. Saying nothing was, for me, the easiest answer there was.

Had was different. Had was afraid to check his own number. Every time he passed the terminal in the training hall corridor, he looked away on purpose.

"Aren't you scared?" Had asked me once. We were passing the terminal together.

"…Of what."

"Seeing the number. I hold my breath every time I walk past it."

I didn't answer. I didn't know it then. Had thought I wasn't afraid, but in truth I was more afraid than he was. Only the direction of the fear was different. Had was afraid his number would rise. I was afraid that something was wrong even though mine was low.

I had no words for that difference then. I have them now. Had was afraid of what he had already done. I was afraid of what I hadn't done yet. I already knew I would do it, someday.

The morning after I received the Reckoning's sentence, I passed the terminal of the Scales as I always did.

I put my hand up. A number appeared on the screen.

Low. As always.

I felt reassured looking at that number. But it was a strange kind of reassurance, I know now. I wasn't reassured because I was a good person. I was reassured because I hadn't done anything yet. I didn't know that the word "yet" was already inside that reassurance.

People were lined up at the market's terminal. A morning scene, every day. Hand up, look at the number, move on with a smile or with no expression at all. Smiling is rare. Mostly people just check. Low, and they're relieved; high, and they're careful.

Near the front of the line stood a stranger, a man. He put up his hand and a fairly high number came up. A few of the people behind him quietly stepped back. The man lowered his hand without a word and turned away. No one held him. No one pushed him either. People just spread out, little by little.

That was how this city punished people. No shouting, no hands laid on. Just a slow stepping-back. I never saw that man in the market again, after that day.

I didn't think of the people stepping back as cowards, back then. I'd have done the same. There was a rumor that standing near someone with high weight for too long could somehow affect your own weight. No one knew if it was true. There was no way to check. And yet people acted as if they believed it. Not because they believed it — because behaving as if they did was safer.

I caught the eye of the old vegetable seller as I passed the line. Every morning she stood at the Scales for a long while, staring at it. Even though the number never changed.

"…How's it look today." I asked, passing, as a kind of greeting.

"Same." she said. "Same every day. That's the frightening part."

I let it pass. I didn't understand it then.

I understand now. What frightened her wasn't a number going up. It was the fact that a number never comes down. The Scales only knows how to add weight. It doesn't know how to subtract. No one has any way to lower their own weight themselves. However much time passes, however well you live, the number stays exactly where it was set. The Scales has no function for erasing a past wrong. Only for adding.

I thought that was only natural, back then. Wrongs don't disappear. I think differently now. That a wrong doesn't disappear, and that a wrong cannot disappear — those are two different claims. The Scales never distinguished between them. Not that it had no wish to distinguish — it had no way to.

I should say a little about my family. Not all of it — only as much as I can now.

I knew everyone's number. All low. Everyone in my house lived carefully, like me. Whether we were careful because our numbers were low, or our numbers were low because we were careful, I still don't know.

There was exactly one exception.

I won't tell that person's story here. Not yet. I'll only write this — because of that one person's number, the rest of our low numbers stopped meaning anything. The Scales calculated each of us separately. For each of us, separately, the calculation was correct. But as a household, as a unit, that calculation explained nothing at all.

The Scales measures individuals. It doesn't know how to measure a family. I learned that too, only later.

After that, every time I checked my number, I found myself picturing that person's number alongside it. Laid side by side, one gave comfort and the other didn't. But to the Scales, those were simply two numbers sitting next to each other. The low one doesn't offset the high one. The Scales doesn't do that kind of arithmetic.

I think I wanted, for a long time, to believe that my low number could pay something back. Even though no such arithmetic exists anywhere.

In our last year of training, we studied demolition procedure. Not the individual kind — the city kind. No one had actually seen one. We only learned it from the textbook.

The textbook read like this. Once a sentence takes effect, that city's connections to the Scales are severed, one at a time, in order. Rations stop first, then passes, and last, residency itself is voided. People have to relocate to another city within a set period. Those who don't must live on land without the Scales, however that land lives.

The textbook then added: "In some cases, no city will take them in." No explanation followed that sentence. Just a line break.

I remember staring at that line break for a long time.

I thought, then, that sentence had nothing to do with me. Just one of the hundreds of sentences in the textbook. Three years later, I would be standing in front of real people, reading that sentence with my own voice. I had no idea, back then.

The teacher also asked us this: "If a city is demolished entirely, what happens to the infants inside it?" No one could answer. The teacher didn't answer either. She just turned the page.

I forgot that question for three years. It came back to me the night I received the Reckoning's sentence. For the first time, briefly, I tried to think of an answer. I stopped before I found one. I think I didn't want to find it.

Walking to the proclamation office, I reached into my bag and touched the Reckoning's sentence document again.

I didn't know exactly how much a city's accumulated weight had to reach before it crossed the limit. The supervisor hadn't given me the precise figure either. He'd only said, "it crossed."

A hundred and twenty thousand people had each added a little, and it had come to that number. Some a great deal, some very little. The Scales adds it all together. Only adds. It doesn't know how to subtract.

That morning I had a thought for the first time. Of a hundred and twenty thousand people, how many stood every morning at a terminal, checking their own number, the way I did. Did they too know that number could never come down.

Thinking about it, I felt strangely nothing. Standing before a hundred and twenty thousand faceless numbers, I thought only that the city was getting what it deserved. How cleanly, how casually that thought had settled inside me — I had no idea, at the time.

At the front gate of the proclamation office, I stopped for a moment before going back in.

There was a terminal by the door too. I didn't raise my hand. I'd already checked that morning. But there was another reason too.

Standing at that terminal, I felt afraid to see a number for the first time. I hadn't done anything yet. Hadn't even decided to run. My body seemed to already know. That I was about to enter a different kind of relationship with that number.

Up to that point, in fifteen years of living, I had never once doubted the Scales. Never once thought it could be wrong. That morning, I still believed exactly that. The Scales is right. Entirely right. The problem wasn't the Scales. The problem was what the Scales's being right could make me do.

Our city has a totalizer board too. A large board mounted on the wall beside the proclamation office, showing the whole city's accumulated weight as a single number. People don't usually look at it. Their own number is already more than enough to carry, without minding the whole city's.

That morning, for the first time, I stood in front of it and looked at our city's number. Far from the limit. Safe.

I thought the Reckoning must have a board like this too. The people there must have stood in front of it every morning, checking not their own number but the whole city's. I couldn't imagine, then, what that would feel like. I only understood after I stood in front of one myself — that standing before a board like that, and watching it creep closer to the limit day by day, are two entirely different things.

I just opened the door and went in.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. The Last Line

_Previously_ — Pheugon retraces the rules of the world the Scales
measures: that weight only ever adds, never subtracts; that one person in
his own family carried an exceptional weight. Walking to the proclamation
office, he stops for the first time before the city's totalizer board. He
imagines the Reckoning must have one too, and opens the door.

There's an archive room on the third floor of the proclamation office. A herald can go there to check the full text of any case assigned to him. The ordinary individual cases I usually handled had no full text to speak of — a one-page summary was all there was.

A city case was different.

The clerk at the desk checked my papers and looked at me for a long moment.

"…The Reckoning case."

"…Yes."

"First time, with a city case."

"…Yes."

The clerk went into the back and came out with a thick bundle of paper. Much thicker than the one I'd brought in my bag.

"This is the full text. What you have is the summary. You'll read the summary on proclamation day, but you need to verify against the full text."

I took the bundle. It was heavier than I'd expected.

The reading room had no window. A desk, a chair, one light coming down from above. A soundless room. The whole archive was designed that way — a place where a person confirms, in silence, sentences the Scales has calculated.

I started from the first page.

The opening part was all things I already knew. City name, coordinates, jurisdiction, the date its Scales connection began. The Reckoning was an old city. Its Scales connection was old too. Over a hundred years.

The second part was the basis for calculating accumulated weight. This was where the unfamiliar content began. How the whole city's weight was calculated, how individual weights were summed, even the history of how the summing method itself had changed several times.

Reading that part, I had a strange thought. That the people actually living in that city had no way of knowing the number produced by such a complicated calculation. They must have just looked at the board every morning. No one would have known what was being calculated behind it.

The third part was the sentence itself. Demolition. Forty-day grace period. Method of execution. This part was nearly identical to the summary. Only the sentences were a little longer, a little more legal.

An appendix was attached at the back of the full text. A weight-calculation ledger. A table organized by household, with names all struck out, only numbers remaining. District 12, household 3, accumulated 47. That kind of thing.

I turned through that table for a long while. Pages passed and it didn't end. A hundred and twenty thousand lives strung out in numbers and figures. Beside some numbers the weight was very low. Beside others, very high. Between the low and the high, no explanation at all. They just sat side by side.

Turning through that table, I thought of the man I'd seen in the market that morning — the one people had stepped back from because his number was high. I tried counting how many like him might be in this table, and stopped. I stopped because this time I couldn't count. I could count up to a hundred and twelve. This wasn't a number that could be counted.

The door opened and the supervisor came in sometime in the middle of the afternoon.

"…Still reading."

"…Yes."

The supervisor glanced at the full text on the desk.

"Everyone's like that at first. A city case is a different volume."

I didn't answer, just kept reading. The supervisor stood there a moment, then asked.

"Anything catching on you."

I could have asked, then. About Article 4, Clause 11. But in that moment I chose not to. I didn't know why, at the time. I know now. I was afraid that if I put the question to the supervisor, he would carry the answer away for himself instead of me. It had to be my question. Not yet knowing why, I still wanted to keep it.

"…No." I said. "There's just a lot of it."

The supervisor nodded and left. The door closing sounded unusually loud in the quiet room.

I stopped partway through and found an item that wasn't in the summary. It was labeled "Precedent Reference."

A few case numbers were listed there. Numbers for other cities' cases. I didn't know what they meant. I thought about asking and decided not to. I didn't think it was information I needed right away.

I learned later what that item was. The precedent database. The place the Scales draws on past verdicts to adjust a sentence. I passed those numbers by, that day. I had no idea I'd walk into that room myself, months later, and lay a finger on one of those numbers.

I kept reading. The sentences were short and exact. The Scales's sentences always were. Almost no adjectives. When they appeared, they were only there to describe a figure.

Reading a long while, I noticed something strange. Not once, in this whole document, did the word "why" appear.

The Scales doesn't use why. It uses what, how much, when. Never why. I thought, at the time, that was the Scales's honesty. That it doesn't invent reasons. I think differently now. The Scales doesn't leave out why because it's honest. The question of why is simply outside the Scales's range of calculation. It isn't hiding a reason. It has no idea what a reason is.

The closer I got to the final pages, the quieter the room seemed to feel.

Execution clauses, appeal procedure — a clause that was, in practice, meaningless in a city case. There's no way for a hundred and twenty thousand people to file an appeal all at once. Then, transitional measures. Then, supplementary provisions.

At the end of the supplementary provisions, one paragraph was set in type one size smaller than the rest. I'd already seen it once at home. But in the full text it looked a little different. It wasn't only the smaller type — the letter spacing sat faintly out of line with the body text. As though the entire document had been completed, and this had been slipped in afterward, by someone.

Article 4, Clause 11.

Should this city, within forty days, be confirmed to have turned, this sentence shall be void.

I looked for a footnote beside the line. Every other clause had one — what precedent it came from, when it was enacted, how many times it had been applied.

This clause had no footnote.

Nothing at all. A blank. I stared at that blank for a long while. It was the first time I'd ever seen a blank space in a document the Scales had written. The Scales doesn't leave blanks. When it doesn't know, it writes "unknown." This clause didn't even say unknown. There was simply nothing.

I went back to the desk and asked the clerk.

"…This clause has no footnote."

The clerk took the document and checked it.

"Ah, that one." the clerk said. "That's just how it is. This clause never gets a footnote."

"…Why not?"

"I don't know. I really don't. It's just always been that way."

I stood there a moment longer.

"…Does this clause come up often?"

"Only on whole-city cases. Never on individual ones. And whole-city cases themselves are so rare." The clerk shrugged. "This is only the second time I've seen one in the flesh, myself."

"…And the first?"

"About twenty years ago. When I was new here. The clause was exactly the same, back then. Nobody paid it any mind."

"…What happened, that case?"

The clerk's face went thoughtful for a moment.

"Demolished. That city. Ran out the forty days and was demolished. The clause never triggered."

"…Why not?"

"Who could say." the clerk said, straightening the papers. "Never confirmed, I'd guess. Whatever it took — confirmation, it never happened."

I held onto that answer. Never confirmed. What would have happened if it had been confirmed, nobody knew. It had never been tried.

"…Who was the herald, that time."

"Don't know that either. It's not in the record here. A herald's name doesn't stay on the document. It just says 'the herald.'"

Hearing that, I felt a strange relief. I didn't know why, at the time. I know now. In that moment I first understood that no name would stay attached to the thing I was about to do. And that it didn't feel bad.

Nobody paid it any mind. I heard that and just nodded. I didn't understand, then, what that sentence was doing to me. That "a clause nobody bothers with" could be heard two ways at once — as a dead letter, or as a clause no one had ever tested — I wouldn't understand the difference until that very evening.

I returned the full text and came down the stairs.

The stairs from the third floor to the first are long. A window at every landing, so light crossed my face once with every flight down.

Coming down those stairs, I kept repeating one sentence to myself. "Should this city, within forty days, be confirmed to have turned." Confirmed. Confirmed by whom. I asked myself that question for the first time, right there.

And having asked it, I didn't try to find the answer. That was strange. I was usually the kind of person who couldn't let a curiosity go. But something held me back in front of that question. I know now. It wasn't that I wasn't curious. It was that I was afraid of knowing the answer. I was already, faintly, sensing that the answer might be about myself.

There was a terminal of the Scales in the first-floor lobby. I just walked past it. The second time that day.

Outside, the sun was low. Afternoon. I'd been in that room all day.

Walking home, I lost my way twice on a route I'd walked a hundred times. A familiar street, and still my feet kept straying somewhere else. I learned that day, for the first time, that when your mind is somewhere else, your body goes there too.

An old man tending a stall at the mouth of an alley called out to me. Someone who usually just gave me a light nod in passing.

"…Herald, you're late tonight."

"…Yes."

"Caught a big one, they say."

I stopped walking. So the rumor was already going around.

"…Who told you."

"Rumor, is all. A whole city, wiped off the map." The old man shook his head. "Handing that to a boy. What a world."

I couldn't say anything. The old man didn't wait for an answer either — he went back to arranging his stall.

Walking through that alley, I thought, for the first time, that no one else in this city knew the Reckoning's story as well as I did. I was the only one in this whole city who had read the full text. That table, with a hundred and twenty thousand lives written into it — no one else had seen it, but me.

That thought weighed on me strangely. It weighed on me, and it wasn't unwelcome. I should have found it more frightening, that the weight wasn't unwelcome. I didn't know that yet.

That night, back home, I opened the summary again. The same sentence I'd seen in the full text. But strangely, the summary felt heavier. In the full text, that clause had been one among hundreds. In the summary, there were only two paragraphs — the sentence, and the clause. With only two paragraphs, the weight split in half.

Demolition and void. Just those two, and nothing else. I didn't know, that night, that I would come to stand between them.

I traced the last line of the summary again with my fingertip.

This sentence shall be void.

I don't know how many times I read that sentence over. I didn't count.

The second time that year I'd forgotten to count.

That night I ate dinner with the paper set beside me. Eating alone, the sound of my own spoon rang unusually loud.

The table was built for four. Now only one seat gets used. The other three sat empty. I'd long grown used to seeing those empty seats. That night they felt strange to me again.

Three empty seats — I counted them again that night, for no reason. Three. If the Reckoning was a hundred and twenty thousand, our house was three. I learned, that day, that the size of a number doesn't decide how heavy it feels. A hundred and twenty thousand and three both felt equally heavy, inside me.

No — that isn't quite right. To be honest about it — three was far heavier. A hundred and twenty thousand still had no face.

I opened the paper again. I read Article 4, Clause 11 once more. This time without sound, just following the shape of the words with my mouth. A herald isn't allowed to read a sentence aloud before proclamation day. No rule forbade simply moving your mouth along with it. I used that gap.

That night I mouthed that sentence some dozen times, without sound. No sound came out, but I already knew that was a kind of reading too. I hadn't broken the rule. But I knew, even then, that I'd broken its spirit.

That night I stayed up unusually late. Through the window I watched the neighbors' lights go out one by one. This was a quiet city. No one made noise late at night. Everyone had learned to live quietly, to guard their own weight.

I liked that quiet. I wondered if the Reckoning was a quiet city too, like this one. It wasn't. I already knew that. What kind of city the Reckoning was — I already knew.

Whether I had any right to say I knew that city was another matter entirely. I had never walked a single one of its streets. And yet I was certain I already knew what it was. That night I didn't ask myself where that certainty had come from.

Before putting out the light, I folded the paper again. I pressed the crease once with my fingertip. By now the paper was worn from more folds and unfolds than I could count.

That wear was, strangely, a comfort. It meant the paper had been worn soft by how long I'd stared at that sentence. Staring longer doesn't produce an answer. And still I kept staring.

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. Not Once

_Previously_ — In the archive room, Pheugon reads the Reckoning's sentence
in full. Unlike every other clause, Article 4, Clause 11 carries no
footnote. The clerk tells him that twenty years ago the same clause
existed but was never triggered, and that city was demolished all the
same. That night, Pheugon mouths the clause twelve times without sound.

The next morning I knocked on the supervisor's door.

I had an excuse ready. Checking proclamation procedure. First city case, so wanting to go back over the steps — that shouldn't sound strange.

I repeated that excuse to myself several times outside the door. It felt strange. Normally I'd have just knocked and gone in. That day I stood there a long while with my hand on the handle.

Looking back, that wasn't hesitation. I already knew what I was going to ask. My body just seemed to already sense where that question would take me. Those few seconds with my hand on the door may have been the last few seconds I was still allowed to have decided nothing.

"Come in."

The room was cool as always. The supervisor didn't look up from his papers.

"What is it."

"…Wanted to check something about procedure."

"What."

I paused a moment, then asked.

"…Article 4, Clause 11."

The supervisor looked up from his papers.

"What about it."

"Saw it reading the full text. There's no footnote."

The supervisor leaned back in his chair. His face was weighing something.

"There isn't, no." the supervisor said. "In this city — no, as far as I know, across this whole region — that clause has never actually mattered."

"…Not once?"

"Not once." the supervisor said. "I've done this thirty years. This makes three city cases I've seen. All three times, that clause just sat there on the paper. Nothing ever happened."

I nodded, hearing that. But a different question was rising underneath.

"…Why did nothing happen?"

"Because they were demolished." the supervisor said. "All three cities ran out their forty days and were demolished. The clause never triggered because it never triggered — what other reason would there be."

I know now that the supervisor said that without thinking at all. But how it landed on me, he had no idea. He was calling that clause a dead letter. I heard it a different way.

"…So the clause is just there, then? Meaning nothing at all?"

"That's how rules are." the supervisor said. "A lot of old rules nobody uses anymore. But nobody strikes them out either. No reason to."

"…Who wrote it, that clause?"

The supervisor thought for a moment.

"Don't know. I don't know either. The Scales's articles have just always been there, they say. No one knows who wrote them. When I was new I asked a senior about it — he didn't know either."

"…Isn't that strange? Being applied without anyone knowing it."

"It's strange." the supervisor said. "But have you ever seen the Scales be anything but strange. That's just how it is. It runs whether we understand it or not. That's why it's the Scales."

Something caught on that answer. I couldn't have said exactly what, then.

I can say it now. The supervisor lumped two things together under the same word — "strange." That the Scales runs and keeps running even when it can't be explained, and that one of the Scales's own articles leaves a judgment to a person. But those were two entirely different kinds of strangeness. One is a calculation no one can explain. The other is a spot the calculation couldn't reach, so it was handed to a person instead. The supervisor didn't know the difference. Neither did I, at the time. But I needed to know that difference far more urgently than he did.

"…Why doesn't this clause attach to individual cases?"

"An individual case is one person." the supervisor said. "Whether one person suddenly turned or didn't, there's nothing to confirm. You just watch the next figure come up. Lower, it's lower. Not lower, it isn't."

"…But you said the weight already stacked up never comes back down."

The supervisor glanced at me.

"Right. So the clause isn't needed for an individual. What's already stacked is stacked, and after that, you just don't add more. A city is different. A city is a matter of whole or nothing, so that one clause decides everything."

I turned the thought over. A mercy an individual doesn't need becomes everything for a city. A strange structure.

"…Why was this clause written in the first place, then. If no one was ever going to use it."

The supervisor looked at me a moment, then set down his papers.

"I wondered that too, once. When I was new." the supervisor said. "I asked a senior. He laughed. Said maybe it wasn't written so no one would use it — maybe it was written in case no one could."

"…What does that mean."

"Don't know." the supervisor shrugged. "Just something that got said once. Don't take it too seriously."

I took it very seriously. I turned it over all that day. A clause written in case it couldn't be used. A clause written and left on the page even while believing it would never trigger. Why write a clause like that. Why leave it on paper, knowing no one would ever use it.

I know now. That wasn't a clause the Scales wrote. Which is why the Scales's own logic could never explain it.

"…What does it take, for it to trigger?"

The supervisor looked at me a moment.

"By the text — if the city is confirmed, within forty days, to have turned, that's it."

"…Who confirms it?"

"Not in the text." the supervisor said. "It just says 'confirmed.' Doesn't say who does the confirming."

I think I'd been waiting for that answer. Hearing it only made things more complicated.

"…So if no one confirms it?"

"Then it doesn't happen." the supervisor said, picking his papers back up. "The Scales calculates a city's state every day. But there's no category in that calculation called 'turned.' The Scales measures weight. It can't measure direction. That's why the clause has never triggered. Not because the condition was never met, but—"

The supervisor stopped.

"…No, I don't know. I don't know that part exactly either. It just never happened. Thirty years."

I held onto the end of that sentence for a long time, that day. "Not because the condition was never met, but—" The supervisor had started to say something else and stopped. I wondered, then, what he'd meant to say. I know now. He knew it too, vaguely. That the condition wasn't unmet — there had simply never been a way to confirm it. But he never put that into words, not the whole way through.

"…And this one, then."

"This one, what."

"…The Reckoning. What if it really does turn."

The supervisor looked at me. He paused a moment before answering.

"Pheugon. There've been three city cases, once-a-decade cases. All three times, it didn't happen. You just handle the procedure you've been given, exactly right. Read the clause too, along with everything else, exactly as it's written. What comes after that isn't your job."

"…Yes, sir."

"If you're worried, you don't need to be. That kind of thing doesn't happen."

The supervisor meant that as comfort. To ease my mind. Afraid I might be frightened at the thought of a city of a hundred and twenty thousand people falling, he told me that kind of thing simply doesn't happen.

Hearing it didn't ease my mind. Hearing it, I felt some possibility become clear and sharp for the first time. It doesn't happen. But if the reason it doesn't happen isn't that the condition is never met but that it's never confirmed — then turned around, the sentence read like this too: as long as it's never confirmed, nothing will happen, no matter what.

I didn't know it myself, walking out of that room, that I was already reading the sentence turned around. That wouldn't come into exact words until much later, near the very end of the forty days.

As I was leaving, the supervisor called me back.

"…Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"Why are you asking so much detail about this. The other trainees never ask like this."

I stopped short for a moment.

"…No reason. It's just my first case like this."

The supervisor looked at me a moment, then turned his eyes back to his papers.

"All right. Being thorough is a good thing."

The supervisor must have seen something in my face that day. If I'd ever had the chance to ask him directly, much later, I would have. I never got that chance. What the supervisor saw that day, I still don't know exactly. But I know one thing. He could have stopped me, that day. He didn't.

I went straight back up to the third-floor archive room. The clerk looked a little surprised to see me.

"…Back again."

"…Could I see the past city cases? Three of them, I heard."

The clerk went in and came back out with three case files. Thin ones. Less than half the thickness of the Reckoning's.

I looked through them right there. All three cities had unfamiliar names. Places I'd never heard of. The clerk said their Scales connections had been severed so long ago they no longer even appeared on maps.

All three cases ended the same way — the full forty days passed, and demolition. But one of them, the most recent one, had a strange sentence attached. A short note in the margin of the results report, written by hand, it looked like.

Day thirty-nine: incidents of theft within the city dropped sharply. Observed as a meaningful change, though not reflected in the Scales's figures.

I read that sentence twice.

"…What does this mean?"

The clerk looked at the note and tilted her head.

"Couldn't say. Probably something the case officer jotted down at the time. It's not part of the official record, though. Margin notes carry no effect."

I traced that note with my fingertip. Day thirty-nine. One day before the city fell. A sharp drop in theft meant that someone who'd been stealing had stopped. But that change hadn't been reflected in the Scales's figures. The weight already stacked up doesn't come back down.

And the next day, that city was demolished.

I wondered who had written that note. That person must have seen something too, the way I had. That the city was changing. But they only wrote it in the margin. Never said it officially. Why not. I didn't know the answer, then. I know now. That person had no way to confirm it either. What you see and what you can declare are two different problems.

The other two files had no margin note like it. They just read, cleanly, "forty days elapsed, clause not triggered, demolition executed." I wondered what the difference was between those two and the third. Maybe those two cities really had changed nothing at all. Or maybe they had changed, and no one had written it in the margin.

What frightened me most was that there was no way to tell the two apart. If it isn't in the record, a thing that happened becomes a thing that never happened. I thought for the first time that what the Scales fails to record might far outnumber what it does.

I held the files a while longer before returning them.

"…Thank you."

"Come back if you need to." the clerk said. "But I've never seen a herald dig into a city case like this before."

I didn't answer. I went down the stairs.

I walked the corridor past the wall where the herald roster hung. Numbers beside the names. Beside mine, 112. Beside the others, mostly two-digit numbers. Of them, only three on this list had ever handled a city case. The three cases the supervisor had mentioned.

I looked for those three names. Unfamiliar names. People from a generation well above mine. They must have read that clause too. Had they stopped, at that last line, the way I had? I wondered, but there was no one to ask.

I would never come to know what those three people felt, reading that line. Whether they saw something in it the way I did, or simply read it as procedure and moved on. But I can guess one thing. None of them can have been to that city what I was to mine. I didn't know it yet, that day, that this fact set me apart from the other three in a place entirely different from where they stood.

Walking out of the proclamation office, I looked up at the sky. Overcast. It looked like rain was coming.

I stood a moment at the door. I counted the days left until proclamation day. A notice for the Reckoning case was posted on the proclamation office board. Twenty-one days from today.

Twenty-one days. Seeing that number, I began, for the first time, counting time. Until then I'd thought of the clause only as a rule with a triggering condition. From that moment it was different. I felt the clause as something with a deadline. Having a deadline meant there was someone who could do something within it. I didn't yet think that someone was me. But that the clause needed a "someone" inside it at all — that much, I was already feeling that day.

Rain started on the way home. I had no umbrella. I just walked through it. Feeling my wet clothes grow heavier, I felt, strangely, glad of that weight. At least this was a weight the Scales wasn't measuring.

That night I began planning to run. Though at the time, I didn't call it that. I called it, "needing time to think." I know now. That wasn't needing time to think. I had already finished thinking.

And what that thought had been, I kept hidden even from myself, for a very long time.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. That City

_Previously_ — Pheugon presses the supervisor about Article 4, Clause 11.
The supervisor tells him it has never once triggered, in three city cases
across thirty years, and tries to reassure him there's nothing to worry
about. Pheugon hears it the opposite way — that as long as it's never
confirmed, nothing will ever happen, no matter what.

I have never been to the Reckoning.

And yet I know the Reckoning. I know that sounds like a contradiction. To explain it, I have to go back three years.

I've tried to write this several times and stopped. I still don't know if I can write all of it, even now. I can't write all of it. Even I haven't finished sorting through it. But I need to write this much, or no one will ever understand why I ran.

Three years ago, a person stayed in our house for a time.

Someone from the Reckoning. I won't write the name here. Not yet. I didn't know exactly, then, what business had brought that person to our city, and I don't fully know even now. I heard it had something to do with some matter between the two cities. That's about all I remember.

My father took that person in. That was the kind of man he was. He was good about letting in strangers.

I still don't want to call that my father's fault. It really was a good thing about him. I learned, then, that a good quality doesn't always lead to a good result.

The room that person used had been a storeroom. My father hurriedly put a bed in and made it a room. I sometimes passed that door and saw light through the crack. That person wrote something late into the night. He said he was copying over my father's storeroom ledger. Verification work, he called it.

I wasn't curious, then, what kind of verification that was. I thought it was adult business. That was explanation enough for a twelve-year-old.

I'm curious now. Curious far too late. I can still see, when I close my eyes, that light leaking through that crack in the door. What numbers passed back and forth under that light, I may never know, for the rest of my life.

For a few months, that person ate dinner with our family. Talked with my brother and me. When we asked about the Reckoning, he'd answer with a smile.

"Nobody's ever wronged there." that person said. "The Scales measures everything exactly."

I believed that. I thought precise meant good.

I'd come to think of that line again, three years later. I didn't know what it meant, then. I still don't fully know now. I only remember that his face, just after saying it, trembled for the briefest moment.

What happened after that, I won't write out in detail here. Voices heard through the door, evenings when my father went silent for days, my mother crying alone in the kitchen — I have pieces like that. I can't string them together. I was twelve then, and grown-ups know how not to finish a sentence in front of a child.

The one thing I know for certain is this. After that, something was recorded on the Scales. Someone in our house had their weight rise. Exactly who, exactly how much, I don't know either. I never asked. I didn't have the courage to ask.

I believe that weight was accurate. The Scales is always accurate. But being accurate, and my knowing the full account of that calculation, are two different things. I don't know the full account. I still don't know it now. I may not know it even by the time this book is finished.

I wrote that I heard my mother crying alone in the kitchen exactly once. To be precise, it was twice. The second time was much quieter. It frightened me more than the first. Crying loudly at least gives whoever hears it the illusion they might be able to do something. Crying quietly doesn't even offer that illusion. I stood outside that door and did nothing at all.

I didn't knock on that door. I still don't know if that was right. At the time it felt like I shouldn't. I thought it would only make things harder for my mother, if she knew I'd heard her crying. Thinking about it now, that may have been a judgment made for my own sake. Pretending not to hear was easier for me than hearing and still doing nothing.

That winter, just once, I saw my father's hand at a terminal of the Scales. He put his hand up. A number appeared on the screen. He looked at it for a long time. I was standing beside him. I couldn't see the number from where I stood.

"…Father."

"…It's fine." my father said. Before I'd even asked anything.

I've turned that "it's fine" over for three years now. That wasn't said to me. My father was saying it to himself. I noticed that, at the time, and never once told him that I had.

It was around then that I started checking my own weight every day. In Chapter 2 I called that just a habit. Here I should write it precisely. It wasn't a habit. It was a beginning. From that winter on, I couldn't bear it if my number so much as moved. Keeping it low, lower still, felt like the one thing I could still do.

I think I said something like this to myself at the time — that since one person in our house had already had their weight rise, the rest of us had to make sure we never let ours rise, no matter what. Whether that's sound reasoning, I know now it wasn't at all. The Scales doesn't calculate by household. My being low doesn't lower my father's number. But I was doing that arithmetic at the time. A child's arithmetic. I never managed to let it go, even after I grew up.

I wrote, in Chapter 1, about the day Had asked me why I never stopped. I wrote that I couldn't answer. In truth, there was an answer. I couldn't afford to stop. The moment I stopped, I felt something would catch up to me. What that was, I didn't know precisely at the time either. I know now. It was that light, leaking through the crack of that door, that winter.

The day that person left, I walked out to the edge of town to see him off.

I still don't quite know why I did. I think I wanted to ask something. But standing in front of him, in the end, I couldn't ask anything at all.

"…Sir."

That person turned around.

"…Yes, Pheugon."

I stood there a long while before I spoke.

"…Take care."

That person looked at me for a moment. As if about to say something, then deciding against it.

"…All right." that person said. "You too. Be well."

He turned and walked away after that. I watched that back for a long time.

I still remember that back today. I didn't hate that person, then. I didn't know how to hate yet. Hatred grew much later, very slowly. I couldn't even say where it took root.

I didn't go into the room he'd left behind for a while. It was months before I even went in. The bed was still there, the room already reverting to a storeroom's look. A few sheets of paper had fallen in a corner. I picked them up. Dense with numbers. What numbers, I didn't know. I set the paper back down. Somehow it felt like something I shouldn't hold onto.

Thinking about it now, I wish I'd kept that paper. But I was twelve then, not old enough to make that call. That paper is nowhere now. It was probably thrown out, sometime, when the room was cleared out again.

I wrote about the table in Chapter 3. That the table which once sat four now sees only one seat used. That table had been there since before that person ever came. I remember, every time I look at it, the seat he sat in too. He always sat to my father's right. My brother sat across from him.

After my brother left, I wouldn't let anyone sit in that seat for a while. When my mother asked why, I couldn't answer.

I think I believed, back then, that as long as that seat stayed empty, a place would still be kept for my brother to return to. A child's belief. My brother never came back, and in the end that seat just stayed empty.

After that, our family came apart.

My brother left home that winter. I still don't know where he went. He left without a word to me.

I remember the night he left. He turned back once, at the door. I couldn't tell if he was looking at me or just checking his direction. That was the first night I learned, on my own, how not to cry out loud. Not taught — learned. No one showed me how. I simply figured it out for myself that night.

My mother went to another city the following year, where relatives lived. She asked me to come with her. I didn't go.

By then I was already training to become a herald, and I told myself I didn't want to give that up. That was the explanation I gave myself. It probably wasn't the real reason. What the real reason was, I still don't have the confidence to say, even now.

My father is still in this city. He doesn't live with me. He lives alone, on the other side of town. I see him now and then. Not often. Even when we meet, we don't talk about it.

We both learned, somehow, how to avoid that subject. We learned it well.

The last time we met was a few days before I was given the Reckoning case. My father said little, as usual. At parting, he said just one thing.

"…What case are you reading these days."

"…A small one." I lied. It wasn't a lie yet, then — I hadn't received the Reckoning case at that point — but in that moment, I think I was already practicing how to hide something.

My father nodded.

"…Take care of yourself."

I know that was the best my father could say to me. He wasn't a man who could say more. That isn't his fault.

Parting from my father that day, I thought, for the first time, whether he too still wore that face — the one where something caves in — whenever he heard the name Reckoning. I didn't want to find out, so I never once said that name in front of him, that day.

In Chapter 1 I wrote about the lie I told in the supervisor's room. When he asked, "Do you have some connection to the Reckoning," I answered, "No."

That was a lie. Now that I'm here, I can see exactly how much of a lie it was. In that moment I played at being someone with no connection to the Reckoning at all. It was the opposite. I was one of the people most deeply entangled with that city. Without ever having set foot on a single one of its streets.

Why did the supervisor ask that question. Maybe he'd noticed some expression that crossed my face whenever the Reckoning came up. If there was such an expression, I never knew it myself. I always believed I hid my face perfectly. Looking back, that was probably only my own illusion.

The Scales calculated that matter. Calculated it precisely, I'm sure. I don't doubt that.

Precise, and nothing came back. My brother didn't come back, my mother didn't come back, our house never became four again. I thought for a long time about how those two sentences could stand together, and in the end I stopped thinking about it.

No — that isn't true. I didn't stop thinking about it. I went on thinking, in a different way. I decided to pour all that thought into one name — the Reckoning. I set that whole city where that one person had once sat. That wasn't fair. I know that now. I didn't, then.

The day I received the Reckoning's sentence document, my hands didn't shake. I wrote, in Chapter 1, that this was strange.

It isn't strange to me now. I felt, in that moment, for the first time, that the Scales was right. Just as the weight it had set on my house was accurate, so would this weight, set on the whole of the Reckoning, be accurate too. That city falling felt to me like justice. A city whose name I hadn't even said aloud for three years, finally getting what was owed it.

But I never once asked myself, then, whether I had any right to call that justice. Without asking, I found the clause. The clause that could void that justice.

The moment I read that clause, I thought exactly one thing. That it must never trigger. Whether that thought came from fear, or from something else entirely, I didn't ask myself, then.

That night I stared out the window for a long time. Our neighborhood was dark. Somewhere far off, on the other side of the city, I imagined a light my father had lit alone. I looked that way for a long time.

That night I thought of the word mercy for the first time. To be precise, I admitted for the first time that Article 4, Clause 11 meant mercy. Until then I'd only thought of it as a rule, a procedure, a possibility. The moment I called it mercy, something changed. Mercy had never come to our house. My father's weight was accurate, and because it was accurate, no one ever shaved a single point off it. I was taught that was as it should be. And yet for the Reckoning, for that city, the door of mercy had stood open from the very start.

I couldn't bear that unfairness. That night I didn't call it unfairness, and I didn't call it anger, either. I just quietly decided. That door had to stay shut. I would do whatever I could, so that no one ever opened it.

I didn't know, that night, that the decision would take the shape of running.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. The Night Before

_Previously_ — At the bottom of the Reckoning's sentence document,
Pheugon found Article 4, Clause 11. If the city turns within forty days,
the sentence becomes void. He admitted, for the first time, that this was
mercy — and that this mercy had never once come to his own house. That
night he made a quiet decision: that door had to stay shut.

Departure was three days off.

The supervisor had set it that way. Two days' travel to the Reckoning, a day's rest on arrival, then the proclamation. The forty days begin the moment I read the first line in that city's square. So the days I spend traveling don't count against the forty. That's simply passage.

I don't remember well how I spent those three days.

It's strange. I remember all hundred and twelve of my sentences. The merchant's face, Had stopping three times on his own name, even the cool air in the supervisor's room. But those three days are a blur. I must have already been half a different person, by then. Someone who makes memories and someone who has decided not to leave any live different lives.

The third night came. The next morning, I was due to set out on the road to the Reckoning.

On the second day, I went to the training hall.

There was no reason to go. I was already a full herald; there was nothing left to learn in that room. And yet I walked past it and opened the door anyway.

The room was empty. High ceiling, no windows. The room where my own voice used to come back to me. I stood in the middle of it and said nothing at all.

Our cohort's names were still up on the wall. Had's name too. Not erased. Not erasing the names of the washed-out was this city's way. Weight gets erased. Records don't.

I stood a moment in front of that name. Had's question came back to me. Why don't you ever stop. That night, for the first time, I tried to think of an answer. Because if I stop, something catches up to me. But that night I already knew. I wasn't trying to stop, then. I was about to veer off onto an entirely different road. That was a different thing from not stopping. I didn't know the difference, at the time.

I think I wanted to confirm something, standing there. Whether this room still recognized me. I know how that sounds. But that's exactly the frame of mind I was in, standing there. The room didn't answer. The wall was just a wall. With no one to make a sound, there was no sound to send back.

I walked past the supervisor's door on my way out of that room. It was shut. There was someone moving inside. I didn't knock.

What would have changed if I had. I've asked myself that question many times since. The answer is always the same. I wouldn't have known. And because I wouldn't have known, I still sometimes picture myself standing in front of that door with my hand raised, and staying that way. A hand that, in fact, I never raised.

On the morning of the third day, I watched from my window as the cart for the Reckoning was made ready. The driver was watering the horse. One of the crates loaded onto that cart would have my name tag on it. A herald's travel provisions.

I didn't watch the cart for long. I looked once and closed the curtain.

I packed my bag.

What a herald carries on the road is fixed. A change of clothes, a water flask, a wax envelope for the original sentence, and a wristband proving identity. The wristband connects to the Scales. Crossing between cities, it has to be scanned for the passage to be logged.

I packed to that list. And on top of it, I added things not on the list.

Six loaves of dried bread. A second change of clothes. Kindling. I left the wristband out.

I knew exactly what it meant, leaving that wristband behind. Without it, I'm not "recorded" anywhere. No city keeps me on record as having passed through. I wanted to call that an oversight. It wasn't an oversight.

When the bag was packed, I pushed it under the bed. And I sat there a long while, looking at the space under it.

I set the wax envelope on top too. The one holding the Reckoning's original sentence. That was an item on the list. A herald must always keep the sentence on his person. Packing it, I asked myself. Why am I taking this. I'm not even going to read it.

I had an answer then, and I have one now. I couldn't throw it away. Throwing it away wouldn't have made it cease to exist — it would still be sitting somewhere in the Scales's record, waiting to be assigned to someone else. I couldn't stand that. Neither that someone else might read that sentence, nor that someone else might discover that clause. I decided, then, that this document should belong to exactly one person, in the whole world. Me.

It was a strange kind of possessiveness, thinking about it now. Deciding to hold, all by myself, a document that could save an entire city. At the time I believed that was caution.

In the evening I thought about going to see my father.

To be precise, I'd already decided not to go, and only pretended to think it over so I could confirm that decision again. I changed my clothes. Walked as far as the door. Went back inside.

I've thought a long while about why I didn't see my father that night. I wanted to write that I was afraid — afraid he'd look me in the eye and know. But I know now. It wasn't that I was afraid my father would stop me. I was afraid he wouldn't. If my father had said just one thing — "all right, don't go" — I would have used that as my excuse, and truly not gone. I didn't need an excuse. I already didn't want to go.

I took out a sheet of paper instead. I meant to write a letter.

I wrote three lines and crossed them out. Wrote two more and crossed those out too. In the end nothing was left at all.

I didn't understand, for a long time, why I couldn't write that letter. I understand now. A letter needs a reason written into it. And I couldn't write my real reason down. Writing it would have meant admitting to myself that it was real. I wasn't ready to admit that yet. So I chose to leave nothing at all. It felt, at the time, like the more honest choice. It was, in fact, the least honest one.

Instead I walked all the way to the other side of the city. To the mouth of the alley where my father lived.

I didn't go up to the house itself. I stood at the mouth of the alley and looked at the window. The light was on. My father stayed up late.

I watched that light for about ten minutes. I started toward it twice, and stopped both times. I still wonder. Did my father know I was standing in that alley. I never asked. I don't think I'll ever get to ask.

The light shifted once, as if my father had crossed in front of the window. I saw that shadow and turned away.

I didn't say goodbye to my father, that night. Even writing this sentence now, it's the thing that troubles me most. Everything else I've managed to put into words. This one, I still can't.

I left the house past midnight.

I didn't lock the door. There was no need to. Only one person lived in this house now, and that person had nothing left to take.

The street was quiet. In our city, the lamps went out one by one as night fell. I walked through that dark with ease. Fifteen years of living on this street.

There was a terminal of the Scales on every corner. Every time I passed one, I pushed my hands deeper into my pockets.

I had no intention of raising my hand. My body moved that way regardless. Habit outlives reason. That night I felt my body still moving as the old me, while my mind had already gone somewhere else.

I walked not toward the Reckoning, but the other way.

The road passed through the market. Full of people by day, only bare stalls remained by night. The spot where the merchant who'd tampered with his scale weights used to stand was there too. My first proclamation. That man came back into the market six months later, and I saw it from a distance. He didn't recognize me, that time. Even if he had, he probably wouldn't have let on.

Passing that spot, I had a strange thought for the first time. That man had served out his full disposition and gone back to standing in the market, while I was now running in exactly the opposite direction. He had stood his ground before the Scales; I wasn't standing mine. I didn't hold that thought long. Holding it felt like it would stop my feet.

There was a gatekeeper. Even at night, someone stands watch.

I had to pass him. Without the wristband, I couldn't pass through officially. But the gatekeeper knew me. Proclamation office people get through gates on their faces alone.

"…Where you off to?"

The gatekeeper asked. His voice was sleepy.

"…An errand." I said. "By the supervisor's orders."

A lie. My second lie that night. The first had been in the supervisor's room, saying "no" when he asked whether I had some connection to the Reckoning. The Scales would have calculated each of those two lies. Precisely. And what those two lies together would carry me toward — that, the Scales could never have calculated. It has no category for that.

The gatekeeper nodded. He didn't ask anything more. That I was a herald, that I was under the supervisor's orders, was enough. No one suspects a herald. A herald is someone who reads without feeling, and someone who reads without feeling is assumed to have no reason to run.

I understood, in that moment, for the first time, that the blankness I'd trained into my face was protecting me. The supervisor had taught me how not to stop. He hadn't taught it only to be used while reading. That night I put that same face to a different use.

The steps carrying me out past the gate, I'd expected to feel heavy. They didn't.

That struck me as strange. This was the step turning my back on a city I'd lived in fifteen years. Something should have caved in. But I only put one foot in front of the other. Looking back, I don't think that was strength. I think I had already been practicing this step for a long time. In my mind. My body was only following the practice.

The gatekeeper called something after me. I think it was a good night. I didn't turn around. I only raised one hand.

Half an hour's walk from the gate, an old road led down to the riverside.

At the end of that road was a well, I'd learned during training. Not part of the formal curriculum. A senior had mentioned it once, in passing. That the wells watering each city are joined underground to one another. Originally a waterway, and for a long time now, a road people walk too.

"The authorities don't know?" I'd asked.

"They know." the senior had said. "They just don't bother to close it off. Whoever uses that road is usually someone who wants to leave the city. Letting them go is a simpler calculation for the Scales than stopping them."

I'd let that pass at the time. I thought it had nothing to do with me.

I'd forgotten that story for three years. And yet that night, walking out past the gate, it was the very first thing that came back to me. What I'd thought forgotten hadn't been erased. It had just been waiting, quietly, until it was needed. I wanted to believe that was coincidence. I don't believe that anymore.

The well stood at the end of the riverside road. It looked long abandoned. Moss on the coping stones, an old plank laid across it instead of a cover.

I stood in front of it and hesitated for a long time.

I wrote hesitated, but even now I find it hard to say exactly what I hesitated over. It wasn't whether to go in. I'd already resolved to go in. What I hesitated over was something else — that doing this meant I could never be the person I was before, again. Crossing that line took time.

I pushed the plank aside. Cold air rose from inside. Mixed in with the smell of water was another smell, not water at all.

I lowered my bag first, tied to a rope. I heard the sound of it vanishing into the dark. No splash. Which meant the drop to the bottom was quite deep.

I looked in. I couldn't see anything. My fingers found iron rungs set into the wall. Less a stairway than a ladder.

In that moment I thought of the road to the Reckoning. The cart, the highway, the inn we'd stop at along the way, the procedures waiting on arrival that couldn't even be called a welcome. That road was set, recorded, predictable. This well was the opposite. No one knew. No one was waiting for me. I liked that difference. Looking back, that was the trouble. I chose not the safe road, but the one no one knew about.

And I set my foot down.

I remember that moment with strange clarity. The touch of the first iron rung against the sole of my foot. Cold, and slick. Stepping onto it, I thought, for the first time, how deep this stairway might go.

Whether that thought came from fear or from curiosity, I couldn't answer that night. I can answer now. I was afraid. And at the same time I was calculating. The two carried the same weight in me, in that moment.

I stepped onto the second rung. The third. I heard the plank settle back over the opening above me. I hadn't pushed it shut myself. Might have been the wind. Might not have been.

I still remember that sound. Wood meeting stone, short and dull. That was the last sound of the world above ground I heard that night. I gave it no meaning at the time. I hear it differently now. As though someone had closed a door for me. Whether that helped me go, or kept me from coming back — I still don't know.

The sound of water grew nearer beneath my feet. I hadn't touched it yet. The air against my toes just grew steadily damper.

I climbed down. I did not look back.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. The Conduit

_Previously_ — The night before leaving for the Reckoning, Pheugon packed
in secret and left the house without telling anyone. He turned away from
his father's door without a goodbye, and passed the city gate wearing a
herald's blank, trained face. At the end of the old riverside road, he
climbed down into an abandoned well. He heard the plank settle back over
the opening above him, and stepped into the dark.
