There were twenty-two iron rungs.

I counted them going down. There was nothing else to do but count. My foot touched water on the twenty-second rung.

Cold. Far colder than I'd expected.

I've never forgotten that temperature. My body lodged its first protest right then. My mind had made its decision long before. My feet were only just then realizing what was happening.

The floor wasn't level. The water came to my ankles, then to my knees, then back to my ankles. I felt my way along the wall as I walked.

The wall was stone. Smooth. Worked by hands, for a long time. Not a natural cave. Someone had made this. A very long time ago.

Feeling that wall, I wondered, for the first time, who had dug it. Dug to carry water, presumably — but then why dig it wide enough for a person to walk through? Too precise a width to be coincidence. I never got an answer to that question, walking this road for a long while after.

The tunnel didn't run straight. It curved gently, then bent sharply, and sometimes split into two. I always chose whichever branch had the stronger current. No real reason. I just thought the side the water ran harder must lead somewhere.

The ceiling height wasn't constant either. Some stretches I had to bend at the waist to pass through; others my hand couldn't reach even stretched all the way up. I wondered what the difference meant. Had the stretches meant to carry more water been dug wide, and the ones meant to carry less, narrow? Or had it simply followed the shape of the ground?

I made those questions in the dark, alone, and folded them back up, alone. No one there to answer. That was the first time I learned, in my body, that making a question and getting an answer might have nothing to do with each other. Until then I'd lived believing that a question, once asked, brought an answer along with it. That had always been true at the training hall. Ask, and the supervisor answered. No one was here.

The sound was strange.

Water sounded from everywhere. But I couldn't tell the direction. It seemed to come from behind, then from ahead. The sound bouncing off the walls mixed with the sound of the actual current.

I understood, for the first time, that in this dark, my ears grew just as useless as my eyes. I thought of the room at the training hall, where sound came back to me. At least that had one wall. Here the walls were on every side, at every angle. I couldn't even count how many times a sound bounced before it returned.

I never let my hand off the wall. The moment I did, I felt I'd lose my direction entirely.

The wall sometimes had grooves cut into it. Feeling along with my fingertips, I found lines of even depth. At first I took them for cracks in the rock. But the spacing was too regular. Someone had cut them on purpose.

I only learned later that those grooves were markers. Left by people who traveled this conduit often, so as not to lose their way. I didn't know how to read them, then. They weren't letters. It was a language read only with the fingertips, and I had never learned that language. I only understood, much later, that the answer had already been carved into the path I was walking.

I walked a long while. There was no way to measure time. No sun, so I couldn't tell day from night either. I just counted steps. A hundred, two hundred, and at some point I stopped counting that too.

I don't remember exactly the moment I stopped counting. That's strange. I was someone who counted everything countable. A hundred and twelve sentences, a hundred and eleven erased words. But in that dark, counting lost its meaning. The numbers weren't taking me anywhere.

At one point I thought I heard another sound.

Footsteps. Not mine.

I stopped. Held my breath. Nothing but the sound of water.

I still can't be sure, even now, whether that was real. This conduit is said to connect several cities. It wouldn't have been strange for someone besides me to be in it. Someone wanting to leave a city too. The senior had said as much. If so, whoever's footsteps those were, they were here for the same reason I was. I didn't call out to that person. That person didn't call out to me. In the dark, people learn on their own, somehow, how to pretend not to notice each other.

I felt that presence a few more times after that. The water rippling in a different rhythm for a moment, or something knocking, far off. I never once confirmed it. I never once walked toward the sound. I never crossed paths with anyone, the whole time. Maybe that too was one of this road's rules. A rule the water arranged by itself, so that no two people would ever meet.

At the time I thought that was fortunate. I wouldn't have known what to say if I'd met someone. Now I think differently. If someone else had been in that dark besides me, that person probably had no way to speak to anyone either. We were both avoiding each other in the same water.

The footsteps didn't come again. I walked on.

I was hungry.

I took out the dried bread and ate it. Tore it with wet hands, chewed in the dark. I couldn't taste anything at all. Just the sensation of it going down my throat.

I had a strange thought, then. What were the people of the Reckoning eating right now. I knew the forty days hadn't even begun for them yet. They must be eating dinner right now, not knowing whether I would come, or wouldn't. I let that thought go before it went further. I didn't want to picture that faceless hundred and twenty thousand again.

I finished the bread and opened my water flask. There was water enough all around me. I found that funny. I wasn't rationing the flask because I had no water to drink — I was rationing it because I had no way of knowing whether this water around me was safe to drink.

When the flask was down to half, I sealed it again. I had to save it. I didn't know how many days this would take.

It was then I began, for the first time, to calculate. Half a flask left. Five loaves of bread remaining. How much to use per day, to hold out how many days. I realized, then, that this calculation calmed me. Numbers were the one thing that answered me, in that dark. Ask, and there was always, without fail, one answer.

I only learned there was such a thing as a sense of direction after I lost it.

Above ground, you just know where you are — by the sun's position, the wind's direction, the shape of a familiar building. Down here there was none of that. Every wall looked the same. The water ran every direction at once. I couldn't even tell whether I was getting closer to the Reckoning or farther away.

I thought, that day, that this was frightening. I think differently now. I hadn't lost my direction. I had never had one to begin with. I entered this dark with no plan for where to go once I'd avoided the Reckoning — only the thought that I would go somewhere that wasn't the Reckoning. For someone with no direction, losing a sense of direction is only pretending to lose something that was never there.

Another fork came up. This time I picked left, with no certainty either way, flipping a coin in my head.

I've gone back over that choice many times since. I wrote that there was no reason for picking left. Was that really true? Thinking about it now, I think I noticed, without quite realizing it, that the water sounded a little louder on the left. That a stronger current would lead somewhere bigger, I must have guessed, somewhere in myself. If so, it wasn't a choice with no reason at all. I was calculating even then. Only not clearly enough to call it calculation.

An hour, maybe, or two. Time had already lost its meaning. I felt my legs give out. Walking through water was many times harder than walking on land. The water gripped my legs and wouldn't let go.

Once my foot slipped and I went in to the knee. My bag nearly got soaked. I just barely caught it and hauled it up.

In that moment I made a sound for the first time. Not a scream. Just a sharp intake of breath. Hearing it bounce off every wall and come back, I felt, strangely, reassured. At least I'd confirmed this dark wasn't entirely empty. My own sound, at least, came back.

The water kept getting deeper.

From ankle to shin, from shin toward knee. I lifted the bag above my head. The wax envelope couldn't get wet. Whatever was inside it, it still had to be mine.

Writing this sentence, I ask myself. Why hold onto it so carefully. It would have been so much easier just to let it go in the water. I think I believed, then, that protecting that document meant protecting my own rightness. As long as I held it, I wasn't a fugitive — only "holding" it. I told myself that lie.

The fingertips resting on the wall began to ache with cold. I didn't know yet that long enough in water does that to you. My body was cooling, and I only registered it as cold.

Ahead, the sound of the water changed.

Different from anything I'd heard so far. Louder, and more regular. Like something large blocking the water.

I slowed my steps.

Very faint light began to show. Not total darkness — something leaking in from somewhere up ahead. I walked toward it.

Seeing that light, I felt relieved. Looking back now, I laugh at how simple that was. Light didn't mean safe. I didn't know that yet.

The tunnel widened. The ceiling rose, and instead of walls, thick stone pillars stood at intervals. Something like a way station. The current split here and ran in several directions. Somewhere above, a small crack let in a thread of faint light.

I stood beneath that light for a moment. Looked up. The crack was no wider than a finger. And yet the light coming through that narrow crack felt brighter than any darkness I'd walked through so far. My eyes stung.

I stood looking up at that shaft of light for a long time. Who might be passing above it, right now. Does whoever's standing on the ground just above that crack know there's a road like this beneath their feet. I let time pass on thoughts like that. It had been a while since I'd had anything to do besides walk.

And at the end of that station, a large iron structure stood blocking the waterway.

A sluice gate. I didn't know, then, that's what it was called. I just thought of it as a large door of some kind. It stood open. Water flowed freely beneath it. Higher up on the door was an iron wheel with a handle. I could only guess, then, what it opened and closed.

I stood in front of it and caught my breath. A little farther and I thought I'd reach another city. Which city, I didn't know. I didn't want to know. Anywhere but the Reckoning would do.

I felt something like relief, in that moment, for the first time. A mistaken relief. I had no idea how far I'd already come, what time it was, or by what schedule that gate operated. I was only reassured because I'd seen light. People are reassured that easily.

Somewhere, a very low mechanical hum sounded. Distant, regular. I didn't know what it was. It was nearly lost in the sound of the water.

I know now. That was some device, somewhere, running the whole of this conduit. At the time I just took it for part of the water's noise and let it pass. There are things in this world that keep running, always, without anyone paying them any mind. That day, I was standing right in front of one of them.

I moved to where the water ran shallower and passed beneath the sluice gate.

Past the gate, the tunnel narrowed again. The station's light fell away behind me, and ahead was dark once more. I stepped into that dark again. This time it frightened me a little less. My body seemed to have grown a little used to the dark.

That familiarity was an illusion — I would learn that only a few steps further. The dark isn't something you get used to. I only wanted to believe I had.

A few steps on, I turned to look back. The light from where the gate stood was still visible. Very small now.

I turned my back on that light and walked forward again. My legs were heavy. I adjusted my bag on my shoulder, put a hand to the wall. At the next fork, I'd pick a side again, with no reason for it. I wasn't afraid of that. I still didn't know, then, exactly what I ought to have been afraid of.

Writing this chapter, I keep coming back to the same sentence. I didn't know, then. I didn't know, then. When this book is finished, I want to count how many times I've written that sentence. I imagine it will be too many to count. Knowing something, and having known it for a long time, are different things. I already knew a great deal, in that dark. I just wasn't ready yet to call it knowing.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. The Gate Closes

_Previously_ — Pheugon walked for hours through the dark conduit, with no
way to gauge direction or time. Once he sensed someone's presence but
could never confirm it. He passed the first sluice gate, standing open,
and felt a brief relief. He didn't know yet that the relief was
misplaced.

I met the second sluice gate a long while after that.

By then I was tired enough to have forgotten the difference between walking and standing still. My legs didn't feel like my own. I'd lost count of how many hours I'd dragged my feet through that water. I'd stopped counting long before.

I still remember that exhaustion in my body. Not a muscle ache — an ache in the bone. A kind of tired that four hours of daily reading at the training hall had never taught me.

At the training hall, the hard part was my throat. Here it was my legs, my back, my fingertips. I learned that day, for the first time, that a whole body can grow tired — not any one part of it, but the fact of having a body at all.

The current kept pushing at my calves. My body got no rest even standing still. I had to keep bracing against that current just to stay upright.

This gate was different from the first.

Shut. Completely.

An enormous steel plate blocked the tunnel. Floor to ceiling, wall to wall. Barely a gap. Water only slipped through a small crack at the bottom.

I stood in front of it and felt it with my hand. Cold, slick. The same iron wheel as the first gate's was mounted here too. I tried to turn it. It didn't move.

I threw everything I had into turning it, that time. It didn't budge. Looking back, of course it didn't. That wheel wasn't built to be turned by hand. I didn't know that yet, and because I didn't know it, I kept trying. Trying something you know can't be done, and trying something without knowing it can't, are two different things. I was the latter.

I pushed at the wheel with both hands, then with my shoulder. Again and again. The inside of my palms scraped raw. The wheel didn't so much as tremble. It sat there like part of the wall itself, as if it had never been a thing meant to turn at all.

Far off, I heard that same low mechanical hum again. This time it felt a little closer. Regular, and indifferent.

Hearing that sound, a strange certainty settled in me. Whatever was making that sound had no idea I existed. I thought about calling out toward it, and decided not to. No one shouts at a machine. I already knew, by instinct, that it would be useless.

I tried shouting.

"…Hello!"

My voice went out and came back. Bounced back and forth so many times that by the end it barely sounded like my voice at all. No one answered.

I called again. Louder this time. Until my throat felt like it was tearing.

Only my own voice came back.

I thought of the training hall then, for the first time. The room where my own voice returned to me. The supervisor said it was so I could grow used to it. Used to it. That out there, no one answers. I had only ever taken that as training for reading. That it was also how to live — I learned that for the first time, in front of this gate.

It wasn't only true for reading. There are moments in living, too, when no one answers. The supervisor probably never meant to teach that far. But the training ended up carrying me here, all the same.

I sank down where I stood.

I thought of turning back. Retracing my steps, I thought I could find my way to the first station. But in that moment I realized. I didn't remember how many forks I'd passed.

That was the quietest moment in this whole chapter. No screaming, no tears. Just, quietly, the fact settling into place — that I didn't know the way back. Some fears make no sound. They just take their seat as fact.

I retraced my steps a little. Twenty paces, and another fork appeared. I couldn't tell whether it was the same fork I'd passed before. Every fork looked exactly alike.

I came back to the gate.

The station — I'd decided to call that wide space that. From there, at least three paths branched off. I couldn't be certain of any of them. Retracing my steps might carry me deeper in, not out.

In that moment I made a strange decision. To stop moving at all. Better to stay in one place and wait for the door to open than to wander further astray by choosing wrong. I still don't know if that decision was right. But making it, strangely, I felt easier. Having nothing left to choose was, somehow, a relief.

I settled into the shallow water right in front of the gate. Back against the wall, knees drawn up, and sat.

The light leaking from the crack above seemed to dim a little at a time. It might have been my imagination. But watching that change, I felt for the first time that time was still passing, even in this dark. I simply had no way to measure it.

I stared at that light for a long time. Whether the sun was setting, or something else had drifted across that gap, I couldn't tell. But watching it grow fainter, I felt real fear for the first time. Not losing my way. Not the gate being shut. It was that time was flowing on, leaving me behind. That was the most frightening thing.

I stop, every time I write this part. From here I want to make excuses. That this was an accident, that I didn't know, that I'd never have come if I'd known when this gate closed. All of it is true. But being true doesn't make it an excuse.

I wondered, at the time, why this gate was shut. I didn't think anyone had closed it to stop me. There was no reason to think that. No one knew I was there. I just thought I'd had bad luck.

I was wrong. It wasn't bad luck. This was simply the schedule.

A plate was fixed to the gate. Letters engraved into metal. I read it by the faint light off the wall.

Scheduled Closure. Alternating Duty Section.

Below that, in smaller letters, a few numbers. I guessed they must indicate times. What time it closed, what time it opened. But not knowing what time it was now made those numbers useless to me.

I ran my hand over that plate again and again, as if doing so might change the numbers. In that moment I understood, for the first time. This door hadn't shut to punish me. This door simply closed at this hour, always. I had only, unluckily, happened to be right here at that hour.

For a long time I couldn't tell punishment from accident apart. Punishment is something someone hands down after seeing you. An accident happens without anyone seeing you at all. I'd thought the two were the same, in outcome — either way, I was trapped in the water. I know now. They're different, from the inside. Someone being punished at least knows why they're there. Someone in an accident doesn't even have that. I learned, in front of that gate, that there's something harder to bear than punishment.

I later learned the details of how this sluice worked, from someone else. Every city joined by this waterway, including the Reckoning, shares the same schedule. While one section is open, another must always be closed. Otherwise one city floods and another runs dry. So this gate opens and shuts several times a day. Someone like me getting caught in that gap couldn't have been the first time. I'd simply had one of the ordinary things happen to me.

That fact made me feel smaller still. I'd wanted, somewhere, to believe I'd been singled out by especially bad luck. That would at least have made this whole thing into a story that made sense. But it wasn't. I was just an ordinary case. The world hadn't recognized and imprisoned me — it hadn't recognized me at all, and had simply gone on running as usual. And I happened to be inside that usual.

I thought of the Reckoning's sentence. A hundred and twenty thousand people. Maybe that city was going through something similar, I thought, for the first time. The Scales hadn't measured that city's weight because it hated it. It simply measures. Cross the limit, a disposition follows. That too is scheduled operation. In that moment I felt, faintly, that being trapped in this gate and the sentence handed down to that city were the same kind of thing. Neither had happened because anyone hated anyone. That thought gave me no comfort at all.

These cities' waterways run on an exact schedule. Some sections open, some close, so the water never overflows, and each city gets exactly what it needs. That schedule had no place in it for something like me. No reason it should. While the Scales was weighing a hundred and twenty thousand lives, this gate weighed nothing at all and simply closed on its own time. It was the first rule I'd met, in this world, that had no idea who I was.

I stared at that plate for a long while.

A strange thought came to me. My whole life, I'd always been inside some rule or another. The Scales's rules. Herald training's rules. The supervisor's rules. Every one of them had seen me. They weighed my weight, they judged my voice, they wrote my name on a list.

This gate did not see me.

And that was more frightening still. Up to then, I'd always believed punishment was more frightening than being ignored. I learned that day. Being ignored is more frightening. Punishment at least knows who you are, before it falls. This door didn't know who I was. Had no need to.

Water kept flowing past my ankles, ceaselessly, through that narrow gap. That sound of water was the only regular thing left in this place.

I listened to it and thought of the Scales. The Scales sees everything. It weighs one lie, one stolen loaf of bread, down to the last exact figure. This waterway had no such eye. No one was weighing me here. No one was recording that I existed here at all.

I felt a strange relief and a strange terror, both at once, in that moment. The relief was this — nothing here was being added to my weight. The terror was this — nothing here was on my side, either. When the Scales was weighing me, I at least knew someone was counting my existence. In front of this door, I didn't even have that.

I leaned my back against the wall. My legs had no strength left in them at all.

I set my bag on my knees. The wax envelope wasn't wet yet. I checked, and put it back in the bag.

Even in that moment, I was worried about that document. Laughable, thinking about it now. Sitting in a place where I might drown, I was checking whether a piece of paper that held a city's fate had gotten wet or not. The human mind, even before the largest things, reaches first for the smallest.

I checked the flask. Half full. Five loaves of bread remained.

I began counting numbers again, then, for the first time. I wrote in the last chapter that I'd stopped. That wasn't true. I hadn't stopped — I'd only paused. The moment I sat down in front of that gate, I went back to being someone who counted. There was truly nothing else left for me to do, by then.

How much water was there. How many days would the bread last. When would this gate open again. How many days left until proclamation day.

I set those questions up in order and began filling in the answers, one by one.

The first question had an answer. Half a flask. The second had one too. Five loaves of bread, one a day — five days. The third question had no answer. I had no way to read the numbers stamped on that metal plate. The fourth had no exact answer either. I didn't even know how long I'd been walking.

It was then I noticed, for the first time, that I was setting unanswerable questions beside answerable ones. I solved the ones with answers first. The flask, the bread. Those were easy. I left the unanswerable ones standing as questions. When does this gate open. How many days are left until proclamation day. I didn't throw those questions away. I just set them aside. Like someone who believes an answer will come, eventually.

My body shook. I'd been in the water too long. I pulled myself further up, out of the water, toward the wall. The stone floor was damp too, but better than the water.

That night — though I didn't even know if it was night — I tried to sleep. I closed my eyes. The sound of water filled my ears entirely. I drifted in and out. I don't know how many times. That night I learned, for the first time, that some things can't even be counted.

Once, waking, I forgot for a moment where I was. I thought I was in my own bed and started to turn over, before the touch of the stone floor startled me fully awake. Those few seconds of mistake hurt worse than anything else that had happened that day.

It was a brief moment, but I remember it. Those few seconds of mistaking this for home. That became, for a long time after, the most vivid moment of everything I went through. A person knows exactly where they are, most precisely, in the instant their most comfortable illusion breaks.

I closed my eyes again. The sound of water hadn't changed. That something, at least, stayed constant felt, strangely, like a comfort, in that moment.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. The First Day

_Previously_ — The second sluice gate was shut. Not an accident — a
scheduled closure. This world had not closed the gate to punish Pheugon;
it had simply gone on running as usual, and he happened to be inside
that usual. In the dark where no one answered, he held out the night by
counting his water and his bread.

I don't know exactly when the light disappeared completely.

The faint light that had leaked through the crack above me vanished while I drifted in and out of sleep. I waited for it to come back. It didn't.

That was the first time I knew a day had passed. Not something I saw. Something I felt. If the light went out once and didn't come back, that meant night had come. That was how I counted the first day.

The dark split into complete dark and slightly-less-complete dark. I felt the difference between them with my skin, not my eyes. When there had been light, the air had felt somehow different too. Probably my imagination. But even an illusion was something to hold onto.

I kept opening and closing my eyes, knowing it made no difference whether they were open or shut. My body wanted some familiar motion to perform.

The water rose to my knees.

At first it had been my ankles. Waking from sleep, it was my knees. I didn't know when. I couldn't tell whether the water itself had risen, or whether I'd simply kept sliding toward the lower ground.

Seeing that water, I felt real fear for the first time. What I'd felt before had come from not knowing which way was which. This was different. Water rising meant that as time passed, my position kept getting worse. I learned, then, that things can get worse even while you sit perfectly still.

I didn't know why the water was rising. Some other gate might have opened somewhere else, sending the current this way. Or this stretch might simply run on a cycle like that. I had no way to find out. All I had was the fact — the water was rising.

Watching it, I thought again of that low mechanical hum. Something, somewhere, running the whole of this waterway, going on perfectly fine right at this very moment. To that device, this was just another day's routine. Send water this way, close that gate. I was an accident caught by chance in one corner of that routine. The water rising wasn't meant to threaten me. It was only flowing toward where it had to flow.

I got to my feet. My legs shook. I felt my way along the wall to somewhere a little higher — a small ledge at the edge of the station. The water there only reached my instep.

That ledge wasn't wide. Two steps and it ended. I crouched in that narrow space and looked up. The crack the light had leaked through was still dark.

On a chance, I tried to reach that crack. If I climbed the stone pillar, my hand might reach. I gripped the pillar and hauled myself up. My foot slipped. Twice, three times. The last time my knee scraped the stone and drew blood.

I only stopped trying once I felt that blood with my hand. Only then did pain seem to properly return to me at all. Before that, my body hadn't had the room to feel its own hurt as hurt. Strangely, the blood from my knee calmed me, in that moment. It felt like one certain thing had appeared. At least the pain wasn't lying.

I was hungry. I took out the bread.

In that moment I began calculating for the first time. To be precise, I was picking up the calculation I'd started the day before, sitting in front of the gate. From here, it was real calculation. Not a rough count — a calculation to survive.

Five loaves of bread. I'd already eaten one, so four remained. I tore off half of one and ate it, and tied the rest back up.

I decided, then, to eat half a loaf a day. That would hold out eight days. The flask was half full. There was water all around, but I didn't know if it was safe to drink. I had to ration it. A few mouthfuls a day, only.

Doing this calculation, I felt, strangely, calmed. Numbers hadn't abandoned me. Everything else in that dark had abandoned me. Only numbers still answered, when I asked.

Chewing the bread, I thought of home. Of the table, to be precise. The one with four seats where now only one gets used. I tried to remember what our last dinner there had been. I couldn't.

A strange thing. I was someone who remembered everything countable. A hundred and twelve cases, a hundred and eleven erased words. And yet I couldn't remember what our last dinner had been. I learned that day, for the first time, how easily the things you don't count simply vanish. I'd spent far too long, living, hanging onto only what could be counted.

I drew a line on the wall with my fingernail.

That was the only way I had to count days. Scratching a mark into the stone with a fingertip. One line. Meaning today was the first day.

I wasn't even confident about exactly which day it was. Counting from the night I left home, this might already have been the second or third day. I decided to start counting again from the moment I got trapped in front of this gate. That was the number that mattered to me now.

Beside that line I began another calculation.

Days left until proclamation day. By the supervisor's schedule, I should be somewhere in the middle of the road to the Reckoning by now. Two days' travel, a day's rest on arrival, then the proclamation. By the original plan, I should have been standing in that city's square in three days.

Doing that calculation, I felt a strange emotion. Relief. I hoped those three days would pass — that the city would cross its proclamation day without me. What happened when a herald didn't show, I didn't know exactly. But I knew something would be delayed. That delay felt, to me, like success.

Reading this sentence now, I look strangely at who I was then. Even in that moment of terror, I hadn't stopped calculating. Trapped in the water, I was still checking whether time was flowing in the direction I wanted. Too precise a thing for someone that frightened to be doing.

When would this gate open.

I felt again at the numbers carved into the metal plate. I could feel the depth of the grooves with my fingertips, but not what number they made. I stood a long while in front of that plate.

I felt, for the first time, a desperate need to know something. Until then I hadn't been someone who fretted much over what I didn't know. Training had made me that way. Ask, if you don't know. If no one told you, let it go. But this number, I couldn't let go. This was a number my life depended on.

I struck the plate. No response. Of course not. This wasn't a thing you could speak to.

I thought of the Scales again, in that moment. The one they say exists in the Reckoning. It doesn't speak, but it at least shows a number. Put a hand up, and something answers. This gate didn't even offer that much. I realized, for the first time in my life, that I had never before stood this long in front of something that gave no answer at all. Someone raised in the world of the Scales takes it for granted that asking always gets some kind of response. I learned, in front of that door, just how thin that assumption had always been.

Once, amid the water's noise, I thought I heard something like a human voice. I held my breath and listened. Nothing was there. Just water striking stone, sounding that way by coincidence.

I had that same illusion several times after. Sometimes it seemed like someone calling my name in the water's noise; sometimes like someone murmuring something close to a sentence. I learned later that this wasn't rare at all. A person can bear a place with no sound at all better than a place with constant, irregular sound — because in the second kind of place, the mind starts trying to find words inside it. The ears can't stand being bored. I didn't know that then. I was just afraid.

Having no way at all to gauge how much time had passed was the hardest thing to bear.

At the training hall, a bell rang. At home, the sun crossed the window. Here, there was nothing. I tried measuring time by how many times I opened and closed my eyes. I soon learned how inaccurate that was. Sometimes I opened my eyes after a single breath's worth of closing them; sometimes after sleeping a long while. And there was no way to tell the two apart.

I experienced, then, for the first time, calculation itself breaking down. The flask and the bread could be counted in numbers. Time couldn't. I found that difference hard to bear. When something countable and something uncountable sit in the same place, the uncountable swallows everything else.

So instead I decided to count paces. Walking back and forth across the station. Twelve steps, wall to wall. I decided that about a hundred round trips meant, roughly, some fixed amount of time had passed. There was no basis for that. I just needed something to count.

One round trip, two, three. I counted the numbers silently, not aloud. I had no strength left to make a sound yet, and no reason to. I was saving my voice, then. Without even knowing why.

I still don't know what those numbers meant. But while I walked and counted like that, I could forget, for a little while, that the water was rising, that the gate wouldn't open. Counting was that, for me. Even when it gave no answer, it still let me endure.

After a long while of walking back and forth, I stopped. Out of breath. Walking in water took far more strength than I'd thought. I sat back down on the ledge and caught my breath.

In that moment I noticed something strange. I was counting my steps, and at the same time, counting something else. The days left until the Reckoning. The forty days that city's people didn't even know had started yet. I was turning those two numbers over side by side in my head. The speed at which my own life was running down, and the speed at which that city's remaining time was running down. Strangely, it comforted me that the two were shrinking in the same direction. Every hour I held out here meant that, over there, the possibility was shrinking too.

My hand shakes, writing this sentence. I truly thought that way, then. I set my own dying time and one city's dying chance on the same scale, and found comfort in it. How terrible a calculation that was, I would only learn much later.

My body shook. From too long in the water.

I wrung out my wet clothes as best I could. It did nothing. Water was everywhere. There was nowhere to dry.

That night — though I didn't even know which night it was — listening to my teeth chatter, I thought: I could die like this. Strangely, the thought didn't frighten me. I think I already knew, then, that there was something I feared more than death. That the Reckoning would survive. That frightened me more.

I hugged my knees. The wound on my knee throbbed. My clothes were still wet, showing no sign of drying. I curled myself as small as I could. It was the only way I had left to keep any warmth.

I was hungry. I wanted more bread. I held back. I'd already eaten today's share. I kept that rule for myself, exactly. Half a loaf a day. Yesterday, and today too. I checked how much bread was left with my fingertips, again and again. What was left after taking one from four, then half again from that. The number was exact. That exactness was, in that moment, almost my only comfort.

I felt a strange pride, then. That I'd kept the rule even while hungry. It sounds absurd, I know. But that was the one rule left, in that moment, that I could still keep. Everything else had slipped out of my hands — the height of the water, when the gate would open, where exactly I was. In that helplessness, there was exactly one thing I could still decide for myself. How many pieces of bread to eat. I held onto that one thing.

Rereading this sentence now, it makes my skin crawl. In that moment I was setting my own life and one city's fate on the same scale. And without any hesitation, I thought the city's side had to weigh more — no, had to be made to weigh more. What that meant, I didn't know, then.

I thought of the supervisor.

By now the supervisor probably believed I was somewhere on the road to the Reckoning. Or perhaps he'd already begun to sense something was wrong. A herald doesn't stray far from the set schedule. Running a day late might happen. Going entirely out of contact doesn't.

I pictured the supervisor's face, then. The cool room, the roster on the wall, the number 112 beside my name. I wondered if he might be looking at that number again right now. That thought hurt me, strangely, in that moment. I didn't think I'd betrayed the supervisor. I only felt sorry that if he was worried about me, that worry, right now, in this moment, could do nothing at all.

I didn't hold onto that regret for long. Holding it felt like it would throw off my calculations. I went back to the line on the wall, to the numbers of water and bread. That was the safer place to be.

I traced the first line I'd scratched on the wall again with my fingertip. One. A beginning.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. The Second Day

_Previously_ — On the first day trapped, the water rose to Pheugon's
knees. He held out by rationing bread and water, counting steps in place
of time he had no way to measure. In that calculation, he found a strange
comfort in treating his own dwindling time and the Reckoning's dwindling
days as the same thing — all while, for no reason he understood, saving
his own voice.

I scratched a second line onto the wall.

The water seemed to have risen a little higher than yesterday. I couldn't be certain. The bread was down to three and a half loaves. The flask was a third full. I confirmed those numbers and leaned back against the wall.

On the second day, I made a sound for the first time.

To be precise, I spoke. Not a groan, not a cough — a sentence. With no one there.

This is the chapter hardest for me to write. To explain that moment exactly, I have to start by saying why I began with that particular sentence, of all sentences.

I've rewritten this chapter more times than any other in this book. What I wrote first was far shorter. I didn't want to write this night in detail, at the time. Not out of shame. It was hard to hear that voice again. But I came to understand that without this part, nothing else in this book makes sense. So I wrote it again.

I was hungry. Hungrier than yesterday. My body wanted something bread couldn't fill.

I sat with my back to the wall and opened my mouth. I didn't know why I opened it, at the time. I know now. I wanted to hear a sound. Not water. A human voice.

The only voice I could make, in this world, was one. My own. I realized that, then, for the first time.

Sitting against the wall, I opened my mouth. No sound came. My throat had been shut too long. I cleared it a few times. The sound rang strange. Like someone else's cough.

At the training hall, the supervisor taught us to treat the voice as part of the body. That it stiffens if it isn't used every day. I'd forgotten that. Barely a day and a half without using it, and my throat had already become a stranger to me.

I drank a little water. More than I'd rationed. My throat needed loosening. In that moment, that mattered more than saving the flask.

It was the first time I broke a rule I'd set for myself. A small thing, in itself. And yet I remember breaking it, still, clearly. The one rule I'd believed I could keep, I broke myself, just to make a sound. The wish to speak was greater than the wish to keep the rule.

The first thing to come from my mouth was the sentence.

"…The Reckoning."

I said those two words and stopped. My throat was raw. Unused to making sound.

I swallowed and began again.

"…Given that the accumulated weight of this city's acts has been confirmed to exceed the limit."

A trained sentence. The way of speaking bred into my body from a hundred and twelve readings. I hadn't even known I had it memorized. It came out of my mouth on its own.

I've wondered a long time why that was the sentence I started with, of all sentences. The answer was simple. It was the one sentence I owned completely, word for word. A person with nothing else reaches for the most familiar thing first. The most familiar thing I had was a sentence document.

I kept reading. The entire Reckoning sentence. I made several mistakes. Reversed the order, corrected myself, repeated one line twice.

If this had been the training hall, the supervisor would have made me start again. Here, no one made me start again. I kept going, wrong as I was. The first time. Reading on, uncorrected.

City name, accumulated weight, basis of calculation, grace period, method of execution. I read those sentences again, in the dark, once more. I said the numbers too, as best I remembered. A hundred and twenty thousand people. Accumulated weight past the limit. Saying those numbers, I felt, for one brief moment, that they weren't numbers but people. It faded again quickly.

This time it carried no effect. The rule that reading it aloud gives it effect — that was only true in its set place, its set procedure. Here, however loudly I read, it was only sound.

I found that fact, strangely, comforting, in that moment. Whatever I did here, nothing would happen to the Reckoning. I could play with that sentence freely, with an easy mind.

The last line came.

"…Article 4, Clause 11."

I said that line slowly, letter by letter.

"…Should this city, within forty days, be confirmed to have turned, this sentence shall be void."

Having said it, I went quiet a long while. Only the water's sound filled that silence.

That was the first time I'd ever spoken that sentence aloud. Reading it on paper and speaking it with my mouth were different. Paper, your eyes can pass over. Your mouth carries the sentence through your body. I felt, the moment that sentence crossed my throat, for the first time, exactly how heavy it was.

I said the sentence again. And again.

The first time, it was only a sentence. The second time, something caught in my throat. By the third, it had begun to change.

"…Within forty days… if I turned."

I didn't know what I was saying. My mouth moved before I did.

"…I won't turn."

I remember exactly the moment I said that sentence. In the dark, my legs sunk in water, listening to my own voice come back to me. I didn't even know, then, who I was speaking to. The Scales? That city? Or just the wall?

After a brief silence, I spoke again.

"…I won't turn. I have nothing to turn from."

Having said that, I laughed. No sound, just my throat moving.

I couldn't explain, at the time, why I'd laughed. I can explain it a little, now. In that moment I realized, for the first time, that I wasn't speaking toward the Scales, but toward the clause. That clause had been made to confirm whether a city had turned. I wasn't a city. And yet I kept answering as if the clause were asking me the question too. That I wouldn't turn. Why I answered that way, I still don't fully know, even now.

"…I was right."

I said that. For the first time, clearly.

"…I have the right to hate that city. My brother left. My mother went. My father lives alone. That was the accurate weight. The Scales measured it that way."

My voice grew louder. So as not to be drowned out by the water.

"…So why does only that city get a door. A door that never came for my house."

I stopped a moment after that, then went on.

"…My father's weight was accurate. The Scales measured it precisely. No one shaved any of it off. I was taught that's how it should be. But the Reckoning. That city has had its door standing open from the very start. Whatever a hundred and twenty thousand people did, if they only turn within forty days, all of it just — never happened."

My voice cracked. I kept going.

"…Is that fair. Is that what the Scales does?"

Only the water answered.

I didn't cry, saying that. Strange, thinking about it now. It was a thing I'd held back so long, and still, in the dark, I held back the tears. Even with no one watching. I know where I learned that habit. The night my brother left home, I taught myself, that night, for the first time, how not to cry aloud. What I learned that night had followed me all the way into this dark.

Once the words broke loose, they didn't stop.

I talked about herald training. About Had. About the merchant. A hundred and twelve cases, the erased words. I said all of it to the water, to the wall, to the dark.

I drew my knees up and rested my chin on them. My voice grew a little softer.

"…The night my brother left, he turned back once, at the door." I said. "I couldn't tell if he was looking at me or just checking his direction. I didn't say anything, then. I still don't know, even now, what I should have said to him that night."

I paused a moment, then went on.

"…My mother cried in the kitchen twice. The second time was worse. I never knocked on the door."

Copying these sentences out again, my hand slows down. I realize, all over again, that I had never told anyone these things before that night. Most of what I set down in Chapter 5 was, in truth, first spoken aloud in this dark. I only wrote it onto paper later. This waterway is where it first became words.

I talked about my father too.

"…My father said 'it's fine,' once, at the terminal of the Scales. He wasn't saying it to me. I knew that, at the time. I didn't say anything."

The more I spoke, the stranger it felt, how much easier my voice became. Strange, that easing came with pain. I doubted that ease even as I kept speaking. Even while I doubted it, my mouth didn't stop.

I know the reason now. Those words had been pooling inside my body for three years. Water that pools goes stagnant. That night, for the first time, I let it flow out. Not to the right person, not in the right place. I only let it flow to a place I could let it flow. Even so, it was better than letting it stagnate.

"…I've done nothing wrong."

I said that.

"…I don't lie, I don't touch other people's things, I don't get angry. My weight is the lowest there is."

Having said it, I went quiet.

The water's sound rose loud again. The moment my voice stopped, the water filled that space back in. I found that strange. The world flowed on the same, whether I spoke or not. And still I wanted to go on speaking.

Right after I said that sentence, I felt a strange unease. I know now what it was. In that moment I remembered, for the first time, the lie I'd told. "No, sir," when the supervisor asked whether I had some connection to the Reckoning. The lie to the gatekeeper. I knew the Scales had calculated both those lies. Precisely. I could no longer be certain, any longer, that my weight really was the lowest.

I spoke again, trying to shake off that unease.

"…And still, I'm right."

Once more.

"…I'm right."

Trying to say it a third time, no voice came. Not because my throat had given out. I felt, in that moment, that I myself no longer believed the words. The third sentence simply scattered, inside my mouth.

I still haven't finished that scattered sentence. What was meant to come after "I'm right" — I went on searching for it for a long time after. I didn't find it that night. I couldn't say, even now, that I've found it completely.

I found a different sentence instead. Much later, after I'd come to this city. I won't write what that sentence was, here. I haven't reached that place yet.

I kept talking, on into that night, without stopping.

The sentences grew shorter. Eventually only words were left. "…Heavy." "…Turn." "…Why."

I remember every one of those words, even now. Everything that came out that night was something I was saying for the first time. Words I'd never said to anyone. I pulled them out, for the first time, into a dark with no one there. That it was a strange kind of first confession, I didn't know, at the time.

I kept talking until my throat gave out entirely and no more sound would come. After that, only my mouth moved. Without sound.

The water kept on the same as before. Nothing answered.

I asked, a long while after. My voice was barely more than a whisper.

"…Are you listening?"

Of course no one answered. And yet, asking that, I couldn't shake the strange feeling that I was asking it toward someone. Not the Scales. The Scales wasn't something you asked that of. So who, then? I didn't know, at the time. I still don't know exactly, even now.

I would go on to ask that question again, in other places throughout this book. Writing this chapter, I realize freshly that it was the first question that ever rose out of this dark. A question with no answer. But that it had no answer doesn't mean it was asked for nothing — I would only learn that much later.

My body gave out and I leaned against the wall and closed my eyes. My voice used up entirely, my body felt strangely light. As if something had been emptied out of it. I was still hungry. But that hunger was a little more bearable than before.

And that night, for the first time, I felt the strange sense that I wasn't entirely alone. Not that someone was there. I mean that, for the first time, I heard my own words. In three years. Until then I had only avoided, erased, and swallowed my own words. That night, for the first time, I let them out, into the open, and heard them come back to me from outside. That is different from someone hearing them for you. And still, it was not nothing at all.

Even now, thinking of that night, I can't tell whether it was prayer or just rambling. Maybe there's no need to tell them apart. I had only, for the first time in three years, used my own voice as my own. Not a sentence I'd been told to read — a sentence I had made myself.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. The Third Day

_Previously_ — On the second day, Pheugon spoke aloud for the first time.
Reciting the sentence document from memory, the words turned into his own
story. What began as "I was right" ended in "So why am I here." He
released that question into the water and lost consciousness. What he
remembers next is the sensation of his body moving. He was not the one
moving it.

On the third day, the water stopped rising.

My legs knew it first. It had stayed at my knees for days now, unchanged. I couldn't tell whether that was good news or bad.

Bad news. I know that now. The water no longer rising meant this stretch was designed to balance out at exactly that level. No one had drained the water to save me. It was simply water flowing the way it always flowed.

My body had been submerged too long; the feeling in my legs had gone dull. I pinched my calf now and then. It hurt, but the hurt seemed to arrive from far away.

The air hung heavy and damp. Every breath filled the inside of my nose with the smell of stone. Nothing but that smell. No sound, no light, no other scent.

I didn't know, then, that my body was slowly leaving me. I only thought I was tired. What happens to a body that holds out three days in water — that was the first time I was learning it, myself.

Two loaves of bread remained. The flask was nearly empty. I scratched a third line on the wall.

I felt my own hand slow down, drawing that line. No strength left. Even scratching the stone with my fingertip had become work.

I sat back against the wall. Something felt wrong with my body. I shook without being cold. I was hungry, and yet the thought of bread didn't come.

My head felt heavy. My neck, leaned against the wall, kept lolling to one side. I shook it a few times, trying to right it.

Once, opening my eyes, my vision blurred. I'm not even sure what "blurred" means, in a place with no light. But that's what it was. As if a thin film had grown over the inside of my eyelids.

I only learned later that this was a frightening sign. At the time I just thought I was tired. I kept blinking. It didn't help.

I opened the flask and took a mouthful. I'd rationed that water carefully, days before, and now I barely registered how precious it was. My body seemed to be taking what it needed on its own. My mind couldn't keep pace with the calculation.

Writing this sentence, I realize freshly that by then, the person I'd been was already beginning to let go of the calculation. I wrote, earlier in this book, several times, that calculating was what let me hold out. But from the third night on, even that calculation started to falter. The strength to calculate was leaving my body along with everything else.

Sometimes the water's sound seemed to fall into a different rhythm than before. Rising steadily, then swelling once, hard. I couldn't tell whether that was real or something my ears had invented.

I opened my mouth again.

The sentences didn't come as easily as they had on the second day. My throat was more ruined now. I managed only a few broken words, in a ragged voice.

"…I was right."

I began there again. The sentence I hadn't finished the night before.

"…I was right. That person came to our house, and our house came apart. The Scales measured it accurately. So I'm right."

Speaking it, I knew it was a repetition. I was saying, word for word, what I'd said the day before.

I know now why I repeated it. Because that sentence had never been finished. A person repeats an unfinished sentence, over and over. Until they find the end of it.

"…I was right."

I said it again. This time something new attached itself.

"…And yet here I am."

I said that sentence and stopped.

That moment was strange. I had believed there was no relationship at all between being right and being here, in this place. And yet the moment I said those two sentences side by side, I felt something come loose. Why would a person who's right be here. That question began, for the first time, to take shape.

I tried to push that dislocation away. My body shook once more.

"…No." I said aloud. "This is just an accident. I just came at a time the gate was closed. This has nothing to do with that."

I was trying, desperately, to keep two things apart, in that moment. That I was trapped in the water, and that I had run. I wanted to stop those two things becoming the same story. One was just bad luck, an accident. The other was something I had chosen. I believed that as long as I kept them apart, I could still remain the person who was right.

A person wants to draw a line between what they did and what happened to them. As long as the line holds, the responsibility splits in half too. I held onto that line, and it held me through that night. Not for long.

But I know now. The two really were related. If I hadn't run, I would never have been in this conduit to begin with. The accident happened on the road I had chosen. It wasn't a thing "accident" could fully cover.

I hugged my knees under the water. And spoke again.

"…I didn't run."

My voice cracked.

"…I just… wanted to see that city fall. Is that wrong? Our house fell apart. Isn't it wrong for that city, that one city, to go untouched?"

I said that and caught my breath.

"…I'm not a bad person."

Saying that, I heard something different in my own voice, for the first time. Not certainty. A plea. "I'm not a bad person" isn't something a person says when they already know they aren't. It's something said by someone who doubts.

I tried to erase that doubt by counting numbers again. A hundred and twelve cases. No misjudgments. My weight, the lowest in my cohort.

"…I read a hundred and twelve cases." I said. "Not one wrong. The Scales confirmed it."

I felt, in that moment, for the first time, that I was using numbers as a shield. Numbers had always calmed me, in that dark. This time was different. The more I spoke them, the more unsteady I felt. That the hundred and twelve had been right, and whether what I was trying to do here, now, was right — those were two different questions. I was trying to bury them both under the same number.

I stopped speaking for a long while.

I stop longest, writing this section. Rereading what came out of me here is still hard. And still, I won't cut it. Without this part, what happens afterward makes no sense. I resolved, at the start of this book, that I would rather not write it at all than write it without the parts that shame me. Facing this sentence, I confirm that resolve again.

My body swayed. Whether from sitting too long in the water or something else, I couldn't tell. I opened my mouth again.

I pictured my brother's face, then my mother's, then my father's, one by one, in order. Brother first, then mother, then father. Last I tried to picture my own face. Strangely, that was the blurriest of all.

"…Let my brother come back."

I didn't know where that came from. I hadn't meant to say anything like it.

"…Let my mother come back. Let my father… not live alone anymore."

I still don't know whether I can call this a prayer. I spoke it not knowing who I was speaking to. The Scales? The Scales wasn't a thing that listened to this kind of thing. And yet I spoke as if something were listening.

I fell quiet after that and listened. Not expecting an answer. Only out of habit — a feeling that speaking should be met with some kind of response.

The water's sound stayed the same. I wasn't disappointed by that. I hadn't believed, to begin with, that I had any right to expect an answer.

Looking back, that's strange. I was asking for help while believing, at the same time, that I had no right to receive it. I never thought that contradiction was strange, at the time. It felt, to me, entirely natural. A right person has no need to ask for help, and a person who needs help has already done something wrong — that's what I'd been taught, growing up. Anyone raised in the world of the Scales thinks that way.

And still I asked. I went on asking even while believing I had no right to. That this was strange, too, I didn't notice, at the time. I just couldn't help asking. There was nothing else left that I could do.

"…But that city must not be allowed to turn."

I came back to that, again. I don't know how many times I'd already circled back to it.

"…There was never a door like that for my house. That city — only that city — mustn't be allowed a door."

Saying it, I noticed something strange. I was praying for two things at once. That my house would go back to how it had been. And that the Reckoning would never, ever be allowed to go back.

That those two wishes could come from the same mouth at the same time didn't strike me as strange, then. It does now. I wanted mercy for myself while trying, at the same time, to keep mercy from reaching someone else. How much of a contradiction that was, I couldn't see, that third night.

I kept speaking, carrying that contradiction.

"…If there's mercy." I said. "It should have come to my house first. Why did it go to that city first."

I stopped partway through. What I'd just said sounded strange to my own ear. I seemed to believe mercy came in a line, taking its turn — someone first, someone after.

In that moment, for a very brief instant, I felt something was wrong. If mercy comes standing in a queue, then I was, right now, furious that someone else had taken my place in line. But does mercy really come that way? I couldn't answer that question. I didn't even have the strength, then, to ask it. I just pushed the feeling aside and picked anger back up. Anger, at least, gave me strength to hold on.

My body felt feverish. I touched my forehead. I couldn't tell, by hand, whether it was hot or cold.

I learned later. I was in a state of mild exhaustion, by then. Three days with barely any food, my body constantly submerged in cold water — of course I was. But in that moment, I couldn't tell whether it was my body or my mind that was the problem.

I closed my eyes and opened them again. The dark was the same. That closing my eyes and opening them made no difference at all felt, for the first time, genuinely frightening.

I couldn't name that fear. Whether what I'd just said was prayer or curse, I couldn't tell, then. I still don't know, exactly, even now. Maybe it was both.

Around then, a strange face rose in my mind. The face of the person who'd come from the Reckoning. Three years ago, at the edge of town, the face that had said to me, "You too. Be well." I didn't know why it came to me now.

I tried, then, to hate that person. In the dark, my body coming apart, I tried to aim anger at that face. It didn't work well. Strangely, that face wouldn't hold still as a target for anger. It was only a sad face. That fact bewildered me. I had decided, long ago, to hate that person. And now, calling the face back up, my hatred just slipped out of my hands.

"…Why am I here."

I said that. Like talking to myself.

Having said it, I heard the sentence again, as it came back in my own voice.

I felt, in that moment, that the question carried two meanings at once. One was narrow. Why, of all places, was I trapped here, in this waterway, in front of this closed gate. That one had an answer. Because I'd entered during the closed hours. The other was wider. I was right — so why was I here, now, in this place. That one had no answer.

"…I was right." I said once more. My voice had grown small.

"…So why am I here."

Only the water answered.

That question followed me a long while after this. I still don't have a complete answer to it, even now. I only know this much. The moment I first put that question into words, out loud, I felt, faintly, for the first time in my life, that being right and being all right might not be the same thing at all.

I repeated the question a few more times. My voice grew smaller each time. By the end only my lips moved, hardly any sound left at all.

"…Why am I here."

"…Why am I here."

That sentence rose to the water's surface, and, unanswered, sank back down again.

I didn't count how many times I repeated it. Writing this book, this is the first time I don't regret not having counted. Some things stay whole only if they're never counted.

My body slowly tipped to one side. I didn't try to stop it. Still leaning on the wall, I ended up half-sitting, half-lying. The water reached nearly to my waist. I could barely feel the cold anymore.

I didn't speak again that night. Not because my throat had fully given out. Not because I had nothing left to say. I simply stayed still, having let the question flow out into the water. Where that question drifted, I didn't watch. I no longer had the strength to.

I can't tell whether I fell asleep or lost consciousness, there. The next thing I remember is the sensation of my body moving. I wasn't the one moving it.

I'll end this chapter here. What happened next, I'll write in the next chapter. This is the whole of what I was still able to do for myself, in that dark. Everything after this was no longer my own doing.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. Cast Out

_Previously_ — On the night of the third day, exhausted past bearing,
Pheugon poured out words that might have been prayer or might have been
curse. What began with "I was right" ended with "So why am I here." He
released that question into the water and lost consciousness. What he
remembers next is the sensation of his body moving. He was not the one
moving it.

The current lifted my body.

I thought it was a dream. Three full days in that water — it wouldn't have been strange to dream of water lifting me.

It wasn't a dream. I only learned that later. The sluice gate had opened. On its own schedule. Not for me. Things had simply gone back to normal. The same as when it had closed.

I turned this fact over many times since. That door hadn't shut to trap me, and it hadn't opened to let me out, either. A set hour had simply come. I could have died inside that indifference, and I was also saved by that same indifference. That the thing that saved me and the thing that had trapped me were the same kind of thing — I still find that strange, even now.

Before I came fully to my senses, my body reacted first. I think I heard the sound of water rushing hard through the narrow gap past the gate. Or maybe that's a memory I've pieced together since. What's certain is that, at some moment, the water where I'd been sitting suddenly pulled me in.

My body was swept up in the current.

I couldn't resist. I had no strength left to resist. I struck the wall a few times. I don't remember clearly whether it hurt. I only flowed.

I don't remember that moment in detail. What the body remembers and what the mind remembers are different things. My body still, now and then, recalls the force of that current, the shock of striking the wall. My mind kept nothing at all. I wasn't there to keep it.

The current carried me on a long while. Through narrow stretches, through wide ones, narrow again. I don't know how many times I breathed, carried along in that flow. Whenever my head broke the surface for a moment, I drew breath by instinct.

Once my body struck the tunnel wall hard. At the shoulder. In that instant I came briefly back to myself. Pain had woken me. I opened my eyes and saw nothing. Still in the dark. I closed them again. I already knew it made no difference, open or shut.

In that brief moment I had a strange thought. If I died like this, no one would ever know. The supervisor would believe I was somewhere on the road to the Reckoning; my father would assume I'd arrived safely. No one would ever search this waterway. Strangely, that thought didn't frighten me. I had no strength left even to feel afraid.

The current turned once more. I gave my body over to it entirely. There was no reason left to resist, and no strength left either.

Light appeared.

Faint, at first. Flickering every time the current shook my body. I couldn't tell whether it was real or something I'd imagined.

I've gone back over that moment many times since. Was I swimming toward that light, or was I just letting the current carry me. I still can't say for certain. Maybe it was both. My body moved toward the light, apart from my will.

At first the light was only a matter of brightness. But as it grew closer, color began to appear. Gray shifting into a hazy gold. Only seeing that color again did I realize how completely I'd forgotten, in three days, that color existed at all.

The light kept growing. The current slowed. I felt myself come out into some wide, open space. It felt as if the ceiling had disappeared. Wind brushed over my head.

Wind. It took a moment to recognize the feeling. Three days without ever feeling it. There's no wind, underwater. I felt that wind on my face, and something in my body responded. Something like a sign that I was still alive.

The current pushed me upward. A narrow vertical passage. My fingertips brushed the wall. Smooth stone. A familiar touch. I had no room left to think about what it meant.

The water gave me one great push. And I struck something solid. Stone. Round, mossy. I only learned later — it was the coping stone of a well.

I think I lost consciousness there. I don't know if it was brief or long.

The first thing I felt, opening my eyes, was light. So bright my eyes hurt. I closed them again, and opened them, little by little.

Sky.

The sky, seen for the first time in three days. I don't know how to describe that color. Blue wasn't enough of a word. I understood, in that moment, for the first time, how enormous a thing color was. In the dark there had been no color. Not light, not dark. Simply nothing at all. Seeing the sky, I was overwhelmed by the fact that the world held color at all.

Looking up at that sky, for one brief moment, I couldn't tell whether this was a sky I was seeing dead, or seeing alive. Strangely, that distinction didn't seem to matter, in that moment. The sky was simply there. Not waiting for me, not turning me away. Just there.

The touch of sunlight on my face was unfamiliar too. For three days my skin had known only the temperature of water. Now there was a different temperature. Stinging, drying, falling from above. It took me a while even to recognize it as sunlight.

My body was soaked. I lay sprawled on stone ground. My arms and legs had no strength left at all. Moving even a single finger, opening my eyelids the whole way, was hard.

I had no strength to look at my own fingertips, but I could tell by feel alone that my fingertips had gone wrinkled and swollen. My mouth tasted of salt and iron, mixed together. A low ringing wouldn't stop in my ears. Every blink made the light look slightly different.
A chill I hadn't felt in the water came over me now. As my soaked body began to dry, I felt colder, somehow, than I had underwater.

I could feel the warmth of the stone beneath me. Which meant the sun had been heating that stone a while already. Daytime. I still didn't know what time it was, or exactly how many days I'd been trapped. The wall bore three scratched lines, but I had no way to count how much time had passed since I'd drawn the last one.

In that moment, for the first time in my life, I realized I had completely lost track of the days. It was one of the very few moments in this whole book where I lost count of a number. Strangely, it left me feeling clear. Before something uncountable, I no longer needed to count at all.

Sound came back slowly too. At first just a ringing hum. Then, one by one, it broke apart into separate sounds. Far off, a cart wheel rolling over paved stone; a bird's cry; someone bargaining in a low voice. To ears that had known nothing but water for three days, those sounds were too many, too loud. I lay there with my eyes open, trying to pick them apart one by one, and soon gave up. This city was simply living out its day, as if nothing at all had happened.

That's when a voice spoke.

"…You're not from here, are you?"

I only rolled my eyes toward the sound, not knowing where it came from. I had no strength to lift my head.

I heard that question first, and only after, lifted my eyes and saw the person. That was the order it happened in. The voice came first.

A child's voice. I managed to turn my head, just a little. Beyond the well's coping stone, someone was looking down at me. Backlit, so I couldn't make out the face.

Bare feet. Dust on the tops of them. One toe was tapping idly at a pebble. It might have been getting ready to run, or it might just have been bored.

I tried to answer. What came out of my throat sounded like water. It wasn't words.

For three years I'd made my living by voice. And in that moment, what came out of my throat wasn't a human sound at all. That fact was, strangely, humiliating to me.

I still think about it, why that first question wasn't "Are you all right?" but "You're not from here, are you?" Usually, when someone's pulled out of the water, don't you ask first if they're all right? But that child asked first whether I belonged to this city or not. I'd learn the reason later. In this city, which side you belonged to had to be confirmed before who was hurt. I didn't know that yet. I only felt, then, that the order of the question was strange.

The owner of that voice said nothing for a moment. Seemed to be looking me over. I felt that gaze on me and still had no strength to lift my head any further.

"…You're not dead?" the child asked again. The voice had come a little closer than before. Sounded like they'd crouched down.

I answered by trying to move my fingers, just a little. That was all I could do, in that moment. The child looked down at that small movement for a long while.

I remember those few seconds very slowly. My body had completely broken down, and yet some strange corner of my mind stayed oddly clear. In that brief moment I thought: where am I. Starting from that well, three days ago — how far had I drifted, carried along, to end up here.

And right after, another thought came. I had run to avoid going to the Reckoning. The opposite direction, toward somewhere I knew no one. But this current had flowed regardless of my will. I had never once chosen where this water would take me.

I wrote, in earlier chapters, that at every fork I chose a direction with no basis for it at all. Looking back now, those choices had never meant anything to begin with. Where the sluice gates would open and close, which way the water would push me — I had decided none of it. I had simply been carried along by the water. To the very city I had tried so hard to avoid, no one but the water itself had brought me.

I've stood a long time, before this fact, between the word coincidence and some other word. Calling it coincidence was easier. Calling it anything else was not. I would only come to accept that other word much later, in a different chapter of this book. Here, I'm still not ready to use it.

I gathered my strength and lifted my head. And I looked, not toward where the child was pointing, but somewhere beyond it.

Those few seconds are the moment I've returned to, in memory, more than any other in this entire book. In that moment I still knew nothing. Who I would meet in this city, how long I would end up staying here — none of it. I only lifted my head. And with that single motion, the next chapter of my life had already begun.

Beside the well's coping stone, a bronze plate was set into a low post. Rusted. An old plate.

I strained my eyes to read the letters. Blurred. I strained again.

The two characters slowly came into focus.

The Reckoning.

I looked at those letters. For a long time.

The plate was cold. My fingers hadn't even touched it, and still, I believe, even now, that I felt that cold.

I want to say that no thought came to me, in that moment. But that isn't true. Thoughts came. Only there were too many of them, all at once, and none of them ever became a sentence.

The first thought was this. This is a mistake. I've misread some other sign. I held onto that thought and looked at the letters again. The blur didn't clear, and yet those two characters didn't change at all.

The second thought was this. A little further on might be a different city. The Reckoning is large; this well might just be on its outskirts. I couldn't lean on that thought for long either. I had no strength left to move my body and confirm it.

By the third thought, I could no longer make any more thoughts at all. I simply accepted the fact. I had arrived exactly where I had run from.

I remember what my body did, in that moment. My heart beat slowly, but hard. One long breath came in and didn't go back out for a long time. I only understood later that it was shock. At the time I just felt that something was wrong with my body.

I had run. Away from the Reckoning. Fled into the waterway, trapped for three days with no idea which way I was going. And the place the water had spat me out was, of all places, this city.

I didn't even have the strength, then, to think about whether that was coincidence or not. I only watched those two characters, and felt my whole body say, no. No. It has to be no. And still, there before my eyes, those letters stood. Blurred, and yet unmistakable.

I lay on the stone ground, unable to look away from those letters. Above me the child said something more. I couldn't make out the words.

I've thought about this moment many times since. That someone who fled should be washed back up in the very place they fled from. I didn't know, then, how common a story that is, in this world. I thought it was some especially cruel coincidence, meant only for me. I see it differently now. Maybe it wasn't coincidence at all — maybe there had simply never been so many places left to flee to, from the start. Every waterway leads somewhere, in the end. The places a person can be carried to are narrower than they think.

I'm placing this chapter at the end of Part One. That noon, I lay sprawled at the wellside of the Reckoning. A boy who had run, been trapped three days in the dark, was cast out into the very heart of the place he'd run from. Where the current had carried me, and the moment it cast me out — I had chosen neither. That was the whole of what Part One taught me.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Forty-Day Clause, Part Two

The Reckoning

Chapter 13. You're Not From Here

_Previously_ — Trapped behind a closed sluice gate, Pheugon spent three days in darkness. For the first time, he spoke aloud — words he had never said to anyone. On the third day the gate opened, and the current carried him out. He opened his eyes to an unfamiliar well and an unfamiliar sky. A passing child looked down at him and asked — "You're not from here, are you?" He looked up at the sign. The Reckoning.

The question came first, and the name of the city came after.

I remember the order. Exactly that order. I heard the question first, and only then did I lift my eyes to the sign. If it had happened the other way around, I think I would have passed out again before I could answer.

THE RECKONING.

Two words cut into stone. A bronze plate set into a low post beside the well's coping. Rusted. An old plate. It meant the city had been calling itself that for a very long time.

I think I laughed, in that moment. No sound came out. My body had no strength left for sound. But the corners of my mouth moved. What kind of laugh it was, I still can't explain. Washed up in the very place I'd fled — after three days, no less, and naked and scoured by the water — whether that was funny or terrifying, I still haven't decided.

"…Are you all right?"

The child asked again. I hadn't answered the first time.

I tried to answer, but only a watery sound came out of my throat. It felt like three days' worth of swallowed water was still inside me.

The child bent down and threw a rope toward me — one of the ropes that always hung at the well. I caught it with both hands. My hands had almost no strength left, but the child looped the other end around the post and began hauling, putting their whole weight into it.

Not a light body. Or rather — not weight so much as technique. I learned later that this child had grown up at the well. Someone who had learned rope work in their bones.

I was dragged up over the coping stone. And collapsed there.

The stone was warm. The sun was out. The first sun I'd seen in three days.

I still remember that warmth. The first sensation I felt, after three days in the dark. Not resentment, not fear — just warmth. The body was that simple. Even while the mind was tangled, the body still knew warm as warm.
