My eyes slowly adjusted to the light. I turned my head to the side. A low wall ran around the well, and beyond it I could see rooftops. All the same color. Same tile, same pitch. In the city I came from, every roof was a different color — depending on means, depending on when it had last been repaired. Here it wasn't like that.

A few people passed by, glanced toward the well, and kept walking. No one stopped. As if pulling a person out of the water wasn't unusual here — or as if, even if it was, no one stopped for what wasn't their business.

I thought, at the time, that this was coldness. I learned later that it wasn't. The people of this city weren't cold. They were all busy keeping their own numbers. Spending time on someone else's business, meddling in someone else's business — either one could be weighed. So no one stopped walking.

The child crouched beside me. Around fourteen, I guessed. Maybe a year younger than me, but the eyes didn't match that.

"You came out of the conduit?" the child asked. "From down there?"

"…Yes."

"It should've been closed. It's not the hour it opens, these days."

I didn't answer. I had no strength to answer, and I didn't know what to say.

The child looked me up and down. Assessing eyes. Not rude — just trying to be accurate.

"Your wrist," the child said.

"…What?"

"There's nothing on your wrist."

I looked at my own wrist. Nothing there but wrinkled, waterlogged skin. I didn't understand.

"Everyone from here has one," the child said. "A tag. Fitted when you're born. You don't have one. So—"

The child stopped and looked at me again.

"—you're not from here, are you?"

I didn't know, then, what the tag was. Two days later, in the square, I'd see one for myself. And the moment I did, I'd feel for the first time how differently this city ran. Until then, I just sat there in a stolen body.

"…No," I said. "Not from here."

It wasn't a lie. It couldn't have been, really. I truly wasn't from this city.

"Where'd you come from, then?"

I couldn't answer. I had no idea what would happen if I said the real name of my city out loud. I knew nothing about whether people commonly used the conduits to reach this place, or whether that alone would raise suspicion.

The child seemed to read my silence differently.

"It's fine," the child said. "You don't have to say if you don't want to. Plenty of people here are like that."

"…People like that?"

"People with things they don't want to talk about." The child shrugged. "What's your name?"

I wasn't at all ready for that question.

If someone asks your name, you have to give one. I froze in front of that simple rule. My name was the name that would be written on the Reckoning's sentence document. The name on the herald roster. I knew, by instinct alone, that I couldn't say it here.

Something came out of my mouth before I decided anything.

"…Had," I said.

Only after I'd said it did I realize what name that was.

Why that name came out first, I didn't know at the time. Had was the name of a boy who'd left the training grounds three years before. Maybe I picked it because I'd never run into him again. Or maybe it was for some other reason. I carried that question a long time after that day.

"Had," the child repeated. "I'm Trope."

Trope stood and looked down at me.

"Can you stand?"

I tried and sat back down. My legs had no strength at all. Trope sighed and pulled me up by the arm. Strong grip.

Standing, I checked my bag. It had stayed on my back through the current, and the strap hadn't torn. I couldn't check whether what was inside had survived. I couldn't open it in front of Trope.

Inside the bag was the sentence document. The Reckoning's sentence of demolition. I'd wrapped it in oiled paper, but after three days underwater I assumed it couldn't have survived. It had. I'd learn later that this was no accident.

Trope started walking ahead. I limped after her.

"Where are we going?" I asked.

"Somewhere to dry off, first." Trope didn't look back. "You show up in the square looking like that, everyone's going to stare."

Walking, I saw the city for the first time.

Quiet. That was my first impression. The city I came from was loud in the mornings. The market opening, merchants shouting, children running.

This city wasn't like that. People walked. Quietly, at an exact pace. Neither hurried nor idle. The streets were clean. Not a single piece of trash. Every doorstep swept.

And people kept looking up.

I didn't know, at the time, what that habit was. I thought they were checking the sky. Watching the weather. The next day I'd learn they weren't looking at the sky at all. They were looking at numbers hanging high on the building walls.

An old man passed us and nodded to Trope. Trope nodded back the same way. I couldn't tell if they knew each other, or if that was just how everyone greeted everyone in this city.

"Are people here always this quiet?" I asked.

Trope slowed for a moment.

"It's not quiet," Trope said. "Everyone's counting."

"…Counting what?"

Trope didn't answer. She just started walking again.

I thought, at the time, that not answering meant indifference. It wasn't. Trope wasn't holding back words from me — she was someone who had to re-confirm the answer to that question, herself, every single day. I only understood that much later.

We turned a corner and a small square came into view. Not many people. Something stood in the middle — I only caught it in passing and didn't know what it was. A post about twice a person's height, with something fixed to the top.

"What's that?" I asked.

Trope answered without even looking toward it.

"A terminal. There are plenty more besides that one."

"…A terminal that measures what?"

Trope stopped walking and turned to look at me. For the first time, her expression shifted, just slightly. I couldn't tell if it was surprise or something else.

"You really don't know?" Trope asked.

I couldn't answer. I didn't know whether to choose pretending not to know when I did, or actually not knowing.

"…This is my first time seeing one," I managed to say.

A lie. Few people knew what a Scales terminal was as well as I did. But I truly had never seen one shaped like this city's before.

Trope didn't press further. She just started walking again. But after that, the way she looked at me seemed slightly different.

I couldn't read that look, then. Now I know. Trope didn't start suspecting me at that moment. It was the opposite. She had never met anyone who claimed not to know the Scales, and she'd started to wonder what kind of person that made me.

Trope led me to a small shed not far from the well. It looked like a laundry-drying place. A few empty lines hung there, and an old blanket lay folded in a corner.

"Dry off here," Trope said. "I'll come back in a bit."

"…Why?"

"Why what?"

"…Why are you helping me?"

Trope looked at me for a moment. There was something in her eyes I couldn't read yet.

"It'd be strange to just leave someone who nearly drowned," Trope said. A very simple answer. Simple enough that it made me suspicious.

Trope left the door half-open on her way out.

I wrapped myself in the blanket and sat huddled in the corner of the shed. I listened to the sounds outside. Footsteps, a door opening and closing, someone occasionally calling someone else's name. None of it carried any urgency. In the city I came from, evening brought the market closing and children being called home, all layered on top of each other at once. Here the sounds didn't layer. One at a time, in order.

About half an hour later, Trope came back. She was carrying a set of dry clothes and a piece of bread.

"Put these on," Trope said, tossing the clothes to me. "They're my brother's. He doesn't wear them anymore."

"…Your brother?"

"Doesn't live here," Trope said, curtly. A tone that said not to ask more.

I didn't ask more. I took the clothes and changed. Trope stepped outside and stood with her back turned.

I was halfway through the bread when Trope came back in and sat down across from me.

"Had." Trope used my fake name. "What are you going to do here?"

I hadn't prepared an answer for that.

"…I don't know," I said. This time it wasn't a lie.

"Do you have anywhere to go?"

"…No."

Trope looked at me a moment, then nodded. She didn't ask more. She was someone who rationed her questions. I couldn't tell, then, whether that was consideration or indifference.

"I'll come back tomorrow morning," Trope said, standing. "You can stay here. Nobody much comes by."

"…Why do you keep looking out for me?"

Trope stopped at the door. She seemed to be choosing her answer.

"I don't know," Trope finally said. "Just because."

Then she left.

I thought about that "just because" for a long time afterward. Set against what I learned later, that answer wasn't a lie. Trope really didn't know the reason. No — it wasn't that there was no reason, but that she must have thought it was still too soon to put that reason into her own words. I wouldn't learn what that reason was until much later.

I pulled the blanket around myself. Then I opened my bag and checked inside.

The sentence document was wet, but not torn. The lettering hadn't run. I wrapped it back in the oiled paper and pushed it deep into the bag.

That was the first time I was truly alone inside this city. Stranger, somehow, than three days of darkness. In the dark, at least, I'd known where I had to go. Not now. I was sitting there with the very sentence I'd fled from, hidden in a bag, in the very city that sentence was meant for.

Evening light came through the gaps in the shed. I sat leaning against the wall, wrapped in the blanket. My body was exhausted, but sleep wouldn't come.

I'd hated this city for three years. In those three years, I'd never once actually seen it. I'd only known its name. That hating a place for three years on nothing but a name was possible — until that moment, I'd never once thought it strange.

And now I was lying under this city's blanket, wearing this city's clothes, eating bread handed to me by this city's child.

I didn't see that contradiction clearly that night. I had no strength left to see it. A body that had survived three days had no energy left to think. But I felt one thing. That hatred could go this quiet in front of the thing it hated. I was afraid of that. I didn't yet know what came next, once hatred went quiet.

Somewhere in the distance, something like a bell rang once. Low and short. I didn't know what it signaled. Trope was already gone, and there was no one to ask.

I closed my eyes. And for the first time in a long while, I fell asleep without counting anything.

I wouldn't learn until the next morning that this was the last night in this city I would fall asleep without counting.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. The Days Remaining

_Previously_ — The child who pulled Pheugon from the well at the Reckoning was named Trope. Pheugon hid his real name and lied, calling himself "Had." Trope brought him dry clothes and bread and let him sleep in the shed. The city was quiet and clean, and people kept glancing up. Pheugon didn't ask why, and fell asleep without counting anything.

The first thought I had when I opened my eyes that morning was a number.

Habit. Three years of checking my weight first thing every morning — the habit had stayed in my body even in this city, where nothing marked my wrist. I raised my hand, then realized there was nowhere to raise it to, and lowered it again.

I still can't quite explain the emptiness of that moment. Having no number should have been a relief. It wasn't. I didn't know how to live without a number.

Morning light came through the gap in the shed door. I folded the blanket and went outside.

Outside, people were already moving. Someone carrying a water jar, someone with a broom. The same quiet I'd seen the day before held true in the morning too. Nobody was running.

At that point I was still thinking of this city's soon-to-vanish fate and this morning scene as two separate things. I didn't yet know that they were the same morning of the same city — that there was no contradiction between them at all.

Trope hadn't come yet. I hesitated in front of the shed a moment, then decided to walk, staying close by.

Around a corner I found the same kind of post I'd seen the day before. A post about twice a person's height. A plate fixed to the top, with a number floating on it.

I went closer.

39.

That was all. No other letters. No city name, no clause number, no explanation. Just a number.

I stopped where I stood.

I knew that number. Knew it exactly. Forty days' grace. Sentence of demolition. The number I'd been counting since the day I received the document in the supervisor's office. And that number was already being counted here, in the middle of this city, without me.

I stood in front of it a long time. It made no sense. A sentence only takes effect once someone reads it aloud. I hadn't read anything yet. Not a single letter.

And yet the number already stood at thirty-nine.

A woman passing by stopped beside me and looked up at the plate. Older woman, carrying a market basket. She looked at the number for a long while, then walked on with no expression at all.

I followed her and asked.

"…Excuse me."

She turned.

"What's that number?" I asked.

She looked at me strangely for a moment, then seemed to place me as an outsider. Her expression softened a little.

"The days remaining," she said. "If you have to ask, you really must be from out of town."

"…Days remaining."

"Until demolition," she said, curtly. "Our city. The notice already came. Just the one who reads it hasn't come yet."

I couldn't breathe.

"…The one who reads it?"

"The herald," she said. "The Scales finished its calculation. The sentence document's already out. But it only really starts once someone reads it aloud. That person hasn't come yet. So everyone's waiting. Nobody knows what happens if that number hits zero and no one's come."

"…Has this happened before?" I asked. "In another city."

The woman shrugged.

"I've heard of it," she said. "Some far-off city, back when my grandmother was young, is what they say. But I don't know anyone who's actually seen it. A whole city ending up like this is very rare. So nobody really knows what to do, either."

She looked back up at the plate.

"Our Adjudicator's keeping watch on that number. Whatever happens, he's the one who'd know best," she said. "So we're just waiting. Until he says something."

I couldn't say anything, standing there. She probably had no idea why I was standing there like that. It was the first time I truly understood it. The Scales sends its notice to a city the moment its calculation is finished. It's true a person has to read it aloud for it to take effect, but the Scales has already fixed when that person will arrive. The fortieth day. What happens if no one comes that day — nobody knows. I didn't know either.

After the woman left, I looked up at the plate again.

39.

That was a notice. Not a verdict — a notice. I understood the difference for the first time that day. The Scales had its own way of telling a city this news without me. My running away didn't leave this city in the dark about anything. This city already knew. What was coming for it. How much time was left. Only the hand to actually open that door was missing.

Only I knew that hand was mine.

I kept walking toward the square. The farther I walked, the more plates I saw. On building walls, at the well, at the market entrance. Different sizes, but all the same number. 39.

People walked past them with a glance and moved on. No crying, no shouting. What Trope had said yesterday came back to me. "Everyone's counting."

Nearer the market, the crowd thickened. A vegetable seller was setting out her stall, a woman selling cloth was arranging her wares. One person was haggling over a price with a merchant. The merchant wasn't giving ground.

"You want a discount because we've got thirty-nine days left?" the merchant said. "Goods sold today get today's price."

A few people nearby laughed at that. Real laughter. I found it strange. Under a number counting down to the city's end, people were still bargaining, still laughing.

I read that, at the time, as calm. I read it differently now. It wasn't calm — it was endurance. Keep doing today's work today — that was the only method these people had left. If there's nowhere to flee, and no way to turn the number back, all that's left is to do today's work today.

Somewhere, someone in an official's uniform rang a bell and called something out. I froze, thinking it was the sentence document.

"West well maintenance starting tomorrow. No water in the mornings," the official called. "Use the east well."

Just a routine notice. People nodded and dispersed. The city was counting the day of its own end while still maintaining its wells.

I felt something in front of that scene I couldn't quite name. I expected to feel anger, and didn't. Not because this city was calm about it, but because the fact that it was still moving as a city at all was what shook me. I had already thought of this city as a place already finished. But this city didn't yet think of itself that way.

Leaving the market on my way to the square, I saw an old man.

He was standing in front of a plate mounted on a wall, not moving. Unlike the others who glanced and walked on, he stood rooted to the spot. The hand gripping his cane was trembling.

I stopped and, passing beside him, looked at his face.

He was crying. No sound. Just tears. He didn't wipe them away.

I've never forgotten that old man. The only tears I saw that day. Everyone else was calm; only he wasn't. I wondered, then, why this one man couldn't endure it the way the others did. I ask it differently now. Were the others really enduring it — or had they, like the old man, already spent all the strength they had for crying?

I felt I should go over and say something to him. I didn't. I didn't know what to say. I didn't know if I had any right to say anything at all to this city.

I just walked past. That was the biggest lie I told that day. Bigger than the name Had. I walked past that old man pretending to know nothing, pretending none of it concerned me.

On one side of the square, some children were playing. A game where you threw stones into a chalked circle — listening closer, they were counting numbers as they played.

"Thirty-nine, thirty-eight, thirty-seven—"

Each throw lowered the count by one. I couldn't tell whether it was a rule of the game or just the children bringing the number they saw every day into their play.

Something in me twisted, watching them. An emotion I couldn't name exactly. Not anger, not sadness. That these children had turned their city's ending into a counting game, and how natural that looked — that was what shook me.

One child spotted me and asked.

"Mister, can you count too?"

"…I count."

"How many left, you think?"

I didn't answer. The child seemed satisfied without an answer and went back to the game.

I sat down in the shade at the edge of the square. One question wouldn't leave my head.

Why wasn't anyone fleeing?

In the city I came from, half the people would have already packed their bags. A whole city's demolition, with thirty-nine days' grace — anyone who could move would have moved in that time.

Not here. All that morning, walking, I hadn't seen a single person packing, not a single cart leaving.

I found it strange, almost frightening. Knowing a whole city would disappear, and still doing today's work, selling today's goods, drawing today's water. As if the number thirty-nine weren't real, or as if they believed it had nothing to do with them.

It wasn't that. I only understood by that evening. It wasn't that they didn't believe the number. They believed it too well — so well that there was nothing left to do but believe, and so they chose to live today instead. I didn't know the difference then.

Trope arrived beside me without my noticing.

"What are you looking at?"

I startled and shifted a little.

"…Why isn't anyone leaving?" I asked. "This city's disappearing, you said. Within thirty-nine days."

Trope glanced at the square's plate. Her expression didn't change.

"Wherever you go, there's a Scales," Trope said.

"…What difference does that make?"

"Leaving gets you nothing. Every other city has a Scales too, and its own weight there. Nothing you've built up here disappears. You carry all of it with you."

I thought I understood that at the time. Actually I'd only understood half of it. What Trope meant wasn't only about the system. I wouldn't fully understand it until weeks later, when I heard her mother's story.

"And," Trope added. "This is my city."

She said it without looking at me. She was looking at the square's plate.

39.

I looked at her profile for a long time. I didn't know what to say, so I said nothing.

We sat like that for a while. Trope spoke first.

"Had," Trope asked. "You don't say where you came from, you don't know where you're going. So what are you going to do with your thirty-nine days here?"

I had no answer to that question. Or rather — I had an answer, but couldn't give it. I'm here to watch this city fall. Just imagining saying it made my stomach turn.

"…I don't know," I said again.

"You keep saying that," Trope said. Not laughing, but not mocking either. Just stating a fact.

"…Sorry."

"Nothing to be sorry about." Trope stood. "Come with me, then. If you've got nothing to do, there's something you can help with."

"…What kind of thing?"

"Errands at the Scales. It's what I do."

My body went stiff at that. I hadn't expected the words "at the Scales" to carry so much weight.

"…What do you do there?"

Trope started walking and answered.

"You'll see when we get there."

Following Trope, I looked back once more at the plate in the square.

39.

I haven't forgotten that number for a single day since. It was a different kind of number from the hundred and twelve cases I'd counted over three years. Those numbers had belonged to other people. This number was one I'd made. No — not made by me. Made by the Scales. And yet I felt, standing in front of it, as if I'd carved it in myself.

It wasn't guilt. Not yet, at that point. It was closer to a strange sense of ownership. This number is mine. I brought it to this city. I had fled, but this number hadn't followed me — it had already arrived here first, waiting for me.

I still couldn't fully feel the hundred and twenty thousand people inside that number as people. Trope was the one exception. Trope had a face, a name, a hand that had pulled me out of the water the day before. The rest — a hundred and twenty thousand — were still just a number to me.

I don't remember exactly when the rest began to gain faces. Only that it started that day, in that square, in the long moment I spent looking at Trope's profile.

Trope was already walking well ahead. I quickened my step to catch up.

I didn't know, then, what "errands at the Scales" would mean, or what I'd see standing in front of it. I just followed Trope. In a fled city, under a fled name, with a fled sentence in my bag.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. Before the Scales

_Previously_ — The number "39" hung everywhere across the Reckoning. People lived beneath it, and still no one fled. Pheugon asked why, and slowly began to sense that the number had a hundred and twenty thousand faces. Trope took him along to help with errands at the Scales.

The Reckoning's Scales was nothing like the one I knew.

In the city I came from, there were six terminals. Three in the square, two at the market entrance, one in the herald office corridor. Small, quiet machines — you raised your hand, and a number appeared.

The Reckoning's Scales was the entire square.

Trope led me to the middle of the city, an open plaza with clear sky on every side. In the center stood a huge plate, facing the sky. As tall as three or four people stacked together. Beneath it, a long line stretched out.

"This is the main office," Trope said. "Everyone comes here to check. The small plates around the neighborhoods are just notices — the real checking happens here."

Everyone in line held their wrist out in front of them. Something was fastened to each wrist. The tag Trope had mentioned the day before.

Up close, it was a thin metal band. A small groove on the inside. When their turn came, people touched that groove to a stand beneath the plate. A moment later, a number appeared on the plate, above, in the position corresponding to that person.

I found the method strangely elaborate. Where I came from, raising your hand made a number appear on a small screen only you could see. Quiet. Not here. The Reckoning put a person's weight up on a plate the whole square could see, right above that person's head.

That was this city's way. Weight wasn't a private matter here. It was public. I saw that difference for the first time that day, and it would be a long while before I understood what it meant.

The line moved forward little by little. The turn came for the young man standing ahead of me. He touched his wrist to the stand.

A number appeared on the plate, at his spot. It must have risen quite a bit from the day before. His shoulders visibly stiffened.

A few people nearby glanced at the number. No one stared outright, but he clearly knew they'd all seen it. He bowed his head and hurried away.

I saw this city's cruelty for the first time in that scene. It wasn't the Scales that was cruel. The Scales only produced a number. What was cruel was where that number hung. Where I came from, you saw your own number alone. Even if you'd done something shameful, that shame stayed inside you and ended there. Here, that shame hung before the eyes of the entire square.

That day, I thought this the more honest way. If you have nothing to hide, there's nothing to fear. How naive that thought was, I would only understand later.

Trope didn't join the line — she turned off to the side. There was a small table there, and an old man sitting behind it. He seemed unwell; two canes stood propped beside him.

"You came," the man said, smiling at Trope.

"I'll do it for you again today," Trope said.

Trope carefully lifted the man's wrist and touched it to the stand. He didn't seem able to raise his hand that high on his own. A moment later, a number appeared on the plate. He looked up at it and nodded.

"Same as before," he said. "Good."

"It's not good, it's just what should happen," Trope said. "You're not doing anything these days anyway, Grandfather."

"Doing nothing is the good thing, then," he said, laughing.

Trope set down a water bottle beside him and asked briefly how he was doing. The man seemed to know her well. Like people who'd known each other a long time.

In that short exchange, I began to see a little of who Trope was. A girl who rationed her words about other people's business unhesitatingly lifted the hand of an old man who couldn't move well. I hadn't yet managed to connect those two as the same person.

Standing there awkwardly, the old man looked up at me and asked.

"Who's this?"

"Had," Trope answered for me. "He's agreed to help for a few days."

"Outsider," the man said, glancing at my wrist. "No tag."

"…Yes."

"Tags get fitted at birth," the man said. He explained without being asked — old people sometimes do that. "Can't take it off. There's no way to take it off. You wear it until you die. Proof you belong to this city."

"…And after you die?"

"Even I don't know that," he laughed. "Haven't died yet."

Trope laughed a little beside him. I found the laugh unfamiliar. It was the softest expression I'd seen on her face so far.

I would remember that laugh for a long time. I'd see it a few more times after that, but each time it surprised me. Probably because I'd already noticed, that first time, how rare it was.

That wasn't all the table work involved. Trope helped two more people there. One a woman who couldn't see well, another a young woman carrying a small child on her back. The woman with the child had no free hands, so Trope touched her wrist to the groove for her.

"You help like this every day?" I asked.

"Not every day," Trope said. "Whenever someone needs it."

"…Do you get paid?"

Trope looked at me as if I'd said something strange.

"No," Trope said. "Should I?"

I couldn't answer. Where I came from, errands got paid in coin as a matter of course. This city seemed to run on a different rule.

When the work was done, Trope touched her own wrist to the stand.

A number appeared on the plate.

4.

A low number. Very low. I felt a small jolt of surprise seeing it. I'd vaguely assumed that someone Trope's age would have accumulated more weight by now.

Trope looked at the number with no expression at all. As if she saw it every day — which, indeed, she did.

"…That's low," I said.

"It should be," Trope said, lowering her wrist. "Living low is the goal, isn't it."

I took that as obvious at the time. In a city with a Scales, everyone lives that way — low, cautious. So did I. But something else was mixed into Trope's answer. I couldn't put my finger on it then. I learned later. For Trope, a low number wasn't a goal. It was proof. Proof that she was different from her mother.

Trope looked at me this time.

"Had — aren't you going to check?"

I should have anticipated that question, but I hadn't.

"…I don't have a tag here," I said, raising my wrist. Bare skin, nothing on it.

"You can do it temporarily," Trope said, pointing toward the table. "Outsiders can check too. You just write down your name and where you're from."

I stiffened.

I was afraid of two things at once, in that moment. One was having to write a name. If I wrote the name Had into this city's official record, there was even the smallest chance that record could later be cross-checked against my real name. The other was simpler. I didn't want to see my own weight. There had been a reason I'd suddenly stopped my three years of daily checking. I wasn't yet ready to face what I was doing, myself.

"…It's fine," I said. "I won't."

"Why?"

"…No reason."

Trope looked at me for a moment. A look similar to the one from the day before, when I'd said I didn't know what a Scales terminal was. But this time, she held it a little longer.

"Strange," Trope said. "Everyone's curious."

"…I'm not curious."

"That's exactly what's strange." Trope didn't press further. She just left that remark and moved on to the next person.

I've come back to that moment many times since. Trope didn't start suspecting me that day. I know now. That day, Trope had simply met, for the first time, someone who didn't want to see their own weight. The people of this city aren't afraid to see their own numbers. Low, and they're relieved; high, and they're careful. Nobody wants not to see it. I was, for the first time in that city, someone who didn't dare face their own number.

Even after Trope moved to the next person, I stood by that table for a long while. The stand was right in front of me. Close enough to reach.

In that moment I felt my hand trying, on its own, to rise. Three years of the same motion, every day. My body remembered the rule. I watched that hand and forced myself to hold it back. It felt like holding down someone else's hand.

What I was really afraid of, I didn't know exactly, then. I know now. I wasn't afraid the number would be high. I was afraid it would be low. That the number of someone who'd fled — someone who had decided to abandon an entire city — might still read low. That even the Scales wouldn't notice that.

We left the square and walked. The sun had passed midday.

"You come here every day?" I asked.

"Almost," Trope said. "Grandfather and that woman who can't see well — there's no one else to help them."

"…Isn't it hard?"

Trope glanced back at me.

"What's hard about it?" she asked back. Not mocking — genuinely curious.

I couldn't answer.

"It's just something I do every day," Trope said, facing forward again. "You don't think about hard or not hard before you do it. You just do it. If I don't, nobody looks after their numbers."

"…Why does that matter? What changes if their number isn't seen?"

Trope stopped walking.

"If a number suddenly jumps," Trope said. "You know what that means? Someone did something to that person. Robbed them, or cheated them, or hurt them. If you don't watch a number every day, that kind of thing can happen and pass right by, unnoticed. That's what scares me. Something passing by, unnoticed."

I had no answer to that.

It was the first time I considered that the Scales might not simply be a tool of punishment. For Trope, that number wasn't a judgment — it was a signal. A signal telling her that something bad had happened to someone. I'd never once held that view before. To me, a number had always been the size of a sin. To Trope, it was sometimes a trace of what someone else had done to a person.

"Who's your grandfather?" I asked, trying to turn the subject a little.

"Neighbor," Trope said. "I've known him since I was small."

"…And your family?"

Trope's pace slowed for a moment.

"None," Trope said. "It's just me."

I couldn't ask further. I felt there was something inside that short answer, but I didn't know whether I had any right to ask what it was.

I thought about that answer for a long time that night. How light the tone was in which "it's just me" came out. Someone who tells a heavy story in a light tone has already told that story hundreds of times before. I didn't know that then. I'd learn it a few days later.

Trope walked me back to the shed. Before we parted, she suddenly asked.

"Had's not your real name, is it?"

I thought my heart would stop.

"…Why do you say that?"

"You're always half a beat late answering to it," Trope said. "Nobody's ever late answering to their own name."

I said nothing.

"It's fine," Trope said. "You don't have to say. I've got things I'm not saying either."

Then she turned and walked away. I watched her back for a long time.

I should have understood, in that moment, that Trope had already seen right through me. But that day, I only felt relief. Relief at not being caught. That I'd already been caught, in all but name, I couldn't see that yet.

Alone in front of the shed, I looked back once toward the square. In the distance, that huge plate was visible. Everyone's numbers hung there, facing the sky. The sun was setting above it.

Looking at that plate, I thought: this city doesn't know my number. However long I might live here, whatever I might do, there's no spot for me on that plate. I thought that was freedom. I know now. It wasn't freedom — it was absence. I was the one person in this city who was inside it but didn't belong to it. How dangerous that position was, I didn't know then.

Having no number on the plate, and having no name on the plate, were different things. I was free of the number, but not of the name. Somewhere, on the herald roster, I was still written down under my exact name. Pheugon. Assigned to the Reckoning. That name was waiting for me, unknown to this city's Scales, somewhere outside this city.

I pushed that thought away as I closed the shed door. I believed, then, that this was possible. That in a few days, or a few weeks, that name and that place would have nothing more to do with this city. I believed I wasn't hiding — I was only waiting. Without being able to say exactly what I was waiting for.

I didn't check my weight in that square that day. I didn't check it for a long while after, either. Trope was the only one who found that strange, and that it happened to be her who noticed the strangeness — looking back — was no accident.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. Her Mother

_Previously_ — In the main square of the Reckoning, Pheugon finally refused to check his own weight. Trope found this strange, and before they parted, quietly said she'd already guessed his name wasn't really Had. Pheugon ended the day believing he hadn't been caught.

The next morning, Trope came late.

I sat in front of the shed and waited a long while. The sun was well up before she appeared. Her expression was a little different than usual.

"…Is something wrong?" I asked.

"No," Trope said. Too quick an answer.

I didn't press her further, then. I thought I had no right to ask. Looking back, it wasn't a matter of right. I was simply afraid. Not ready to hear someone else's heavy story.

We walked toward the well that day. Where Trope drew water every day. The same well I'd first washed up at.

Filling the water jar, Trope suddenly said.

"I used to play here a lot. When I was little."

"…Alone?"

"With my mother."

I stiffened a little, hearing that word. It was the first time Trope had brought something up on her own.

Trope kept talking, fiddling with the jar's handle.

"My mother taught me how to use a rope, here. You saw how good I am with a rope, right? I learned it from her."

"…And now?"

Trope finished filling the jar and stood up.

"She's not here," Trope said. A flat voice. "She got pushed out."

Trope hoisted the jar onto her shoulder and ran her hand once over the well's coping stone.

"My mother's hand left a mark here, probably," Trope said. "She drew water here every single morning. The stone's a little worn. There, that spot."

I looked where she pointed. The stone really was slightly worn — a mark left by the same hand brushing the same spot for years.

"…Do you remember? Her face."

"Yes," Trope said. "Clearly. It's strange. It's been five years and it hasn't faded at all. I remember her voice too. Waking me up in the morning, calling my name."

Trope paused for a moment.

"But I don't remember much about the last day," Trope said. "Strange, right? The most important day. I came home from school and she was gone. What the weather was like that day, what I ate — none of it, I don't remember any of it."

I knew the phrase "pushed out." I knew it as a concept. When accumulated weight exceeds an individual's limit, everything that person uses stops, one by one. Housing, employment, market pass, rations. Without anyone laying a hand on them, that person ends up outside the city.

I only knew it as a principle. I'd never seen who it actually happened to.

"…When?" I asked.

"Five years ago," Trope said. "I was nine."

Trope picked up the jar and started walking. I followed alongside. I didn't know what to say.

"What happened?" I asked, carefully.

Trope stopped walking for a moment, then started again.

"It wasn't anything big," Trope said. "At least, not any single thing. But it added up. After my father left first, my mother raised me alone for years, little by little. Cheating on a price, or lying to get more rations. Nothing big, any of it. But it all added together."

"…So it went over the limit?" I asked.

"Yes," Trope said. "One morning her market pass was blocked. The following week her rations stopped. Then the house too."

"…And you?"

"I was a child," Trope said. "None of that attaches to children. I got to stay."

Something felt wrong to me about that. A structure where only the child stayed and only the mother had to leave. But I couldn't put words to exactly what was wrong.

"Where did your mother go?"

"I don't know," Trope said. "All I know is she left the city. No word since."

"…Alone? Leaving behind a nine-year-old?"

"There was nothing to choose," Trope said. Her voice sped up a little. For the first time, a defensive tone. "The house was cut off, the rations were cut off — from that day on, my mother had no way to live in this city. I could stay, so she left me here. She didn't abandon me."

Trope seemed to turn that sentence over in her own mind after saying it.

"…She didn't abandon me," Trope said once more. This time it seemed less spoken to me than to herself.

We walked in silence for a while. The jar was heavy, and Trope's pace slowed a little. I offered to carry it instead; she shook her head.

I thought, in that moment, how many times Trope must have said that sentence to herself. She didn't abandon me. I understood for the first time then that there are people who have to say a sentence hundreds of times just to get through today.

Near the shed, Trope set the jar down. I found myself curious and asked.

"So who did you live with after that?"

"Just alone, the first few days," Trope said. "Then that grandfather came looking for me. The one at the Scales. He used to be our next-door neighbor. He came and cooked for me, found me a place instead of home. From then until now."

"…He's not family, though."

"No," Trope said. "Does that matter?"

I couldn't answer.

"The Scales doesn't know family," Trope said. "It doesn't weigh who looks after whom, anything like that. But people weigh it. Grandfather just weighed it and came. A nine-year-old left all alone."

Listening to that, I felt a strange comfort. That someone stepped in, in a spot the Scales couldn't reach, even inside this exact, precise city. I thought, at the time, that this was an exception. I know now. It wasn't an exception — it was a different kind of arithmetic that this city had made for itself, in every spot the Scales could never finally reach.

Trope set the jar down and sat against the wall. I sat beside her.

"Aren't you angry?" I asked. "At the Scales."

Trope looked at me. Her expression shifted, for the first time, but it wasn't anger.

"Why would I be angry?" Trope asked back.

"…It took your mother."

"The Scales didn't take her," Trope said. "The Scales just did the math. It only calculated what my mother did."

"…Still, though."

Trope looked up at the sky for a moment.

"Because it was right," Trope said.

I had no answer to that.

"What my mother did — it was real," Trope went on. "The lies were real, the cheating was real too. I didn't know when I was little, but it came back to me piece by piece as I grew. I know everything the Scales counted. Every last piece of it. I'd like to say the Scales was wrong. I can't. All of it's true."

I felt something strange, standing in front of that.

I hadn't known, until then, that knowing the Scales is right and enduring the loss of your family to the Scales could exist inside the same feeling. I'd thought the two stood opposed. If it's right, you can bear it; if it's unjust, you get angry. Trope stood somewhere in between. Knowing it was right, and still, every day, that spot stayed empty.

"…You don't resent her?" I asked again. "Your mother."

"Resent her?" Trope asked back. "I know what she did wrong. But that's different from resentment. My mother did it to feed me. I know that too."

Trope pulled her knees up and hugged them.

"The Scales doesn't count reasons," Trope said. "It doesn't weigh reasons. It only weighs what was done."

That stayed with me a long time.

It was the first time I felt the Scales' precision as something frightening. For three years, I'd thought that precision made me safe. Precise meant nothing to feel wronged about. Sitting next to Trope, I first understood how narrow a thought that had been. Being precise and not feeling wronged were different problems. Trope's mother had been calculated with precision, and that calculation hadn't been wrong. And still Trope was left alone at nine.

I thought of my own family's story. Someone from the Reckoning had done something that ruined us. The Scales had calculated that precisely too. The disposition had come out precise too. I'd had no complaint about that disposition for a long time. Because it was precise. But that day, sitting next to Trope, I felt for the first time how thin that sentence had been. Precise, and yet nothing came back. Trope's words laid themselves exactly over my own old sentence.

"…I'm sorry," I said. I didn't know what I was apologizing for either.

Trope looked at me strangely.

"Why are you sorry, Had?"

I couldn't answer.

That was the first time I began to see one person of this city not as part of the city I hated, but simply as a person. It wasn't something I'd wanted. I kept wanting to bundle this city together as the number a hundred and twenty thousand, one mass. It was easier to hate that way. Trope kept slipping out of that bundle.

Trope suddenly changed the subject.

"Pheugon," Trope asked. "Was it like this back home? Someone getting pushed out."

I couldn't answer right away.

"…It was," I finally said. "Not often, though."

"Have you seen it? Someone getting pushed out."

I thought of the hundred and twelve cases. The sentence documents I'd read aloud myself. Several of them had ended in the exact same disposition as Trope's mother. Exceeded the limit, market access suspended, then pushed out. I couldn't remember a single one of their faces. I'd erased them. A hundred and eleven more times over.

"…Yes," I said. "I've seen it."

"How did it feel?"

I nearly told her the truth, right then. I'm the one who reads those sentence documents aloud, I nearly said. The words rose to my throat and I swallowed them. Swallowing made everything inside me heavier. I didn't know, then, where that heaviness came from. I know now. It wasn't the weight of the lie. It was the weight of realizing, in front of Trope, for the first time, that I wasn't a bystander — I was the one who carried it out.

"…I don't know," was all I said.

Trope didn't press further. She just nodded and stood.

"Let's go. Need to draw water once more before dark."

We went back to the well. While Trope lowered the bucket, I sat on the coping stone and looked again at the spot she'd pointed to earlier. The place worn by a hand. A mark that hadn't disappeared in five years.

Looking at that worn stone, I thought: the Scales can't measure that. That's not weight. It's a mark time left behind, and the person who left it is nowhere in this city anymore. But the stone still testified that she had been there. I thought, for the first time, that things the Scales couldn't measure might be piled up like this all over the city. I didn't hold onto that thought for long. I wasn't ready yet to see what it meant.

The bucket came up. Trope poured the water into the jar and glanced my way.

"Why do you keep looking at that spot?"

"…It's nothing."

"That's not a nothing expression," Trope said, hoisting the jar to her shoulder. "Fine. I won't ask."

Every time she said that, I felt something strange. Trope was someone who didn't press. But not pressing, and knowing nothing — those were different things. I mistook the two for the same, at the time.

On the way back to the shed, the sun was sinking low. At the end of the alley, the plate that announced the days remaining came into view. Trope passed it without lingering on it. By now, so did I. Watching a number every day eventually does that to a person. I felt that as frightening for the first time that day.

Trope left first, and I sat alone in front of the shed, watching the sky darken.

"Because it was right." That one sentence followed me all through the night. I set it against my own story. When someone from the Reckoning wrecked our family, the Scales had calculated that too, precisely. The disposition had come out precise too. And yet I hadn't been able to say what Trope said, in front of that disposition. Because it was right. I'd never once put that sentence into my own mouth.

Trope could say it in front of the Scales that had taken her mother. I couldn't say it in front of the man who'd wrecked our home. The same sentence, but one of us lived by it while the other hadn't managed to swallow it in thirty-one years. I didn't know, that night, where that difference came from. I only knew clearly that the difference unsettled me.

And in that unease, I noticed one small thing more. I'd already been in this city for several days now. Under the name Had, helping with errands at the Scales, drawing well water, asking after an old man's health. I was becoming, little by little, a person of this city, day by day. And as I became that person, I kept forgetting the sentence I'd brought to this city, more and more often.

I didn't think, that night, that this was a dangerous thing. I know now. Forgetting and hiding are different things. I thought I was hiding my name. In fact I was forgetting myself. And of the two, the one that would hurt me more, in the end, was the second.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. The Herald

_Previously_ — Trope's mother had exceeded the weight limit and been pushed out of the city five years before. Trope didn't resent the Scales for it — "Because it was right." That sentence unsettled Pheugon for a long time; he'd never once been able to say the same about his own family's ruin, and for the first time he noticed he was becoming, little by little, a person of this city.

Starting the next day, I took on one more job.

Trope hadn't asked for it. The old man had asked first. "Could use another pair of young hands." Carrying goods for the market stalls. I said I would. No other reason. It beat sitting in the shed just counting numbers.

I thought, at the time, that this was just a way to pass the hours. I know now. It wasn't. Doing that work, I began learning the names of this city's people, one by one. Learning a name means lifting a person out of a number into a person. I did that work every day without understanding, for a long while, what it really was.

The market work was simple. Helping set up the stalls in the morning, carrying goods, helping close up in the evening. The merchants knew I was a stranger, but within a few days they stopped minding much.

"Had." The vegetable seller called my name. Very naturally attached to me by now. "Move that sack over there."

I still reacted half a beat late every time someone called that name. Exactly as Trope had once pointed out. But lately that half-beat was shrinking, little by little. I thought it was only becoming familiar.

It wasn't that. It wasn't familiarity — it was dye taking. I didn't know the difference then. Growing familiar means adjusting only on the surface. Being dyed means that name becoming real from the inside out. I was becoming Had, little by little, every day, and Pheugon was receding by that same measure.

About a week into working the market, something like an incident happened.

There was a wooden board at the market entrance where the day's notices were posted. Well maintenance, ration schedules, things like that. That day an official came to post a new notice, but the person who usually read it aloud wasn't there.

"My throat's gone," the usual reader said beside the official, his voice hoarse. "I won't be able to read for a few days."

People had gathered. Everyone seemed curious what the notice said. The official looked briefly at a loss, then glanced around.

"Anyone here who can read?"

No one stepped forward readily. The people of this city didn't seem to enjoy standing up to represent something. I too stood still, up to that point.

Then a paper crossed in front of my eyes.

"You." The official looked at me. "You're young — you can read, can't you?"

I froze.

In that moment, I thought two things at once. One: no. The moment my voice slipped out, three years of training would show plainly. The way of not stopping, not catching, not holding my breath at a name. If I read in a way this city's people didn't know, someone was sure to ask what it was.

The other thought was scarier. In that moment, I already wanted to read. I wanted to hold the paper in my hands. I'd gone too long without doing the thing I'd done every day for three years.

I took the paper.

The content was nothing much. A notice that the west distribution station's hours were changing. A sentence composed by the Scales. Short and dry.

I took a breath. Then I read.

Without stopping. Without catching. Straight through.

Looking up when I finished, a few people were watching me with strange eyes. The official was one of them.

"…That's good reading," the official said. "Where'd you learn that?"

"…Nowhere," I said. "I've just read a lot."

Not a lie. But that not-quite-a-lie answer was, in its own way, a bigger lie. I was someone who had read a hundred and twelve cases. Shrinking that down to "I've read a lot" — in that very moment, I was erasing the weight of what I'd done, myself.

The official didn't ask further. I was relieved.

"Mind doing it again sometime?" the official asked. "Just until his throat's better."

I hesitated over my answer.

I should have refused, then. I know now. I had a hundred reasons to refuse. And yet I agreed. Why, I couldn't properly explain to myself, even that night.

"…All right," I said.

From then on, roughly every three days I stood in front of that board. I read the notices. Ration times, well maintenance, changes to market rules. All trivial sentences. And yet every time I read those trivial sentences, I felt something loosening, little by little, somewhere deep inside my body.

I didn't know what it was, at the time. I know now. I'd been trained into someone who couldn't live without using his voice. Reading a hundred and twelve cases, I'd thought that training had turned me from a person into a tool. But in this city, in a place where no one knew me as a herald, I began using that voice again. And when I found that the voice was still mine, I felt strangely relieved.

I wasn't a tool. I was just someone who happened to be good at reading. I hadn't known until then how much that distinction mattered.

One day the old man was listening to me read and said.

"Your voice, it's good," he said, leaning on his cane. "No anger in it, no rushing. Just pleasant to listen to."

I had no answer to that.

"Where'd you learn to read like that?"

I nearly gave him the true answer, standing in front of that question. At the herald training grounds, I nearly said. The words rose to my throat and went back down. I swallowed it that day too. But the swallowing felt different from before. Before, I'd swallowed it out of fear of being caught. That day — I'm not entirely sure, even now. Maybe it was a swallowing born of wanting to say it.

"…Just, ever since I was little," I said.

The old man nodded. He didn't ask further.

After that, people started coming to me first whenever there was something to read. I read distribution slips aloud for old people who couldn't read them, looked over contracts brought by unfamiliar merchants. Each time, I walked a little further into this city.

I thought this was kindness. Helping people who needed it. I know now. It wasn't just kindness. I was still doing, in the city I hated most, the thing I did best. Every time I did it, people trusted me a little more, and the more of that trust I received, the less able I became to go far from this place.

That week, one evening, the old man invited me to his table. Trope was there, along with the woman who couldn't see well and a couple of other neighbors. A small courtyard, a single table, everyone gathered around it.

"Sit, Had," the old man said. "There's extra food."

I first tried to decline. But Trope had already set a bowl in my place. There was nothing to do but sit.

The meal was quiet. This city's people rationed their words even at meals. But the quiet wasn't awkward. It was, instead, comfortable. I found that unfamiliar. Where I came from, someone's voice was always raised over the table. Here there was only the sound of spoons.

"Does Had have family?" one neighbor asked. A question tossed out without much thought.

I stopped mid-spoonful.

It was probably the simplest question at that table, that evening. Something everyone else would have let pass without a thought. Not for me. In front of that question, I thought of my house all at once, of the man from the Reckoning, of my scattered brother. And I swallowed all of it.

"…I'm alone," I said. This time, not a lie.

"Ah," the neighbor said. "That's how you drifted all the way here, then."

I didn't answer. Trope glanced at me and looked back at her bowl. As if trying to change the subject. But someone else spoke up first.

"Why'd you come to this city?" the old man asked. No ill intent. He always asked that way.

I sat there with no answer prepared.

It was at that table that I felt, for the first time, that lying to these people wasn't easy anymore. When I'd first told Trope "Had," there'd been no weight to it at all. Just a fake name thrown at a strange child. Lying now, at this table, in front of people who'd sat me down to dinner — that was an entirely different weight.

"…Passing through," I managed to say. "Just ended up staying."

The old man nodded. He didn't press further. No one did. This city's people respected each other that way. I thought I had no right to receive that respect — and yet I received it.

I would remember that table for a long time. The clink of spoons, the old man's laugh, the sound Trope made clearing the bowls. Listening to those sounds, I thought, for the first time: if this city disappears, these sounds disappear too. I couldn't hold onto that thought for long. It felt wrong to hold onto.

Nearly a month passed, and I gained a quiet place among this city's people. Had, who helps with Trope's errands. Had, who helps with errands at the Scales. Had, who reads the notices. People started attaching "that young man" instead of just "him" after my name. It meant becoming someone whose face was known.

I only thought that was good, at the time. I believed I was succeeding at hiding. But hiding was supposed to mean disappearing. Instead of disappearing, I kept getting filled in. A spot for me was slowly opening up in this city's fabric.

Later, much later, I would learn how dangerous those weeks had been. What I'd hidden was one name. But while hiding that name, I failed to hide something much larger. Myself. I had blended into this city, and someone who's blended in can no longer see that city as a single number. I didn't know that then. Nor did I know it would become the thing that hurt me most, later.

That evening, I stood a long while in front of the Scales terminal for the first time. I didn't raise my hand. I just stood, looking at that smooth surface.

I tried, that night, to bring back why I'd come to this city. The Reckoning. The sentence of demolition. Article Four, Clause Eleven. But those sentences no longer surfaced as vividly as before. Instead, the old man's voice, the vegetable seller's name, the way Trope hoisted a water jar onto her shoulder — things like that kept pushing into their place.

I was afraid of that. I'd come to hate this city. But to hate, I needed a faceless mass. Once faces began to appear one by one, hatred no longer stood as easily as it used to.

I first heard, that night, of a place called the room of records. The old man mentioned it in passing. "If you want to know why the Scales judges the way it does, go to the room of records behind the square. There's something called precedent, I hear, but I don't know much about it myself." The moment I heard that, I felt like I'd found something to hold onto.

Thinking back on it now, that wasn't an accident. I was afraid of myself, starting to be dyed into this city, and I was looking for a place to set the Scales up as my enemy again. The room of records was exactly the refuge I needed.

That night, before I fell asleep, I took out the sentence document I'd buried deep in my bag. It had been a long time. The oiled paper still bore the marks of water, but the lettering was intact.

The Reckoning. Sentence of demolition. Forty days.

I sat holding that paper for a long while.

I felt something strange in that moment. This paper was still the reason I'd fled. But that reason no longer burned the way it used to. I couldn't tell whether it had simply dulled, or was turning into something else. I know now. It hadn't dulled. I was already, by then, holding two feelings at once. The wish that this city would fall, and the wish to sit again at the old man's table. I learned for the first time, that day, that both feelings could live in the same person.

I wrapped the paper back in the oiled cloth and put it in the bag. Then I lay down.

I decided, that night, to go to the room of records the next morning. With the intention of finding a crack to bring the Scales down. But beneath that intention was another one I hadn't quite looked into myself. By finding that crack, I wanted to find a reason to hate this city again. A city that kept growing faces — I wanted to turn it back into a number.

That night I didn't know how differently those two feelings weighed. One was logic. The other was fear. That what I believed was logic was actually something fear had ordered — I wouldn't learn that until much, much later.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. The Room of Records

_Previously_ — Pheugon began reading notices aloud for the city, at people's request. Hiding his name, he was slowly woven into the city's fabric. He sat at the old man's table, and felt, for the first time, that lying was no longer easy. That night he heard, for the first time, of a place called the room of records.

The room of records was behind the square, down three flights of stairs.

The next morning I headed there without a word to Trope. I didn't want to hear her ask where I was going. If she asked, I'd have to make up one more lie.

The air changed as I went down the stairs. The sounds of the square above grew fainter, and a cool, dry smell took their place. The smell of paper. The kind of smell that comes from a great deal of very old paper gathered together.

The moment I caught that smell, I felt strangely at ease. Paper smell had nothing to do with the Scales — just the smell of old things.

At the bottom of the stairs was a door. Wood, its handle worn pale from long handling.

I pushed it open.

The room was bigger than I'd expected. Shelves reached the ceiling on every wall, packed with scrolls and boxes. Several long tables stood in the middle, papers stacked on those too.

An older woman sat at one end of a table. She wore glasses, and there were ink stains on her fingertips.

"New face," the woman said, looking at me over her glasses. Her eyes looked unusually large behind the lenses. "What are you looking for?"

I didn't know how to answer for a moment. I want to know why the Scales judges this way would have been too blunt.

"…I'm curious how the Scales decides sentences," I said. Not a complete lie.

The woman looked at me a moment longer. Then shrugged, as though it weren't a particularly surprising question.

"People like that come around now and then," the woman said, standing. "The precedent section. This way."

I felt relief, in that moment. I wasn't the only person in this city curious about the Scales. That fact made me, strangely, a little less lonely. Looking back now, that was a misunderstanding. The others were only curious. I wanted to bring it down.

The record-keeper led me to one shelf. Better organized than the rest.

"Precedent," the record-keeper said, pulling out a scroll. "The Scales doesn't just look at the current weight when it sets a sentence. It adjusts based on similar past rulings."

"…Based on them, how?"

"For instance." The record-keeper unrolled the scroll. "Say someone steals something. Whether that person later admits their wrong in their own words and makes amends, or denies it to the end — that doesn't register in this weight. Only the value of the stolen goods registers. But the Scales finds similar past cases, and it looks at how those cases turned out, and adjusts this sentence up or down a little accordingly."

I turned that over in my mind.

"So… who decides that? Whether a case is similar or not."

"The Scales," the record-keeper said. "The Scales does all of it. It compares sentences, calculates degrees of similarity. There's almost no room for a person to step in."

I understood, for the first time in that moment, a little more about where the Scales' precision came from. The Scales wasn't just weighing this one case. It carried the entire past on its back. This city's past, and the past of other cities too. I couldn't imagine how vast a memory that would be.

"Are there cases from other cities too?" I asked.

"Plenty," the record-keeper said, pointing toward the shelves. "The Scales trade records with each other. What ruling happened where, and what came of that ruling afterward. It all piles up here."

I spent that whole morning digging through the precedent records.

At first I didn't even know what I was looking for. I just read the Scales' sentences, over and over. Precise, dry, exceptionless sentences. Individual cases, shop disputes, quarrels between neighbors. All roughly the same structure. Facts, grounds, disposition. Sentences with no room for feeling to enter. I'd read sentences like that for three years. But these ones here were written for a different purpose than the ones I'd read. What I'd read were sentences delivering a verdict aloud. These were sentences justifying the verdict to itself.

Around noon, I asked the record-keeper again.

"Are there any precedents where the Scales was wrong?"

The record-keeper's hand stopped. She looked at me for a long moment.

"What do you mean by wrong?" she asked back.

"…Where the sentence came out mistaken, or the grounds were mistaken."

"No," the record-keeper said. Firm. "Twenty years I've worked here, and I've never once seen a record like that."

"How would you check? Whether it's mistaken or not."

"There's no need to check," the record-keeper said. "The Scales is a calculating machine. Has a calculator ever gotten two plus two wrong? The Scales is the same. Correct input, correct output."

I found myself briefly at a loss in front of that answer. It had no gap in it. It was the first time I dimly sensed that what I was looking for wasn't a calculation error in the Scales. The calculation isn't wrong. Then the crack had to be somewhere else.

"…Input," I said. "The input could be wrong, couldn't it."

The record-keeper looked at me again. Longer this time.

"That," she said slowly. "Is a different question."

She paused a moment, choosing her words. Her gaze looked me up and down over the rims of her glasses.

"Young man, what are you looking for here." Her question, more direct than before.

I stiffened.

"…Nothing," I said. "Just curious."

The record-keeper didn't seem to believe that answer. She didn't press further, either.

"Whatever you're looking for," the record-keeper said, turning back to her papers. "There's one thing twenty years here has taught me. The Scales isn't wrong. That much is certain. Whether it's frightening or not, that doesn't change."

I held onto those words for a long time.

I held onto that sentence.

"Who does the input?"

"Records do," the record-keeper said. "Testimony, documents, witness statements. That's what people do. The Scales just takes it in and calculates."

"So if someone puts a wrong record in."

The record-keeper was silent for a moment.

"In theory," she said, carefully. "It's possible. But I've never seen a record of that actually happening. What comes in here all goes through review."

"Who reviews it?"

"The Scales," the record-keeper said. "The Scales again."

I felt something strange in that circle. A person makes the record, the Scales reviews the record, and the Scales sets the sentence based on that record. There was exactly one point in that circle where a person got to step in. The moment a record is first made. Only that moment is touched by a human hand.

I couldn't pin down that point precisely that day. I only left the room with a sense that it existed somewhere.

I decided one thing clearly, leaving that room. The Scales must waver somewhere. If I found where it wavered, I'd have in hand the words to bring this city down. I didn't know then how dangerous that resolve was.

That evening, Trope asked.

"Didn't see you today."

"…I went somewhere."

"Where?"

I stopped, briefly, in front of that question. A few days earlier, I would have made up a lie without a thought. That day was different. Piling one more lie on her suddenly felt heavy. I wasn't exactly sure why.

"…Just, here and there," I managed to say.

Trope looked at me a moment. Her eyes seemed ready to press further, but in the end she didn't.

"All right," Trope said. "You don't have to say if you don't want to."

She always said that. She'd said it since the first day we met. Every time, I'd felt relief. That day, it wasn't relief that rose in me — it was something else. I was stacking lies on this person, and she kept accepting them. That accepting made me heavier.

I went to the room of records for three days straight.

The record-keeper grew less wary of me than before. She stopped asking my name. She just called me "the student, there." I found that comfortable.

"I'd like to see records from other cities," I said on the second day.

The record-keeper glanced at me, then pointed toward a shelf much older than the others, further back in the room.

"There," the record-keeper said. "Not well organized. They come from many different cities, and each city has its own way of keeping records."

"Why? Why would records from other cities be needed here?"

"The Scales learn from each other," the record-keeper said. "It's rare for something to happen for the first time here in the Reckoning. Most things have already happened somewhere, once. So it references the outcome from elsewhere and fine-tunes this sentence."

"…Are those records accurate?"

"The Scales compiled them, so." The record-keeper said. But this time, her voice had lost a little of its earlier force. "Though — I can't be certain of that myself. The originals aren't kept here. The original record is received by that other city's Scales, and what's here is what's been reorganized into our Scales' format."

Something snagged in me at that.

"Reorganized — does that mean the wording could differ from the original?"

The record-keeper took off her glasses, then put them back on.

"Only the format changes," the record-keeper said. "The meaning doesn't. That's the Scales' principle."

Listening to that, I strangely wanted to believe it. That the meaning doesn't change. But the very fact that I wanted to believe it made me doubt it all the more. I couldn't explain why, at the time. I know now. I was too desperate to find a crack. Desperation often makes doubt look like the truth.

I couldn't sleep that night, holding onto that feeling. The Scales isn't wrong. The record-keeper had said so too, and I'd confirmed it myself over three years. Not one miscarriage among the hundred and twelve cases. But if the precedent the Scales relies on had already been altered, somehow, in the hands of whoever first recorded it — I couldn't think that sentence through to the end. The more I thought about it, the more my fingertips trembled.

I was already looking for something by then, without even knowing what exactly. Not a flaw in the Scales, but a flaw in the ground the Scales stood on. If that existed, it wouldn't be the Scales' fault — it would be the fault of whoever made that ground. I couldn't put into words, that night, why that distinction mattered so much to me. I know now. I wanted to bring the Scales down. I didn't want to bring down the fact that the Scales was right. I couldn't distinguish, then, that those two things weren't the same.

I decided to go back to the room of records the next day. This time, I planned to start not with this city's own precedents, but with records that had flowed in from other cities.

On the third day, I sat in front of that old shelf all day.

Each box had a city's name written on it. Names I'd never heard before. On some boxes the lettering on the outside had faded so much the city's name couldn't even be read. I opened those boxes one by one. The texture of the paper, the handwriting, the way sentences were composed — all a little different from box to box. Some cities wrote much longer sentences; others were shorter and drier than ours.

Rifling through those boxes, I was overtaken by a strange feeling. This room held, compressed, the lives of cities I'd never once visited. Who had stolen what, who had cheated whom, who had been pushed out. All different names, different faces, but the same structure, over and over. I felt a strange exhaustion in front of that repetition. This whole world was weighing people with the same weight, in the same way.

The record-keeper passed by me around evening and said.

"You there — what are you looking at so hard?"

"…Nothing," I said. "Just browsing."

"Serious browsing," the record-keeper said. Some amusement in her voice. "Coming back tomorrow too?"

"…Yes."

"I'll mark the spot, then. No sense finding it all over again each time."

The record-keeper tucked a small slip of paper into the box I'd been looking at. I found that gesture, oddly, kind. Looking back now, I know how strange that situation was. I was searching for a hole to bring this city down through, and this city's own people were helping me do it more easily. Without knowing any of it.

I had no idea, then, what that would lead to. I was just looking for one crack. I believed that if I found it, I could hate this city again. I didn't know then how thin that belief was. Finding a crack wasn't going to make the old man's table disappear, or Trope's retreating back. I was just enduring day after day, holding both of them at once.

And three days later, I truly found it. A person's name was attached to it. Mena.

I didn't know, when I first saw that name, that it was even a person's name. It was just one of many characters written on the outside of the box. I had no idea, that day, how far that name would carry me, or what I would lose by the end of it.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. The Misquotation

_Previously_ — In the room of records, Pheugon learned that the Scales adjusts sentences based on past precedent. Records are made by people; the Scales only takes them in and calculates — he dimly sensed there was exactly one point where a human hand touched the process. Three days later he found a name on an old box. Mena.

I headed for the room of records early that morning.

I found the spot the record-keeper had marked and opened the remaining boxes one by one. Most had city names on them, and inside, the large and small incidents from that city, arranged in precedent form. A few boxes held almost identical content. Someone who stole something, someone who couldn't repay a debt, someone who quarreled with a neighbor. Stories not much different, city to city.

I was growing tired in that repetition. I still didn't know what I was looking for. There was nothing to do but keep opening boxes until something caught.

Then, on the bottom shelf, in a spot less noticeable than the others, I found the small box.

The box was smaller than the others.

The writing on the outside was faded, but readable. Not a city's name. A single person's name, and beneath it, in small letters: "Original testimony."

I lifted the box off the shelf and set it on the table.

Until then I'd assumed every box carried a city's name. This one was different. It carried a person's name. I found that strange. Why was this one alone filed by name instead of city?

I opened the lid. Inside were a few sheets of paper, and a scroll far more worn than the rest. The paper felt so thin it might crumble at a touch. Dust came off on my fingertips.

I unrolled the scroll.

Handwriting. Not a sentence printed by the Scales. An honest but unpolished hand. There were even traces of having been written in haste.

A name was written at the top.

MENA.

I began reading the sentences beneath it.

It was testimony. Something about a certain city, a certain incident, and a person named Mena who'd been involved with it somehow. Exactly what the incident was, I couldn't fully make out — the writing broke off in places and had worn away with age. But the final paragraph had survived relatively intact.

I went to that person many times. I wanted to hear why he'd made that choice. Every time, he turned the conversation aside, or fell silent. On the last day I went to him, I asked. Why won't you say anything at all. He didn't answer. This one never explained himself. I am setting that down as it is. It is all I know.

I read that sentence twice. The room was so quiet that even the sound of turning a page felt loud. The sentence hadn't changed by the third reading either.

This one never explained himself.

A simple sentence. An observation about someone. Not a judgment — just a fact. Mena hadn't written that the man was bad. She hadn't written whether he had repented or not. She had only written that he never explained.

I was about to put the scroll back into the box when I noticed there was more paper inside.

Printed paper. The format the Scales produces. A precedent summary.

I unfolded it.

The first line held a summary of the case. Same incident as the testimony I'd just read. Same date, same names involved. My eyes moved down.

I wanted to see how the Scales had summarized this testimony.

According to the testimony of witness Mena, this one never repented to the end. This is applied as a mitigating factor in future similar cases.

I read that sentence and stopped there.

I laid the two sheets side by side in my hands.

The scroll read: This one never explained himself.

The precedent summary read: This one never repented.

I forgot to breathe, in that moment. The two sentences looked similar. As if only a few characters differed. But those few characters were saying something entirely different. "Never explained himself" was a statement about what the man had done. "Never repented" was a judgment about the man's heart. Mena had written only the first. But what survived in the Scales' record was the second.

I stood up from my seat and sat back down again. My hands were shaking. I kept straightening the two papers on the table, over and over, though they hadn't actually gone crooked.

I understood, immediately, what this meant. I'd spent three years confirming that the Scales never miscalculates. But this wasn't a matter of calculation. This was the material that had gone into the Scales, wrong, from the very start. Mena had said "he never explained himself," and the Scales had read it as "he never repented," and preserved that judgment, fixed in place, as a reference factor. And that reference factor was, by the record-keeper's own words, still adjusting the sentences of other cases, somewhere, at this very moment.

I cross-checked the scroll against the summary several more times. I wanted to be sure I hadn't misread something.

I hadn't. "Never explained himself" and "never repented" were unmistakably different sentences. The original held the first; the Scales' record held the second.

I fought the urge to run to the record-keeper.

I was, strangely, calm in that moment. The excitement, if anything, quieted me. For three years, reading a hundred and twelve cases, I'd lived leaning on the fact that the Scales had never once been wrong. That sentence was the pillar holding up my life. And now, in my own hands, I held proof of a crack in that pillar.

I began thinking, in that moment, about what I could do with this.

I searched further through the box. I wanted to know who Mena was, what city she was from.

Behind the testimony scroll was a short introductory paragraph. Worn away by half, but a few sentences remained legible.

…One who keeps records. Belongs to no city; travels between many, writing down what people say. Sees people from a place the Scales cannot reach, in a way the Scales cannot reach…

I read that passage several times over.
