It was the first time I imagined a person named Mena. Someone whose face and name I knew nothing of. And yet a single sentence she'd left behind was being used, after all this time, inside the Scales' grounds, with an entirely different meaning. I felt that was unjust. Whether I felt it was unjust on Mena's behalf, or on behalf of whoever that sentence had pointed to, I couldn't tell, at the time.

The record-keeper passed by and glanced at the paper in my hand.

"Looking at Mena's record?" the record-keeper asked. She looked surprised. "Not many people know about that one."

Her voice dropped slightly. I didn't miss that change.

"…Is it a famous record, normally?"

"Not exactly famous." The record-keeper shrugged. "There's talk. That Mena's records are scattered across several cities, and each one quotes them a little differently. I've only heard it as rumor myself. Never checked."

I went stiff at that.

"…Several cities? Not just here?"

"So they say," the record-keeper said. "Though that's not particularly strange, really. It's common enough for a record's format to shift a little as it passes between cities."

Listening to that, I was privately drawing a different picture in my head. It wasn't the format changing. It was the meaning changing. I thought, in that moment, for the first time, that this might not be a single simple mistake. If Mena's sentence had been misquoted not just in this city but elsewhere too, then the error was much older, and had spread much wider, than I'd assumed.

I copied both sentences onto a piece of paper. I was afraid I might not be able to see the original again.

Original: This one never explained himself.

Precedent: This one never repented.

Written out side by side, the difference stood even clearer. Explaining is an action. You can do it or not. Repentance is a matter of the heart. The very thing the Scales couldn't measure itself, the thing closest to what the Adjudicator called "direction." I knew the Scales couldn't weigh that on its own. And yet here, right inside the Scales' own grounds, sat a judgment about someone's heart, brazen. And it was a judgment the original testimony had never made at all.

I was certain, in that moment. This was the crack. If I brought this to the Adjudicator — no, if I told anyone about it before that — one of the Scales' grounds would collapse. And if that ground collapsed, other rulings built on top of it would shake along with it. For the first time, I felt like I had a clear plan.

I stayed drunk on that feeling all through the afternoon. For three years I had only ever been the one who read. The one who spoke someone else's sentence aloud. Now, for the first time, I felt like someone who knew more than the Scales did. That feeling was frighteningly sweet.

I didn't know, that night, how short-lived that certainty would be.

I noticed a small notation at the bottom of the precedent summary. A case number, and a list recording which other rulings had cited this case as a reference factor since. Not a short list. Ten-some lines of case numbers were laid out.

Looking at that list, a chill ran down my spine. This wasn't a single error attached to one person. Every case listed there might have received a slightly heavier sentence, on the grounds of that wrong sentence, "never repented." I hoped that even a few of them had happened right here, in this city. That would let me show that this error had already exercised real force, right here, in the very city I was standing in.

At the very bottom of the list was an item with the most recent date. I copied that case number onto my paper.

I didn't know. Where that number would take me. I just decided, that day, to follow it the next morning.

The record-keeper came to tidy up the room toward evening and saw me.

"Late again today," the record-keeper said. "Find anything?"

I hesitated for a moment.

"…No," I said. I quietly closed the box. "Just looking around."

The record-keeper nodded without much thought and went back to tidying.

I didn't feel guilty telling that lie, that night. Strange thing. I'd felt heavy in my chest every time I'd lied to Trope, these past days. But not in that moment. I believed I was doing something right. I learned, for the first time, that a lie grows lighter in front of something right. I would learn later how dangerous that lesson was.

I met Trope at the well. Filling her jar, she caught sight of me and tilted her head.

"Your face looks strange today," Trope said. "Something good happen?"

I nearly let out a laugh.

"…Why?"

"Your steps are faster than usual," Trope said. "And you keep touching your chest. Hiding something?"

Only then did I realize I'd been unconsciously touching the paper inside my shirt.

"…It's nothing," I said.

I nearly told her, right then. I found proof that the Scales is wrong, I nearly said. I imagined, briefly, saying that in front of Trope. I couldn't picture what expression she'd make. I couldn't hold that imagining for long. Her mother's face kept overlapping it. Would the Scales being wrong be a comfort to Trope, or would it be something else. I didn't know.

"Strange one," Trope said, lifting her jar. She was smiling. "Well, you'll tell me eventually, I suppose."

Trope walked on ahead. I looked at her back for a moment, then followed.

I thought about that smile all that evening. In front of it, I felt the weight of the paper I was carrying, differently, for the first time. It was no longer evidence for me alone. That evidence was aimed at the very city Trope lived in. I pushed that fact aside, hard. Not yet, I told myself.

Walking home, I held the paper tight against my chest.

I didn't toss and turn that night, for once. In fact, I slept easily, for the first time in a long while. I believed I'd found something. Proof that the Scales isn't perfect. Holding that proof, I felt I could go back to seeing this city as a number. A city built on an imprecise calculation. What was there to be sorry about in that city's falling?

That was what I was thinking as I fell asleep. The next morning, I took the paper and went back to the room of records. This time, I intended to trace all the way through to how this precedent had actually been used in a real ruling. If I confirmed that ruling had come out wrong, I'd have solid proof.

I didn't know, then, that what I'd find at the end of that trail would be entirely different from what I was hoping for.

That night I read those two sentences aloud several times over. Never explained himself. Never repented. Every time I read them back and forth, the difference felt larger. Someone who never explained himself could simply be a quiet person. Someone who never repented was a sinner. The Scales — no, the Scales' grounds — was turning silence into sin. I stayed caught in that thought for a long while, and eventually fell asleep.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. And Yet the Verdict

_Previously_ — Pheugon compared Mena's testimony against the Scales' precedent record. Mena had written "This one never explained himself"; the precedent read "This one never repented." He found a list of other rulings that had cited this wrong sentence as a reference factor, and copied down the most recent case number.

The next morning, I took that case number to the record-keeper.

"Can you find this number?" I asked.

The record-keeper looked at the number and raised an eyebrow slightly.

"This is a recent one," the record-keeper said. "Give me a moment."

The record-keeper went to another shelf and brought back a case file. A thick document. I took it and spread it on the table.

The case had happened here, in this city — the Reckoning.

I went stiff in that moment. I'd vaguely assumed this would be some other city's affair. It wasn't. Right here, in this very city, to a person of this very city, that wrong quotation had actually been used.

The first page held a summary of the case. The name was covered over. The record-keeper had explained to me once before that this city's precedent files kept the names of those involved masked over. Instead there was a case number, a date, and the charge.

A fraud case from the market. A merchant who had cheated buyers repeatedly on prices, taking advantage. Multiple victims. Weight had accumulated close to the limit.

I turned the page.

The second page held the grounds for the sentence's calculation. And in that list of grounds, the very reference case I'd been looking for. Quoted verbatim.

…According to the referenced precedent, in similar cases where the accused "never repented to the end," the sentence tends to be increased. In this case as well, the accused refused to give a final statement, which is consistent with that tendency.

I read that sentence and my fingertips trembled. I gripped the document so hard the corner of the page crumpled.

I was certain, in that moment. This was it. Because of this sentence, this merchant's sentence had grown heavier. The wrong quotation, "never repented to the end," had paired with this man's action of "refusing a final statement," and increased the sentence. I was watching that cause and effect with my own eyes.

I kept reading through the document, right there.

Why this merchant had refused to give a final statement — the reason was noted briefly on the next page. A memo left by a legal officer.

The accused acknowledges the act itself but refuses the statement procedure entirely. Reason unconfirmed.

I paused for breath on that sentence.

Reason unconfirmed. I thought that phrase resembled, exactly, the situation Mena had testified to. The person Mena had met had "never explained himself, to the end" either. The reason was unknown. And here, that same reasonless silence had led straight into "never repented," and from there into a heavier sentence. I felt a chill at how naturally that chain had happened. No one would have felt anything strange about it. One sentence quietly turns into another sentence, that sentence quietly makes the punishment a little heavier, and no one ever notices.

I decided, in that moment, that I had to bring this to the Adjudicator — or anywhere at all. If I laid this document next to Mena's original testimony, side by side, it seemed like it would prove this case's sentence had been wrong. I pictured that scene in my head several times over. Laying the two sentences out before the Adjudicator, saying, "This is wrong." I hadn't thought past that point, what would happen next. I only imagined that far, and leaned on the imagining.

I felt happy, in that moment, for the first time in a while. Or something close to happiness, anyway. I'd spent my time in this city constantly feeling hunted. A name I had to hide, words I had to swallow, guilt that grew heavier, little by little, every day. But that moment was different. For the first time in a long while, I felt I was standing on the right side. A place where I knew more than the Scales. I didn't know, then, how briefly that place would be granted to me.

I didn't go straight back to the shed. I read the document once more from the start, right there. This time, not to find fault, but to make the evidence more solid. I was certain this error must have affected the other cases on that list too. If one was wrong, the other rulings built on it would shake as well. I thought that shaking might reach all the way to this city's own sentence of demolition. That evening, I genuinely believed that.

I stayed drunk on that thought for thirty minutes. I pictured not the city falling, but the city's sentence of demolition itself being voided. That the Scales' grounds were wrong, so the whole thing had to be recalculated — and then the forty days, the clause, none of it would matter anymore. I still had no idea how flimsy that picture was. I already believed I had won.

I read the document again from the start. This time meaning to make sure I hadn't missed anything.

The grounds for the sentence weren't a single line. Several items were listed. I went through them one by one.

  1. Damage amount — a clear figure.
  2. Number of victims — seven.
  3. Repeated occurrence — at least three times, same method.
  4. Confession — the act acknowledged.
  5. Reference precedent — the "never repented to the end" tendency.

I circled item five. This was the error I'd come for.

I hadn't yet thought of one thing, at that moment. What items one through four, by themselves, were saying. My eyes were only on item five. That was what I'd come looking for. A person only sees clearly what he's looking for. The rest passes like background. I didn't yet know, that day, that this background wasn't background at all — it was everything.

I kept reading. The next page showed how much weight the Scales had converted each of these five items into. I went through the numbers one by one.

Weight from the damage amount. Weight from the number of victims. Weight from repeated occurrence. And last, weight from the reference precedent.

I looked at that final number.

It was tiny.

I looked at the number again. Checked it, then checked again.

The weight added by the reference precedent item was an extremely small fraction of the total sentence. The first four items — damage amount, victims, repetition, and confession — had already brought the sentence, on their own, to almost the same point.

I tried, in that moment, to read the fact differently. Small is still something, I told myself. An error is an error. I clung to that thought. But the more I clung to it, the larger the other number looked.

I calculated it. What would happen if the reference precedent item were removed entirely, and the sentence recalculated from the remaining four items alone.

I knew the Scales' method of calculation. I'd learned it over three years, reading sentences like this. I copied the numbers onto paper and subtracted and added them, one at a time.

The result came out.

Almost the same.

Only the tiniest difference remained. That difference wasn't large enough to decide whether this merchant would be pushed out of the city or not. With the reference precedent item or without it, this merchant would have received the same disposition.

I sat in front of that number for a long time.

I collapsed, in that moment. Silently, very quietly. I had come this far to prove the Scales was wrong. I believed I held that proof in my hands. But now, in front of my eyes, sat a different conclusion. The quotation was wrong. That's a fact. Mena had said "never explained himself"; the Scales had transcribed it as "never repented." That was an unmistakable error. And yet that error hadn't changed this case's outcome. Even without that error, on the strength of the other evidence alone, this merchant would have been pushed out just the same.

I saw, straight on, for the first time in that moment, what I had actually come here hoping for. I had wanted the Scales to be wrong. Only if the Scales were wrong could this city's fall remain a righteous thing. But the Scales hadn't been wrong. One quotation had gone bad, and that badness had changed nothing. I knew two things at once, in front of that fact. One: the crack I'd been looking for was real. The other: that crack couldn't bring anything down.

I flipped back through the document from the beginning again. In case I'd miscalculated somewhere. However many times I checked, the same conclusion came out.

The Scales was not wrong.

Not even this time.

I looked at the disposition attached at the back of the document. Pushed out. A date was noted. I calculated the date. Not long ago. Very recently.

I realized something strange, in that moment. This merchant had been pushed out while I was already in this city. I'd passed through the market every day and never once known when it happened. A person quietly disappears, and by the next day some other stall fills that empty spot. I would have walked right past that spot. Without knowing.

I tried, for a moment, to bring back the faces I'd seen at the market. A gap between stalls — someone who'd been there a few days ago, now gone. I couldn't remember. I'd only just started learning this city's faces, not long enough yet to notice a face that had disappeared.

The record-keeper passed by and stopped when she saw my face.

"Are you sick?" the record-keeper asked.

"…No," I said.

"You don't look well."

"…I'm fine."

The record-keeper looked at me a moment longer, then, without saying more, went on.

I don't know how long I sat there in that room that day. I kept running the same calculation over. Remove one item, add it back. The result was the same every time. I tried to find a way to make it come out differently. There wasn't one. The numbers were honest. The numbers didn't lie for my sake.

I sat in that room late into the evening.

I first, dimly, felt a sentence that would come to run through this whole book. What is precise and what is right are different things. But I'd been mistaken to think that sentence would help me. I'd believed that sentence took aim at the Scales. That the Scales might only be precise, and not necessarily right. But now, the document in my hands was saying the opposite. One of the Scales' grounds hadn't been precise. And yet the Scales' conclusion had been right. That those two things could run apart from each other this way — I hadn't known that until that moment.

I'd found the misquotation. It was real. I hadn't invented it. But that error wasn't a key that could bring this world down. It was just an error. An error that changed nothing. I found that more frightening. If the Scales had been wrong, I could have taken hold of that wrongness and done something with it. But the Scales was wrong and right at the same time. In front of that, there was nothing at all I could do.

I thought, for the first time, differently about what the name of the Scales meant. The Scales measures weight. If the weight is correct, the Scales is right. Deciding right and wrong wasn't the Scales' job. But I had gone on believing, all this while, that as long as the Scales was precise, that precision was rightness itself. Only holding this one document did I come to know, in my body, that the two were different.

I put the document back together and returned it to the box. My hands felt heavy.

I don't remember well the path I walked leaving that room that night. Whether I passed through the square, through the market, I'm not sure. But I remember one thing clearly. That was the first time I asked myself the real reason I'd started this investigation. I hadn't wanted to find the Scales' error. I had been looking for an excuse to hate this city again. And when that excuse collapsed this emptily, I lost the ground on which to build my hatred back up.

I didn't go find Trope that evening. I went straight to the shed. Wrapped in the blanket, leaning against the wall, I called back to mind that morning's happiness. It seemed absurd. Just a few hours before, I'd believed I'd found a new reason this city would fall. Now that reason lay scattered into a few numbers in my own hands.

I thought, for the first time, of my father's face. After the man from the Reckoning had left our house, the disposition the Scales had calculated and handed down. I had endured three years believing that disposition was precise. That old sentence — precise, and yet nothing came back — returned to me that night, wearing a different face. I now knew how precision and rightness differed. But that knowledge gave me no comfort at all. Quite the opposite. If the Scales was precise and right at the same time, then I had nowhere left to lay the blame on the Scales. Whatever I could blame now had to be something other than the Scales.

I couldn't name what that something was, that night. I only sat in the dark for a long time. A darkness of the same kind as the darkness of the conduit that had trapped me for three days. Back then, water had risen to my knees; this time, nothing rose at all — and yet I felt just as short of breath.

And it was out of that darkness, still not having found my way clear of it, that I would meet the Adjudicator for the first time.

Looking back now, everything I truly needed to learn in this book was already there, that night. I believed that if I proved the Scales wrong, something would come loose. It took a single day to learn that belief was wrong. But what I truly lost, in that one day, wasn't evidence. It was the ground on which to build my hatred. Only after losing that ground did I understand how heavily I had been leaning on it, all along.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. The Adjudicator

_Previously_ — Pheugon found that Mena's misquotation had actually been used in one of this city's own rulings. But recalculated without that citation, the result was nearly identical. One of the Scales' grounds was wrong; its conclusion was right. He sat in the dark, having lost the ground on which to hate this city again.

About ten days into my visits to the room of records, the record-keeper told me something.

"The Adjudicator's asking for you."

I nearly dropped the paper in my hand.

"…Me?"

"Who else would it be — you're the only outsider who comes here every day these days," the record-keeper said. Her tone suggested it was nothing much, but to me it could be nothing but a great deal.

Every kind of thought crashed over me at once, in that moment. Had he found out who I was. Or had he simply noticed an outsider rifling through precedents for too long. I had no way of knowing the answer. Only one thing was certain. I couldn't not go. Not going, in itself, would raise suspicion.

"Today?" I asked.

"Now," the record-keeper said. "He said to send you straight over once he'd seen your face."

I stood up without even tidying the box. Climbing the stairs, I thought several times about taking a roundabout route to the Adjudicator's office. A pointless thought. This city had few streets, and whichever way I went, I'd end up at the same door.

Climbing those stairs, I remembered something from my training days. The supervisor had once said that a herald's face is sent ahead to each city's administrator. I'd let that pass over me at the time. I thought it was a procedure that had nothing to do with me. That a picture of my face had already come to this city before I had — I hadn't even imagined that.

The Adjudicator's office was on the third floor of the Scales building, at the end of the corridor. I stood in front of the door and steadied my breath.

I knocked, and an answer came right away.

"Come in."

The voice was low and calm. I opened the door.

The room wasn't large. A desk, two chairs, a wall filled with filing cabinets. Through the window, the square was visible below. The plate announcing the days remaining was clearly visible from there too.

On top of the filing cabinet sat an old model of a scale. Too worn to be decoration, and too precisely placed to be something left there by neglect. Not a speck of dust on it. The papers on the desk were stacked with their edges squared. The moment I stepped into that room, I felt that its owner was someone who let nothing sit carelessly.

A man sat behind the desk. He looked to be about fifty. Half his hair had gone gray, and his eyes were deep. No sign of hurry in him.

"Sit," he said.

I sat.

He said nothing for a moment, just looked at me. That gaze felt strangely familiar. Not a judging look. A confirming one.

"I hear you've been coming to the room of records often," he finally said.

"…Yes."

"Going through the precedents."

"…I had something I was curious about."

"What's that."

I hadn't prepared an answer.

"…How the Scales judges," I managed to say.

The Adjudicator tilted his head slightly. He didn't look like he believed it. Nor did he press.

"You're not from this city," he said. Not a question — a confirmation.

I stiffened.

"…No," I admitted. There was nothing left to hide about that. It had already come out in front of Trope.

"Where are you from."

I hesitated again in front of that question. I couldn't say the real name of my city. But strangely, in that moment, a lie didn't come easily to my mouth. I tried to count how many times I'd already lied in front of this man. I couldn't count them. Already too many.

"…Somewhere far away," I said.

The Adjudicator showed little reaction to that answer.

He opened a desk drawer and took out a sheet of paper. Its edges shone from handling. He didn't turn it toward me. He just set it in front of himself and looked down at it.

"There's a roster of the herald assigned to come to this city," he said. "Whole-city cases are special — we get advance notice too. Name and face. Expected arrival date."

I forgot to breathe.

"That herald," the Adjudicator continued, "never came on the scheduled day."

The room went quiet.

I looked, in that moment, for somewhere to run. The door was behind me. The window was three stories up. I couldn't go anywhere. I could only sit, and wait for whatever he said next.

The Adjudicator looked at me again. There was still no anger in his eyes, no sharpness of interrogation. Just something he'd been carrying for a long time.

"You," he said.

I said nothing. I could neither deny it nor confirm it. My body froze where it sat.

"Don't be alarmed," the Adjudicator said. His voice softened a little. "I didn't call you in to report you."

"…How did you know."

"The face," he said. "There was a sketch attached to the roster. Not an exact likeness, but the features matched, well enough. And when I heard about a person who wasn't from this city, going through the precedents day after day, I didn't think that was a coincidence."

I felt a strange emotion, in that moment. Fear was the largest part of it, but beneath it was something else. Something close to relief. For weeks, I hadn't been able to show my true self to anyone. And this man already knew. Knew, and hadn't reported me. Why that fact should put me so strangely at ease — I couldn't explain it, then.

"…Why aren't you reporting me," I finally asked.

The Adjudicator put the paper back in the drawer.

"What would reporting change," he said. "If I caught you and forced you to read — would that be a proclamation? That wouldn't be a proclamation. Just sound on paper."

I didn't fully understand what that meant, at the time.

I didn't notice, then, the speed at which his hand closed that drawer. Not hurried, not forceful. A gesture as if whatever was inside that drawer had nothing to do with his own feelings. I only learned later that this wasn't restraint — it was habit. He had probably closed that drawer with that same motion, countless times, for years.

"What's your name," the Adjudicator asked. "Your real one."

I hesitated for a long time.

"…Pheugon."

The Adjudicator mouthed the name once, silently, without sound. As if confirming a name he already knew.

"Pheugon," he said again, this time aloud. "Good. I'll call you that."

"…To Trope, I'm still Had."

"That's your business, not mine," the Adjudicator said. No reproach in the tone.

I felt, in that moment, for the first time, that I didn't need to hide in front of this man. The first time in weeks. I only realized later, looking back, how tense my body had still stayed all the way until I left that room.

"Why did you call me here," I asked. "Just to tell me you're not reporting me?"

The Adjudicator glanced out the window for a moment. The plate in the square was visible.

"No," he said. "I wanted to see what kind of person you were."

"…Why?"

"A whole-city case is rare," he said. "This is the fourth one since I've sat in this chair. And all four times, the herald ends up sitting in that chair over there. Eventually."

I didn't fully understand what he meant.

"Has this city had this happen before?" I asked. "A herald not coming."

The Adjudicator was quiet for a moment.

"It has," he said.

"…What happened?"

"That's enough for today," he said. I felt that this was a way of avoiding the answer. But that avoidance felt, oddly, more careful than irritating. As if he'd judged that I wasn't ready yet to hear it.

"Why did you go through the precedents," the Adjudicator asked, changing the subject. "You said you wanted to know how the Scales judges."

"…Yes."

"Did you find anything."

I hesitated a moment, unsure how much to say, then opened my mouth.

"…I found an error," I said. "But the conclusion didn't change."

The Adjudicator's expression turned interested, for the first time.

"Is that so," he said. "That's a good find."

"…A good find? Nothing changed."

"Nothing changing is the finding," the Adjudicator said. "Everyone assumes that if the Scales is wrong, something must come apart. Now you know it doesn't."

I didn't fully grasp the weight of that, at the time.

"That's enough for today," the Adjudicator said, tidying his papers. "I've learned your face now. That's enough."

I stood. I walked toward the door, then turned back.

"…Why do you leave someone who's run away alone?"

The Adjudicator looked at me. A long look.

"That," he said. "I'll tell you next time."

"…One more thing." I asked again, hand still on the door handle. "What happens now. Since I didn't come."

The Adjudicator's expression shifted, slightly, for the first time. Only for a moment.

"That's the Scales' business," he said. "The Scales doesn't stop just because one person didn't come. It finds a way."

"…A way?"

"That's enough for today," the Adjudicator said. I felt, from his tone, that there was no more room to press.

"Go on," he said, turning his eyes back to his papers. "You may keep going to the room of records. I won't stop you."

I opened the door and went out.

I turned that phrase, "it finds a way," over and over in my head, walking down the corridor. I didn't know, then, exactly what it meant. I only left that building carrying a vague unease.

Out in the corridor, I felt my legs shaking.

Leaving that room that day, I realized for the first time that I wasn't entirely alone in this city. I didn't know, then, whether that was a comfort. Having one more person who knew me also meant one fewer place to hide.

I thought for a long time that night about the air of that room. The Adjudicator's gaze, that calm. He hadn't judged me. And yet strangely, that calm had felt heavier than any judgment. I didn't understand why, at the time. I know now. He hadn't judged me because he hadn't judged me — he had already, before me, watched three people like me. Those three memories had settled, unmoved, behind his calm.

And I would hear, not long after, about that third person.

I didn't go straight to the shed. I walked across the square. My feet carrying me I didn't quite know where.

Trope was at the well. Drawing the evening water. She saw me and waved.

"Had," she called. I still answered to that name. She didn't know that, a few minutes before, in another room, I'd been called by another name.

"…Yes," I answered, walking over.

"Where'd you go? Haven't seen you since midday."

I stopped, in front of that question. I had just given my real name to the Adjudicator's face. Not half an hour after saying that name, I had to answer again under the fake one.

"…The Scales office," I said, half the truth. "I had business there."

"What kind of business at the Scales office?" Trope asked. An unsuspecting question.

"…Nothing much," I said again.

Trope looked at me a moment, then laughed.

"That again," she said. "Fine. I won't ask."

In front of that laugh, I felt, for the first time, something I could barely bear. She was trusting me. By not asking. And the larger that trust grew, the heavier the weight of the lies I was carrying grew alongside it. That day, I had shed my name in front of the Adjudicator and had to put it back on in front of Trope. Moving between the two, for the first time, felt exhausting.

That night, lying in the shed, I called the Adjudicator's gaze back to mind.

He had recognized me. And hadn't caught me. He hadn't yet told me why. I wanted to know the reason. Was it simple pity? Or something else? He'd said, "the herald ends up sitting in that chair, eventually." I couldn't tell what that meant. Only, from his tone, I could feel that he'd watched several people like me, before me.

I thought, for the first time that night, that I might not be the only person who had ever fled in this city. That thought made me feel a little less alone. And, at the same time, more afraid. If I wasn't the only one who had fled, where were they now? I would come to know the answer to that question soon enough.

I admitted one more thing, for the first time, that night. Leaving the Adjudicator's room, I had felt, oddly, lighter rather than more afraid. Being found out had made me lighter. Hiding takes strength. I had been spending that strength for weeks, and now that it was no longer needed in front of one person, at least, I understood, only then, how exhausted I had been.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. Why You Ran

_Previously_ — The Adjudicator summoned Pheugon for the first time. He already knew Pheugon was the herald who'd been assigned to this city and never arrived, but he didn't report him. "The herald ends up sitting in that chair, eventually." Pheugon was gripped by the thought that he might not be the only one who had fled.

For three days, I walked past the Adjudicator's office several times.

I didn't go in. I had no good reason to. Unless the Adjudicator called for me first, I'd have to make up my own reason to go to him.

I couldn't explain to myself, those three days, why I kept walking that corridor. I already knew that in front of that one man, I didn't have to hide. And that knowledge kept pulling me back toward that door.

On the fourth day, I finally knocked.

Over those three days, Trope noticed I was preoccupied with something.

"You've been strange lately," she said the second evening, carrying a water jar. "Talking less. Seem to keep drifting off somewhere."

"…That obvious?"

"You can see it," Trope said. "I've lived alone long enough — I can tell that kind of face on sight."

I had no answer to that.

"Well, you don't have to say if you don't want to," Trope said again. I'd lost count of how many times she'd said that by now.

That phrase landed unusually heavily that day. In front of the Adjudicator I could set my name down; in front of Trope I was still wearing it. In those three days, moving between the two of them, I first truly felt how exhausting it is to carry two faces inside one person.

On the third night, I finally decided. I would go back to the Adjudicator and hear whatever he hadn't finished saying last time. I didn't know myself what I would hear. Only that the fact that in that room alone I didn't have to hide kept pulling me back to that door.

"…Why are you back," the Adjudicator asked, lifting his eyes from his papers. Not a dismissive tone.

"…I had something to ask."

"Sit."

I sat. Less trembling than last time. Still, my palms sweated.

"What's your question."

I'd prepared a question in advance. But sitting there in front of him, an entirely different one came out.

"…Why did you run away," I asked him, instead.

The Adjudicator's eyebrows lifted slightly.

"That's what I want to ask you," he said.

I went stiff at that.

"…Are you asking me, right now?" I asked back.

"I'm asking," the Adjudicator said, crossing his arms. "Since you brought it up first, I'll ask you the same thing right back. Why did you run away."

The room went quiet.

It wasn't that I hadn't expected that question. I'd known someday I would be asked it. But now that the moment had actually come, I realized I had no prepared answer left standing.

"…I was afraid," I managed to say.

The Adjudicator looked at me.

For a long time.

The air in the room didn't move at all. Outside the window, laborers carrying something made a faint sound that faded away. The Adjudicator didn't even seem to blink. I wanted to look away from those eyes, but strangely, my feet stayed rooted to the spot.

The silence went on. In that stretch of time, I imagined several times what he might say. That he'd press harder. Or pretend to believe me. Neither happened.

I have turned that silence over, countless times, in the days since. I learned, that day, for the first time, that there are different kinds of silence. Some silences are empty. Some are full. His was the latter.

"You," he finally said, slowly. "That isn't the face of someone afraid."

I lost my breath at that. A cold feeling deep in my throat. Nothing physically wrong, and yet I'd forgotten, for a moment, how to breathe.

"…What do you mean by that."

"I've watched people a long time," the Adjudicator said. "A frightened person's eyes waver. The voice cracks, the hands don't know where to go. You didn't waver your eyes just now, saying that. Your hands stayed still too."

I looked down at my hands. He was right. They weren't shaking.

"…So what if they were," I said. My voice rose a little. "People are afraid in different ways."

"Could be," the Adjudicator conceded readily. "But I saw something in your eyes besides fear."

"…What."

The Adjudicator didn't answer. He just kept looking at me.

I wavered, in that moment, for the first time. For three years, I'd had exactly one answer for myself, about why I'd run. Afraid. That answer wasn't one I'd made. It was an answer that had simply attached itself to my mouth, naturally, from the day after I ran. I'd used that answer, unquestioned, in front of Trope, and in front of myself. But the first time I spoke that answer aloud, in front of the Adjudicator, I felt for the first time how thin it sounded.

"…I'm a fearful person," I went on. Defensively. "I was like that back at the training grounds too. Everyone said so."

"Said what."

"…That I never stop reading, no matter what. My supervisor said so."

The Adjudicator tilted his head slightly.

"Someone who never stops reading," he said. "Ran away out of fear?"

I couldn't answer.

In that moment, for the first time, I saw the contradiction myself. For three years, I had never once stopped, reading. I wasn't someone who stopped from fear. And yet, faced with the single greatest proclamation of all, I hadn't even read it — I'd fled. How could those two things live inside the same person. I put that question to myself, for the first time, in that room.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "Sometimes I don't know myself why I ran."

"A lie," the Adjudicator said quietly. Not an angry voice.

I looked at him.

"…What?"

"I said it's a lie," he said again. "You know. Yours isn't a face that doesn't know. It's only that you're not yet ready to say it aloud."

I didn't know how to argue against that. I wanted to argue. But no words came to argue with. Because he was right. I already knew something, in that moment. I couldn't put it into an exact sentence, but I was certain that something sat there. I was straining every muscle in me not to open it.

"…What do you think I know," I asked.

"I don't know that," he said. "Only you know that. I've only seen, on your face, the face of someone who already knows the answer."

The room went quiet again.

The Adjudicator nodded. He didn't press.

"It's all right," he said. "You don't have to know it right away. There were plenty of things I didn't know at first either."

"…Have you ever run away, Adjudicator?"

The Adjudicator laughed briefly. A bitter laugh.

"I've never run," he said. "Instead I've sat in this chair for more than thirty years, watching people who ran. I'm not even sure, myself, how different that is from running."

I had no answer to that.

"You," the Adjudicator asked again. "What did they teach you, in your training?"

"…How not to stop," I said. "How not to listen. How not to catch on a name."

"Why learn that?"

"…So I could read without feeling. So no name, no disposition, could shake me."

The Adjudicator nodded.

"But," he said. "Right now, in this room, you're shaking, in front of me. That reaction doesn't happen to someone untrained. You're someone very well trained. And yet a person like that — why did you fall apart, of all places, in front of this city?"

I found myself at a loss again, in front of that question.

"…I don't know," I said again. This time, truly not knowing.

"You might not know," the Adjudicator said. "I'm not demanding an answer from you today, either. I just don't want to watch you keep lying to yourself."

The Adjudicator glanced out the window for a moment. The plate in the square was visible. That day's number was one less than the day before.

"If there's one thing I've learned in this chair," he said, looking back at me. "It's that a person never knows why they ran, at the moment they run. They find out later, much later, in some entirely different place. And not even because they wanted to know — they come to know it whether they want to or not."

"…How do you know that, Adjudicator?"

"Because I've watched it three times," he said, curtly. "Three people fell apart in front of this very chair. All three only understood why, later. Only one of them had time enough to tell me."

My ears sharpened at that.

"…Who was it?"

The Adjudicator didn't answer. He only glanced toward the drawer for a moment, then looked back at me.

"That story," he said. "When it's time, I'll tell it. Not today."

"May I ask one more thing," I said.

"Ask."

"…What happens if I don't read it? This city."

The Adjudicator's expression stiffened slightly.

"You said last time that the Scales finds a way even without a person," I continued. "What does that mean?"

The Adjudicator was silent for a long while.

"Another herald comes," he finally said. "The Scales reassigns. That's the way."

"…When that person comes, do they read it in my place?"

"They do."

"Then what about me? Do I just— become as if I never happened?"

The Adjudicator looked at me for a long time.

"That," he said, "you'll come to know. That's as far as I can tell you today."

"…Thank you," was all I said, standing.

"Pheugon." The Adjudicator called my name.

I turned back, hand still on the door handle.

"That thing you said, that you ran because you were afraid," he said. "I don't believe it. But I know you want to believe it. How frightening that difference is — you'll come to know that too, someday."

I gave no answer and opened the door.

I left that room without being satisfied by that answer.

That day, walking the corridor, I chewed on those words the whole way. "That isn't the face of someone afraid." That single sentence kept colliding inside me. I wanted to deny it. I tried to find grounds to deny it. I couldn't find any. I truly, in that moment, hadn't known myself what expression had been on my face.

Up to that point, for three years, I had believed I was a child who didn't get angry. I'd told myself the difference — not that anger didn't rise in me, but that I chose not to show it — and I'd believed I understood that difference. But in front of the Adjudicator, I saw the back side of that sentence for the first time. What if it wasn't that I didn't show anger, but that I was storing it somewhere. What if I hadn't run out of fear, but for some other reason.

I didn't manage, that day, to learn what that other reason was. I only felt, for the first time, that everything I'd believed I knew about myself wasn't certain at all. It was a strange sensation. Not the ground shaking — realizing the ground I'd been standing on had never been where I thought it was, to begin with.

On the way home, I chose a long way around on purpose.

I didn't cross the square. I turned down several narrow alleys instead. I didn't want to run into anyone. Not Trope, not the old man, no one.

Walking that road, I went back over my three years again. I was a child who never did wrong. Not that anger didn't rise, but a child who chose not to show anger. I'd been proud of that distinction. But one question from the Adjudicator had shaken the whole distinction. Not showing anger, and storing anger somewhere — those were different things. I might have mistaken the two for the same thing, all my life.

Walking, I remembered the day I first heard the name of the Reckoning. In the supervisor's office, receiving the document, laughing. I called that laugh relief. Silent, and very brief. That night too, I called that laugh back to mind again. And for the first time, I asked myself whether it had really been relief. If it was relief, relief from what. I left the alley without answering that question.

By the time I reached the shed, night had already fallen deep. I sat wrapped in the blanket, leaning against the wall, and turned the Adjudicator's last words over again. I just don't want to watch you keep lying to yourself. I didn't know, at the time, why that sentence stayed with me so long. I know now. It was a kind of sentence I had never once heard before. Not scolding me, not comforting me — and seeing me, exactly, for what I was.

I stayed inside that unsettling all night, before I fell asleep. I kept thinking of that third person. The one whose name the Adjudicator still hadn't given me. I tried to imagine where that person might have gone, what face they might be living with now. I couldn't quite picture it. I didn't yet know how much that person resembled me.

And the next day, still carrying that unsettling with me, I went out to the square — and saw, for the first time, a person who never got angry.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. The Man Who Does Not Get Angry

_Previously_ — The Adjudicator told Pheugon he was lying to himself. "That isn't the face of someone afraid." For the first time, Pheugon felt the things he'd been certain about himself begin to shift. He carried that unsettling with him out to the square the next day.

The next morning, I was cutting across the square toward the market.

An ordinary morning, no different from any other. People walked, carried water jars, opened their stalls. The plate announcing the days remaining hung as high as ever.

My steps were slower than usual, that morning. The unsettling from the night before still hung in my legs. I learned, that day, for the first time, that there are days when even walking feels like an effort.

Leaving the shed, I ran into Trope. She was on her way to fill her jar.

"You look a little better today," Trope said.

"…Do I?"

"Better than yesterday, at least." She shrugged. "I won't ask what happened. But looking better's a good thing, so."

I laughed a little at that. Only I knew that "looking better" wasn't quite accurate. I had only shaken through the night, and by morning grown a little used to the shaking.

Trope's words felt strangely comforting to me that morning. Someone saying I looked better also meant someone had kept watching me. I found that gaze both a burden and, at the same time, a comfort.

Trope went off her own way soon after. I kept walking, across the square, toward the market.

The square was slowly warming under the morning sun. I could feel the stone underfoot growing warm. The smell of baking bread drifted from somewhere, and people carrying yoke-poles of water passed by at a steady pace. An ordinary morning.

Then, on one side of the square, I saw people gathered. Not a few. More than twenty, it looked like. All fixed on one spot, their voices raised.

I went closer, curious what was happening.

A man stood at the center. Worn clothes, a small sack in his hand. People surrounded him, shouting.

"That man stole it!" a woman shouted. "I saw it with my own eyes!"

"I didn't steal anything," the man said. His voice trembled. "This is mine."

"Don't lie!" another man cut in. "That sack — I saw it at that stall earlier. Same exact shape."

The voices grew louder. Some people pointed fingers; some stepped closer, one foot at a time. The man clutched the sack tight against his chest and backed away. Dust rose faintly under his feet, and a few shoes scraped against the stone.

I felt an unfamiliar emotion, watching that scene. I hadn't once seen people raise their voices like this since coming to this city. A quiet, precise, unhurried city. And now that quiet was completely broken.

I found myself thinking again, in that moment, about where this city's quiet came from. I'd assumed it was because of the Scales. Every action left a weight, so people were careful even about raising their voices, about getting angry. But what I was watching now was flipping that thought on its head. Even with the Scales, people could fall apart in an instant. Fear of weight was no match, in the end, for the urge to corner someone.

"I didn't steal it," the man said again. Nearly in tears now. "This is my son's. I bought it for his birthday."

"Then why'd you try to run?" someone shouted. "If you didn't steal it, why were you trying to run?"

"Look at his eyes, that's the look of a thief!" another voice cut in. The man had no answer, just clutched the sack tighter.

"…I was afraid," the man said.

I froze at that answer. I was afraid. The exact same word I'd said in front of the Adjudicator, four days earlier.

I felt a strange awareness in that moment. Was that man using that word as a shield, like me — or was he saying it because he really was afraid? I couldn't tell. Only, that word rang unusually loud that morning.

The people seemed angrier at that answer. Someone shoved the man's shoulder. He staggered back.

I stood frozen there, not knowing what to do. No courage to step in, no heart to simply pass by. I just stood behind the crowd, watching.

I realized, in that moment, that I was watching this scene the way three years of training had taught me. Not engaging emotion, only confirming facts. Whether this man had really stolen it, or was being unjustly cornered — I was trying to judge it the way the Scales would. But the Scales wasn't here, in this place. All that filled this space was people's voices alone. I felt, for the first time, how dangerous a vacuum that was. When people judge each other in a place with no Scales, there's no margin of error checked against anything at all.

An old woman, at the edge of the crowd, waved her cane at the man.

"People like this are why our city's come to this!" the old woman shouted. "Days running out, and still stealing!"

A few people murmured in agreement. I found that strange. No one had yet actually proven this man had stolen anything. And yet people were already tying him to the whole city's sin.

Then, one person caught my eye.

A man standing at the edge of the crowd. Empty-handed. It was rare, in that square, to see someone standing with nothing in their hands. Most everyone carried a jar, a basket, a tool. He wasn't raising his voice like the others. Wasn't pointing fingers either. He simply stood there, quietly watching the man being cornered.

I thought, the first time I saw him, that he was just another passive onlooker. This city had plenty of those. People who didn't involve themselves in others' business.

But something was different.

The other onlookers had no anger, but they also had no expression — they looked uninterested. He was different. He was clearly watching the disturbance. Watching the man, the sack, the crowd closing in — watching all of it, one by one. But there was no anger on his face.

Not indifference. I sensed that difference dimly, in that moment. An indifferent person looks away. He didn't look away. He simply — wasn't angry.

I looked at that face for a long time. Strangely, I couldn't take my eyes off it. Why, I couldn't explain then, and can't fully explain now. Only that it was the one face in that square of a different kind.

His clothes weren't anything special. Ordinary city clothing, not much different from anyone else's. His age was hard to guess. At times he looked much older than me; other times, not old at all. I couldn't find a single distinguishing feature on that face. The only thing I could find was that expression — that stillness.

The disturbance didn't last long. An official heard the noise and came running.

"What's going on here!" the official shoved through the crowd.

Several people spoke at once. The official raised a hand for quiet.

"Hand over the sack," the official said to the man.

The man held out the sack with a trembling hand. The official opened it and checked inside. A small wooden toy was inside.

"…Where'd you get this?"

"I made it," the man said. "My son's birthday's the day after tomorrow."

The official looked a little more inside the sack, then turned to the stall owner standing nearby.

"Did this man take your goods?"

The stall owner glanced into the sack and shook his head.

"…No. All my goods are here. I must have been mistaken."

The crowd dispersed, one by one. No one apologized. The very people who had just been shouting walked off in their own directions, as if nothing had happened.

The man held the sack to his chest again and walked away with his head down. No one saw him off.

I felt anger, watching that scene. Anger that people had cornered a man on no grounds at all, and anger that they'd scattered as if nothing had happened. I knew where that anger came from. I knew, better than most, how a baseless judgment can break a person. I had stood, for three years, on the side that delivered such judgments.

But that anger felt strangely familiar. I'd felt something like it somewhere before. In front of the Adjudicator, in the moment I myself had shaken, there'd been something similar there too. I couldn't say exactly what it was, at the time. I know a little more now. I was someone who got angry in front of injustice. Very precisely, too. Only, that anger always tilted in one direction. It burned hot in front of an injustice done to someone else, and went quiet in front of an injustice I myself was committing.

I looked around for that man again — the one who hadn't gotten angry.

He was already walking toward the wronged man. Said a few words. I couldn't hear what. The distance was too far.

The man briefly lifted his head. Then walked on again. The one who hadn't gotten angry stood there a moment longer, then walked off on his own way.

That was all I saw of him, that day. I didn't know his name, and had no expectation of seeing him again. I just thought he was a strange man. The one person, in this whole city, who didn't get angry where anger belonged.

I had no idea, then, who that man was. I know now. What I saw that day was one of the most important scenes in this entire story. And I didn't notice it at all. I simply walked past him. Only his face, strangely, stayed lodged in my mind a long while after.

That evening, the old man brought up the disturbance, as if he'd already heard about it.

"There was some trouble in the square, I hear," the old man said, leaning on his cane. "A toymaker nearly got mobbed."

"…Did you see it?"

"No, I heard about it," the old man said. "Doesn't happen much in this city. Something's off in the air lately."

"…Off, how?"

"Fewer days left," the old man said, glancing toward the plate with his chin. That day's number hung low. "When people get anxious, they want to grab hold of someone, anyone, and get angry at them. I was the same at that age."

I listened, and called back that face from the square. The one who hadn't gotten angry.

"…There was one person there who didn't get angry," I said. I didn't quite know myself why I brought it up. "Everyone was shouting, but not him."

The old man looked at me for a moment.

"What did he look like?"

I tried to picture that face. Strangely vivid. But trying to put it into words, there was nothing particular about it at all. Common clothes, common build, common gait.

"…Just ordinary," I said. "But his face stays with me, strangely."

The old man nodded. An empty sort of nod.

"There are people like that in this city, now and then," the old man said. "People who don't get angry. I've seen a few myself. Why, I don't know."

"Why's it odd that someone doesn't get angry?" Trope broke in suddenly, from where she sat eating. She was there at the table that evening too.

"…Not that it's odd," the old man said. "That it's rare. In this city, someone who doesn't get angry is either someone who's never done wrong — or someone else."

"Someone else?" I asked.

The old man shrugged.

"That, I don't know either," he said, laughing. "The older you get, the more there is you don't know than do."

I laughed off that answer. At the time, I didn't take it deeply to heart.

I didn't press further, hearing that. I thought, then, that it was just one of this city's ordinary oddities. I didn't see that man again for a while, after that day. But strangely, that face alone didn't fade, even as days passed.

I turned back toward the market. Work was still waiting for me that day.

I kept thinking of that face all through the day's work. I couldn't explain why he hadn't gotten angry. It wasn't cowardice. A cowardly person avoids the place entirely. He hadn't avoided it. He'd watched it through to the end. Only, no anger.

I'd never once seen that combination before. A face watching without fear, and yet not angry. I hadn't known that was possible. I'd lived thinking that not getting angry meant looking away. If you don't look, you don't get angry. He hadn't looked away, and still, no anger had risen in him.

I sketched that face over again, several times, that evening, in my mind. It didn't last. The day's other events kept overlapping it. The Adjudicator's words, Trope's laugh, the two sentences I'd found in the room of records. That night, before falling asleep, I called that face to mind one last time, and then, in the end, let it go. I wouldn't call it back until much later, in an entirely different place.

And three days later, I'd hear an entirely different piece of news, in front of that very plate in the square.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. Another Herald

_Previously_ — In the square, a man was cornered as a thief on no evidence at all. Everyone else was angry; one person alone was not. Pheugon didn't recognize him, and simply filed him away as a strange man.

Three days later, I was walking past the Scales office when I stopped.

Beside the main entrance, in the spot where notices always went up, a new sheet of paper hung. The paste hadn't fully dried, and the edges curled slightly. They fluttered whenever the wind blew. A few people had gathered in front of it, murmuring.

I felt no particular unease, in that moment. Notices went up often in this city. Well maintenance, ration schedules, market rules. I was about to just walk on past.

But my feet stopped. Among the murmuring voices, a familiar word reached me.

HERALD.

I moved in among the people.

The notice was short. The dry phrasing peculiar to a document produced by the Scales.

Regarding the Reckoning's sentence of demolition. Due to the non-arrival of the previously assigned herald, a new herald has been assigned. The new herald has already departed for the Reckoning. Expected arrival date to be announced.

I read that twice, three times.

My body went completely stiff, in that moment. The letters registered with my eyes, but it took time for the meaning to reach my mind. I'd spent these past days among this city's people — helping with errands at the Scales, reading notices aloud, sitting at the old man's table. All that while, somewhere out beyond this city, an entirely different course of events had already been underway.

Someone standing nearby said, quietly.

"Finally coming, is it." An older man. "How long we've waited."

"Waited?" someone beside him asked.

"Of course," the man said. "Nothing's settled until the herald comes. Staying this way just gets more unsettling. Better it come fast and get the sentence read, whatever it is."

I felt something twist inside me, listening to that.

Up to that point, I had gone on vaguely believing that my running had bought this city time. That if I didn't read, the Scales couldn't do anything. But this man was saying the exact opposite. My not reading, in itself, was heaping a different kind of unease onto this city. The unease of hanging, unresolved.

The talk around me went on.

"Why'd it take so long to get assigned, though?" a woman asked. "Our days are down this much already."

"Guess it takes time to find someone, even for the Scales," someone else answered. "Not a job just anyone can do. Someone specially trained, they say."

"Trained, how? Isn't it just reading a paper?"

"You don't know," the first man cut in. "My nephew was training for that. You wouldn't believe how hard it is. Keeping your voice from shaking, never stopping. Years of practicing nothing but that."

"But what happened to the one who was supposed to come first?" the woman asked.

"Don't know," the man shrugged. "Lost, maybe, or couldn't make it. The Scales doesn't tell you that part."

"Poor thing. Must've had some reason."

I felt a chill run through me, listening. Years of practicing nothing but that. I was someone who'd gone through those years too. And yet here, in this city, people weren't talking about my years — they were talking about the years of a person whose face they didn't even know yet.

I stood in front of that notice for a long while.

I felt something had gone wrong in my calculations, in that moment. I didn't yet know exactly what. I just realized, for the first time, that I'd been vaguely assuming the world would wait for me, while I hid here in this city. The world hadn't waited. The Scales had moved on without me. Found someone else, and already set them walking.

I read the phrase "has already departed" on the notice again.

Already. That word weighed the most. I'd thought, all this while, that I was postponing something. Deferring a choice. But that word was saying the deferral was already over. Someone else was already walking. Toward this city.

I thought about going to find the record-keeper, then didn't. Instead I stood in front of the notice until the crowd dispersed.

I imagined, standing there, that new herald. Someone around fourteen or fifteen, like me? Or someone older? I tried to picture the face and nothing came. I knew nothing about that person at all. And yet, strangely, I felt certain they'd be very much like me. Someone trained to read without feeling. Someone who never stops at a name. Someone who'd sat in the same room, at a different time, and heard the same words from the same supervisor.

I knew that person would read all the way down to that small line of print at the very bottom of the sentence — Article Four, Clause Eleven. Would that person, like me, stop at that line? Or just pass over it? I had no way of knowing the answer. I only knew, for certain, that they would read that sentence not knowing anything about this city at all.

I went to step into the Scales building, then stopped.

I wanted to go find the Adjudicator. Wanted to ask him what this meant. But my feet wouldn't move.

I felt a strange fear, in that moment. If I went to the Adjudicator, I felt he would finish the words he hadn't finished last time. "That, you'll come to know." I was afraid that "coming" had already arrived. I wasn't ready yet. I didn't even know what I was supposed to be ready for — I just felt unready.

In the end, I didn't go into the Scales building. I turned and walked toward the square instead.

By the square, word of the notice had already spread. People stood in twos and threes, talking. Expressions varied. Some relieved, some anxious.

I stood in front of the plate announcing the days remaining. Looked at the number.

People kept passing by all around. Someone glanced up at the plate and moved on; someone didn't even look. How quickly this city's people had grown used to that number — that, oddly, struck me as strange that day.

That number looked different that day. I'd watched this number every day. But that day, for the first time, the fact that it had kept counting down without me felt frightening. I'd thought of this number as one I'd made. That by running, I was exercising some kind of power over this number. But now, this number was simply flowing along at its own pace. With nothing to do with me.

I understood, in that moment, for the first time. My running hadn't stopped anything. I'd believed I was holding something back. Time for this city to turn on its own, the possibility of the clause being triggered, whatever it was. But the Scales, when I didn't come, simply sent someone else. Why had I not thought of that, for so long?

I sat down at the edge of the square. The strength went out of my legs.

For the first time, sitting there, I began to weigh what running away truly meant. I'd believed that running would stop something. Time, or the sentence, or the clause. But the reality in front of me was saying the opposite. I'd stopped nothing. I had only made one more person walk toward this city.

That person was, at this very moment, still walking toward this city. Not knowing anything, like me. No — unlike me. That person would surely come knowing exactly what they had to read. What only I hadn't known, what only I had missed by running — that person might already know it.

I couldn't move for a long time, in front of that thought.

Toward sundown, I finally got myself up. On the way back to the shed, I stopped several times. My feet kept wanting to turn a different direction. Toward the Adjudicator's office, or toward the room of records. I corrected them, each time.

That evening, I tried not to meet Trope. I didn't have the confidence to face her. I now knew there were two heralds hanging over this city. One already living among its people, blended in; the other, still faceless, walking toward it. And Trope knew about neither.

But near the shed, in an alley, I ran into her anyway. She stood with an empty water jar.

"You haven't eaten," Trope said, setting the jar down. "What's with your face."

"…Nothing."

"How many times is that today?" Trope crossed her arms. "At least I know something happened today, from that look."

I nearly told her everything, in that moment. That a new herald was coming. That I was the reason. The words rose all the way to my throat, and went back down again. I swallowed it that day too. But that swallowing was unusually heavy, that day. I knew, by then, that what I was swallowing wasn't just one name.

"…Nothing happened," was all I said.

I didn't bring up the notice to Trope that day. She didn't seem to know about it yet. She wasn't someone who kept a close eye on the Scales office's notices. That silence felt, for a moment, like a small reprieve. Until she heard that news, I felt I didn't have to fully face it myself yet.

Looking back now, that wasn't a reprieve — it was simply putting it off. She would hear that news soon enough. Not from me, but from someone else. And on that day, I would have to pretend, once again, not to know.

Trope didn't press further. She picked up her jar and walked off toward her house. I watched her back for a long while, then returned to the shed.

Back at the shed, I wrapped myself in the blanket and sat for a long time.

I turned one sentence over, the whole night. I had run away. But that running hadn't been enough. I didn't know yet, at the time, exactly what that "not enough" meant. I only felt, for the first time, that I was standing in some strange place — neither having left this city, nor properly belonging to it. I was one of the people who knew this city's sin most exactly. And yet I still hadn't properly looked that fact in the face.

I didn't sleep that night, for a long while. Through the window, far off, I could see the square's plate glowing faintly even in the dark. The number was still counting down. Without me. With nothing to do with me.

I didn't know then where this failure of arithmetic would take me. I only sat that night, for the first time, in front of the fact that everything I'd believed I was controlling had already slipped from my hands.

I had thought, when I ran, that it was one complete act. You run, and it's over. I believed nothing more needed to be done after that. That not coming to this city, not reading this sentence, would be enough on its own. But the world didn't take my running that way. The world didn't leave an empty space alone. Wherever a space opened, the world filled it with something else. I hadn't even known how quickly that filling would happen. A day, two days, was enough. While I hesitated, the world had already set the next person walking.

I felt helplessness, for the first time that night, in front of that fact — a different kind than anything I'd felt in front of the Scales, or in front of the Adjudicator.

I suddenly thought again, that night, of the face I'd seen a few days before in the square — the one who didn't get angry. Why that face came to mind, in that moment, I couldn't explain then, and still can't fully explain now. Only, I remember, that face had held a different kind of stillness than the helplessness I felt that night. I envied that stillness. And, at the same time, I couldn't understand it.

That night, I took the sentence document back out of my bag, for the first time in a while. The oiled paper still bore the marks of that old wetting. Holding that paper, I imagined the day someone else would read this sentence in my place. That imagining came unusually vivid. A stranger's voice, reading this sentence in front of this city's people. In front of Trope. In front of the old man. I couldn't picture where I would be standing, in that scene.

I folded the paper back up and put it in the bag. And that night, for the first time, I asked myself this question. Do I really want this city to fall? Or do I just want to not have to read this sentence? I didn't yet know, that night, how different those two questions were.

Looking back now, all of Part Two comes down, in the end, to one sentence. I was washed up in this city looking for a place to hide, and while hiding I became dyed into it, and dyed into it, I went looking for a way to bring the Scales down, and failed, and failing, I faced, for the first time, the price of having run.

I didn't know, that night, that the price wouldn't end there. The arithmetic would keep going wrong, and I would face that wrongness again, more than once.

I counted again, that night, that I hadn't even reached the halfway point of the forty days. A little over twenty days left. I turned that number over in my mouth. Twenty days ago, that had seemed like infinite time. The days that remained felt far heavier than they had before.

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Forty-Day Clause, Part Three

The Forty-Day Clause

Chapter 25. In Search of the Text

_Previously_ — When Pheugon failed to come, a substitute herald was
assigned to the Reckoning, and he had already set out for the city.
Only then did Pheugon understand that fleeing, by itself, had
stopped nothing.

The news reached me from Trope's mouth.

It was dawn. A line had already formed at the well, and Trope had
gotten there before me and filled two water jars. These days I helped
her every morning. I'd started because I owed her something; somewhere
along the way it had just become habit.

"A new herald is coming," she said, setting down a jar. "The one who
was supposed to come didn't, so they've swapped in someone else.
There's a notice posted outside the Adjudicator's office."

I couldn't say anything.

My hand, winding the bucket rope, stopped for a moment, then moved
again. Luckily Trope wasn't looking at my face. She was peering into
the jar.

"Strange, isn't it," Trope went on. "That a herald just doesn't show
up. I've never seen that happen before. Maybe he caught a cold."

I nearly laughed. A cold. Trope thought I had a cold. Looking back,
that would have been kinder. A cold, at least, gets better.

"…When's he coming?" I asked.

"Not sure. People are saying about a week. The notice didn't give an
exact date. Just 'as soon as possible.'"

I counted the number silently. Subtract seven from the days
remaining, and the city still had some room left. But that room
wasn't room for me.

"Why do you look so shocked, Pheugon." Trope looked at me. "Is a
change of heralds really such a big deal?"

"…No." I picked up the jar for her. "It's just news to me, that's
all."

Trope looked at me a moment longer, then didn't press. That was who
she was. She never dug. I was grateful for it, and guilty in exactly
the same measure.

She didn't know I was a herald. Because she didn't know, she could
hand me that news as if it were nothing. I was living off that
nothing. At the time I didn't even know to call it what it was.

That whole evening, I thought about one sentence.

If, within forty days, the said city is confirmed to have turned,
this sentence shall be rendered void.

In a hundred and twelve readings, I had never seen a sentence like
it. It wasn't attached to individual cases. It appeared only on
city-wide ones — a sentence that showed up maybe once a decade.

The first time I read it, I'd skimmed past the words "confirmed to
have." I'd assumed the Scales would do the confirming. The Scales
confirmed everything.

But these past few days, the phrase kept catching on something.

The Scales measures weight. The Scales does not measure direction.
I already knew that. It was something I'd learned in the first ten
days after I arrived in this city. But faced with that sentence, I'd been deliberately forgetting
it. Why I'd been forgetting it, I still wasn't honest enough with
myself to say.

The new herald would arrive in seven days. When he arrived, he would
read all the way to that sentence too. And then — would it be
confirmed, or not?

I didn't know. And not knowing was unbearable.

Even then, I couldn't explain to myself why it was so unbearable.
Without that explanation, I went ahead and decided what to do next.
That's the strangest part of this whole story — I knew what answer I
wanted before I knew what the answer was.

I spent that whole morning walking the square.

There were boards all through the Reckoning announcing the days
remaining. When I'd first been washed up in this city, the number had
read thirty-nine. Now it had dropped much further. People passed the
boards without lingering on them anymore. They'd grown used to it. I
found that strange — that in a city whose demolition date ticked down
every day, people could just walk past the number.

I stopped in front of the bakery. The owner was handing over the last
of her bread to a customer who'd come after closing. In the old days
she'd have slipped it over quietly, off the Scales' books. This time
the customer insisted on paying, and she flatly refused to take it.

I watched the scene for a long time.

That day I took it for ordinary kindness. It was only later I
learned that scenes like it had been multiplying across the city
around then. But my head was too full of other things that day to
take notice. Looking back now, I know what a mistake that was.

I crossed the square. There really was a notice posted outside the
Adjudicator's office. Short. Substitute herald assigned. Arrival
date not yet set. I stood in front of it for a long time. A couple
of passersby read it and moved on without comment. To them it was
just an administrative notice.

Not to me.

As I walked, I thought about what kind of person the substitute
herald might be.

Faces from training came back to me. We'd all been taught the same
way — how not to stop, how not to listen, how not to snag on a name.
Whoever came wouldn't be very different. Someone who read without
feeling, precisely, to the last line.

At the time I thought of him as something faceless. A tool, moving
exactly as it had been trained to move. That I had once wanted to be
that tool myself — that, in the moment, I didn't remember.

He would have had a supervisor too. He would have had a name too. He
must have, at some point, practiced reading a false sentence with his
own name written into it. I didn't think of any of that then. I only
began seeing him as a person much later, after he actually appeared
at the gates of this city.

When he arrived, he would read this sentence document. From beginning
to end, precisely. And he would read down to the last line — Article
4, Clause 11 — without stopping. Just as I had.

I stopped walking mid-thought. I was in the middle of the square.
People passed around me.

He didn't know this city. Didn't know Trope. Didn't know that the
baker had refused to take her change, or who had helped whom at the
well. To him, the Reckoning was just a name on a page.

I thought that fact should have reassured me. It didn't. The
opposite feeling kept rising instead, and I was afraid to give it a
name.

The room of records sat behind the square, three flights down.

I'd been there before — it was where I'd found Mena's record. That
time I'd gone digging through precedents, the past rulings the Scales
drew on. This time what I was after wasn't a precedent. It was the
original text the precedents quoted — the clause itself.

"I'm looking for the original text of Article 4, Clause 11," I said.

The archivist was an elderly woman. She studied me a long moment over
her glasses.

"It's not here."

"…Not here?"

"The precedents are here. Records of precedents citing the clause.
But the clause text itself, we don't keep here," she said, pushing a
drawer shut. "That falls under the Adjudicator's authority, not the
herald office's."

"Why?"

For the first time, the archivist looked me straight in the eye.

"I don't know. I've worked here twenty years and never once seen the
original text of that clause."

"Twenty years."

"I was twenty-two when I first came here." She took off her glasses
and wiped them. "In that time I've seen exactly three city-wide
cases. This one makes four. And I've never once heard of that clause
actually firing."

"All three?"

"All three." She put her glasses back on. "They fell. All of them."

"And why the clause never fired—"

"I don't know." She went on straightening her papers. "I'm just the
person who files the precedents. I don't even know who wrote the
clause. Nobody does. I hear even the Adjudicator doesn't know."

I made her say it again.

"Even the Adjudicator doesn't know?"

"He said so himself. Long ago." She glanced at me. "Ask him yourself,
if you want."

I stood there a moment.

"I can see the precedents, though," I said.

"You already have. Last month."

She remembered me. I couldn't say anything.

Only then did it strike me that I hadn't really been hiding in this
city at all. People remembered me. They didn't know my name, but they
remembered my face. Hiding, it turned out, wasn't the same as being
erased. It just meant no one asked.

Climbing back up the stairs, I passed a Scales terminal.

There were terminals like it all over the Reckoning. Rest a hand on
one and your own weight came up. People did it out of habit, hand on,
hand off.

I hadn't touched one since arriving in this city. I hadn't touched
one back home, either, not since the day I walked out of the
supervisor's office. Forty days now, coming due, and not once.

I can explain, even now, exactly why I didn't. It wasn't that I was
afraid the number would be low. It wasn't that I was afraid it would
be high, either. I knew that number would be accurate, at that very
moment as at any other, and it was the accuracy I was afraid to see.
Accuracy always frightened me. That was the sickness running through
this whole book.

I stopped in front of the terminal, then walked on without raising my
hand.

At the end of the alley, I saw the man who doesn't get angry again —
the same man I'd seen in the square before. This time he was pulling
a handcart, and its wheel had dropped into a ditch. A few passersby
