stopped as if to help, then said they were busy and went on. He
didn't get angry. He struggled alone to free the wheel until,
finally, a passing child helped him lift it out.

I watched a moment, then walked on.

At the time I just thought he was unlucky. A wheel in a ditch, no
one helping. An ordinary day. I didn't even wonder why he stayed so
calm about it. I had no room for that. My head was already full with
a different sentence.

The Adjudicator's office was on the third floor of the Scales
building, at the end of the corridor. I'd been there twice before —
once because he'd summoned me, once because I'd gone looking for him.

This time I was the one seeking him out again.

The door was closed. I stood in front of it and hesitated.

I tried, standing there, to work out what I was actually asking.
"Show me the original text" would have been enough. But behind it I
was hiding what I really wanted to ask — whose job the confirming
was. Whether that job passed to the man arriving in seven days. Or—

I didn't finish that thought. If I finished it, I was afraid I'd
catch myself out — find out exactly what it was I wanted.

I knocked.

There was no answer for a long while. Just as I raised my hand to
knock again, a voice came from inside.

"…Come in."

I still remember the silence between those two words. I asked him
later why he'd taken so long to answer. He didn't answer that either.
By then I already knew that not answering was one of his ways of
speaking.

I opened the door.

The Adjudicator was sitting at his desk. He didn't look surprised to
see me. He'd taken to acting, these days, as if he already knew I was
coming. Maybe he really did.

As always, papers were piled on his desk like a mountain. Through the
window, the square lay below, and the board announcing the days
remaining was visible from there too. His desk was positioned exactly
where that board could be seen best. I didn't think that was an
accident.

"What brings you," he asked.

I steadied my breath.

"Article 4, Clause 11," I said. "I want to see the original text. Not
the one line attached to the sentence document."

The Adjudicator set down his pen.

"Why that."

"I feel like I need to know it for certain."

"Know what."

I couldn't answer right away. I hadn't sorted out for myself what it
was I needed to know.

"…I heard a new herald is coming."

The Adjudicator's eyes narrowed briefly. He said nothing. He glanced
out at the square once, then back at me.

He looked at me a long time. I couldn't read what that look meant
then. I can read it now. It wasn't judgment. It wasn't worry, either.

It was resolve.

"Sit," he said. "This will take a while."

I sat. The Adjudicator rose and walked to the bookshelf. On the
bottom shelf was a drawer with a lock on it. While he took out the
key, I watched his back.

It looked like the back of a man who had put this moment off for a
very long time.

The key turned in the lock with a short, heavy sound. I listened and
looked out the window. The board in the square still showed its
number. One less than what I'd seen passing through just before.
Midnight must have come and gone while I wasn't watching.

That was the first day I actually felt the number drop. Before then
it had just been a sign. From that moment, it wasn't.

"No one's ever shown you this," the Adjudicator said, drawing an old
scroll out of the drawer. "In twenty-some years, I've taken it out
exactly twice."

Even hearing that, I had no idea. What kind of person I'd be when I
walked out of that room that night — I truly had no idea.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. Article Four

_Previously_ — Pheugon went to the Adjudicator to ask for the
original clause text. The Adjudicator looked at him a long time,
said "This will take a while," and drew an old scroll from a locked
drawer. "No one's ever shown you this."

The Adjudicator unrolled the scroll across his desk.

It wasn't paper. The material was older than that — its edges gone
brown and brittle, its color faded along every fold. I'd never seen
anything like it. Every other document in the Reckoning came printed
by the Scales. This one had been written by hand.

He moved his fingertips carefully as he spread it flat. Not the hands
of a man handling something old — the hands of a man handling
something long put off.

"Older than the Scales," the Adjudicator said, as if reading my
thought. "All of Article 4 is. This clause existed before the Scales
took its current form."

"…Who wrote it?"

"Don't know," he said, short. "That's the first strange thing about
this clause."

"No one knows? Not even you?"

"Not even me." He looked at me. "No one who has ever sat in this seat
knew. Not the Adjudicator before me, not the one before him. I went
back through the records — this clause was here before the Scales
office even existed. Who wrote it, when, why — there's no record at
all."

"Have you tried to find out?"

"Once," he said, short again. "When I was young. Never found it."

He didn't explain further. I didn't ask further. I understood that
his silence was the whole of his answer to that question.

The scroll held several clauses. Article 4 governed city-wide cases —
the ones where accumulated weight crossed the limit and a demolition
sentence fell on an entire city, not a single person. Clauses one
through ten were familiar territory: conditions for sentencing,
grace periods, execution procedure, order of resettlement for the
displaced. Exactly as I'd been trained. Even the handwriting was
neat, its sentences not so different from what the Scales printed —
precise, dry, leaving no exception.

Reading through those clauses, I remembered training. The sentence
structures were identical to what my supervisor had made us memorize
— subject, condition, disposal. Sentences with no room for feeling to
get in. I'd read sentences like that for three years, and it was for
reading them well that I'd been sent this far.

Clause 11 stood apart.

The margin above it was unusually wide, as if whoever wrote the body
had stopped there, and someone else had filled in the blank space
below, much later.

The handwriting differed from the other clauses. Finer, more
careful. As if, after finishing the body, someone had sat down
separately, at another time, and added this one line on its own.

I read the line without making a sound.

If, within forty days, the said city is confirmed to have turned,
this sentence shall be rendered void. The confirmation shall be
performed by the one who read this sentence aloud.

I read it twice.

What I'd known ended with the first sentence. What was attached to
the sentence document went exactly that far. The line after it —
"The confirmation shall be performed by the one who read this
sentence aloud" — that, I was seeing for the first time.

Even reading it then, I hadn't yet let the meaning arrive. My eyes
read it; my mind hadn't caught up. There are moments like that. Later
you remember the moment exactly. In the moment itself, you feel
nothing.

"This is the original," the Adjudicator said. "Only the first
sentence goes out on the sentence document. Legal requirement.
Procedure and effect are all people need to be told — no need to
publish how the confirmation works."

"Why not publish it?"

The Adjudicator thought a moment.

"I don't know that either. That's another of the clause's strange
points. It could be a document made to hide something. Or—" He cut
himself off.

"Or?"

"Or it was hidden because it shouldn't be known by just anyone."

I sat in the Adjudicator's office and listened to what he had to
tell me.

He'd held this seat thirty-one years. In that time there had been
three city-wide cases. The same three the archivist had mentioned.

"I was the Adjudicator all three times," he said. "All three times
this clause was attached, and all three times I took out this
document. Just as I'm doing for you now."

He laid his hand on the scroll. It looked as if the weight he'd
carried for so long had settled into his palm.

"All three times, I hoped," he said. "It's shameful, but it's true.
That this time might be different. That the sentence might actually
move. At thirty, I truly believed that."

"And now?"

He didn't answer.

"…Who to?"

"To the herald."

I looked at him.

"It happened the same way every time," the Adjudicator said. "When
the herald arrived, I showed him this document. Told him the
confirmation was his to perform. And—"

He stopped.

"And?"

"Every time, nothing happened."

"All three cities fell." The Adjudicator went on. "The first was when
I was thirty. That herald read the sentence and left. He must have
known the confirmation clause existed. He never came back."

"Why not?"

"Who knows. Maybe he thought his job was done. Maybe he didn't
understand what there was to confirm." The Adjudicator's fingertip
traced the scroll's edge. "Whether that city actually turned or not —
no one knows. There was no one to confirm it."

I turned the words over. It sounded strange to me, that no one knew
whether it had turned or not. I'd been taught the Scales knew
everything. Yet faced with the one question that mattered most, even
the Scales didn't know, and no person knew, and a whole city had
vanished with no one knowing at all.

"So that city," I asked. "Might really have turned?"

"Who knows," the Adjudicator said again. "That's the most frightening
part of this clause. Without someone to confirm it, having turned is
indistinguishable from not having turned. The Scales can't tell the
two apart."

"What about the second one?"

"The second herald wasn't from that city. Came from somewhere else.
Read it precisely, left precisely. He never came back either."

"Did you show him this document too?"

"I showed him," the Adjudicator said. "He heard the whole thing, then
asked me: 'So you're saying I have to come back later and say whether
this city has changed or not?' I told him yes. He laughed. 'How would
I know that.' He was right. He'd left without meeting even ten people
in this city."

I felt something tighten in my chest, listening.

I thought, at the time, that I understood that feeling. Looking back,
I'd understood only half of it. In that second herald, I wasn't
seeing myself — I was seeing the man who would arrive in seven days.
He too would read that sentence without meeting even ten people in
this city.

Listening, I felt something go cold in some corner of my chest. I
didn't know what it was then. I know now. It was a premonition.

"The third one," the Adjudicator said, looking at me. "The third was
from this city. Born and raised here. Someone who knew better than
anyone why this city had come to this."

The Adjudicator's voice changed, just slightly. Slower than before.

"That one left the city as a child. To train. And years later, was
assigned back to this very city as a herald. The city he was born in.
The city he knew better than anyone."

"…He must have known why it was falling apart, too."

"He knew. In detail," the Adjudicator said. "I don't know about his
family. But I knew he didn't remember this city fondly. His hands
shook, handing over the papers. Not the shaking of gladness."

I couldn't say anything.

"What about him?"

"He didn't read it."

I looked at the Adjudicator.

"He fled the night before the proclamation."

I repeated the sentence to myself several times. He fled the night
before. I knew that sentence wasn't about me. And still, I kept
finding my own name slotted into it.

"So that city."

"Fell. On the fortieth day, no one read. The Scales simply executed.
No confirmation, nothing. If there's no one to read, there's nothing
to confirm."

The room went quiet.

I looked at my hands. They weren't shaking. They should have been.

I thought, in that moment, that the Adjudicator was testing me. I
know now he wasn't. He was just telling me facts. That it felt like a
test was my own problem.

"…Why did he flee?" I asked. My voice sounded strange.

"Don't know. I never got to ask. I never saw him again after that."

The Adjudicator started rolling the scroll back up. I watched his
hands.

"Adjudicator." I called out.

"Yes."

"What is confirmation. That sentence only says the one who read
performs the confirmation — it doesn't say what confirmation actually
means."

The Adjudicator's hands stopped, mid-roll.

He looked at me for a very long time. I still find that look hard to
describe. It wasn't the look of a man withholding an answer. It was
the look of a man gauging whether I was ready to hear one.

"That," he said slowly, "we'll talk about tomorrow."

"…Why not now?"

"You've heard enough for one day."

I couldn't press further.

The Adjudicator put the scroll back in the drawer and locked it. The
sound rang louder than before.

"Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"I'm not going to ask why you're clinging to this clause so hard," he
said. "You don't look like you'd give me a real answer if I did."

I said nothing.

"Come back tomorrow," he said. "Same time."

I got up and walked toward the door. When my hand was on the handle,
his voice came again from behind me.

"Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"Seven days, you said. The substitute."

"…Yes."

"I hope tomorrow won't come too late for you."

I didn't fully understand the weight of that sentence then. Walking
out, I simply took it for concern. I know now. It wasn't concern. It
was a warning.

It was already evening by the time I got out to the hallway.

The square was visible through the window. People were drifting home
one and two at a time. A few children had gathered by the board that
announced the days remaining, pointing at the number, talking about
something. I couldn't hear what.

Walking down the stairs, I found myself turning the Adjudicator's
words over again. The third one, someone who knew better than
anyone why the city had come to this.

I turned that sentence over the whole way down those stairs. I
didn't know, at the time, why I couldn't look away from it. I know
now. It was my own story. Not yet finished — but my story had already
gone ahead of me and arrived there first.

Trope was still at the well. It was the hour when the last few people
fetching evening water were thinning out.

"You're late," she said. "Coming from the Adjudicator's office?"

I froze for a second.

"…How did you know?"

"That's exactly the path from there." Trope smiled. "Why, is it a
secret?"

"…No."

Even then I stacked one more lie on top. I'd said no, but it was a
secret. I told her nothing that day. And the fact that I was grateful
she didn't press — looking back now, I see how convenient an excuse
that gratitude was.

Trope slung the water jar over her shoulder and glanced at me.

"You've been thinking hard about something lately, Pheugon."

"…Does it show?"

"Yes." She said, walking. "I know that look. My mother used to wear
it a lot. The face people make when they're thinking about something
that doesn't show up on the Scales."

I had no answer for that.

She didn't press further and just walked on ahead. I watched her
back for a moment, then followed.

On the way home, I passed the herald office's wall. There was one lit
window on the other side of it. The Adjudicator's office. He seemed
to still be in that room.

I watched that light and wondered what he was doing in there. Had he
taken the scroll out again? Or was he just sitting, looking out the
window? I didn't know then. I don't know exactly now either. I only
know that across the thirty-one years he'd spent in that room, he had
wanted, at least once, to see that sentence move. That wish had never
once come true, and still he had taken out that same scroll three
times in that same room, and put it away three times again.

I couldn't sleep that night, back at home. The third herald's story
kept circling in my head. Someone born and raised in that city. Someone
who knew better than anyone why it had come to this. That person
hadn't read it. He'd fled the night before the proclamation.

I envied him. In that moment I didn't even know why I envied him. To
put a name to that envy, I would first have had to admit what I
wanted. That night, I still wasn't ready for that.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. The One Who Proclaimed

_Previously_ — The Adjudicator showed Pheugon the original text of
Article 4, Clause 11: "The confirmation shall be performed by the
one who read this sentence aloud." There had been three city-wide
cases, and all three cities fell. The third herald had been born
and raised in that city, and fled the night before the
proclamation. The Adjudicator said they'd talk about what
"confirmation" actually meant the next day.

The next day I arrived earlier than the time we'd agreed.

I waited a little outside the door. No sound came from inside. Unlike
the day before, I knocked without hesitating.

"Come in."

The answer came quickly this time.

The Adjudicator sat in the same chair as yesterday. There was no
scroll on the desk. Instead he sat with nothing in front of him,
waiting for me. That absence felt heavier than the scroll had.

"Sit."

I sat.

"You know what the Scales does," the Adjudicator said.

"It measures weight. Without error."

"That's right. Precisely. Who stole what, who lied to whom, whether a
hand was raised to a terminal or not — all of it converts into
weight. The Scales does that perfectly."

"…Yes."

"But there's one thing the Scales can't do."

I waited.

"Direction," the Adjudicator said. "The Scales measures weight, not
direction. The posture you're sitting in right now, this weight — the
Scales stamps that precisely. But whether you're in the middle of
turning around, or just sitting still — the Scales doesn't see that.
It reads the same value either way."

"Why?"

"Don't know," the Adjudicator said. "That's simply the Scales' limit.
The Scales is a machine that measures what has already accumulated.
It doesn't measure which way you're facing right now, this instant.
There's no method for measuring that at all."

At first, listening, I just took it as another story about the
Scales' flaws. Familiar territory. I'd spent two months combing
precedents for gaps in the Scales. I still didn't know, at that
point, that this particular story was headed straight for me.

"So whoever wrote the clause," the Adjudicator went on, "handed that
judgment to a person. Because it's something the Scales can't do."

"To just anyone?"

"No." The Adjudicator looked straight at me. "Not just anyone. To the
one who read the sentence aloud."

I looked at him.

"Why that person, of all people."

The Adjudicator chose his words.

"Think about it," he said. "Who knows this city's sin most precisely.
The Scales. But the Scales can't see direction. Who's the next most
precise?"

I couldn't answer.

"The one who read the sentence," the Adjudicator said. "That person
read out loud — with his own mouth — exactly how much accumulated
weight this city carried, and exactly which sins had piled up to make
that number. More precisely than any citizen — more precisely even
than someone born and raised in this city — that person knows this
city's sin, sentence by sentence."

"Wouldn't someone from the city know better?" I asked. "If they've
lived here their whole life, wouldn't they know more than the
herald?"

"They'd know it differently," the Adjudicator said. "Someone from
this city knows it by living it. They know who wronged whom, by
rumor, in their body. But they don't know it as exact numbers. What
weight the Scales assigned to their neighbor's wrongdoing — they can
only guess, not know precisely. The herald is different. The herald
read that number, that item, that sentence aloud, without dropping a
single word. Knowledge no citizen of this city holds."

Listening, I understood it. And the understanding frightened me.
Knowing precisely and loving had nothing to do with each other. If
anything, they might run opposite ways.

"But what if that person hates this city," I asked. My voice cracked.
"What if the person who knows the sin most precisely also hates this
city the most? How can you trust that judgment then?"

The Adjudicator looked at me for a moment.

"You don't," he said. "The clause isn't trusting that person. The
clause is only saying that no one else has the standing to judge.
Not entrusted because trustworthy — entrusted because there's nowhere
else to entrust it."

"What's the difference."

"A great one," the Adjudicator said. "Standing doesn't choose the
person. Whether that person is good or bad, whether he loves this
city or hates it — the clause doesn't ask. It asks exactly one thing.
Did he read it."

I still had nothing to say.

"So the clause asks that person," the Adjudicator said. "Whether the
sin you knew and this city, as it stands now, are different. There's
exactly one person in the world with the standing to answer that. The
one who read this city's sin most precisely."

"How does the confirmation work," I asked. "Just… think it? Or does
he write something down?"

"Speech," the Adjudicator said. "Aloud. This clause was consistent
from beginning to end — not thought, not signature. A voice. One
sentence is enough. 'This city has turned.' But it can't be said just
anywhere."

"Where, then?"

"On the fortieth day," the Adjudicator said. "In the square. Right
before demolition executes, with the whole city watching. The same
place, the same hour as the proclamation. This time, the confirmation
has to be spoken there."

Hearing that, I felt something go cold through my body. It meant that
the man who had fled would have to go back to that very spot, and
this time with the whole city watching.

I heard that and stayed still.

The room was quiet. Faint voices drifted in from outside the window.

I suddenly remembered what Trope had said. The face people make when
they're thinking about something that doesn't show up on the Scales.
I hadn't been able to answer her then. Now I thought I understood.
What face I'd been wearing, all this time.

I still remember that moment sentence by sentence. It's strange.
People usually remember big events in a blur. But that moment alone
comes to me one sentence at a time, each arriving separately. Maybe
that's exactly how it happened inside my head. One sentence at a
time, in order, everything came down.

Without that man. No confirmation.

Without confirmation. The clause doesn't move.

If the clause doesn't move. The demolition never becomes void.

If I don't read. It means I am — permanently — the only person who
ever read this city's sin aloud, and now never will.

If I don't read. This city will certainly fall.

I'd already run this arithmetic once before. Five months ago. That
day in the supervisor's office, when I received the papers. I'd
looked at those papers and felt relief. I hadn't known then what that
relief was. I know now.

I hadn't fled thinking this city would live.

I had fled knowing this city would certainly die.

That day in the supervisor's office, I heard the number a hundred
and twenty thousand. I told myself, at the time, it was a number I'd
never tried to count. But in truth I'd already started counting the
instant I heard it — not as people, but as a possibility. For a city
of a hundred and twenty thousand to fall, the clause would have to
stay silent. For the clause to stay silent, I would have to not read.
I had already finished that calculation in the supervisor's office, at
the very moment I took the papers in my hand.

My hand hadn't shaken. I called that composure. The steadiness of a
man who'd read a hundred and twelve cases. That was a lie. A composed
man is a man whose calculation is finished.

I looked at my hands.

They were shaking.

Too late. Hands that had never once shaken through a hundred and
twelve readings were shaking now, here. And I knew this shaking
wasn't coming from fear. It was the shaking of a man caught.

Caught at what. By myself.

My weight had been the lowest in our whole cohort — low enough that
the supervisor checked twice. Everyone assumed it was diligence. So
had I.

I know now that it wasn't diligence. I wasn't a child afraid of
weight. I thought, someday, there'd be a sentence I would have to
read, and I didn't want to lose my standing to read it. That the
sentence would turn out to be the Reckoning's — I hadn't thought that,
back then. Or maybe I already had. Since I was twelve.

Living with the lowest possible weight, and holding the most
precisely qualified standing — I'd lived three years not even knowing
those were the same thing. And the hands that had earned that
standing had fled, in the end, precisely so as not to read the
sentence. The person most qualified had chosen, most certainly, not
to use that qualification.

For the first time I recalled the moment I'd received the papers
assigning me to the Reckoning. Seeing the city's name, I'd laughed.
Without a sound, just for an instant. That was relief, I'd told
myself then.

I was wrong for three years. That wasn't relief. It was certainty.
The certainty that the Reckoning had come to me. The certainty that
if I read that document, a chance existed for the clause to fire.
And if I didn't read it—

I recalled the night I first found the clause. That small print.
Turns. I read the word three times. That night, I told myself I
hadn't "quite grasped what it meant."

A lie. I understood it precisely. I'd read the word three times not
because I didn't understand it. The calculation to eliminate that
possibility had already begun, right then.

The supervisor had once asked. You got something with the
Reckoning? I'd said no.

That lie was proof I'd already begun planning to flee. I already
knew, then, that if I didn't read this city, the one person who could
save it would cease to exist, permanently.

I hadn't fled. I had killed. A city where nothing had happened yet —
killed forty days before, in the hallway where I took the papers.

The Adjudicator watched me for a long time.

He didn't seem to be waiting for me to say anything. He was just
watching. Waiting for me to come down that staircase on my own.

I couldn't say anything. There was nothing to say. No excuse, no
denial. The Adjudicator already knew. What I had only just come to
know, he seemed to have known all along.

He opened his mouth.

His voice was low.

"So it wasn't fear," he said, "that made you run."

I couldn't answer.

I can still repeat that sentence word for word, not a syllable off.
It didn't scold me. He wasn't angry, wasn't surprised. He was simply
stating a fact. And that one fact brought down, all at once,
everything I had spent three years telling myself.

I was a boy who never did wrong. I wasn't a boy who didn't get
angry — I was a boy who withheld anger. I was a boy who took care not
to lose his standing. Every one of those sentences read differently
now. Not caution — preparation. Not restraint — aim.

The day of my first proclamation, I'd erased the shape of the
merchant's mouth. And a hundred and eleven more times after that. I
called it the fruit of training. I know now. I hadn't been practicing
how to erase. I hadn't been practicing how to endure, either. I had
been practicing how to wait — for the one day I would not read,
toward this city.

The Adjudicator rose from his seat. I thought he would say something
more — comfort, or reproach. Instead he simply walked to the window
and opened it a crack. Evening air came into the room.

"I didn't tell you this to blame you," he said, looking out the
window. "I told you because I thought you needed to know. What
happens after that is yours."

I couldn't answer that either. He didn't wait for an answer. He just
stood, looking out the window. His back looked like the back of a man
who had, thirty-one years running, taken the same scroll out and put
it back in.

I don't know how long I sat in the Adjudicator's office.

He said nothing more. Neither did I. The sun was going down outside
the window. The number lit on the square's board quietly cast its
light over the whole city, behind my back.

I walked out of that room that day a different person. To be
precise, I hadn't become a different person. I had, for the first
time, seen the original one. For three years I'd thought I was a good
boy. That goodness had been aimed, with precision, in one direction
the whole time — that, I learned for the first time that day.

I don't know what Jonah thought, walking toward Nineveh. I don't
know how he understood himself, aboard the ship to Tarshish. But I
know this now — that fleeing can, sometimes, be a more exact
calculation than arriving.

And I had already carried that calculation out.

I went home that night and sat for a long time.

There was something I wanted to say. I didn't know exactly to whom,
or what. I only felt that if I didn't say something aloud, I wouldn't
survive it. For three years I had made a career of reading aloud, and
in all that time I had never once said my own story out loud.

I opened my mouth, then closed it again.

The next day, I knocked on the Adjudicator's door again.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. The Confession

_Previously_ — The Adjudicator told Pheugon the clause's true
trigger condition: only the one who read the sentence aloud can
confirm it. In that moment, Pheugon understood the real reason he'd
fled — not fear, but calculation. "So it wasn't fear that made you
run." That night Pheugon sat unable to speak, and the next day he
knocked on the Adjudicator's door again.

After I knocked, I realized I hadn't decided what to say.

The Adjudicator opened the door. Nothing had changed from the day
before. Same seat, same desk. And yet I crossed that threshold as an
entirely different person than the day before.

"Come in."

I went in. I sat without waiting to be told.

There was nothing on the desk again today. Where the scroll had lain
yesterday, only the wood grain showed now. That emptiness pressed on
me all the more. There was nowhere to hide.

Morning light came in through the window at a slant. I watched it
touch the edge of the desk and slowly creep along. While I failed to
start speaking, the light moved two finger-widths.

"…Adjudicator."

"Yes."

I opened my mouth and closed it again. Three times, maybe.

The Adjudicator didn't rush me. He didn't look at his papers either.
He simply watched me.

"There was someone," I finally said, "who came to our house from the
Reckoning. Three years ago."

The Adjudicator's expression didn't change at all. He seemed to have
already guessed.

"My father took him in. That was the kind of man he was. Quick to
take in strangers."

I stopped speaking there for a long while.

"…Go on," the Adjudicator said. It wasn't a push. It was more a way
of letting me know there was another door where I'd stopped.

"He used a room. A converted storeroom — my father hurried a bed in
and made it into a room. There was a light on in there every night.
He was writing something. Said it was a verification task, copying
over my father's ledger."

"…What kind of verification."

"I don't know," I said. That wasn't a lie. "I was twelve. I thought
it was grown-up business. I still don't know."

The Adjudicator didn't ask further.

I nearly said I could still close my eyes and see the light leaking
through that door crack, then stopped myself. If I said that too, I
was sure my voice would break. Instead I caught a breath and moved to
the next sentence.

"For a few months it was fine," I went on. "We ate dinner together.
He talked with me and my brother. When we asked about the Reckoning,
he'd answer smiling. He said the Scales measures everyone so
precisely, no one ever gets treated unfairly."

"You believed that."

"…Yes." I looked at him. "I thought accuracy was a good thing."

"And now."

I couldn't answer.

"Then," I went on, "something changed. I don't know exactly when.
There were more evenings my father said nothing for days at a time. I
heard my mother crying alone in the kitchen. Twice. The second time
was quieter. I stood outside the door, then just turned back."

"Didn't knock."

"…No."

"Why."

I gave that question real thought for the first time.

"…I thought if I got caught, it would be harder for my mother. But I
know now. That wasn't for my mother. It was for me. Pretending not to
hear was easier than hearing and not being able to do anything."

The Adjudicator said nothing. There was no pity in his eyes. But no
indifference either. He was just listening.

"That winter I saw my father put his hand on a Scales terminal.
Once." I said. "A number came up on the screen. I didn't see the
number. He looked at it a long while and said, 'It's fine.' I hadn't
even asked."

"And that man."

"He left that winter. I walked with him as far as the edge of town. I
don't know why. I think I wanted to ask something, but in the end I
asked nothing at all. I just said goodbye."

"Did you hate that man."

"Not then," I said. "I didn't know how to hate yet. The hating grew
later. Very slowly."

The Adjudicator moved for the first time. He drew his fingertip once
along the edge of the desk.

"His name," he asked. "Do you know that man's name."

I looked at him.

"…I know it."

"Not going to say it."

"…No."

He nodded. He didn't press further. I understood then that he had no
intention of forcing me any further than I was willing to go.

"Our family fell apart after that," I went on. "My brother left that
winter. Without a word to me. I still don't know where he is."

"Did you ever look."

"No," I said, looking down at my hands. "I wouldn't have known what
to ask him if I found him."

"Your mother."

"She went to live with relatives in another city the next year. She
asked me to come with her. I said I didn't want to quit training. That
was what I told myself then."

"The real reason."

I stayed silent a long time.

"…I don't know. Even now."

"Your father."

"He's still in that city. We don't live together. He lives alone, on
the other side of the city. We see each other occasionally. We don't
talk about it."

"Not once."

"Not once."

The Adjudicator closed his eyes briefly, then opened them. It wasn't
surprise. It was more like the motion of putting something away
inside himself.

"The last time we met," I went on. This was something I hadn't been
asked, but it came out on its own. "It was a few days before I
received the Reckoning case. As we were parting, my father said one
thing. 'What case are you reading these days.' I lied, said it was
something small. It wasn't a lie then, since I hadn't been given the
Reckoning case yet — but I was already, in that moment, practicing
how to hide something."

"What did your father say."

"'Take care of yourself.' That was all."

"That was the most he could do."

I nodded at that. Strangely, hearing that sentence from someone
else's mouth, it sounded different. It was the very sentence I had
told myself for three years, but coming from the Adjudicator it
sounded like neither an accusation nor an excuse. Just a fact.

The room went quiet. Through the window came the smell of evening
drawing close.

"The Scales must have calculated it," I said. This was something I
wanted to say. "How much everyone's weight went up — I don't know
exactly. I never asked. But I know the Scales calculated it
precisely. The Scales is never wrong."

"But."

"But nothing came back." My voice cracked. "My brother didn't come
back. My mother didn't come back. Our family never became four
again. A precise disposal came down, and it fixed nothing."

That was the first time I ever said that sentence aloud. Three years
I'd carried it only inside myself. The moment it came out as sound, I
understood for the first time how heavy it was. Some sentences weigh
far more coming out of a mouth than they do written on paper.

"So I gave that anger away," I went on. "Not just to that one man —
to the whole city. I put the whole city where that man had been. I
know it isn't fair. I didn't know that then."

"Aside from the fact that he was from this city," the Adjudicator
asked, "did you have any other grounds to hate the whole city."

I had no answer for that.

"None," I said, after a long while. "I'd never met a single person
from the Reckoning. Not until I came to this city."

"And still you hated it."

"…Yes."

"And after meeting them."

I thought of Trope. The baker. The merchant fixing his weights. The
people drawing water at the well.

"…I don't know," I said. "Faces appeared. Once a face appears, it
gets hard to hate. And that doesn't mean the hating stops. It just
gets harder."

The Adjudicator heard that and passed no verdict. I was grateful for
it. And at the same time, frightened. Without a verdict, I had to
keep walking through it alone.

"For three years," I opened my mouth again. "I did nothing wrong. No
lying, no anger, kept my weight as low as it could go. Everyone
thought it was diligence."

"You thought so too."

"…Yes. But I learned yesterday. It wasn't diligence. I was guarding
my standing. Someday there'd be a sentence I'd have to read toward the
Reckoning, and I didn't want to lose the standing to read it. I'd been
preparing that, three years, without even knowing it."

The Adjudicator looked at me a moment.

"That's the part hurting you most right now."

I nodded. My throat closed and no words came.

The Adjudicator was quiet a long time.

I thought he'd say something. Comfort, or reproach. He did neither.

"I heard your story," he said. That was all.

"…That's it?"

"That's it." He looked at me. "If I told you it's all right, that
would be a lie. I don't know why your family fell apart. Whether
what you felt was fair or not isn't mine to judge either. All I know
is that you said that story out loud today, for the first time in
three years."

I turned that over.

"That," he said, "is enough for today."

"…Adjudicator." I asked. "Why don't you comfort me?"

He thought a moment.

"Comfort is easy," he said. "Saying it's all right takes less than
five seconds. I hear your own father said that to you too. You just
told me yourself. What did that do for you?"

I couldn't answer.

"Nothing," the Adjudicator said. "That sentence was for your father's
own sake. It wasn't for you. I don't want to add one more of those
onto you."

"Then what are you doing for me."

"Listening," he said. "That's all. But I don't think that's nothing.
You went three years without ever getting all of that out to anyone.
Today you did. That's not because I did anything. You did it."

I found that answer disappointing. At the time. I think I'd been
hoping for more. Forgiveness, or denial — some sentence that would
close this story out. The Adjudicator didn't give me that. I know now.
That was the most honest thing he could have given me.

Walking out, I found it strange that I felt neither lighter nor
heavier. I felt something should have changed. I'd finally told the
whole story, after three years. And yet my brother still hadn't come
back, my mother still wasn't in that city, my father still lived
alone on the other side of it.

Speaking hadn't fixed anything. I learned that that day. Just as the
Scales' disposal had fixed nothing, my confession fixed nothing
either. But one thing was different. The Scales' disposal had kept
me locked inside it, alone. Confession had, at least, brought one
other person into that room.

I came out into the hallway; it was evening. The square was visible
through the window. People stood in line at the Scales terminal. An
ordinary sight. But that day the line looked a little shorter.

I didn't know why it looked that way then. I thought it was just my
imagination. I know now. It wasn't imagination. From around then, very
slowly, this city really was changing.

Trope was at the well. She saw me and, unlike usual, didn't start
talking. She just patted the spot beside her with her hand. An
invitation to sit.

I sat.

For a long while neither of us said anything. People fetching water
came and went, one and two at a time. An evening bell rang somewhere
far off.

"…You have the face of someone who set down something heavy today,"
Trope said first.

"…Does it show?"

"Yes." She glanced at me. "But it's not the face of someone who set
down all of it. It's the face of someone who set down half."

I laughed at that. A sad laugh.

"…That's exact."

"I know that face," she said, resting a foot on a stone. "I used to
wear it a lot myself."

I looked at her. For the first time, I seriously considered that she
too might be carrying something as heavy as mine.

"…Will you tell it someday, Trope? Until you've set it all down?"

She didn't answer for a long while.

"…I don't know," she finally said. "I've never thought of it as a
weight I could set down."

I didn't fully grasp what that meant, at the time.

I know now. She was already handing me, that day, the first line of
her own story. I didn't catch it. I was too caught up in telling my
own. The price for that came later, in a different place.

We didn't say anything more that evening. We filled the jars, walked
together, and parted. As we said goodbye, she added one more line.

"…I'll be here tomorrow too."

I didn't understand, that night, why that sentence stayed with me the
way it did.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. Little by Little

_Previously_ — Pheugon said his family's story aloud to the
Adjudicator for the first time. The Adjudicator gave neither
comfort nor judgment — he just listened. That evening at the well,
Trope said, briefly, "I've never thought of it as a weight I could
set down." Pheugon let the words pass.

I noticed it first outside the bakery.

The baker had always been strict about keeping accounts. Trope had
told me so before. But that morning, I watched her send an old man
off without paying at all.

"…Pay me next time," she said. "Just take it today."

The old man bowed his head over and over as he left. The baker
watched him go for a moment, then went back to kneading dough. As if
nothing had happened.

I stood there and watched it. Something felt strange, though I
couldn't place what. Two months earlier, I'd have walked right past
that scene. A kindness from someone in the Reckoning — I wouldn't
have felt a thing. That day, my feet stayed rooted to the spot.

I passed by again the next day, half wondering if it had been a
coincidence. But that day too, the baker said the same thing to
someone else. "Pay me next time." Hearing it a second time, I
understood this wasn't chance — it was a decision.

Until then I'd only known the people of this city as numbers on the
Scales. The baker had been a single line to me — "strict about
accounts." That day I saw, for the first time, that there was another
sentence behind that line.

A few days later I saw it in the market.

The merchant who'd cheated with his weights — a name I recognized
from the precedent files. He was crouched in front of his stall,
working at something. Coming closer, I saw he was repairing his
weights, filling back in the part he'd shaved off.

"…What are you doing?" I asked.

He glanced at me, then went back to the work.

"…Setting it right."

"Who told you to?"

"No one told me," he said. "It's just been weighing on me lately."

I had no answer for that.

In that moment I remembered the name I'd seen in the precedent
database. A weight, a disposal. A person who'd survived only as
numbers. But what stood in front of me now wasn't a number. It was a
pair of hands. The hands that had shaved the weights down were now
filling them back in.

The habit of counting came back.

I'd once been the boy who counted a hundred and twelve cases. Now I
was counting something else. What wasn't stolen. What anger wasn't
raised. Words that got spoken. A few of these caught my eye every
day.

The girl next door, who picked up an old man's dropped cane and
carried it back to him. She'd lost her mother. Trope told me. Since
her mother's name had vanished from the Scales, that child hadn't
spoken to anyone. That day she'd grabbed the cane and run to the old
man.

A young man who'd stopped to help someone who'd tripped and dropped
her water jar. He'd always kept his eyes on his own path before —
I'd heard that at the well.

Two neighbors who had lived turned backs on each other, meeting
briefly in an alley one day and exchanging a short greeting.

Counting these, I thought of the habit from training. That habit of
counting a hundred and twelve cases was, here, counting a completely
different set of numbers. Numbers the Scales had never once counted.

One day I went and stood in front of a Scales terminal.

I checked the baker's name. A number came up. I compared it to the
record from forty days ago. No change. I checked the merchant too.
The same.

I stood there a long while, looking at it.

I thought the Scales was missing something. It seemed strange that
a man now refilling weights with his own hands showed the exact same
number as when he'd been shaving them down. But thinking again, it
wasn't strange at all. That man hadn't undone the act of shaving the
weights. He hadn't erased the past — he had simply started living
differently, from now on. The Scales had no field to tell the two
apart.

That evening I went and asked the Adjudicator.

"Why isn't the number moving? People are clearly changing."

The Adjudicator looked at me a moment before answering.

"Weight accumulates," he said. "What's already piled up doesn't go
down. Bread you stole stays on the Scales. Not stealing today doesn't
erase yesterday's theft."

"So no matter how much they change, it means nothing?"

"It doesn't mean nothing," he said. "It just doesn't register on the
number. The number and what it's worth aren't the same thing."

At the time, that distinction sounded strange to me.

"Then no matter how much this city changes," I asked, "if the number
doesn't drop below the limit within forty days, it just falls? Even
if people have genuinely changed?"

The Adjudicator looked at me a long time.

"That's exactly why the clause exists," he said. "There are things
numbers can never prove. The clause was made knowing that. Something
only a person can see — not a number."

"But that person is me."

"That's right," he said. "So I won't ask what you've been counting
lately. I'll just say one thing. The numbers you're counting might be
more accurate to you than the Scales' numbers."

I carried those words out of the room. Walking the hallway, I counted
things off on my fingers. The bakery, the weights, the cane, the
purse. Four. Counting to four, I felt strangely settled. Counting
still calmed me. Only now the things being counted had changed.

The pile of papers sat on the Adjudicator's desk that day too. I'd
never paid much attention to the way he sorted them, but that day I
noticed. A clerk had mentioned in passing that he used to push
papers aside as he went. Lately, though, the Adjudicator seemed to
linger over each page before turning to the next.

"Are you counting something too, Adjudicator?" I asked.

He stopped his hand.

"…Something like that," he said. "Forty years, I've read these
papers as numbers. These days I read the names. I can't explain the
difference myself yet."

I turned that over for a long time.

I know now. That distinction was the one that decided this whole
city's fate. The Scales knew precisely how heavy the city was. But
which direction the city was walking, right now, appeared nowhere on
the Scales' screen. Direction wasn't a number. It couldn't become
one.

Once I saw a strange scene in the square.

An older man stood in front of someone else, holding something out.
A small pouch. The one receiving it looked confused.

"…What's this."

"I owed you something," the old man said. "Seven years ago. You
probably don't remember. I do."

The recipient truly looked like he didn't remember. The old man
didn't explain, just pressed the pouch into his hand and turned away.

I asked someone passing by. "What was that about?"

"Don't know," the man shrugged. "There's been a few like that
lately. People suddenly going around paying off old debts."

The Scales had already recorded that seven-year-old debt. Converted
into weight. That weight wouldn't vanish just because the man handed
over a pouch. I knew that, and still I couldn't stop thinking about
that scene for a long time. What the Scales couldn't erase, that man
was trying to erase anyway, knowing it wouldn't erase.

Around that time I got to know one person. I won't write her name
here. She carried water for a living, and years earlier, the weight
piled up from theft had put her just one step short of the limit. I
saw her number once, by chance, at a Scales terminal. The room she
had left before crossing the limit was small enough to count on one
hand.

She hadn't stolen once, since. I learned that from Trope. Three
years, Trope said. And still the number sat exactly where it had
three years before. One wrong step away from crossing the limit.

"…So is she," I asked, "still in danger, even after three years of
not stealing?"

"Yes," Trope said. "That's how the Scales sees it. Three years of not
stealing isn't a number."

I couldn't say anything for a long while after that answer. That
three years of patience couldn't move one single number — that was
the first time it truly frightened me. What frightened me wasn't the
Scales' coldness. It was that the coldness was accurate.

More days passed. I kept counting. Some days five things, some days
two. Some days none at all. On those days I felt uneasy, wondering if
the city was slipping back.

Around then I noticed the strange place I was standing. I had fled
this city hoping it would fall. And now I was falling asleep every
night counting the number of good things I'd seen that day. When
that shift happened, I can't name an exact date. I just opened my
eyes one day and found it had already happened.

That fact unsettled me. Lying awake at night I'd ask myself: do you
want this city to live. The answer was different every time. Some
nights, yes. Some nights, no. The most frightening nights were the
ones when I couldn't answer at all.

For three years I had cultivated exactly one feeling: that the
Reckoning deserved punishment. That feeling was simple, and because
it was simple, it was strong. But in the two months I'd spent in this
city, another feeling had begun growing alongside it. I carried both
feelings at once. Both were real. If one of them had been a lie, it
would have been easier.

Once I learned the baker's name, hating this city as a single mass
grew harder and harder. But liking these people one by one didn't
mean I had forgiven the city they'd washed into being. I didn't know
how to hold the two apart.

The baker kept giving discounts on credit. Eventually she put up a
sign about it outright. The market merchant, once he'd fixed all his
own weights, started offering to check the neighboring stalls' as
well. The girl who'd picked up the cane now traded morning greetings
with that old man every day.

Small things. Small enough that the Scales would never once take
notice.

The number on the board announcing the days remaining kept ticking
down too. Thirty-nine, the first time I'd seen it. Now it was well
past twenty and falling. People still glanced at the number every
time they passed it. But their expressions had changed a little from
before. At first everyone had looked at that number with fear. Now,
sometimes — only sometimes — people looked at it with something more
like endurance.

I had no way of explaining that difference to the Scales. Fear and
endurance use the same muscles in the face. The Scales doesn't
measure faces. At first I couldn't tell the two apart either. Only
seeing it day after day did it slowly become visible.

I found myself slowing down every time I passed the bakery now. Same
at the market, same at the well. Two months ago I'd wanted to move
through these streets as fast as possible. Now my pace slowed on its
own.

At the time I thought that change of pace was just habit. I know now.
It wasn't habit. It was attachment. I was growing fond of this city —
the city I had decided to hate.

Trope had been quiet lately.

I noticed, but didn't press. Since my confession, I'd been busy
sorting through my own affairs. What she'd said at the well — "I've
never thought of it as a weight I could set down" — came back to me
more than once, but something else always interrupted before I could
ask her about it.

One evening I saw her standing at a Scales terminal. She hadn't
raised her hand — just stood staring at the screen. Different from
usual. Usually she raised her hand, checked the number, and turned
away at once.

That day she stood there a long time.

I started toward her to call out, then stopped. Her shoulders looked
stiff somehow. I felt something heavy coming off her back, but I
couldn't put it into words.

I didn't call to her that day. Looking back, that was a mistake. At
the time, I believed it was kindness — leaving someone alone who
seemed to want to be alone. I'd made that same judgment three years
before, outside a kitchen door. I still hadn't learned, at that point,
that the judgment was wrong.

I decided that I'd go find her the next day and ask.

That night, before I fell asleep, I thought back to the day she'd
first pulled me out of the water. "…You're not from here, are you?"
The face she'd made asking that. It had been almost two months since
then, and I realized how little I actually knew about her. Her
mother had been driven out. She didn't resent the Scales. That was
about all I had.

I understood, that night. I had only just barely learned how to tell
my own story. I hadn't learned, at all, how to listen to someone
else's. I didn't know, then, that I would be learning it the very
next day.

Through the window, the number on the board announcing the days
remaining glowed faintly in the dark. I watched it a long time before
closing my eyes.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. Just Not For Me

_Previously_ — Small changes had begun appearing all over the city.
The Scales' numbers hadn't moved. The Adjudicator said "the number
and what it's worth aren't the same thing." Trope had gone quiet,
and Pheugon had seen her standing a long time at a Scales terminal.
He decided to ask her about it the next day.

The next day I found her at the well.

The usual hour. She sat on the low stone wall, her water jar beside
her. She hadn't started drawing water yet.

"…Trope."

She looked up. She started to smile at me, then the smile stopped
halfway.

"…What is it."

"…Is something going on lately?"

Instead of answering, she ran her fingertips along the rim of the
well.

"…Nothing."

I didn't believe that. Her face said she knew I didn't believe it,
either.

Evening light rippled on the water in the well. The footsteps of
people drawing water grew fainter and farther. Soon we were the only
two left.

"…Trope." I called again. "Remember what you said, the day you
noticed I'd been going in and out of the Adjudicator's office?"

"…What did I say?"

"'The face people make when they're thinking about something that
doesn't show up on the Scales.'"

She laughed a little. "You remember that."

"You have that face right now."

Her laughter faded.

After a long while, she spoke again.

"…I won't ask what you heard in the Adjudicator's office," she said.
"You'll say it when you want to say it. Instead, let me say something
too. Trading."

I looked at her.

"…What."

"There's something I've been thinking about too, lately," she said.
"I couldn't find where to put it into words."

I sat down beside her. We stayed that way a long while without
speaking.

"…You stood at the Scales terminal a long time yesterday," I finally
said. "I saw you."

Her hand stopped.

"…I was just standing there."

"…Without raising your hand."

She looked at me. It was a look that seemed to be measuring — how
much she could say, how much I could bear.

"…Pheugon." She asked. "You've heard about the clause. From the
Adjudicator."

I was startled. I'd never told her that.

"…How did you know?"

"In the square. I'm not the only one who's noticed the Adjudicator's
window staying lit late these days. And—" she paused. "Your face has
changed. Lately."

"I've known about that clause," she went on, "since I was little."

I looked at her.

"Everyone born in this city knows. By rumor. No one knows the exact
content, but everyone knows there's a thing called the Forty-Day
Clause, and that it's never once fired. Parents use it to scare
children, too. 'If you don't behave, a clause like that will never
move for you.'"

"…You grew up hearing that."

"Yes," she said. "I believed it. That if a city became good, it
could be forgiven. But Pheugon, I—"

She looked straight at me.

"I've never once thought there was no god."

I had no answer for that. It wasn't the direction I'd expected.

"A lot of people misunderstand," she went on. "When I say I don't
resent the Scales, they think I don't believe in something. That's
not it. I believe it exists. Forgiveness, and whatever hands it down
— I believe those exist."

"But."

"But." She looked down at the water jar. "I don't believe it reaches
me."

Evening light rippled on the water.

"…What do you mean by that."

"The city can be forgiven," she said. "I believe that. What you've
been counting lately, Pheugon — I know about that too. The bakery,
the weights, the cane. I've seen it too. This city really is
changing. I believe that's real."

"…But?"

"I'm not."

She said it very quietly. Not an angry voice. Not a sad voice,
either. Just a voice stating a fact.

"There's forgiveness, I suppose," she said. "Just not for me."

I heard that sentence and couldn't say anything for a long time.

I wanted to argue with her then. But I couldn't find the argument.
There was nothing logically wrong with what she'd said. That was what
frightened me more. I didn't know how to defeat her words.

"…Why," I finally managed. "Why aren't you, Trope?"

"Remember my mother?" she asked.

"…Yes."

"My mother crossed the weight limit and was driven out. The Scales
calculated it precisely. I don't doubt that. But there's something I
want to know."

She looked at me.

"Do you know what my weight is?"

I didn't know.

"I know," she said. "I check every day. Other people check to feel
reassured. I'm different. I check because I'm afraid the number
might suddenly spike. Because I'm my mother's daughter. I think the
limit she crossed might be in me too. Inherited. Everyone says the
Scales doesn't measure by family. I can't bring myself to believe
that."

"The Adjudicator says," I said, carefully, "that the Scales really
doesn't measure by family. The records are kept entirely separate."

"I know that too," she said. "I know it in my head. The Adjudicator
told me that himself. Several times. But knowing and believing are
different. I know it and still can't believe it."

I turned that sentence over for a long time. Knowing and believing
being different things. I knew the Scales was right, and knowing
that had never once lessened my anger. She knew the Scales didn't
measure by family, and knowing that had never once lessened her fear.
Knowing didn't take the place of believing.

She stood up.

"…I'll show you," she said.

I followed her to a Scales terminal near the well. A small terminal
standing in a corner of the square. She raised her hand. A number
came up on the screen.

I looked at the number. Low. Much lower than I'd expected — well
below the city's average.

"…It's low," I said.

"Yes." She lowered her hand. "It's low. Because I haven't done much
wrong. But every time I look at this number, I see another number
alongside it. One that isn't here."

"…What number."

"The number my mother crossed," she said, pointing at the screen
with a fingertip. "It's not written on the Scales. Not anywhere on my
screen. But I feel like it's layered behind this one. The number you
can't see is scarier. At least a visible number, you can check."

I heard that and looked again at the small screen. Only one number
really was showing there. The other number she spoke of was nowhere
to be found. And yet, in that moment, I had the strange sensation of
seeing it anyway.

We went back and sat at the well.

"I was born in this city," she went on. "I was born carrying this
city's sin along with it. My mother's overflowed weight, this city's
overflowed weight — I grew up inside that. It couldn't not have
gotten onto me."

"…But if the city turns—"

"Then the city lives," she said. "I'd live inside it too. But that
and me, personally, being forgiven are different things. The city
living and me being all right are different problems."

That distinction felt foreign to me. And, at the same time, strangely
exact.

"Five years ago," she went on, "the day my mother left, my
grandfather took me and said something. 'You're not guilty. You just
got left behind.' I know he meant it as comfort. But that was the
first time I had a strange thought."

"…What thought."

"Someone with no sin doesn't need forgiveness," she said. "Forgiveness
is for someone with sin. But I was told I had no sin, and at the same
time I was living inside the results of that sin. I felt like there
was no place for me between those two sentences. Not a sinner. Not
someone to be forgiven, either. Just someone left behind."

I understood, in that moment, that she had carried that one fear
every day since her mother was driven out three years before. I had
carried one anger for three years. She had carried one fear for three
years. We each held something different, and were exactly as heavy.

"What about you," she asked, suddenly. "Being angry versus being
afraid — which is harder?"

I couldn't answer right away.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "I was on the angry side. The whole
time. But anger at least has a direction. I know who I'm angry at.
Listening to you, I think fear might not have a direction at all."

"That's right," she said. "Anger points at something. Fear just
points at me."

"I know I'm strange," she said. "Everyone tells me 'you did nothing
wrong.' That's true. I know it too. I never cheated with weights.
Never stole anything. But that doesn't make me safe."

"…Why not."

"Because." She paused a moment. "I'm not afraid of something I did.
I'm afraid of who I am. I'm the daughter of someone who went over the
limit. That fact doesn't change no matter what I do."

I felt something shift, deep down, hearing that.

"Forgiveness," she went on. "For other people it seems to feel like a
rule. Do this and you're forgiven, do that and you're not. Not for
me. For me, forgiveness exists, and it leaves me out of it. Like
everyone's in the room and I'm standing outside the door."

That image stayed with me a long time.

"…Who's in the room," I asked.

"Everyone," she said. "The baker. The weights man. The Adjudicator.
You too. Everyone will eventually walk into that room. I get as far
as the door. Every time. But I can't grab the handle."

"Why can't you."

"Because I don't know if my hand has the standing to grab it."

I started to say something, then stopped. It sounded too much like
something I'd carried for three years myself. I was a boy who took
care not to lose his standing. She was standing outside a door
because she believed she had no standing. Same word, opposite
direction.

"I have something similar," I said. "I spent three years guarding my
standing. You spent three years believing you had none. But somehow
we both ended up standing in the same place. Outside the door."

She looked at me for a long time.

"…No one's ever put it that way to me before," she said quietly.

I couldn't say anything. I had no comfort to offer. Like the
Adjudicator had done for me, I didn't want to tell her it was all
right either. I understood now how empty that would be.

"…Trope," I finally managed. "I don't know either. What I should
say."

"It's all right," she said. And, for the first time, smiled a little.
"I don't need anyone to say anything. Just being listened to is
enough."

Hearing that, I thought of the Adjudicator. He had told me the exact
same thing. Listening. That's all. That was the first time I truly
understood the weight of that word. Listening wasn't nothing.

I turned her words over for a long time that night. Forgiveness
exists, but not for her. I found that sentence strangely familiar. I
was angry knowing forgiveness existed. She was afraid knowing
forgiveness existed. Neither of us had ever doubted forgiveness. Both
of us believed it never reached our own place. I didn't want to hand
that place to anyone else. She believed she couldn't fit into it. The
directions were opposite. The place we were standing was the same.

"…Trope." I called again. "What if — hypothetically — I could
trigger the clause. Would you be saved along with everyone else?"

She looked at me. A long time.

"The city," she said. "I live in it too. But Pheugon, that's the
question that frightens me most. The city living and me living aren't
the same sentence."

"…Why aren't they the same sentence."

"Because," she said. "The city exists as a number. What the Scales
measures. But I, Pheugon — I'm not a number. I'm a person. And a
person has things scarier than numbers."

I didn't fully understand the meaning of that, that night.

We didn't draw water that evening. We just sat there a long while,
until the sun had sunk completely below the horizon.

"…I'll be here tomorrow too," she said again as we parted. The same
words she'd said once before.

I didn't know the weight of those words then.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. The One Who Knows First

_Previously_ — Trope said what she truly believed for the first
time. That the city could be forgiven, but she might not be.
"There's forgiveness, I suppose. Just not for me." Pheugon found no
argument against it. The two of them sat at the well until the sun
went down.

I saw the man again in a market alley.

I'd seen him a few times before — in the square, when someone was
being unfairly ganged up on, while everyone else was getting angry,
he was the one who didn't. I'd taken him for simply an odd man. This
time was different.

At the mouth of the market, a woman was shouting. She'd kicked over a
stall. The stall owner backed away, both hands raised.

"…I didn't steal it! Why would I steal it!"

"Who else would it be!" the woman shouted. "You were right next to my
spot yesterday!"

People gathered. Some took the woman's side, some defended the stall
owner. Voices rose.

I stopped as I was passing through. Two months ago I'd have walked
right past a scene like this. Now I knew some of the faces in this
alley a little. The stall owner had been trading here a long time; the
shouting woman was a stranger to me.

The man was standing in the middle of it.

I'd sensed something strange about him from the first time I saw him.
I couldn't have said exactly why. This time, up close, I understood.

His clothes weren't so different from anyone else's in this city.
Nothing about him stood out. And yet the spot where he stood was
strangely quiet. Right in the middle of the commotion, the noise
seemed to drop a full tone around him.

He was looking at the woman. But something about the look was
different. Everyone else looked at the stall owner with suspicion. He
was looking at the woman.

"…Is something the matter," he asked her.

His voice wasn't loud. And yet it carried clearly through the
commotion.

The woman turned toward him.

"…Who are you."

"You're angry about the missing goods," he said. "But that's not all
of it, I don't think."

The woman's face flickered.

"…What are you talking about."

"You lost something else yesterday too," he said quietly. "Not
goods."

A chill went down my spine.

The woman stood there, unable to speak. A few seconds later, tears
welled in her eyes. The face that had been shouting a moment ago
turned into a completely different face.

I hadn't heard anything at that spot. I had no idea what he knew, to
say something like that. But the woman understood. She sank down
right where she stood and began quietly crying.

The stall owner stood there, still not understanding what had
happened. The crowd drifted off, one and two at a time. What could
have become a fight ended, just like that.

In that moment, I thought of the Scales. The Scales doesn't know
what that woman lost. It only measures what converts to weight. It
could weigh the price of stolen goods, but whatever she'd lost the
day before wasn't among the Scales' categories. And yet he knew.

He walked over to the stall owner afterward.

The man was still standing there, looking wronged. The other man
stopped in front of him for a moment.

"…You're startled," he said.

"…I really didn't steal it," the stall owner said. His voice shook.

"I know," he said. "That you didn't steal it."

"…How do you know?"

"That sack under your stall." He pointed under the stall. "You bought
it from someone else this morning. Paid for it in full."

The stall owner's eyes went wide. I had no idea what that sack was.
Neither did the stall owner ask how he'd known. He just nodded. His
face looked relieved.

I felt two things at once, in that moment. One was astonishment. He
was as precise as the Scales. Precise, but used entirely differently.
The Scales would have used that precision to expose a crime. He used
it to prove there wasn't one.

The other was fear. If he could know that precisely, there must be
something inside me he'd already read too. It was the first time I
wanted to shrink away from someone.

I followed him.

From a distance. He left the market and turned into a back alley. His
pace was unhurried. No trace of being in a rush at all.

I stopped him.

"…Excuse me."

He turned. His face was hard to place an age on. Young in one light,
somewhere ancient in another.

"…Yes."

"What did you say to that woman just now? How did you know?"

He looked at me a moment, then smiled.

"I didn't know," he said. "I guessed."

"You guess that precisely?"

"I don't know what else to call it," he said. "I just tend to watch
people for a long time."

I found the answer strange. It didn't sound like an answer at all.

We walked side by side after that. The alley was narrow, in shade.
Laundry hung overhead. As we walked, he occasionally met the eyes of
someone passing — not quite a greeting, not quite not one. Just a
look that said he recognized them.

I found that strange too. I'd lived in this city almost two months
and still didn't recognize most people passing me on the street. He
claimed not to be from here, and yet he seemed to recognize nearly
everyone.

"…You could've been angry at her," I said. "Shouting, kicking over
the stall. That was rude."

"It was," he said.

"So why didn't you get angry?"

He thought about that for a moment.

"No reason to," he said.

"You just got treated rudely."

"That woman was rude, that's true," he said. "But I also know why
she did it. Once you know why, it's hard to stay angry."

I wanted to push back at that. I couldn't find the argument.

That was the first time I truly thought this man was strange. The
first time I'd seen him, in the square, I'd just thought he was odd
for not getting angry. This time was different. He wasn't holding his
anger back. He wasn't angry at all. Those were two entirely different
things.

"There's someone I know," I said, "who did something bad to my
family. I found myself getting angrier at him the more I got to know
him. The more I understood why he did it."

"That can happen," he said. He didn't deny it.

"But you're the opposite. The more you understand, the less angry you
get. Why is that different from me?"

He stopped walking and looked straight at me.

"What you know is why that person did it," he said. "I know one more
thing than that."

"…What."

"Where that person is right now," he said. "Knowing why someone did
something, and knowing which way they're walking, right now, this
instant — those are different things. Understanding the reason can
make you angrier. But knowing the direction turns the anger into
something else."

I half understood.

"…What does it turn into?"

"That's something you'll have to find out yourself, I think," he
smiled. "I'm not the one who gives answers."

"…If I wanted to know that woman's weight," I asked, "I could just
ask the Scales, couldn't I? Why watch people that long?"

"The Scales tells you what that woman did," he said. "It doesn't tell
you what she's going through right now."

"What's the difference?"

"What was done is the past," he said. "What's being gone through is
the present. The Scales is a machine that measures the past. Not a
machine that measures the present."

I turned that over. It resembled what the Adjudicator had told me.
That the Scales can't measure direction. But this man seemed to know
something even more precise than direction.

"…Are you," I asked, carefully, "from this city, by any chance?"

"No," he said.

"Then where are you from?"

He smiled instead of answering. A smile that deflected the question.

"…Someone I know," I said, suddenly. "Her weight is low, and she's
still constantly afraid. She believes she inherited something. Can
you guess her weight too?"

He looked at me. Something in that look made me freeze up for a
moment. As if he already knew her.

"That person doesn't believe she inherited something," he said
quietly. "She believes that's all she'll ever be."

I lost my breath. I had never told him Trope's name.

"…How did you—"

"I told you, I guess," he said, smiling again. But the smile was a
little different this time.

"…Have you ever met her?" I asked.

"Hard to say," he said. Not an answer.

"If you'd met her, you'd know. How convinced she is. Words won't
change that. I've tried."

"It's not going to be changed by words," he said. "I know that too."

"Then what does change it?"

He didn't answer. Instead he looked at me for a long time. There was
something in that look I couldn't read yet.

"Tell her this," he said. "That she's not the only one who can see
what the Scales can't."

"That's it?"

"That's it," he said. "For now."

That last word bothered me strangely. For now. It sounded as if
there were more, later. I didn't ask when that later was.

"…What's your name?" I asked, right before we parted.

He answered without stopping.

"Just a man passing through."

"Everyone has a name."

"Not everyone," he said. "Some people just haven't started using
theirs yet."

I didn't understand that answer either. He walked off toward the end
of the alley and disappeared.

I went to find Trope that evening and told her what happened.

"He said I'm not the only one who can see what the Scales can't?"
Trope repeated. "What does that mean?"

"I don't know," I said. "But he knew exactly what you'd told me,
without ever having met you. And I never said your name to him."

Trope's face turned complicated.

"…That's frightening," she said.

"It scared me too, at first."

"And now?"

I thought a moment.

"…I don't know," I said. "It's frightening, and at the same time
there's something else there too. That he knew me that precisely and
still didn't get angry. I keep coming back to that."

Trope listened and said nothing. She just looked off into the
distance.

"…I'd like to meet him sometime too," she finally said. "But it
scares me."

"Me too."

We didn't talk about it any more that night. As we parted, she looked
at my face a little longer than usual. Like there was something more
she wanted to ask, and was holding back.

That night before sleep, I made a strange decision.

I wanted to confirm whether he really was a man who never got angry.
Words hadn't been able to confirm it. He'd already had two chances to
get angry and hadn't. I wanted to test him a third time — not by
chance, this time, but in a place I made myself.

Even then I dimly knew it wasn't a good idea. But I'd lived three
years as someone who held his anger back. What it felt like to hold
anger back, rather than simply not have it — I knew that better than
anyone. I wanted to know whether he was truly a man with no anger, or
just a man holding his back, like me.

That night I thought about him for a long time. He knew people
before the Scales did, more precisely than the Scales did. And that
knowledge went in a strange direction. The more precisely the Scales
knew, the heavier its sentence grew. The more precisely he knew, the
lighter he treated people. I couldn't understand that opposite
direction.

I had lived believing precision moved opposite to forgiveness. The
Scales was my proof. Know more precisely, and the sin becomes
clearer; the clearer the sin, the heavier the sentence. But this man,
knowing more precisely, saw not the sin but the person. I didn't know
where I could learn that difference.

I mulled over whether I should tell the Adjudicator about this. Then
decided against it. Even asking, I doubted he'd know the answer. This
man seemed to belong to no one, not even inside this city.

From that day on, I put him at the top of a list of strange people.
There were no other names on that list. I found myself waiting for
the day I'd meet him a second time. Why I was waiting, I didn't know
then.

Right before sleep, I thought once more of that woman's face. Shouting,
then sinking down and crying. That was the first time I saw what face
is left when anger collapses. I never did hear what she had lost. But
the face stayed with me a long time.

I tried to hold that face up against my own. If my own anger
collapsed, what would be underneath. That night I wasn't ready to
answer that question at all.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. Provocation

_Previously_ — Pheugon met the man again. He knew people more
precisely than the Scales, before the Scales did, and still never
got angry. He'd even pinpointed Trope's fear exactly, though
Pheugon had never told him her name. That night Pheugon decided to
test him directly.

I found him again two days later.

It wasn't hard. He walked near the market around the same time every
day. I waited for him at the mouth of an alley.

"…We meet again," he said, seeing me first. No sign of surprise.

"…I've been waiting."

"I know," he said.

That answer alone shook me a little. I tried to hold on to what I'd
rehearsed.

Looking back now, I was already shaking before the test even began.
When the one who comes to test gets shaken before the testing starts,
that means the test itself was badly designed from the outset.

I'd rehearsed this conversation in my head, the night before, more
than once. What to open with, how to push, where he'd break. I was a
herald. I'd spent three years training to handle words precisely in
front of people. And I was about to use that training, for the first
time, for something other than good.

I walked alongside him. He was in no hurry. That unhurried pace only
irritated me more.

The sky was overcast. It looked like it might rain, or might not. We
were alone in the alley. I thought that was for the best. I didn't
want anyone else to hear what I was about to say.

"…Sir." I said. "Last time, you said you knew what that woman had
lost."

"I did."

"I've lost something too. Guess it."

He looked at me a moment.

"…Shall I try," he said.

"My family," he said. "More precisely, the reason it fell apart. You
still only know half of that reason. You don't even know where the
other half is."

I lost my words for a moment. It was something I hadn't even told the
Adjudicator in full.

I didn't want to admit I was surprised, in that moment. My rehearsed
script hadn't accounted for me being surprised.

"…That's right," was all I managed.

"Then," I asked again, "do you know where that person is right now?"

"Who."

"The person who wrecked my family."

He fell silent at the question for a moment.

"Don't know," he said. "That, I don't know."

"You're not lying?"

"No."

I didn't like that answer. I wanted to believe he knew everything.
That was the only way my next question would work.
