"…You're on the side of this city's people, aren't you," I said. A
deliberately sharpened remark. "Not getting angry, forgiving
everyone — that's all a fine story. But that fine story only works
for some people. Go say it to Trope. Do you think it'd comfort her?"

"Trope isn't looking for comfort," he said. No change in his voice.

"Then what is she looking for."

"Same as you," he said. "Someone to be angry in her place."

I lost my words for a second at that.

"…What do you mean by that."

"You've been hating the Reckoning on your family's behalf," he said.
"The anger your family couldn't raise themselves, you're raising it
for them. Trope is similar. Only the direction's reversed. You do it
by hating someone else. Trope does it by hating herself."

I wanted to argue with that, and no words came. He was too precise.
That precision, I learned that day, could render a person entirely
helpless.

"Then," I said. "If you're that precise. This city's people who
stole weights, cheated on rations — you'd know all of it. Name them,
one by one."

"Why."

"I'm curious," I said. My voice was already sharpening.

"Those are things the Scales already knows," he said. "No need for
me to say them again."

"Then tell me what the Scales doesn't know," I pushed. "This city's
people, all pretending to be good on the outside while still hoping,
inside, for the Reckoning to fall. There must be people like that.
Name them."

He looked at me a long moment at that.

"There is one," he said quietly.

My heart leapt.

"…Who."

"You."

I fell apart completely, in that moment.

I hadn't prepared for that answer. I'd expected him to name someone
else. That he would point at me — I hadn't thought of that. Or maybe
I had thought of it. I just hadn't expected he'd say it so precisely,
so quietly.

"…What would you know." My voice shook. "I want this city to live,
these days. I've been counting it, every day. How the city is
changing."

"You're counting," he said. "But counting and wanting are different.
You're still half and half. The part that wants it to live, and the
part that once wanted it to fall. Both are still in you."

"…That's not true."

"No?" He looked straight at me. "Then why did you just ask me for
someone else's name. You're still hunting for a guilty party inside
this city. So that there'd still be proof this city deserves to
fall."

I had no answer for that. It was exact. He'd said what even I hadn't
known about myself, before I did.

"…Doesn't that make you angry?" I asked. My voice cracked. "I was
just rude to you. I did it on purpose to test you. Doesn't that make
you angry?"

He looked at me quietly at that question.

"No," he said.

"Why not! I did it on purpose! I was trying to make you angry!"

"I know," he said. "I knew that too."

"And still?"

"And still," he said. No change in his voice.

In that moment I understood what I'd wanted. I'd wanted him to get
angry. I'd wanted him to break, the way I did. If he broke, I could
have felt at ease. If it were proven impossible not to get angry,
then what I'd endured for three years could be called an ordinary
weight, the same as everyone else's.

He didn't break.

I pushed harder.

"…You've never lost anything, have you," I said. I wasn't choosing my
words anymore. "That's why you can talk about forgiveness so calmly.
Someone who's actually lost something couldn't talk like that."

At that, he closed his eyes for just a moment, then opened them.

I still remember that instant. It was a flicker. Brief, but I saw it
clearly. But it wasn't anger. I didn't know the difference then.

"…I've lost something," he said quietly.

"What."

He didn't answer. I saw the silence as a chance to press harder.

"Not lying? Prove it. Say what you lost. If you can't say it, it was
just something you said for effect."

"There's a difference between what can be said and what should be
said," he said. "This isn't something to tell you right now."

"There you go dodging again."

"I'm not dodging."

"You are!" I shouted it. My voice echoed through the alley. A few
passersby turned to look.

"…Why don't you get angry?" I finally shouted.

The alley went quiet. A few people passing by turned to look. I had
no room to care.

"Why!" I said again. "I get angry. I know how hard it is, three
years, holding anger back. And you just don't, without even looking
like it costs you anything. Is that even possible? Can a person be
like that?"

He waited for my voice to settle.

"The reason I don't get angry," he said quietly, "isn't that I have
more patience than you do."

"Then what?"

"I know where you're standing, right now," he said. "And I know which
way you're walking, too. See those two things together, and there's
not much room left for anger."

"…What does that mean."

"You came here today to make me angry," he said. "That's hatred. But
underneath that hatred, what you really wanted was to see someone who
didn't collapse before you did. So you could believe you might not
have to collapse either."

I felt entirely stripped bare in front of that sentence.

That was the first day I learned it. I hadn't gotten angry because
he didn't get angry. I'd gotten angry at what I saw in myself. He was
only standing there like a mirror.

I stood there and couldn't say anything. My breath came short. My
eyes stung, and I couldn't tell if it was anger or something else.

"…What are you," I finally said. "What are you, really. Are you even
a person?"

At that question, his expression shifted, for the first time, into
something different. Something between sorrow and gladness.

"That question," he said, "isn't one to answer right now."

"Then when?"

"When you're less angry than you are now."

He turned and walked away. I watched his back for a long time. I
didn't follow. I had no strength left to follow. Raindrops began
falling one by one in the alley.

I stood alone in that alley for a long while.

I'd gone to break him, and I came back broken. I was ashamed of
that. And at the same time there was something strangely light about
it. For three years I'd lived proving I could hold my anger back.
That day, for the first time, someone had seen through the fact that
I couldn't. And the world didn't end when I was caught.

I felt something like what I'd felt confessing to the Adjudicator. I
had expected something to fix itself then too, and been disappointed.
This time I expected nothing. And still, something, just barely,
sat a little differently.

Walking home, I ran into Trope. She looked at my face and started to
ask something, then stopped.

"…Something happen?"

"…Yes," I said. "I lost completely today."

"To who?"

"Him. The one I told you about."

"…What did you do?"

I couldn't answer right away.

"…I tested him," I finally said. "Tried to make him angry. On
purpose."

Trope stopped walking.

"…And?"

"He didn't," I said. "The whole time. Even after everything I threw
at him."

"…Then you didn't lose. He didn't get angry — that's not you losing."

"I lost," I said. "I went to break him, and I got everything I'm
holding onto exposed instead. If that's not losing, what is."

Trope heard that and said nothing more. She just walked beside me,
step for step.

I saw the Adjudicator the next day.

He seemed to notice something the moment he saw my face.

"…Something happen?"

"…I met him," I said. "The man who doesn't get angry."

"Ah." The Adjudicator said it briefly. No surprise on his face.

"You know him too, Adjudicator?"

"I've seen him a few times," he said. "Thirty-one years in this
city, and you meet a handful of people you can't explain."

"What is he, exactly?"

The Adjudicator didn't answer. He just looked at me quietly instead.

"You wanted to make him angry."

I couldn't answer that.

"I don't even need to ask why you did it," he said. "It's written all
over your face."

"…What's written there."

"The face of someone who lost." He said it without smiling. It wasn't
a taunt either. "But not a bad kind of losing. There's a way to lose
like that."

I turned that over for a long time.

The Adjudicator went back to his papers. But he held the pen a long
while without writing anything. I could feel that he had something
more he wanted to say, and was holding it back. I didn't ask.

I never tried to test him again after that day. There was no need
to. I already knew the answer. He wasn't a man holding his anger
back. He was a man who simply didn't get angry. I had to live it in
my own body to finally believe the difference.

But knowing that didn't make me more comfortable. If anything, it
made me more uncomfortable. For three years I'd believed that holding
anger back was a virtue. The moment I saw that a man who truly didn't
get angry actually existed, I understood that everything I'd done was
not virtue but simple endurance. The distance between endurance and
genuinely not getting angry was far greater than I'd ever imagined.

I didn't know then how to cross that distance. I still don't fully
know now. But knowing the distance existed at all was enough to make
me a different person than I'd been before that day.

At the well, when I saw Trope again, she looked at my face for a long
while and then said briefly:

"…I'll be here tomorrow too."

I was hearing that for the third time now. I still didn't fully take
the weight of it in. I watched her back, water jar on her shoulder,
until the dark swallowed it whole.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. The Substitute

_Previously_ — Pheugon deliberately provoked the man who doesn't get
angry. He never broke, and Pheugon broke instead. The Adjudicator
said "there's a way to lose like that." Pheugon, for the first
time, admitted the distance between his own endurance and the
man's true absence of anger.

I heard the news in the Adjudicator's office.

"He's arrived," the Adjudicator said. He slid a paper across to me.

I looked at it. An arrival report. Name, herald office of origin,
date of arrival. I scanned the name. Unfamiliar.

"…Already?"

"He said a week," the Adjudicator said. "It's been exactly a week."

I found that precision strangely frightening.

"Where is he, now?"

"Herald office lodgings," the Adjudicator said. "He came to pay his
respects the moment he arrived. As procedure requires."

"You met him?"

"Yes."

"…What's he like?"

The Adjudicator thought a moment.

"He reminds me of you, three years ago," he said. "Precise. Steady.
Very well trained."

That sentence lodged in my chest.

"…Does he know about me? About the clause?"

"No," the Adjudicator said. "I didn't tell him. No reason to. He came
because he was assigned. A city-wide case. Reading the forty-day
grace sentence. That's all."

"…He doesn't even know about the clause?"

"The clause gets summarized onto the sentence document. He'll know
that much. But not the original text. I'm the only one who has it."

I wanted to meet him myself.

The next afternoon, I went to the herald office lodgings. I knocked
and the door opened right away. Like he'd been waiting.

"…Hello," I said.

He looked at me. He seemed a year or two older than me. His posture
was straight. He wore the herald office uniform, pressed sharp.

"…Who are you," he asked.

"…I'm Pheugon." I hesitated, then added, "I've been staying in this
city a while."

"Ah." He nodded. "Not from this city, either?"

"…No."

"Neither am I," he said. "Fellow sufferers, then."

I nearly laughed at that. I didn't.

He let me into the room. It was tidy. Almost no belongings. A few
sheets of paper lay squared off on the desk. I recognized one of them
as the Reckoning's sentence document.

"…Have you read it?" I asked.

"Of course," he said. "It's the document I'm going to proclaim."

"…How did it feel? Reading it."

He looked a little puzzled at the question.

"How did what feel?"

"…It's a hundred and twenty thousand people."

"That's right," he said. "The biggest case I've heard of. Among the
ones I've been assigned, anyway."

"…Aren't you scared?"

He thought about that for a moment.

"Fear and what I have to do are separate things," he said. "I read.
Precisely. That's my job."

I felt like I'd heard that sentence somewhere before. It was almost
word for word what my supervisor had once told me. In that moment I
felt like I was standing face to face with myself, three years ago.

"…How much do you know about this city?" I asked.

"As much as I need to," he said. "Population, accumulated weight, the
breakdown of violations. I've memorized all of it."

"…And the people? Have you met any?"

"Not many yet," he said. "I bought bread from the bakery, talked to
the lodging manager. That's about it."

"…Why not more?"

"No need to," he said. "I didn't come here to get close to the
people of this city. I came to read the sentence. On the fortieth
day."

I wanted to argue with that. I didn't know what to say.

In that moment I wanted to tell him he was wrong. But he wasn't
wrong. That was exactly the way I'd been taught, three years before.
Don't meet people, so you don't stop reading. That was the core of
the training.

"…Have you heard," I asked, carefully, "about the clause? The
Forty-Day Clause."

"I've heard of it," he said. "It's attached to the sentence document.
The clause that voids the sentence if the city is confirmed to have
turned within forty days."

"Do you know who does the confirming?"

"It says the one who read performs it," he said. Matter-of-fact. "If
I read it, I suppose it would be me."

I lost my breath for a moment.

"…How would you know if this city really turned?"

"I wouldn't," he said. Very honest. "I don't know this city. But
that's not my job. For the clause to fire, I'd need to confirm it,
and if I have no basis to confirm it, then I simply can't confirm it.
I just read, and judge things as I find them."

"…So it wouldn't fire."

"Probably not," he said. Not indifferent — just stating a fact. "If I
had some attachment to this city, I might see it differently. But I
have no reason to. This city, to me, is a number."

I stood there a long while after hearing that.

In that moment I didn't think this man was bad. He'd done nothing
wrong. He was simply working precisely, exactly as he'd been trained.
The problem wasn't that. The problem was that the more precisely he
worked, the more certainly this city would die.

"…If it's not too forward," he said, "may I ask why you're asking
about this."

I wasn't ready with an answer.

"…No reason," I said. "Just curious."

He seemed to find the answer strange, but didn't press.

"…How many days left, now?" he asked, straightening his papers.

I did the math. "…Six."

"Six," he repeated. Then briefly counted something off with a
finger. A finger counting dates. "Plenty of time to prepare, then."

"…Prepare?"

"Voice practice," he said. "A case this size means a long reading. I
need to decide in advance how many times I'll need water, mid-read."

I couldn't answer. He was already standing there, calculating how
many breaths he'd take in front of a hundred and twenty thousand
people.

He straightened his papers into a neat pile and saw me out. At the
door, he added one more line.

"…It was good to meet you, Pheugon."

I knew that greeting was sincere. That made it weigh on me more,
strangely.

As I left the lodgings, a familiar face was waiting at the bottom of
the stairs. The baker. She had something in her hand.

"…Herald, sir," she said. Her voice was careful. "Have this. You must
have come a long way."

What she held out was a loaf of bread, wrapped in cloth, still
steaming.

He took it. With both hands, at a precise angle.

"…Thank you," he said. "What's the cost?"

The baker's face stiffened slightly.

"…It's a gift."

"Ah." He paused a moment, then said, "Thank you. I'll pay next time."

The baker started to say something else, then just bowed her head and
turned away.

I stood there and watched the scene for a long time. He hadn't been
rude. He'd been perfectly courteous. And yet something was missing. I
only understood, much later, what it was. He couldn't tell a gift
from a purchase. No — he could tell the difference. He just felt no
difference between them at all. To the Scales, both weighed the
same.

I had been like that too. Just like erasing what the merchant said,
that first proclamation. I hadn't known, then, what I was erasing.
What this man was failing to receive now — he probably didn't know
that either.

I went to the room of records that afternoon.

I needed no excuse. The room was open to anyone. I stood hidden among
the shelves and watched him.

He was already there. Opening the precedent drawers one at a time,
copying something out. His fingertip moved along the pages at a
steady pace. It never stopped.

The scene felt familiar. Two months ago, I too had sat in that same
seat, opening those same drawers. Until I found Mena's record, I'd
sat there for days.

I'd been searching for something, then. A crack that could bring the
Scales down. What was he searching for now. I was curious. But I
didn't go over and ask. Asking would mean explaining why I was even
here.

He stayed in that seat over an hour. I watched him reach Mena's
entry. His fingertip stopped on that page. Just for an instant.

I held my breath in that moment. Afraid he'd notice — the misquoted
sentence, the wrong word, "never repented." I wanted him to catch on
it. No — I didn't want that. I didn't even know what I wanted.

He turned the page.

He didn't stop.

In that moment I understood. He wasn't the kind of person who'd
catch on that sentence, the way I had. I only realized then that I'd
caught it because I'd lived two months in this city. No one catches
it from the words alone. You have to have lived it to catch it.

I stood there until he'd finished tidying the drawers and left the
room. He never saw me.

That evening I found Trope at the well.

I sat beside her as she drew water and brought it up.

"…I met the substitute herald."

Trope's hand stopped.

"…What's he like?"

I thought for a moment how to explain it.

"…He's like me," I finally said. "Me, three years ago."

"What does that mean?"

"He doesn't know this city," I said. "Doesn't even try to. He just
sees numbers. Population, weight, violation breakdown. He's memorized
all of it. But he doesn't know the baker's name. Doesn't know yours,
either."

Trope heard that and looked down at the water jar.

"…If he reads it," she asked, "the clause won't fire. Right?"

I couldn't answer. There was no need to. She'd already known the
answer when she asked.

"…Six days left, they're saying." She said. I was surprised she
already knew that too. "The baker's wife told me today at noon."

Word travels faster than I do. I hadn't known that.

We didn't say anything for a long while. Evening light rippled on the
water in the well.

"…Pheugon," she finally said. "Not about him — can I ask about you?"

"…What."

"Are you going to read it?"

I couldn't answer right away.

"…I don't know," I said, after a long while. It wasn't a lie. I
truly didn't know.

Trope nodded. She didn't ask further. It wasn't like her to hold back.
Usually she'd have pressed once more. This time she didn't.

She filled her jar and stood. Before turning to go, she looked at me
once more. She started to say something, then didn't.

I watched her back a long time. That evening, strangely, the water
jar on her shoulder looked unusually heavy.

Walking home, I kept thinking of that room.

The tidy room, the squared-off papers, the unwavering voice. That
was the shape I'd built myself over three years. I'd been proud of
that shape. Facing it now like a mirror, I felt for the first time
how dangerous a shape it was.

He doesn't hate this city. Doesn't love it either. He simply doesn't
know it. And that not-knowing was, with perfect precision, blocking
off the only chance this city had to live. I understood that day,
for the first time, that there was something more frightening than
hatred. It wasn't indifference. It was trained ignorance, precisely
made.

Six days.

I counted the number off on my fingers. In six days two people would
stand in the square. Me, who hated this city. And him, who didn't
know it.

I understood precisely, for the first time, that night. That I was
the only person in the world who could save this city. That fact
didn't excite me. It crushed me.

Before sleep, I thought again of the papers laid out in his room. The
squared corners, the unwavering handwriting. I too had lived like
that for three years. Keeping the papers straight. Never letting
feeling mix into the paperwork. I'd been taught that was precision.

That night, for the first time, I thought about how that precision
split into two. One was the Scales' precision. The precision of
measuring weight, the precision of measuring the past. The other was
a person's precision. When a person tries to be precise, all he can
do is imitate the Scales. For three years, I'd been doing that
imitation. He was doing it now too. The only difference was that I
now knew what that imitation erased.

I lay awake a long time that night. Through the window I could see
the lights of the herald office lodgings. A light that seemed like it
would come on at the exact hour and go off at the exact hour.

Watching that light, I thought of my own room, three years ago. I
used to light my lamp like that too. At the exact hour. And put it
out at the exact hour. At the time I thought that was diligence. I
know now. It was fear. If I left an hour unaccounted for, I was
afraid something would slip in through it.

That friend was probably living inside that same fear right now,
without knowing it. I knew it wasn't his fault. And still, standing
in front of the fact that his fear could end this whole city, there
was nothing I could do about it.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. Five Days

_Previously_ — The substitute herald arrived. Precise and trained,
exactly as instructed, with no feeling in it. If he — who knew
nothing of this city — read the sentence in Pheugon's place, the
clause would never fire. For the first time, Pheugon felt the full
weight of being the only person in the world who could save this
city.

Four days ago it had been six.

This morning, the number on the square's board had changed to five.

I stood in front of it and looked at the number a long time. Five. I
counted it off on my fingers. Thumb, index, middle, ring, little.
When all five fingers folded down, that would be the day.

I was still counting, even then. Counting wasn't so much a habit as
a method. I'd learned, three years ago, that turning something
frightening into a number made it a little more bearable. That was
why I'd counted a hundred and twelve cases too. Count people, and it's
heavy. Count numbers, and it's light.

I tried the same method that day. Five. Just a written number. Fi.
Ve. Two syllables. I even counted the syllables. It didn't help at
all.

This time was different. No matter how many times I folded it into
my fingers, the number five wouldn't get lighter.

The habit of counting had started at age ten.

I only remembered that after washing up in this city. Our house had
eleven stairs. I used to count them, climbing up and down every
evening. I don't remember why I'd started. I just found myself doing
it, one day.

I hadn't known, then, what I was afraid of. I know now. I was
counting how many people sat at our dinner table each night. Four. Then
one day three. Then one day one. I told myself I was counting stairs.
I was really counting that table.

I never broke the habit, even in training. My supervisor called it
concentration. I know now it wasn't concentration. It was defense.
Numbers let me step slightly sideways out of the present. When the
present was too heavy, numbers became a handle.

That morning, I reached for that handle again. Five. Five fingers.
This time the handle wouldn't catch. The number didn't let me step
sideways. Instead it set me squarely, facing forward, right in that
spot.

I spent the whole day doing arithmetic.

The first calculation was easy. If I did nothing, that man would
stand in the square in five days. He would read the sentence document
from beginning to end. Precisely. Without wavering. And having read
all the way to Article 4, Clause 11, he would confirm nothing. He
didn't know this city. There was nothing for him to confirm.

The demolition would execute as scheduled.

A hundred and twenty thousand.

I remembered the moment I'd first heard that number, in the
supervisor's office. I'd thought then it was a number I'd never
counted. It was different now. Now I had faces held inside that
hundred and twenty thousand. The baker. The merchant fixing his
weights. The girl who'd picked up the cane. Trope.

Once a face exists, a number stops being a number. I learned that
over forty days. I learned things I hadn't wanted to learn.

The second calculation was more complicated. If I stepped forward.

I would stand in the square and read the sentence document. From
beginning to end. And having read the last line, I would have to say
this: "This city has turned." Then the clause would fire. The
demolition would be void. A hundred and twenty thousand would live.

But the one saying that sentence would be me.

I, who had spent three years living to hate this city, would have to
speak, with my own mouth, the final sentence that saved it.

I ran that calculation over and over. The answer came out the same
every time. And still I couldn't believe the answer. That the answer
was correct, and that I could act on the answer, were different
matters.

I went to see the Adjudicator that afternoon.

"…Adjudicator."

"Yes." He didn't look up from his papers.

"If I do nothing. If he reads it instead."

"Then it's demolished," he said. His voice was calm. "You already
know that."

"…So what am I supposed to do?"

The Adjudicator finally raised his head.

"I won't answer that question," he said. "I told you before. The
decision is yours. I'm not going to tell you to do it, or not to."

"…Why not." I asked. My voice cracked. "You've waited forty years for
this clause, Adjudicator. Surely you'd want to tell me to do it."

The Adjudicator closed his eyes briefly, then opened them.

"…That's true," he said quietly. "I've waited forty years. But that's
not a reason for me to tell you anything. The opposite, actually.
Because I've waited so long, I'm most afraid of my own wish getting
mixed into your decision."

I found that answer strangely honest.

"What you have to confirm," he said, "is whether this city has
turned. Not whether I want it to. If I persuade you into this, the
confirmation stops being a pure confirmation. It becomes a
confirmation with my wish mixed in."

"…What difference does that make? I'm the one making the decision
either way."

"It makes a difference," he said. "There's a difference between
saying what you yourself have seen, and saying something for
someone else's sake. The clause doesn't want the second. The clause
wants what you saw."

I turned that over for a long time.

That evening I stopped at the well. Trope was in her usual spot.

"…Why the face," she asked.

"…Doing arithmetic," I said.

"What kind?"

I wanted to tell her everything. The left column and the right
column. A hundred and twenty thousand against family. But the words
wouldn't come.

"…I think it would make it harder for you, if I told you," I finally
said.

She smiled a little at that. A sad smile.

"…I have arithmetic like that too," she said. "I haven't told you."

I looked at her.

"…What kind?"

"Later," she said, lifting her jar. "I don't have an answer yet
either."

I let that pass, at the time. I was too deep inside my own
arithmetic. Looking back, she'd already handed me a hint that day. I
didn't take it.

We didn't say much more and parted. As we did, she said her usual
line.

"…I'll be here tomorrow too."

Hearing it, I thought for the first time that it sounded like a
promise. I couldn't have said, then, why it sounded that way.

That night I started calculating again at home.

I believed calculation would save me. It had, for three years. Weigh
the case, count the total, count the dates — do that, and somehow an
answer would come. That night, for the first time, I sat before a
problem that wouldn't resolve.

I decided to write it down on paper. On the left, reasons to stay
still. On the right, reasons to step forward.

On the left, I wrote:

Someone from this city wrecked our home. The Scales calculated it.
Precisely. And nothing came back. My brother didn't return. My mother
didn't return. My father still lives alone on the other side of the
city.

I survived three years on that one anger. If I step forward and save
this city, where do I put that anger down?

On the right, I wrote:

The baker. I still don't know her name. She held my hand with
flour-dusted fingers.

The merchant fixing his weights. No one told him to. He was setting
it right on his own.

Trope. Lost her mother. Doesn't resent the Scales. Believes she has
no standing to be forgiven, and still comes to the well every day.

A hundred and twenty thousand.

I looked at that paper for a long time.

The left column was one line. The right column had many lines. But
I knew that wasn't the answer. Having more lines didn't mean winning.
That one line on the left was a line I'd guarded three years to
protect. The lines on the right had formed in two months. Counted by
time, the left should win. But I knew that method of counting itself
was wrong.

I tried calculating another way. In the hundred and twelve cases I'd
read, I'd never saved anyone. I'd only read. I'd only made audible a
verdict the Scales had already reached. The hundred and thirteenth
was different. The hundred and thirteenth, one sentence from my own
mouth could save a hundred and twenty thousand, or leave them to die.

A hundred and twelve to one. I wrote that ratio on the paper, then
crossed it out. Numbers made no meaning here. Some arithmetic can't be
solved by ratio. That night I learned that a second time.

I thought of my brother. The winter he left, I'd calculated how many
days it would be before he came back. Three days. A week. A month. I
counted that way and waited. He never came back. That calculation
still sat unanswered, three years on.

That night I laid the two calculations side by side for the first
time. The calculation of when my brother would return, and the
calculation of when the Reckoning would fall. Both were numbers I
couldn't control. But one I was allowed to let go of, and the other I
wasn't. I wanted to know where that difference came from.

That night I sat for a long while, studying my own hand.

Through a hundred and twelve readings, this hand had never once
shaken. It shook for the first time in front of the Adjudicator. That
day I thought the shaking was the shaking of someone caught. Tonight
this hand was shaking for a different reason. I still hadn't found a
name for what kind of shaking it was.

I thought again about the number five. Five days. A hundred and
twenty hours. Seven thousand two hundred minutes.

I broke the time down finer, thinking it would feel a little less
frightening. It didn't. Seven thousand two hundred was a far bigger,
far more frightening number than five. That was the night I first
admitted that counting couldn't save me.

I folded the paper and put it in the drawer. It wasn't a finished
calculation. It was just one I could no longer continue.

I lay down, and kept counting even lying there.

That night I ran one more strange calculation. If I did nothing, I
would end up someone who had, at last, kept that one sentence I'd
guarded for three years. Just as the Reckoning had given me nothing
back, I too would give the Reckoning nothing back. That calculation
was clean. A debt not repaid. Just end it there.

But there was a strange hole in that calculation. Not repaying a
debt, and making the debtor's children repay it in your place, were
different things. One person from the Reckoning had wrecked our home.
I had something owed to me by that man. But if I didn't step forward,
the price would be paid not by that man, but by the baker, by the
girl who'd picked up the cane, by Trope. I only then understood I'd
never properly done this sum. I'd been pounding on the wrong debtor's
door.

I sat up. The room was dark. A few stars showed through the window.

I found myself wondering, in that moment, how the Scales would solve
this equation. How would it handle the fact that the one who sinned
and the one who pays are different people? But the answer was
obvious. The Scales doesn't ask that question at all. The Scales only
measures who crossed the limit. It doesn't measure who ends up buried
under that weight. That was never one of the Scales' fields.

If that was something a person had to do, then it occurred to me,
for the first time, that I might be the one who had to fill in that
field.

I lay back down. The number five seemed to hang there on the ceiling.

I counted it again. This time not the days — something else. The
people whose names I'd learned since coming to this city. The baker,
the weights merchant, the cane girl, the neighbor girl, Trope. Five.
Five days, five faces. That coincidence caught at me strangely. Each
face seemed to overlap, one for one, with each day remaining.

That night, before sleep, I spoke aloud to myself for the first
time. "What am I supposed to do." No one answered. But saying it
aloud, just that, made me feel like a slightly different person. I
remembered the three days I'd been trapped in the conduit, the first
night I'd spoken aloud there. I'd spoken to no one then too. And now,
the same.

Looking back, what I truly learned that night was this. Knowing I
was the only one who could save this city gave me no strength at
all. That fact only left me standing alone. Standing in a spot where
no one else in the world stood was not a proud spot. It was only a
frightening one.

I don't know what Jonah calculated, counting forty days before
Nineveh. But I know this now — some arithmetic doesn't end even after
the answer comes. That night, I fell asleep not even sure whether I'd
reached an answer at all.

I must have opened and closed my fingers even in sleep. When I woke
the next morning, my right hand was still half curled. The fourth
finger had folded in on itself. As I straightened it, I thought:
yesterday's me and today's me were not the same person. The number was
still five. But the person holding that number had changed, a
little.

The very thing the Scales can never read was, that night, happening
inside me. I hadn't reached an answer. But a direction was forming.
The Scales cannot tell the two apart. That night, for the first time,
I thought that I might come to know my own direction before the
Scales ever could.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. The One Who Walks Out

_Previously_ — The days remaining fell to five. Pheugon stood before
the calculation: stay still and the city dies; step forward and he
becomes the only person who can save it. He wrote his reasons down
on paper and still couldn't reach an answer by the end of the
night.

Five became four.

I did nothing special that day. I just walked as usual. Past the
bakery, through the market, by the well. Trope was sitting by her
jar again that day. We didn't say much. But she was quieter than
usual.

"…Trope," I asked. "Can someone with low weight still get driven
out?"

She looked a little startled by the question.

"…Why ask that?"

"No reason," I deflected. "Just curious."

"…Low weight, and you don't get driven out," she said. "But—"

"But what?"

"…Never mind." She said, adjusting her grip on the jar, changing the
subject. "Finish your own arithmetic, Pheugon."

I didn't ask, at the time, what came after that "but." I took her
changing the subject as simple kindness. I know now. What came after
that "but" was something she'd been about to tell me that day, and
then held back.

As we parted, she said the line again.

"…I'll be here tomorrow too."

I wasn't even counting how many times I'd heard it by then. It had
become too ordinary a thing to count.

Four became three.

I went twice to the Adjudicator's door that day, and turned back
twice. It wasn't that I had nothing to say. It was that I had too
much to say, and didn't know where to start.

That evening at the well, Trope packed up earlier than usual.

"…You're leaving early today," I said.

"…Something I need to do," she said.

"What?"

"…Nothing," she smiled. The same smile as always. I noticed nothing
in it.

She said the line again as we parted.

"…I'll be here tomorrow too."

I let that pass that day too. Looking back, she was probably saying
it with a slightly different weight each time. I couldn't hear that
difference in weight. I was good at hearing sounds. What lay behind
the sound, I still couldn't hear well.

Three became two.

That evening, Trope wasn't at the well. I just thought she must have
something on. I told myself I'd ask her the next day and let it go.

Looking back, that day was the fork in the road. I didn't go looking
for her that day. I was too deep in my own arithmetic. I knew she was
carrying something as heavy as I was, and still I hadn't checked that
weight every day.

I know now. Three days before, when she said "I'll be here tomorrow
too" for the last time — that had been the last day I ever heard her
say it. I didn't notice, at the time, that it had stopped. Something
that has been there vanishing is far quieter than something new
appearing.

Two became one.

The morning the square's board changed to 1, I felt a strange
stillness from the first light of dawn.

The whole city seemed to be holding its breath. People still walked
as usual, the bakery still opened its doors. But the footsteps were
a little slower. The voices were a little fewer.

I went to the well. Trope wasn't there.

At first I didn't think much of it. She was sometimes late. I sat on
the stone wall and waited. Half an hour. An hour. She didn't come.

People fetching water passed by, one after another. I asked them.

"…Have you seen Trope?"

"Not today, no," one woman said. "I don't think I saw her yesterday
either."

"…Yesterday either?"

"I think so. Haven't seen much of her lately."

Hearing that, something went cold in my chest. Only then did I
count, for the first time, that I hadn't actually seen her in two
days. I couldn't understand myself, why that count had come so late.

I started counting then. Half an hour, an hour, an hour and a half.
Counting let me forget the anxiety in small doses. But past two
hours, even counting stopped working.

I went to her room.

I knocked. No answer.

I pushed the door. It wasn't locked.

The room inside was empty. The blanket was folded — more neatly than
usual. No water jar. The small bundle that had hung on the wall was
gone too.

I looked around the room. A small room. Only then did it strike me
that I'd never been inside it before. Two months I'd known her, and I
hadn't even known where she slept.

A nail was driven into the wall. Something had once hung there.
Nothing now. Beside it, a small seashell sat on the windowsill. A
worthless thing, the kind you'd pick up scooping water. And yet she'd
left it behind.

I wondered, in that moment, why she'd left that shell behind. I
thought I might get to ask her later. I didn't know, then, whether
that chance would ever really come.

A sheet of paper lay on the desk.

I picked it up. My hand was shaking.

Pheugon.

I'm leaving the city today. Don't worry. I'm not running away.

I've kept thinking about it. Why this city still doesn't look like
it's turned. Why the Scales' number won't move. You said people
have changed. I saw it too. But the number stays the same.

I know my weight is low. But low and not-heavy are different
things. I was born in this city. My mother's overflowed weight,
this city's overflowed weight — I grew up inside it. As long as I'm
here, this city gets counted with me included in its weight.

If I leave, the city gets a little lighter. Maybe that's the last
bit it needs.

I don't resent the Scales. The Scales was right. I'm just doing
what I can do.

Trope.

I read the letter twice, three times.

I felt anger in that moment. But it wasn't anger at the Reckoning.
For the first time, that anger was aimed at myself.

For three years I had only learned how to hate someone else. I
learned, that day, for the first time, how to be angry at myself. I
hadn't wanted to learn it.

I looked again at one line of the letter. If I leave, the city gets
a little lighter. I knew that calculation was wrong. The Adjudicator
had said it plainly. Weight is recorded per person; once accumulated,
it doesn't decrease. Subtracting her share from the city's
accumulated weight wouldn't change the total at all. She believed
that if she left, the city would grow lighter. That belief itself was
a calculation the Scales had never once taught anyone.

I had a strange thought, in that moment. She knew the Scales as well
as I did. And still she'd made that calculation. Knowing and
believing are different things, she'd told me herself, once. At the
time I'd only understood it with my head. Now I was feeling the
weight of that sentence through a letter in my hand.

I folded the letter and pressed it against my chest. My hand was
still shaking.

I thought of her mother. All I had was what Trope had told me a few
times. The day her mother crossed the weight limit and was driven out
of the city. Trope had followed her all the way to the gate, she'd
said. Her mother never looked back.

I'd wondered, hearing that story, why her mother never looked back.
I thought I understood now. If you look back, your steps stop. If you
stop, you can't walk again. Trope was probably walking, right now, the
same road her mother had walked. Trying not to look back.

I walked out of the room holding the letter.

I understood now, why she'd been quiet these past days. She'd been
doing arithmetic. Same as me. Except I'd been calculating how to save
this city, and she'd been calculating how to erase herself. We'd
spent the same days on two entirely different sums.

I'd spent three years calculating anger. She'd spent five years
calculating her own weight. I'd only managed to break free of my
calculation once someone else caught me at it. Hers was still
uncaught by anyone.

I knocked on a neighbor's door. An old woman answered.

"…Have you seen Trope?"

"Early this morning," the woman said. "Saw her headed toward the west
gate with a bundle. Why, what's happened?"

I couldn't answer.

"…Ask the gatekeeper," the woman said. "He logs everyone who passes
through."

I ran.

Halfway there, I stopped in front of the Adjudicator's office. I
thought about knocking, then didn't. Instead I shouted through the
door crack.

"…Adjudicator! I'm going after Trope! Tomorrow—" I couldn't finish
the sentence. I didn't know myself, yet, what I'd do tomorrow.

Footsteps sounded inside. The Adjudicator opened the door and came
out.

"…What's happened."

"Trope went outside the city," I said. "She left a note."

The Adjudicator's face went taut for a moment. He said nothing, just
looked at me.

"…Tomorrow," he asked. "Will you be back in time?"

I couldn't answer that.

"…I don't know," I finally said. "But I have to go anyway."

The Adjudicator looked at me a long while. Then gave a short nod.

"…Go," he said. "I'll hold things here, somehow."

I felt gratitude and fear in equal measure at that. He hadn't told me
what exactly he meant to hold. I didn't ask.

I ran again.

Passing the herald office lodgings, I stopped for a moment. There was
a light in the window. He would still be in there, preparing
something. Precisely. Steady as ever.

I hesitated a moment, in that instant, over whether to tell him.
That I might not be back tomorrow. That if I didn't come back, he
would be the one who read. In the end I didn't knock on his door.
Saying it out loud would have meant admitting, to myself, that I
might not come back. I didn't want to give that possibility a voice.

I walked on. Toward the gate.

The gatekeeper checked the log.

"…Someone left before dawn. Name was—" He traced the record with his
finger. "Trope."

"…Which way did she go?"

"West," he said. "There's nothing out that way. Why'd she leave?"

"…Was she alone?" I asked.

"Alone," he said. "Not fast, not slow. Just walked at a normal pace.
Didn't seem like someone in a hurry, either."

Hearing that only frightened me more. If she'd been walking fast, that
would have been better. A normal pace meant she'd already made up her
mind, completely.

I didn't answer his question. I looked out through the gate.

The plain stretched out into the distance. Not a single person in
sight. The sun was already high overhead.

In that moment I realized for the first time how vast the world
outside this city was. For two months I had lived entirely inside
these walls. I'd always thought of the space outside them only as the
direction I'd come from, the direction I would someday leave toward.
Now, in that direction, one person was walking alone.

I braced a hand against the gate post and caught my breath. I could
hear my own heartbeat.

I understood something strange, in that moment. I had been swept
into this city. I had never entered it on my own feet. And now I was
about to walk out of it on my own feet. For the first time. And not
to save this city — to bring back one of its children. I thought that
order was strange. A person had come before the city.

One day left. I counted, on my fingers, how many hours of that day
I'd already let slip by. Five. Six. I stopped counting partway.

For the first time, counting was no use at all.

The gatekeeper caught my arm.

"…You heading out too?" he asked. "Tomorrow's the fortieth day. You
know that, right?"

"…I know," I said. "That's why I have to go."

He looked at my face a moment, then asked nothing more. Instead he
brought out a lantern and handed it to me.

"…Light this once the sun sets," he said. "You can't see a thing on
this road at night."

I took the lantern. My hand was still shaking.

"…Thank you," I said.

"Thanks nothing," he said. "Hurry back. I don't know if you're the
one who's supposed to be standing here tomorrow or not, but better
to have someone than not, I'd think."

I didn't fully understand what he meant by that. But it pushed me
forward all the same, strangely.

I realized, in that moment, that I hadn't said anything to either
the Adjudicator or the substitute herald about who would stand in the
square tomorrow. Nothing had been decided. I knew that, and I was
still walking out through the gate. A hundred and twenty thousand
lives mattered less to me, in that moment, than one person named
Trope. I knew that was a strange order of priority. I didn't want to
set it right.

I stepped through the gate.

The sun was sinking to the west. The same direction she had walked.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. Going to Fetch Her

_Previously_ — Trope left a note behind and walked out the gate at
dawn, believing her own weight made the city heavier. Pheugon held
that letter in his hand and, with a single day left, set out
through the gate after her.

Walking out through the gate, I didn't fully know what I was doing.

I had spent three years trying to hate this city. And now I was
walking out its gate to find one of its children. I hadn't known,
until that moment, that both those sentences could come from the same
mouth.

As I ran, I kept turning that strange fact over. If someone had told
me, forty days ago, that I would one day risk my life running to save
a child of the Reckoning, I'd have laughed in his face. And yet here
I was, running. No one had told me to. Not the Scales, not the
Adjudicator. I just ran.

The plain was vast. There was only one road. A dirt track running
straight west. I half ran, half walked along it.

The sun stood high. Dust rose beneath my feet. Every time my breath
grew ragged, I stopped and looked around. Not a single figure in
sight.

As I ran, I kept saying her name inside myself. Trope. Trope. I
didn't know what the name meant. That was the rule of this world. No
one ever explains what a name means. I only knew that the more I said
it, the heavier that name grew inside me.

Some way along, I realized this was the second time I'd walked this
road. Forty days before, I had walked it from the opposite direction,
toward this city. Back then I hadn't thought of it as walking — I'd
thought of it as being swept along. Now I was clearly walking. On my
own feet.

About an hour in, I saw a low pile of stones by the roadside.

At first I took it for just a heap of stones. Coming closer, I saw
names carved into them. Small letters, one at a time. Some looked
old; some looked fairly recent.

I guessed what it was. The names of people driven out of the city.
Who had built it, I didn't know. Family, maybe. Or the people
themselves, maybe.

I searched among the stones for one name. I didn't know Trope's
mother's name. I couldn't find it.

In that moment I thought I understood why Trope had taken this road.
She hadn't fled. She was walking the exact road her mother had
walked. Like someone trying to line up the order right.

I knelt for a moment in front of that pile of stones. I didn't know
why. It just felt like something I should do.

Looking at those stones, I thought of my own family. Our family had
never built a pile of stones like this. My brother had simply
vanished; my mother had simply left. No one had carved a name into
anything for either of them. For the first time, I couldn't say which
was better — carving a name, or simply forgetting. This city carved
the names of those it had driven out. My own family, we had simply
erased each other.

I stood up and brushed the dirt from my knees.

I ran again.

I ran, calling her name. No answer came. The plain was too wide, and
my voice too small.

The sun sank further. More than once I wondered if I'd taken a wrong
turn. Even with only one road, I kept glancing back to make sure I
was going the right way.

In that moment, for the first time, I felt what a world without the
Scales was like. Inside the city, anything could be confirmed with a
number. Weight, date, location. Outside the walls, none of that
existed. I could only walk, only call out, only hope.

I found her as the sun began to lower.

Far off, a little off the road, on a low hill, a single figure sat.
Not a water jar this time — just a bundle beside her, knees drawn up.

I called her name.

She turned. She wasn't startled. As if she'd known I would come.

"…Go back," I said. My breath was ragged.

"…Don't follow me," she said. Her voice had no strength in it. "This
is right. For me."

"…What's right about it."

"If I'm not there," she said, half burying her face in her knees, "at
least one thing becomes certain. If the city doesn't live, it won't
be my fault. If it lives, that won't be because of me either. I just
want out of this equation."

"…The Scales' number won't change whether you're there or not."

"I know," she said. "In my head. But Pheugon, I can't shake this
feeling that something would be different, just because I wasn't
there. I know it doesn't make sense. I know that."

I had no answer for that.

I sat down beside her. Neither of us said anything.

I tried, in that moment, to find something to persuade her with. The
Scales doesn't measure by family, the Adjudicator told me so, your
leaving won't change the city's weight at all. All true things. But
none of it came out of my mouth. I already knew those words would do
her no good. Trope hadn't come this far by numbers.

The sun sank further. The wind was cold.

"…You're cold," I finally said.

"…Yes," she said.

That was all. I didn't try to persuade her further. She didn't push
me away further either.

We sat like that for a long while. Wind, and somewhere far off, a
bird crying. Nothing else.

"…Are you scared?" I asked. A long while had passed.

"…Of what."

"Being out here alone."

She didn't answer, just hugged her knees tighter.

"…I was scared," she said, very quietly. "But strangely, when I saw
you running toward me over there, do you know what the first thing I
thought was?"

"…What."

"'It's over now,' she said. 'I thought, I don't have to be scared
anymore. That's what scared me most. That I could be reassured that
easily."

I had no answer for that either.

After a long while, I held out my hand.

She looked at it a long time. Didn't take it. Then, very slowly, laid
her own hand on top of it.

We walked toward the gate together after that.

It was a long road. Until the sun sank completely below the horizon,
neither of us said a word. She didn't pull her hand away. I didn't
speak again either.

In that silence I kept asking myself, over and over, why I was
there. I had spent three years trying to hate this city. And now I
was walking, hand in hand, leading one of its children back — a
child who had tried, of her own will, to leave it. I never found the
answer. I still don't fully have it, even now.

Walking, I suddenly thought of the man who never gets angry. What
would he say, if he could see me now. I imagined it for a moment,
then stopped. He wasn't here now. In his absence, I felt like I was,
for the first time, alone doing something he might have done. That
struck me as strange.

I wondered, in that moment, for the first time, whether he had ever
once walked out like this to fetch someone. I didn't know the
answer. I didn't know, then, that I would come to ask him one day.

Far, far ahead, a small light appeared.

At first I took it for a star. Then the light grew. Not just one —
many. In the dark, they gathered one by one into a single mass.

The Reckoning.

I stopped walking in that moment. For two months I had lived only
inside that city. Seen from within, that light had just been the
lamps that came on each evening. One at a time, familiar. Seen from
outside, it was different. From this distance, all those lights
became one. Not scattered people — one city.

I looked at that light as if seeing it for the first time. It really
was the first time. I had lived two months in that city and had never
once seen it from outside.

From inside, each light had been its own. The bakery's light, the
Scales office's light, someone's window, lit. I had counted those
lights one by one, living among them. From outside, none of that was
visible. All I could see was one thing. The whole city gathered into
a single light, floating in the dark.

A strange thought came to me in that moment. Did the Scales see the
city this way too. Not as individual people, but as one mass. But I
soon knew that thought was wrong. The Scales has never seen it from
this distance. The Scales only sees from inside, only one by one.
Seeing a city as one whole, from this distance — only a person could
do that.

Trope stopped walking too and looked at the light.

"…It's pretty," she said, very quietly.

I had no answer to that. I'd never once thought of that city as
pretty. And yet, in that moment, I was agreeing with her for the
first time.

We started walking again. The light grew larger.

It was already the middle of the night when we reached the gate.

The gatekeeper came to meet us with a lantern. He asked nothing. He
just opened the gate for us.

As we stepped back through the gate, Trope spoke for the first time.

"…Why did you come to fetch me."

I hadn't prepared an answer.

"…I don't know."

"You came without knowing?"

"…I felt like I had to go fetch you. So I went."

She neither smiled nor cried at that. She just lowered her head a
little. Then walked on again without another word. I walked beside
her, and that was the whole of what we did that night.

I was afraid of that silence. I couldn't tell if it was angry
silence or simply exhausted silence. I know now. It was both. And
there was a third thing in it too. Shame. She had, that day, been
caught — for the first time — wanting to live.

We walked without speaking all the way to her door. She stopped in
front of it.

"…Go in," I said. "Get some rest."

She nodded. Before opening the door, she turned to look at me once.

"…It's tomorrow," she said.

I knew what that meant. The fortieth day. The square. Two heralds.

"…Yeah," I said.

"…Pheugon." She called again. "I learned something to be ashamed of
today."

"…What."

"I thought choosing to die was the brave thing," she said. "But when
you came all that way to that hill, I was happy. Because I wanted to
live. I was ashamed of that."

I had no idea what to say to that.

"…Nothing to be ashamed of," I finally managed.

"I don't know," she said. "Not yet, anyway."

She said no more and went inside. The door closed quietly.

I didn't go back to the shed that night. I just sat on the steps
outside her door.

I still couldn't explain, that night, why I had gone to fetch her. I
still hadn't decided whether to save this city or not. And yet I
hadn't hesitated for even a moment about saving one person named
Trope. That those two were the same question — I still hadn't
managed to connect that, even sitting on those steps.

I know now. That night, for the first time, I did something without
calculating. For three years, and even after coming to this city, I
had calculated everything. Weight, cases, dates, reasons. But running
out through that gate, I hadn't calculated anything at all. I had
simply run.

I didn't know, that night, what that meant. I thought I'd understand
by tomorrow. I didn't fully understand tomorrow, either. Only now,
little by little, do I understand it.

Far off, the square's board glowed in the dark. I didn't look at the
number written on it. I didn't need to.

Tomorrow it would be zero.

I sat on those steps, unmoving, deep into the night. My lantern had
gone out long before. In the dark, I looked down at my hand again.
It had trembled that morning. It wasn't trembling now.

I didn't know, at the time, what that difference meant. I know now.
This morning's trembling was the trembling of a man whose calculation
wasn't finished. This hand, not trembling now, wasn't because I'd
reached an answer. I still didn't know the answer. Only, that night,
I stopped doubting one thing at least. That I was someone who could
go and fetch another person. For three years I hadn't known whether I
was that kind of person or not.

As dawn drew near, I rose from where I sat. My body was stiff. I
walked a few steps toward the square. Far off, the board's number was
faintly visible. Still one.

A few hours from now, two people would stand in that spot. One who
didn't know this city, and one who could no longer say he didn't.
What face I would wear, standing there — I still couldn't picture it.

I walked back to Trope's door. No sound came from behind it. I sat
back down in front of that door, waiting for the sun to rise.

I didn't know, then, what it meant to go and fetch someone. I know a
little now. Going to fetch someone means not asking first whether
that person is right or wrong. It just means going. That night, for
the first time, I did that. Whether I could do the same thing again
for someone else, the next day, I still didn't know.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Forty-Day Clause, Part Four

The Hill East of the City

Chapter 37. The Last Day

_Previously_ — Trope, believing the whole city would die for her weight alone, walked out through the gate alone. Pheugon went out to find her. On the way back, the two of them saw the Reckoning's lights together, for the first time, from a distance.

The fortieth day broke exactly like every other day.

I thought that was strange. The world should have looked different somehow. The sky should have hung low. The birds should have gone quiet. But the sun rose in the same place it had risen yesterday, and the line at the well was the same line as yesterday. The baker opened her shop at the same hour as yesterday.

That morning I found it unbearably unfair. A city was ending, and the sky didn't know it. It wasn't only the sky. The Scales didn't know it either. Today it was weighing people the same way it had weighed them every day before.

Trope hadn't said a word since we'd reached the gate.

The night before, walking her back the whole way, I hadn't known what to say. I knew why she'd tried to leave. She believed her own weight was pulling the city down. Her mother had been pushed out that way, so she believed it was only right that she go the same way.

I didn't tell her she was wrong. I knew it would do no good. Instead I took her hand and walked. It was a long way. The city's lights had started as points and slowly grown larger.

She didn't pull away.

When we passed back through the gate, Trope spoke for the first time.

"…Why did you come for me."

I hadn't prepared an answer.

"…I don't know."

"You came without knowing?"

"…It felt like I had to go get you."

She didn't laugh at that, and she didn't cry either. She just lowered her head a little. Then she walked on again without another word. I walked beside her, and that was the whole of what we did that night.

I was afraid of that silence. I couldn't tell whether it was an angry silence or just a tired one. Now I know. It was both. And there was a third thing in it too. Shame. That night, for the first time, she had been caught wanting to live.

In the morning she shared her food with me. We sat on the steps outside the room where she was staying and ate. Nobody spoke. It felt like the whole city was holding its breath.

"…It's today." Trope said.

"…Yes."

"I'm not scared."

I didn't believe her. Her hand, around the bowl, was shaking slightly.

"…Liar."

"…Yes. I'm lying."

She set the bowl down.

"It's not that I'm afraid of the city falling. I thought it falling was only right, so that part doesn't scare me. What scares me." She stopped, then went on. "What scares me is what happens if it doesn't fall."

I didn't fully understand what she meant, not then.

Now I know. She wasn't afraid of judgment. She was afraid of mercy. Because if mercy came, there would be no way left to confirm whether she really deserved it.

Judgment has rules. Cross the weight and you're pushed out. That can be predicted, and so it can be borne. Mercy has no rules. Trope would never be able to know why mercy came to her. I thought, at the time, that she would have found it easier if she'd simply crossed the weight. I didn't yet know how wrong that thought was.

We sat on those steps a long time after the bowls were empty. The sun climbed a little higher. From the direction of the square, footsteps began to gather.

"…I should go." I said.

Trope nodded. As she stood, she added, very quietly.

"…Whatever happens, thank you for coming to get me."

I had no answer for that.

On the way to the square, I walked this city slowly for the first time.

I'd lived here over a month, and I'd never once looked closely at these streets. Today I did. There was a sign on the bakery that hadn't been there yesterday — anyone who couldn't pay their tab would simply be given the bread. At the edge of the market, the merchant who'd once been caught cheating his scales was recalibrating one with his own hands. A child passing by picked up the walking stick an elderly neighbor had dropped and carried it to him.

Small things. Things too small for the Scales to register at all.

I counted those small things. I couldn't help it. The habit that had counted one hundred twelve cases surfaced here too. Eight, nine, ten. Ten things in ten steps. I didn't dare work out what that came to across the whole city.

The Scales weighed none of the ten. What isn't stolen, what isn't struck, what isn't lied — none of it carries weight. To the Scales, those were exactly as if nothing had happened at all.

That was the first time I felt the Scales' arithmetic was missing something. I didn't know, then, how to put that feeling into words.

The square was already full of people.

I had never before seen the people of the Reckoning gathered like this in one place. On ordinary days, even the lines were only a few deep at the terminals. Today the whole square was packed. People holding children. People leaning on canes. People saying nothing, just staring at the sky. Two old men stood holding each other's hands. One had his face pressed against the other's shoulder.

On the board raised in the center of the square, a number hung in the air.

0.

I looked at that number. I had been counting down from thirty-nine. The first number I'd seen, the day I was washed up in this city, was thirty-nine, and every day since I'd watched it drop by one. Today there was nowhere left for it to fall.

I did a strange arithmetic standing in front of that number. If I'd seen it once a day for thirty-nine days, I had seen it thirty-nine times. Thirty-eight of those times I'd watched it the way you watch a stranger's city. Only the last time had I watched it as my own.

That one time undid all the other thirty-eight.

Familiar faces showed among the crowd. The baker. The market vendor who always drew the longest line at the terminal. The girl next door who used to fetch water with Trope. I didn't know their names. Three months ago that wouldn't have mattered. Now it did.

The baker recognized me and came over. There was still flour on her hands.

"…So you're the one. The boy from the well."

"…Yes."

"You're the one reading it today?"

I couldn't answer. She didn't wait for one — she took my hand. It was a big, rough hand.

"I don't know where to put my thanks." she said. "For coming. For being here."

I couldn't close my hand around hers. She had no idea what I was about to do. She only knew I was the one person who could save this city — not that I had spent all this time wishing it would die.

I remember that hand for a long time. Holding it, I realized for the first time that I was a person with two faces. While this city saw me as a savior, I still thought of myself as a judge.

The Adjudicator was standing on the platform.

Beside him stood an unfamiliar face — a year or two older than me, with a straight, trained posture, dressed in the herald's uniform. The substitute assigned after I'd run.

I had never seen him before. But I could read him at a glance. That stance, that gaze. I'd seen it every day at the training grounds. A face that had learned not to get angry. A face that had learned not to stop.

A few years earlier, that would have been me standing there — that face, that posture. Looking at him, I knew for the first time how far I had come from that place.

He looked at me. His expression didn't move.

"…You're Pheugon."

"…Yes."

"I was assigned two months ago. You never came."

There was no resentment in it. It was a voice simply stating a fact. That made it harder.

"…I'm sorry."

"No apology is needed." he said. "I only have to do my job. We don't write feelings into the record."

I felt like I'd heard that somewhere before. It was nearly word for word what the supervisor used to tell me.

"…How much have you read."

"This one case." he said. "I was told it's the largest there's ever been."

In that moment I found him frightening. Not because he was a bad person. Because he was me. Me, forty days ago. A weightless face, a voice that never stopped, someone ready to read this city.

The Adjudicator drew me aside.

"You took your time getting here."

"…Yes."

"And you found Trope."

"…Yes."

The Adjudicator looked out over the square for a moment. His eyes moved between the people, the number, one another, and the platform. Every face there knew what it was waiting for.

"You know something that boy doesn't." The Adjudicator moved his chin slightly toward the substitute herald. "That the clause exists. What it requires. He's only a reader. He does what he was trained to do."

"…So."

"If you don't step forward, he'll read it. And if he reads it, he becomes the confirmer." The Adjudicator looked at me. "He doesn't know this city. He arrived a month ago. How would he know whether this city has turned or not. Weight can't show him that."

I didn't answer.

"The decision is yours." The Adjudicator said. "I'm not telling you to do it, and I'm not telling you not to. For forty years I've never once invoked this clause. I may find out why today."

His voice was even. His hands weren't. Behind his back, they kept crumpling the edge of the papers.

"One thing I'll say." He lowered his voice. "If you read it, you have to read all of it. Not a summary — the full text. From the first line to the last, Article 4 Clause 11 and all, without missing a line. Leave one line out and the clause itself doesn't activate. That's how it's written."

"…Why."

"I don't know." The Adjudicator said. "Forty years, and I never worked it out. Whoever wrote that clause must have had a reason for setting it up that way. Maybe—" He stopped, then went on. "Maybe it means you can only recognize a city's turning once you know its sin in full. That's only my guess."

I felt the weight of the sentence document in my hand. It was only a few sheets of paper, but my wrist ached.

I heard something in the Adjudicator's voice that day. I didn't know what it was, then. Now I know. It was envy. He had never once had the chance to stand in this place. Forty years living in this city, knowing only that the clause existed, with no one to use it. I had that chance. He hadn't.

I looked at Trope.

She stood at the edge of the square. Alone, among the crowd, her two hands clasped in front of her. When our eyes met, she gave the smallest nod. That was all.

I didn't know what she was waiting for. Whether she was waiting for the city to fall, or waiting for it to live. Her face looked ready for either.

Someone on the platform rang a bell. The square went quiet. A child began to cry and was quickly hushed by its mother's hand. After that, there was truly no sound at all.

The Adjudicator looked at me. Then at the substitute herald. Then at me again.

"Who reads it."

I looked at the substitute herald. He already had the sentence document in his hands. His hands weren't shaking. I used to look exactly like that. His fingers, holding the paper, were spaced at precisely even intervals. That's what training teaches you.

I want to say, even now, that up to that moment I still hadn't been sure what I would do. But that would be a lie. I already knew. It was only my feet that hadn't believed it yet.

I took one step forward.

"…I'll read it."

The square looked at me. It felt like tens of thousands of eyes turning toward me all at once. Trope looked at me. Her clasped hands stiffened slightly.

The substitute herald looked at me, then held out the papers without a word. His face showed no resentment, no relief. As he handed the papers over, he added one thing.

"…Read it well."

It was something trainees said to each other. Before an exam, before a first proclamation — something they said to one another. I'd heard those words dozens of times in three years. I never expected to hear them like this.

"…Yes."

I took the papers. My fingertips felt a familiar weight. The weight of one hundred twelve readings, and now, for the hundred thirteenth time, a different weight. I could feel the difference in my hands.

Trope was still watching me. I sent her no signal. There was no signal to send. Even I didn't fully know, in that moment, what I was about to do.

I still remember, in my fingertips, the moment I took that paper. People sometimes ask me what I decided in that moment. I can't answer. There was nothing that deserved to be called a decision. My feet simply moved forward, and my hand took the paper. The decision came after, while I was reading.

The Adjudicator nodded, very quietly.

"Then." he said. "Let's begin."

Every eye in the square turned toward me. The zero on the board glowed behind my back. A wind passed once, and the edge of the paper stirred slightly. I unfolded it.

I remember that wind. It was very mild. A wind with nothing to do with the smell of water I'd breathed in the conduit forty days before. And yet, strangely, in that instant I thought of that darkness — the three days I'd been shut in, the night I first spoke aloud. Only then did I understand that that night led straight to this square.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. The Proclamation

_Previously_ — The whole city gathered in the square. The substitute herald was already standing there, but Pheugon stepped forward in his place. The Adjudicator nodded. Pheugon unfolded the paper.

The first thing you learn when you learn to read is not your voice. It's not stopping.

I had carved this into my body for three years. Not stopping at a name. Not stopping at a charge. Not stopping in front of someone crying. That day in the square, I had no idea where that training was about to take me.

I still think about this sometimes. What would have happened if I'd been someone who knew how to stop. If I had stopped, I would have stopped in front of that sentence. Because I didn't stop, I made it all the way to that sentence.

The hand that unfolded the paper felt strangely light. Forty days before, the first time I'd seen this paper on my desk, my fingertips had trembled. Now they didn't. The trained hand had come back. I was glad of that, and afraid of it, at once.

At the training grounds, the supervisor used to say: "A herald's hand should feel only the weight of the paper. It must not feel the weight of the person written inside it." I'd believed that for a long time. That day in the square, I learned for the first time that both weights could be felt in the same hand at once.

"The Reckoning." I began.

My voice carried out across the square. It wasn't a loud voice. A trained voice never is. Quiet, but it carries far.

"This settlement, under Article 3, has been found to exceed its accumulated weight limit. The date of judgment was—"

I read the date. It was the same day, forty-one days ago, that I had first held this sentence document in my hands. That day I had laughed, silently, in the middle of a hallway. I never imagined that laugh would lead to this square.

The square was silent. Silent, but the silence was full of held breath. That's the sound a hundred twenty thousand people make when they hold their breath together. For the first time, I felt that number as people.

The people far in the back couldn't have heard my voice well. And yet no one moved. I found that strange. If you couldn't hear, you'd think you'd push forward. But everyone stayed exactly where they stood, as if watching the shape of my mouth was enough, without needing to hear the sound. Maybe they already knew what this sentence meant. Forty days had given them plenty of time to guess.

I learned to count when I was twelve, so I could handle numbers. That day, for the first time, a number handled me.

I read the grounds.

They were the items the Scales had produced. Lies, theft, neglect, cruelty. Each one converted into a number. It even listed when the city's accumulated total had crossed the limit. Three years and six months earlier.

At that date, my voice nearly wavered. Three years and six months ago wasn't far from the time my own family had come apart.

I wanted to believe that was a coincidence. Even now, I can't fully believe it. The Scales never connected those two events. It had no reason to. The one who connected them was me.

My voice didn't waver. Three years of training saved me, in that moment. Or so I thought at the time.

The items went on and on. I read every one of them, without dropping a single line. The Scales wrote in numbers only, but I knew there was a person behind each number. Behind a line reading "neglect item, accumulated total twenty-three thousand," there was probably a child. Days without food, greetings no one ever offered. The Scales reduced all of that to a single number. I was the one who turned that number back into a voice.

Beside me, the substitute herald stood with the document in his hand, watching me. Something passed across his eyes. I understood only later what it was. It wasn't relief. It was awe. He wasn't looking at me and thinking he was lucky not to be the one standing there — he was looking at himself, and seeing that he hadn't been trained enough to stand where I stood.

I would go on to remember his face for a long time. Forty days earlier, I would have stood in that place with that same face. Unshaken, uninvolved. Whether losing that face was fortunate or a loss, I still haven't decided.

At last I reached the disposition.

"Regarding this settlement: following a grace period of forty days from the date of judgment, the sentence of demolition shall be executed."

At that instant, a sound rose from the square. Weeping. Not from one person, but from many at once. The sound of breath, held back too long, breaking all at once. A woman holding a child pressed the child's head to her chest. Two old men gripped each other harder.

I didn't stop.

I looked at Trope. She wasn't crying. Her face was different from everyone else's. It was a face that had already expected this sentence. From forty days before — or perhaps from long before that.

Watching that face, I felt a strange certainty settle in me. A certainty that Trope was the least surprised person in that whole city to hear this sentence. She had already believed, for a long time, that she deserved exactly this.

Close beside me, the baker had both hands pressed over her face. The market merchant sank to the ground, still clutching the scale-weight he'd calibrated himself. Someone let out a low groan, calling out not a person's name but the city's. "Reckoning." Someone said it that way — as if the city were a person, as if to comfort it.

I heard those sounds and did not let them break my voice. That's what the supervisor had taught — the discipline of not listening. But I was listening. Listening while pretending not to — that day, for the first time, I truly put that skill to use.

Maybe I should have stopped there. Most people do stop there. Once the disposition is read, the proclamation is considered finished. I had heard, back at the training grounds, that the clauses attached below were often left unread by custom.

I didn't stop.

Because I had learned how not to stop. How not to stop at a name, a charge, a person crying. There was no exception to that discipline. As long as there were letters left on the page, I read them. That was who three years had made me.

I still return to this moment sometimes. If I had been someone less trained — if I hadn't been, as the supervisor put it, someone who "doesn't stop" — I would have folded the paper right there. The city would simply have fallen. The clause would have gone back to being a dead letter.

The very thing that had made me hate this city was, in the end, what carried me to this place. And now the same discipline that had once made me run — that same discipline was keeping me from stopping.

I thought of Had, at the training grounds. The boy who stopped three times at his own name. "Why don't you stop?" Had had asked me that once, and in that instant, standing in the middle of the square, his question came back to me. I hadn't been able to answer then, and I couldn't now. Only this time, I kept reading without an answer.

Had stopped at a name and never became a herald. I never stopped at anything, and became one. In that square that day, I suddenly envied him. At least he'd known how to stop.

I turned the page. On the next line, in small type.

Article 4, Clause 11.

I had seen those letters once before, forty days earlier. At my desk, alone, in an empty room at night, tracing them with my fingertip. That time I had been alone. Now I stood before a hundred twenty thousand people.
