That night, I'd read this sentence three times. I never asked who was meant by "confirmed" — by whom. I'd assumed the Scales would confirm it. I should have already known, even then. The Scales cannot measure direction. If the Scales couldn't confirm it, confirmation had to belong to someone else. That night, the answer had been right in front of me, and I hadn't seen it. Or rather — I had seen it, and chosen not to. My plan to run was already in place.

Forty days lie between that night and who I am now. In those forty days I was trapped in the conduit, washed up in this city, and came to know its people's faces one by one. Without those forty days, I couldn't have read this sentence now. I could have read it — but I would have read it differently.

My voice came out a beat late. It was so brief that no one in the square could have noticed. But I felt it. Something inside me asked. Are you really going to read it.

I didn't answer. There was no time to answer. The paper was in my hand, the letters were in front of my eyes. Reading was the only thing I knew how to do.

"Article 4, Clause 11." I said. "Should this settlement be confirmed to have turned within forty days, the present sentence shall be void."

Reading that sentence took less than three seconds. But I can still say, even now, that those three seconds felt longer than forty days.

The square stirred. A low murmur spread among the weeping. Many people were hearing of this clause for the first time. It made sense — no adult besides the Adjudicator had known it existed.

The Adjudicator was watching me from the platform. I read something in his expression. It wasn't surprise. He'd already known I knew about this clause. What surprised him was something else — the fact that I had actually spoken it aloud.

I looked over at the Scales. A terminal stood in one corner of the square. Nothing on the screen had changed. The Scales hadn't logged this reading as anything special. To the Scales, it was simply one sentence turned into sound.

The substitute herald glanced my way. The same clause was surely printed in the document he held too. He would never have to read it aloud himself now — I had stepped forward first. I couldn't tell, in that moment, whether he was relieved or disappointed. Maybe neither. He was simply watching, as he'd been trained to, what was happening right in front of him, exactly as it was.

I didn't understand that moment. I felt something enormous had happened, and yet nowhere in the world was there any sign to prove it. No light came on. No sound rang. Not one figure on the Scales had changed.

I think I'd been expecting something strange. The sky splitting open. The ground shaking. Nothing happened. The most important sentence in the world had just become sound, and the world was still turning exactly the way it had turned forty days ago. I didn't know then that this frightened me. Now I know. A great thing happening with no sign at all — that is the most frightening thing in this world.

Now I know. The confirmation the clause required was never the Scales' work. It was a person's work. And the condition for becoming that person was exactly one thing — to read this sentence aloud, to the end. I had just met that condition.

I met it without knowing I was meeting it. That made it more frightening. I felt like someone who'd pulled a trigger without knowing what he'd fired.

Somewhere in the square, a low voice asked, "What does that mean." Someone beside them answered, "It means all it has to do is turn." Another voice overlapped. "Who decides that." No one had an answer. Even I hadn't known it, myself, until a moment before.

One line still remained on the paper. In very small type, just below the clause.

"Confirmation is established by the herald's declaration."

I read that line and lifted my eyes from the page.

The square was watching me. A hundred twenty thousand eyes. The Adjudicator. The substitute herald. Trope.

Trope's face had changed. It was no longer the resigned face of a moment before. She understood now what this line meant. Not as fully as I did — but she understood.

I opened my mouth. Nothing came out.

My throat was dry. In forty days I had never once let myself imagine this moment. More precisely, I had worked hard not to imagine it. When I'd run, the goal had been to make sure this moment never came at all. Now that the goal had failed, I had never been trained for what came next. Training had only ever taught me how to read the Scales' verdict. No one had ever taught me how to overturn one.

In three years I read one hundred twelve cases without stopping once. On the hundred thirteenth, I stopped for the first time. It was after I had finished the clause. All that remained was one sentence. That sentence would not come.

The Adjudicator took one step closer from the platform. He said nothing. He simply stood nearer. I couldn't tell whether that was urging me on or standing with me.

Trope didn't move. Her hands still clasped in front of her, she was looking straight at me. Something was in her eyes, but I couldn't tell whether it was fear or hope. Maybe it was both. Even someone who believes mercy will never come for herself can still wish for it to come to someone else.

The wind rose again. The edge of the paper stirred. I didn't fold it. I couldn't fold it yet. A sentence was forming inside me. It hadn't become sound yet.

How that sentence became sound, I set down in the next chapter. Here I will say only this — that in that moment, I did not know what I was about to say. To say I knew would be a lie. My body knew first. My mind followed a long while after.

The square was still waiting for me. A hundred twenty thousand held breaths, all stopped, aimed at one sentence.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. The Confirmation

_Previously_ — Pheugon read the sentence document from the first line to the last, all the way to Article 4, Clause 11, aloud. The moment he read it, he became the confirmer. The clause required one sentence, spoken by him. The square watched him and waited.

What the clause required was not a long explanation.

It was one sentence. Six words. "This city has turned." That was all. No lengthy testimony required, no grounds to line up. The whole thing hung on that one sentence.

Even now, I find that simplicity strange. The Scales spends dozens of figures just to assess a single item. Neglect, cruelty, theft, lies — each one carried out to the decimal point. And yet saving an entire city took only six words. Weight required so much language. Turning required so little.

I felt that asymmetry was unfair. I still feel it, if I'm honest. How can the system for weighing sin be so meticulous, while the system for weighing forgiveness is allowed to be so bare? But thinking it over again, it wasn't bareness at all. Weight accumulates, so it needs many items. Turning is a direction, so it only needs confirming once. I only came to guess at that difference standing in that square.

I had known those six words. Since forty days before.

They weren't printed on the paper. The clause set down no form for the confirmation. The Adjudicator had told me as much, once. "There's no fixed sentence. But by custom, this is what people say. 'This city has turned.'" In forty years no one had ever used that custom. It had only ever been passed down by word of mouth.

I had turned that sentence over on my tongue many times. In the conduit, on the way to the city, in the dark, in places where no one could hear. Back then I thought it was the one thing I would never say. I had no intention of admitting this city had turned. The moment I admitted this city had turned, the very reason I'd run would fall apart.

I had memorized that sentence out of hatred for it. I didn't know then that hatred is what makes you memorize something best.

In the conduit, on the third day, I had spoken it aloud for the first time. Alone in the dark, water up to my knees. "This city has turned." My voice, that time, had been a mockery. I said it to prove how absurd it was. That city, turned? I had laughed, then.

What lay between that laugh and the silence in the square, I don't know exactly. Only that forty days had passed. In those forty days I had come to know the faces of the people in that city, one by one. And I learned, for the first time, that mockery gets harder once you know a face.

The square was still silent.

I looked at Trope. She hadn't moved. Neither had the Adjudicator. The substitute herald still stood there, holding the papers. Everyone was waiting for me, and yet no one urged me on.

I still can't describe that silence precisely, even now. It didn't feel like pressure. If it had been pressure, it would have been easier. You can resist pressure. That silence wasn't pressure. It was a blank space. A space that would receive whatever I filled it with.

I thought of this city. Its people, living by checking their own weight before the Scales, living carefully so as never to spill over. And I thought of the person from this city who had torn our home apart. Both thoughts were in me at once, neither making room for the other.

I thought of the baker's hand. The hand, still floured, that had held mine. "For coming. For being here." The face that had said those words had no idea what I was about to do.

I thought of one person from the Reckoning. I can no longer remember that person's face clearly. But I still see clearly the back of him, walking out our door. For three years I had believed that back was this city's face.

Between those two faces, the whole city lay. I didn't know which to choose. I still don't.

I looked at the Adjudicator. There was no urging in his face. He was a man who had waited forty years for this place. And yet now that the moment had arrived, he did nothing on my behalf. He didn't even encourage me. He only watched. I learned later that this was his kindness. This sentence was not one anyone else could choose for me.

I looked at the substitute herald. There was no expectation in his face at all. He wasn't from this city, and he understood this sentence far less than I did. And yet he stood there without stirring. His was the face of someone watching another person's forty days as though they were his own.

The wind passed over the square once more. Someone's hem stirred. A child, unable to hold it in, let out a small cough. I learned, then, how loud that sound could feel.

I looked down at the paper again. The last line of the clause. "Confirmation is established by the herald's declaration."

That sentence gave me no instructions at all. When to say it, on what grounds to judge, whether I needed certainty or could do without it — none of it was written down. Whoever had written this clause had left the whole judgment to me and vanished.

In that moment, for the first time, I wondered who had written this clause. Was it the same people who built the Scales? Or was it someone else entirely? The Adjudicator didn't know, and neither did I. I still don't. But I felt certain that whoever it was had foreseen this exact moment. That the person who knew a city's sins best, the person with every reason to hate it most, would end up standing in this place.

I thought that design was cruel. And at the same time I thought there could have been no other way. If this judgment had been given to someone who loved this city, it would have been doubted from the start. If it had been given to someone who didn't know this city at all, it would have meant nothing. Whoever wrote this clause seemed to have decided to trust exactly one kind of person. Someone who hated, and yet had kept watching to the very end.

I looked out over the square. Of a hundred twenty thousand faces, I could recognize only a handful. The baker. The market merchant. The girl who used to fetch water with Trope. The Adjudicator. The substitute herald.

And Trope.

For a moment, very briefly, I thought of home. Of the last dinner our whole family had eaten together, before it came apart. I don't remember what we talked about that evening. I only remember that it was growing dark outside the window, and that someone had laughed. That laugh was never heard in our house again.

I tried to tell myself there was no connection between that evening and this square. One was a private matter, and the other belonged to an entire city. But in that moment, inside me, the two sat at exactly the same weight. The Scales would never have calculated it that way.

I had watched this city change, these forty days. It wasn't the same city I'd first arrived in. The one who used to steal, stopped stealing. The one who never spoke, began speaking to others. A child who'd lost her mother returned a stranger's dropped cane. A merchant who'd cheated his scales recalibrated one with his own hands.

None of those changes registered on the Scales. The weight hadn't gone down. The debt already accumulated remained exactly where it was. And yet I had seen it. I had seen what the Scales could not.

Trope's steps, on her way to check her own weight, had changed somewhere along the way. She used to leave with her shoulders sunk. Lately she left with her head still up. Not because the weight had fallen. Because the eyes that looked at the weight had changed.

The Adjudicator's hands, sorting through documents, had changed too. He used to push papers aside as he filed them. Lately he lingered over each sheet, as if reading the names written on it all over again.

The baker had begun giving away flour. The market merchant calibrated his own scales. None of these were items. They were things the Scales never wrote down. And yet I had seen them, every day, for forty days.

I want to say that in that moment I finally understood why the clause left confirmation to a person. But to be honest, I knew it without understanding it. Understanding and knowing are different things. I couldn't have laid out, in logic, that this city had turned. I simply knew.

Where that knowing came from, I still can't explain, even now. It can't be proven in numbers, the way the Scales proves things. It can't be backed by forty years of experience, the way the Adjudicator's could. I simply, standing there in the middle of that city, knew it.

I don't know how much time passed. To the people standing in the square it must have felt very long. It didn't, to me. To me, that time was the whole of the forty days passing at once.

I passed back through the darkness of the conduit. The feeling of water rising to my knees, the voice that had spoken aloud to no one. I had first told my own story in that darkness. Words that had begun where no one could hear were now about to end in front of a hundred twenty thousand people.

I passed back through the moment I'd opened my eyes at the well. "You're not from here, are you?" — Trope's face, asking. I had given her a false name, then. Now I stood in this city without a single lie left.

I thought of everything I'd kept hidden — that I was a herald, that I hated this city, the true reason I'd run. All of it had been piling up behind this one sentence. As if the entire forty days had been preparation for this single sentence.

I still haven't decided whether that moment was the end of calculation, or something outside calculation altogether. I had lived my life as someone who calculates. I counted one hundred twelve cases, counted dates, counted reasons. And yet before this sentence, there was nothing left to count.

My throat was dry. My fingertips, holding the paper, had gone slightly stiff. The square was still waiting for me. No one urging me on, and everyone waiting.

The stone beneath my feet was the same stone I had walked every day for forty days. But that day it felt unusually hard. The sun stood directly overhead. My shadow lay short, right beneath my feet.

My mouth opened.

I don't remember which came first — the voice, or the resolve. A single sentence simply went out into the square.

"This city has turned."

Only after I'd finished speaking did I realize I was the one who had said it.

I heard those six words strike the far wall of the square and return, very faintly. It was my own voice, and yet it didn't feel like mine. As though someone else, far away, were repeating it after me.

My voice wasn't loud. It was a trained voice. Quiet, but it carries far. It probably took less than a second for those six words to reach the far edge of the square. But it was enough — enough to overturn the city's whole forty days.

I won't write more about that moment. People ask me what I was thinking then, why I said it that way. I don't answer. It isn't that I have no words. Some sentences become lies the moment you attach a reason to them. I don't want to attach a reason to that one. Not even now.

For one instant, the square made no sound at all.

It took the whole square time to understand what I had just done. The Adjudicator's face moved first. He closed his eyes, and opened them again. It had probably been forty years since he'd last seen something like that.

Trope didn't move. Her lips had parted, just slightly.

The substitute herald slowly lowered the papers in his hand. For the first time, I saw something in his face give way. Not the trained face — just a person's face. He had never once been taught this sentence. Nowhere at the training grounds was there any lesson in reading it.

From the back of the square, sound began to spread like a low wave. Someone passed my words to the person beside them, and that person to the next. "It turned." Those two words took time to travel to the far edge of the square. It wasn't the speed of sound. It was the speed of belief spreading.

A low noise came from the direction of the Scales' terminal. People turned toward it. Something was appearing on the screen.

Trope stepped toward me. She started to say something, then stopped. Her lips closed again. I didn't ask what she'd meant to say. I had no confidence, then, to ask.

The baker pressed a hand to her mouth. The market merchant sat where he'd fallen, unmoving. The woman holding her child lifted the child's face up to look. "Did you hear that." she whispered to the child. The child nodded, not understanding a word of it.

That day, in that square, I didn't understand what I had just done. I knew that a hundred twenty thousand lives had just hung on six words from my mouth, and yet I couldn't feel the weight of it. That feeling came later. Much later, and in a very different way.

The Adjudicator walked toward me. His steps were slower than usual. He came to stand in front of me, and without a word, rested his hand on my shoulder. And then he said nothing at all.

I felt the weight of that hand. A weight the Scales could never measure.

He said nothing, and drew his hand away. I couldn't even guess what words he was holding back. I wasn't ready to hear the words of a man who had waited forty years.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. Void

_Previously_ — Pheugon said, "This city has turned." The clause activated. It took the square time to understand those six words. A low sound came from the Scales' terminal.

The screen at the terminal took three seconds to change.

I was counting those three seconds. The habit of counting numbers comes out even at times like this. One, two, three. After three, letters appeared on the screen.

Sentence of Demolition — Void.

That was all. No large lettering, no bell. The Scales displayed that sentence at exactly the same size as every other sentence.

Even now, I find that indifference strange. It was the moment a whole city lived. But to the Scales, it was only one more update. A single item had changed, nothing more. The Scales didn't rejoice, and it didn't grieve. That was only natural. But that day, for the first time, that very naturalness hurt.

Beneath the sentence, in small letters, the grounds were listed. "Confirmer: Herald Pheugon. Declaration received. Article 4, Clause 11 invoked." That was the first time I saw my own name appear on the Scales' screen. Through all one hundred twelve cases, my name had never once appeared there. I had always been the one who read — never the one who was recorded.

That day I entered the Scales' record for the first time. Not as an item, but as a cause. The Scales did not weigh me. It only wrote down what I had done, as grounds. The difference didn't seem large to me then. It feels large now.

A child standing beside the terminal reached out and touched the screen. Fingers landing, then lifting away. Not understanding what it meant, the child looked up at the nearest adult. The adult lifted the child into their arms.

The square began to make sound only after that.

At first it was one person's weeping. Then several. Then it turned into a sound that couldn't be told apart anymore — weeping mixed with laughter, names being called, people simply crying out.

The baker threw her arms around the person beside her. The market merchant sank to the ground and wept into both hands. The woman holding her child lifted the child up into the air. Not understanding a thing, the child laughed along with her.

Two old men held on to each other. One pressed his face into the other's shoulder and said, "We lived. We lived." The other only nodded, without answering.

I recall that sight often, even now. Not many people ever hear a hundred twenty thousand voices weep at once. It wasn't the sound of sorrow. And yet, though it wasn't sorrow, it wore exactly the shape of sorrow. I learned the difference between the two, that day.

Trope didn't cry.

I looked at her. She stood among the crowd, taking in every sound rising around her. But her face didn't match those sounds. It wasn't relief, and it wasn't joy. It was some unfamiliar expression.

I walked toward her. It wasn't easy, cutting through the crowd. Everyone tried to catch hold of me. Reaching for my hand, calling my name, trying to clap my shoulder. I didn't avoid those hands. But I didn't stop, either.

When I reached her, I didn't know what to say.

"…Now." I said. "You don't have to leave anymore."

She looked at me.

"…Yes."

"…The reason you wanted to leave is gone now."

She looked at me for a long time, then shook her head.

"…The reason isn't gone."

I didn't understand what she meant.

"…The reason is exactly the same. My mother was pushed out, and I still inherited that weight. It was the city that the Scales called void. Not me."

I didn't fully understand her words then. Now I do. She was keeping her own share of weight separate from the city's salvation. The city had been saved. But she herself still wasn't sure she had a place inside that salvation.

On the platform, the Adjudicator was announcing something.

I didn't hear all of it. Disposition procedures, future policy for the Scales, records of the clause's invocation. No one else was listening fully either. Everyone was too busy weeping, or laughing, or holding on to one another.

I looked for the substitute herald. He stood alone at the edge of the square. No one had caught hold of him. To the people of this city, he was still a stranger's face.

I went to him.

"…Thank you." I said.

He looked at me.

"…For what."

"…For coming. In my place."

"I only did the job I was assigned." he said. He paused, then went on. "But I learned something today."

"…What."

"That even if I'd come, I could never have read that sentence." He lifted the paper slightly. "Because I didn't know this city."

I had nothing to say to that. He held no resentment. And that very evenness made things heavier for me, not lighter.

I stood in the middle of the square and looked at the people.

People, saved. A hundred twenty thousand of them. I had spoken that sentence for them. When I said it, I hadn't known what it meant. Now, in the middle of these sounds, I was only just beginning to understand what I had done.

This city lives.

This city, the Reckoning, the city that had raised the person who took my family from me — lives.

When that thought first entered me, I didn't yet know what it meant. I received it simply as a fact. The feeling took time to attach itself.

I didn't weep among those people. I didn't laugh either. I just stood there. While everyone around me was mixing tears and laughter together, I couldn't manage either one.

Trope looked at me. The Adjudicator looked at me. The substitute herald looked at me.

What did they see in my face, that moment? I still wonder. I don't know what expression I was wearing. But I knew one thing myself. That something inside me hadn't even started yet.

The sun rose fully over the square. Laughter spread outward through the whole city. Listening to that sound, I began, very slowly, walking out of the square.

The streets were no different from the square.

Doors stood open in every house. Someone had set out a table in an alley and was sharing food. Children ran shrieking through the streets. An old man sat in a doorway, catching every passerby to say the same thing. "Did you hear? Void. Void." Each time he said it, his voice cracked a little more.

I walked through them. No one seemed to recognize me. That felt, strangely, like relief. In the square, everyone had known my name. Here, I was just someone passing through.

That afternoon, walking the streets, I had a strange thought. These people had no idea what I really was. Some knew me as a savior, some as just a stranger, a passing child. Neither knew the real me. Only two people in this city knew the real me. The Adjudicator, and Trope.

When I reached the well, I stopped.

That was the place I'd first landed in this city. The well with moss on its stone rim. Forty days before, I had opened my eyes there and heard the words, "You're not from here, are you?"

People were at the well today too. No one drawing water. Everyone sat perched on the stone rim, laughing. Someone had begun to sing. It was a song I didn't know the tune to, but the person beside them joined in, and the person beside them too.

I set a hand against the stone rim. I found the spot Trope had pointed to. The groove her mother's hand had supposedly left there. I ran my fingertip over it.

In that moment I thought of Trope. Was she, right now, singing along with this song? Or was she sitting somewhere alone, turning over again the words she'd said — "the reason isn't gone." I wanted to know which it was. But I had no strength left to go find out.

A while later, I left the well. The singing grew fainter behind me.

Walking away, I turned back once. The silhouettes of the people sitting on the well's stone rim wavered in the evening light. Someone clapped, someone else laughed. That scene felt to me like a painting. A painting I wasn't inside of.

I didn't know then why it looked like a painting. Now I know. I was the one who had made that scene. But the one who makes something usually doesn't get to live inside what they've made. The one who opens the door and the one who lives inside it are different people.

Passing the bakery, the baker caught me again.

This time she pressed bread into my hands. Still warm.

"…Eat." she said. "No charge today. No one's paying today."

I took the bread. Warmth spread across my palm.

"…Thank you."

"I'm the one who's thankful." she said, her eyes reddening again. "If it hadn't been for you, today would have been the last day this bakery was ever open."

I felt like I should have smiled at that. I tried to. The corners of my mouth wouldn't move.

I still wonder what my face looked like to her in that moment. What did she see in my expression? Sorrow? Being overwhelmed? She didn't ask anything after that — she just patted my shoulder once and turned away. Maybe she saw it exactly right. Maybe she simply thought that wasn't a moment to say so.

I walked a long time with the bread still in my hand. I never took a single bite.

As the sun began to set, I walked out near the city wall.

Few people came out this far. Everyone had stayed in the square or the streets. I sat down with my back against the wall. The evening wind blew. The day's sounds reached me only faintly from here — laughter, singing, someone calling out a name.

Listening to those sounds, I realized something strange. I wasn't sad. But I wasn't happy either. Neither of those two words could explain who I was, in that moment.

I looked at my hands.

They were shaking.

I looked at that hand for a long time. In the square, reading the clause, that hand hadn't shaken. Not when I read the disposition, not when I said "This city has turned." But now, doing nothing at all, sitting with my back against the wall, this hand was shaking.

I didn't have a name for that trembling then. I do now. It wasn't the trembling of fear. It was something long suppressed, leaking out of my body for the first time.

I sat there a long while, the bread resting on my knees. The sky reddened, then slowly darkened.

It was full dark by the time the Adjudicator found me.

I knew it was him from his footsteps first. A familiar walk. He came and set his back against the wall beside me. He stood there a long while without a word.

"…Everyone's looking for you." he said. "No one knows you're here."

"…I wanted to be here."

He didn't press further.

"Today makes forty years." the Adjudicator said. His voice was low. "I spent forty years just reading that clause. I never thought I'd see it invoked."

I said nothing.

"It feels strange." he said. "I'm happy, but it doesn't feel like happiness. Do you know why."

I couldn't answer that question. But hearing it, I felt for the first time that I wasn't alone. The Adjudicator, too, was standing there that evening carrying something he couldn't explain. Only his and mine weren't shaped the same.

"…Adjudicator." I said. "What about that person. What about that person."

I couldn't finish the sentence. The Adjudicator didn't ask who I meant. He simply fell silent for a moment.

"If you mean your family." he said. "I don't know. Where that person is now, what weight they've gone on adding since — I don't know. The Scales would know."

"…I never asked the Scales."

"Why."

I couldn't answer.

That was the first time I admitted it to myself. I was afraid to check that person's weight. Afraid it would come out low. Afraid that person, like this city, had turned somewhere too. That possibility frightened me more than the Reckoning's survival did.

The Adjudicator rested a hand on my shoulder once. The same weight as before, in the square.

"Rest tonight." he said. "Think about tomorrow, tomorrow."

He left me with those words and walked into the dark. I watched his back until it was gone completely.

Far off, the singing still carried. Someone rang a bell. This time it wasn't the bell of proclamation, but simply a bell of joy. I found it strange that the same iron could make both those sounds.

That night, for the first time, I admitted to myself that I was holding something back. I still couldn't name what it was. But I knew clearly, at least, that it could no longer be held back.

I couldn't sleep that night. The next morning, I found the name.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. Anger

_Previously_ — The city lives. While everyone wept and laughed, Pheugon alone couldn't. Night came, and the Adjudicator sought him out. "It feels strange," the Adjudicator said. Pheugon still didn't know why his hands were shaking. He didn't sleep that night.

I watched morning come with my eyes open.

I hadn't slept a wink all night. My body was tired, but my mind was strangely clear. Leaning against the wall, I watched the darkness turn gray, and the gray turn blue.

All that night I thought of exactly one thing. More precisely — I didn't think it. It simply grew larger inside me, on its own. I didn't try to give it a name. I felt that naming it would make it real.

Once the sun was fully up, I got to my feet. My legs had gone numb. Feeling that numbness, a certainty rose in me for the first time. I had to find him today.

All night I'd gone back over what Trope had said. "The reason isn't gone." She'd said the city's living hadn't changed her own share of the weight. When I heard it, I hadn't understood. Only that night did I understand. I had been saying the same thing. Even with the city alive, the reason our home had come apart hadn't changed. My brother still hadn't come back. My mother was still far away. My father was still alone. Trope's sentence and mine were the same shape. Only she had gone quiet before that sentence, and I hadn't been able to.

Finding him wasn't hard.

In this city he was always somewhere quiet. Not a crowded place, not a truly remote one either — somewhere between. I'd seen him a few times before. The one who never got angry beside someone being unfairly cornered. The one who knew a person's weight before the Scales did. The one who never got angry even when I provoked him on purpose.

I found him at the dry well near the gate. He was sitting there alone. The commotion of the day before had already moved entirely toward the city center; here it was quiet.

He looked up and saw me.

"…Pheugon."

"…That's right." I said. "You knew my name."

"I knew it a while ago."

I stood in front of him. I didn't sit.

"I have a question." I said.

"Ask it."

"Why don't you get angry?"

I'd asked this question once before. That time I'd asked it to provoke him. Back then it hadn't mattered whether he answered or not. Today was different. I wanted the real answer.

He didn't answer. Instead he watched me quietly. There was no defensiveness in his eyes at all. That made me angrier.

"I'm angry." I said. My voice came out lower than I expected, even to myself. "And you're not. Why not?"

"…What is it that's making you angry." he asked.

That question became the fuse.

"This city." I said. "This city living. I knew it. I already knew. I knew this would happen. That's why I ran."

My voice began to rise.

"I've known ever since the night I first read that clause at the bottom of the sentence. That you — no, that this world, this clause — would end up exactly here. That mercy wins, in the end, even in the face of an exact verdict. I wasn't afraid of that. I hated it. That's why I ran."

He didn't move.

"If I hadn't run." I said. "This city would have simply fallen. No one would ever have spoken those six words. I wanted it to end that way. I ran wanting this city to die. Not out of fear. That was my calculation."

In that moment I surprised even myself. I had already admitted this in Chapter 27. I'd said it once before, to the Adjudicator. But this time was different. This time I was saying it out loud, in anger. Admitting it and admitting it in anger are different things.

"Our home." I said. My throat closed. "Our home never had a clause like this."

He still said nothing.

"There was a person from the Reckoning living in our house. For months. I still don't fully know what that person did, or how. But afterward, someone's weight in our home went up, and the Scales calculated it exactly. Exactly. I don't doubt that precision. I still don't."

I caught my breath.

"But we never had forty days. There was no vine. No one to confirm any turning. My brother just left. My mother just went away. My father is still alone. No one ever stood in front of our home and said, 'This family has turned.'"

Saying that sentence, I realized how long I'd been holding it in. Three years. In three years I had never once spoken that sentence aloud. I had always been someone who read other people's sentences aloud. My own, I had never once read.

"But this city." I said. "This city is a hundred twenty thousand people. A hundred twenty thousand people cheated the Scales, stole, neglected, were cruel. And yet, like a vine growing, all of them lived at once. Because I said six words. Is that fair? Is that really fair?"

"Trope's mother." I said. My voice couldn't stop now. "She was pushed out five years ago too. That was small lies, piled up too. Not one great sin — small things, piled up because life was hard. The Scales measured her exactly, and she was pushed out. Why didn't she have a clause? Why did no one ever say in front of her, 'This person has turned'? Trope still goes to the well looking for her mother's handprint. And her mother never comes back."

In that moment I knew I was using Trope's grief as evidence for my own anger. That wasn't right. But I couldn't stop, not then. I needed more grounds to say this was unfair. Trope's mother was that grounds.

He didn't answer.

I couldn't bear that silence.

"Say something." I said. "Aren't you angry? Even knowing this isn't fair?"

"…I'm not angry." he said quietly.

"Why not!"

I know I shouted that. It was fortunate no one was near the gate. If there had been, I would have shouted at him exactly the same way in front of them.

"Pheugon." he said. His voice stayed low. A lowness without any anger in it. "What you're shouting at me right now — that isn't aimed at the Scales."

I stopped at that.

Until that moment I hadn't known clearly, even myself, who I was shouting at. His words picked it out. I wasn't angry at the Scales. The Scales hadn't saved this city. The Scales had only updated a number. What had saved it was the clause, and behind that clause stood someone. And that someone, from the very start, was the person sitting in front of me.

"…That's right." I said. My voice shook. "I'm angry at you. The Scales was just accurate. You — you people — made it this way. So you can only give mercy once you know the whole sin. So you hand the key to that door to the one person who has the most reason to hate it. What kind of design is that? I never got to choose anything inside that design. I just stood where you people built the place to stand."

He looked at me for a long time.

There was no surprise in his eyes, no defensiveness, no irritation. I couldn't bear that. I think I wanted him to be angry with me. If he had been, it would have proven my anger was justified.

"…I" he said. "I know why you're angry."

"If you know, why don't you say anything?"

"What should I say." he asked back. It wasn't a scolding tone. It was a genuine question.

I couldn't answer.

In that moment I realized, for the first time, that I didn't know what I wanted from this conversation. Did I want him to apologize? To explain himself? Or to get as angry as I was? I didn't clearly want any of those. I just wanted to see something else shake, the way I was shaking.

"…You never get angry." I said. My voice grew smaller this time. "Not once, in this whole world. I find that frightening. Because I know not getting angry isn't weakness. I know not getting angry is far more powerful."

He gave no answer to that.

I caught my breath in that silence. My body was shaking. It was the trembling that comes after you've shouted.

"Am I wrong?" I asked. It came out almost a plea. "Is it wrong for me to be angry?"

"…I never said that." he said.

"Then why won't you say anything."

"…What you want to hear right now isn't an answer." he said quietly. "What you want is for me to be angry along with you."

I started to argue with that, and couldn't. Because he was right.

In that moment I saw the true shape of what I'd wanted. I hadn't wanted to be justified. I already had that in hand. I wanted agreement. I wanted someone to raise a fist beside me at this world's unfairness. And in this whole world, the one person who had every right to do it — the only one — never once raised his fist.

I sank down in front of him, as if my legs had given out.

"…I remember that night." I said. This time I wasn't shouting. I just spoke. "The night my father raised his hand at the Scales' terminal. He looked at the number, and didn't show it to me. He only said, 'It's fine.' I knew, even then, that he wasn't saying that to me. He was saying it to himself."

He was watching me.

"After that, I kept my own number low. As low as I could, always. I thought that if I did, it would somehow fill in for my father's share. It was a child's arithmetic. The Scales doesn't count that way. I know that. And still I lived that way for three years. Not getting angry, not doing wrong, being perfect."

"…That must have been hard." he said.

There was no pity in it. It was just a voice acknowledging a fact. That made it hurt more, not less.

"Whether it was hard doesn't matter." I said. "I thought if I could endure the hardship, something would come back. Nothing came back. Not my brother, not my mother, not a normal father. But this city — the moment I said six words, everything came back to it. A hundred twenty thousand of them."

We stayed like that a long while.

The wind passed over the dry well. Far off, from the heart of the city, people's voices still carried faintly.

"…I can't go back to the city." I said. It was a sentence I hadn't expected myself. "Not right now."

"…Where will you go."

I looked east. Beyond the wall, a low hill was visible. Somewhere no one much went.

"…I don't know." I said. "I just don't want to be here."

He didn't stand. He didn't try to stop me either.

"…Go, then." he said.

There was neither permission nor refusal in it. Just those words.

I turned those words over for a long time that day. I think I half-wanted him to stop me. If he had, I would have gotten angry at that too. Because he didn't stop me, I lost the next thing I could have been angry at. He had, in effect, taken away one more target for my anger.

Even now I don't regret what I poured out at him that day. Every word of it was true. But I also learned, that day, that after I'd poured it all out, nothing became lighter. Anger doesn't disappear by being spoken. It only moves somewhere else.

I turned and left him there.

Passing through the gate, I met the substitute herald. He was sitting alone in the shade at the foot of the wall. Same clothes as yesterday, same posture. He stood when he saw me.

"…Where are you going?" he asked.

"…I don't know." I said. "I just don't want to be here."

He looked at me a moment, then nodded. He didn't ask anything more.

"…Congratulations." he said. His voice was even. "I think you did the right thing."

That wasn't comfort to me, not then. It made things heavier instead. He saw me as someone in the right. In that moment I wanted to tell him exactly who I'd just been shouting at, and what. I didn't.

I only nodded and passed him by.

A few steps on, I looked back. He was still sitting there. Not an angry face, not a sad one. Just watching me.

I didn't know, that day, what that face meant. Now I do. It was a waiting face. The face of someone who knew that after I'd finished shouting it all out, I'd come back eventually.

I turned my back on that face and started walking east.

Following the wall, the sun climbed higher. Inside the city, yesterday's joy was still going on. The bell rang once more. I heard it behind me as I walked.

I didn't know exactly where I was going, then. I only knew I wanted to get some distance — from this city, from that sound. I didn't admit, at the time, that this was running away. I admit it now. I was running away again. Not from the city, this time — from the feeling the city had given me.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. The Hill East of the City

_Previously_ — Pheugon went to find him and shouted. "I knew this would happen. That's why I ran." He didn't get angry. Instead he asked, "What you're shouting at me right now — that isn't aimed at the Scales." Pheugon started walking east.

The hill was about an hour's walk from the city.

I didn't know the direction exactly. I just walked away from the city. As I walked, the ground rose little by little, and before I knew it I was climbing a hill.

It wasn't a tall hill. The kind everyone from this region knew about, but no one ever had a reason to go to. At the top there was dry grass and a few rocks. Almost no trees. Nothing that could give shade.

I wasn't thinking about shade then. Someone who needs shade is someone waiting for something. I was climbing that hill without even knowing what I was waiting for.

From the top, the whole city came into view.

The Reckoning was a city of low roofs packed close together. A square at its center, streets radiating outward around it. A city wall wrapped the whole thing in a circle. Seen from here, the city looked smaller than I'd thought. Living inside it for forty days, it had felt like the whole world. Seen from here, it fit in the palm of a hand.

I sat looking down at that palm-sized city.

I should have asked myself, in that moment, what I was doing. I didn't. I just sat. I drew up my knees, wrapped my arms around them, and looked at the city.

The wind blew. It was stronger up on the hill than down in the city. My clothes kept flapping.

Inside the walls, I could see the square. Tiny, ant-sized figures were moving. Of the hundred twenty thousand who'd filled that square yesterday, all I could see now from here were a few scattered points of motion. I couldn't tell which point was Trope, which was the Adjudicator.

In that moment I felt a strange relief. No one could recognize me here. I wasn't one of those points either, here. I was outside them. I found that comfortable. And being comfortable made me ashamed.

East of the city, low fields spread out. In forty days I had never once gone out that way. A narrow path ran between the fields, and that path led all the way up to this hill. Probably farmers had once walked it often. Now it was half-buried in grass.

Walking that path, it felt strangely good that no one was there. In forty days I had always been inside someone's eyes. As a herald, as a strange boy, later as a savior. On the road to this hill, for the first time, I became a person in no one's eyes at all.

I sat there for a long while.

For the first hour or so, I wasn't honest with myself. I tried to think I'd just come out for some air. But as time passed, it became clear that was a lie.

I was waiting for the city to fall.

Even writing this sentence now, my hand shakes. I am the person who invoked the clause. The person who saved the city. The person who spoke those six words with his own mouth. And yet, less than a day after saying them, I was sitting on a hill, waiting for that city to fall.

Some will call this a contradiction. I want to say it wasn't. I never once wished for that city to live. I simply could not keep from saying those six words. I learned, precisely, for the first time on that hill, that a wish and an act can be two different things.

I recalled an old story I'd once heard — a herald ordered to proclaim a city's fall, who ran because he couldn't bear the thought of the city being forgiven. It was something the supervisor had once mentioned in passing, at the training grounds. When I first heard it, I hadn't understood that person. Why hate being forgiven? Why couldn't he stand a city he hated surviving?

I understand now. That person in the story hadn't hated a single city. He must have believed the world had to be fair. The rule that the guilty are punished and the righteous are kept safe. Rather than watch that rule broken, he'd chosen to be the one who never showed up to keep it. I know that feeling in my body now.

Noon came.

The sun stood overhead. With no shade, I took the sun on my whole body. My throat was dry. I hadn't brought water.

I thought, then, that not bringing water had been a mistake. I know now. It wasn't a mistake. I think I wanted to punish myself. For what, I didn't know then, and I don't know precisely now. I only know I didn't want to be comfortable that day.

Being thirsty brought back old thoughts. The three days trapped in the conduit. In that darkness, water up to my knees, I'd suffered not from too little water to drink, but too much. Now it was the opposite. On this hill there was no shade and no water. I found the contrast strangely funny. Three years ago I'd learned to speak while submerged in water. Now I was losing words for lack of it.

I wrote, in Chapter 10, that those three days were the first time I spoke my own story aloud. On this hill I was doing the opposite. I said nothing at all. There was no one to speak to. I was the one who had left the place where he'd been.

I thought of the dinner table.

The table where the four of us used to sit. Father, mother, brother, me. And for the months that person stayed with us, five. I don't remember those evenings exactly. Only a few fragments remain. The window growing dark outside. Someone laughing. That laughter never heard in that house again after.

I brought that table back to mind on that hill. It was strange. I had already written about that table in Chapter 3. I'd thought it was a memory already put away. But with hunger and thirst pressing in, the memory came back raw. I couldn't even remember what we'd eaten that evening, but the sense of that empty place stayed with me, somewhere in my stomach.

Almost nothing of the city's sound reached this far. The wind was too strong. Now and then something like a bell seemed to carry, faintly. I couldn't be sure.

I kept watching the city. There was no sign of it falling. The roofs stood where they were. The wall stood where it was. A few threads of smoke rose from chimneys. Someone was cooking a meal.

Watching that smoke, I had a strange thought. What would a city look like as it falls? I'd never once imagined it. More precisely, I'd avoided imagining it. For three years I had wished this city would fall, and never once pictured what that falling would actually look like. Now that I tried to picture it, nothing came.

The afternoon came.

I was still there. My legs went numb, and I shifted position a few times. I tried moving into the shade of a rock, but the rock was small, so was its shade.

Nothing happened.

I still can't say precisely what I was expecting, then. Was I expecting the ground to split? The Scales to reverse its verdict again? Or just expecting anything at all to happen, so my anger would have somewhere to go?

Nothing happened. The city stayed exactly where it was. The people went on living inside it. Whatever I thought, sitting on that hill, the city didn't care.

At first that felt unjust to me. Then it felt empty. I had spoken the most important sentence in the world. And the day after I said it, the world went on turning in a way that took no notice of me at all.

I closed my eyes and remembered the Adjudicator's voice again. "It feels strange today. I'm happy, but it doesn't feel like happiness." He was a man who had waited forty years. I was a boy who had run for forty days. And yet on this hill, our two strange feelings, standing in different places, wore the same shape.

In that moment, for the first time, I wondered. Was the Adjudicator, too, sitting somewhere alone right now, looking into this same unfamiliar feeling inside him? He couldn't be as angry as I was — he hadn't had my three years. And yet he too had looked like a man who'd lost something. The face of someone who had waited forty years for a place, only to watch it filled by someone else's mouth.

I opened my eyes. The city was still there.

The sun began to set.

I was hungry. My throat was parched too. As my body wore out, my thoughts grew simpler. I stopped waiting for the city to fall. I just sat.

Looking back later, I thought this was the most honest moment of all. When I'd first climbed the hill, I'd been someone waiting for something. By evening I was simply someone exhausted. In the place where waiting had been, nothing was left. Even my anger had grown tired.

Far off in the city, lights began coming on one by one. Houses preparing dinner. Forty days before, I had once watched those lights from a distance with Trope. Back then those lights had been the place we had to return to. Now they were the place I'd left.

The same lights, and yet the heart that watched them had completely changed in between. I can't say precisely when that change happened. Maybe it was the moment I said those six words in the square. Or the moment I shouted at him near the gate. Or maybe it was here, now, on this hill.

Night came.

The wind grew colder. I drew my collar closer. The city's lights glimmered far off. Stars began to appear. I turned my back to the city and leaned against a rock.

I thought Trope might be looking for me by now. Maybe she'd asked the Adjudicator. "Where did he go." The Adjudicator would probably have told her the truth. That he went east. Beyond that, he wouldn't have known either. This hill wasn't a place anyone had any particular reason to go.

That night I think I wanted Trope to come find me. And at the same time I wanted no one to come at all. Both feelings were there together. I didn't know, then, which one was real. Maybe both were. A person's heart doesn't come out as a single line, the way the Scales does.

A dog barked once, far away. Then quiet again. I looked at the sky. Stars were multiplying, one by one. Without the city's lights up here, far more stars were visible than inside the city.

I tried not to think anything, watching those stars. It didn't work well. Thoughts kept multiplying too, like the stars. My brother's back as he walked away. The day my mother said it was time to go. My father's, "Take care of yourself." The substitute herald's, "Congratulations, I think you did the right thing." The baker's hand. Trope's, "The reason isn't gone." His, "What you're shouting at me right now isn't aimed at the Scales." Thoughts came up out of order and sank back down.

That night I tried not to look toward the city. My head kept turning that way anyway. I kept watch over that spot without even knowing what I was guarding.

I was hungry and thirsty. And still I didn't want to go down. Going down felt like it would mean admitting something. I didn't know what. Only that as long as I stayed on this hill, I could still be someone holding on to something.

Looking back now, that was strange logic. I had already saved the city. Whatever I did on this hill wouldn't change that fact. But at the time, I felt that staying on this hill was itself a kind of resistance. Resistance to what, I didn't know. Not to the city, and not to him. Probably to myself. I still hadn't fully accepted the thing I had already decided — those six words.

Out of the habit of counting one hundred twelve cases, I began counting the stars. Twenty, thirty, forty. I gave up counting. It wasn't something you could count. There were too many. I realized, in that moment, that I had only ever lived inside things that could be counted. People's names, charges, dates, one hundred twelve cases. And this thing inside me now — whether it was anger or grief, I still wasn't sure — this couldn't be counted.

I fell asleep on that spot that night. No shade, no blanket, my back against the cold rock.

I don't remember what I dreamed that night. But I remember one thing. Just before I fell asleep, I murmured, very quietly, to myself. "…It's still not over." What wasn't over, I couldn't explain then, and I still can't now.

Thinking about it now, that sentence was a kind of threat. I don't even know who it was aimed at. Maybe at myself. That night, on that hill, I think I wanted to believe something more had to happen. Whether the city fell, or the clause was overturned, or even if some sign fell from the sky. That night, I wasn't yet ready to accept that nothing at all would happen.

The wind slowly died down. I curled up between the rocks. The stone was cold, and my back ached against it. I fell asleep that way. No shade, no blanket, no answer.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. Overnight

_Previously_ — Pheugon climbed the hill east of the city and waited for it to fall. Nothing happened. He fell asleep against a cold rock, with no shade and no blanket.

It wasn't light that woke me.

It was something touching my body. Soft, and a little cold. I reached out on instinct. A leaf.

I sat up.

For the first few seconds I thought I was still dreaming. Green filled my whole field of vision. To eyes still half-asleep, I couldn't tell whether it was sky or leaves. I rubbed my eyes with the back of my hand. The green didn't go away.

I still remember those few seconds precisely. That short gap between dream and waking, where nothing needs to be judged yet. I made no calculations at all in that gap. I just looked at the green in front of me. Looking back, those few uncalculated seconds were the cleanest moment of that whole day.

Something had grown overnight.

Between the rocks, right above where I'd slept, a vine had stretched out. Not just stretched — it had climbed the rock and curved upward like an arch, its leaves spread wide. Beneath it lay a solid patch of shade, directly over the spot where I'd slept all night.

I couldn't move.

I tried to understand what I was seeing. I couldn't. Vines don't grow like this. It normally takes months, at least a few weeks. But this had grown overnight — while I was asleep.

The leaves were broad and thick, dark green. The stem was as thick as my forearm. Yesterday there had been nothing here but dry grass and rock. I remembered that clearly, because I'd spent the whole day before sitting right on this hilltop.

I searched for the vine's roots.

They began in a crack in the rock. Almost no soil there. I crouched down to look. No sign of a seed. No trace of anyone having planted it. The stem simply began in that crack in the rock. As if it had always been there.

I looked at that root for a long time. Where this vine had come from, where its seed had blown in from, who had planted it — I never found an answer. I still don't know. But looking at that root, for a moment, an unfamiliar sense passed through me. A feeling like I'd seen something like this before. Like I'd seen something grow from a nameless seed once before. I didn't know then where that feeling came from, and I still can't explain it. I only note here that the feeling was there.

The wind blew, and the leaves shook. Their shadow trembled on my feet along with them.

I followed the vine's whole shape with my eyes. The stem began in the rock crack and split into three branches, two of which twisted together and formed the arch overhead. The third was still short — as though it hadn't yet decided which way to grow, hanging in midair.

Among the leaves, not yet buds, small white points had formed here and there. I couldn't tell whether these were about to bloom, or just markings on the stem.

I looked closely at those white points, and then took my hand away. Sometimes if you look at something too closely, instead of learning what it is, you end up knowing it less. I decided, that day, not to try to find out what those points were. I decided just to accept that they were there.

I stood beneath that arch and looked toward the city. Morning sunlight came through the vine's leaves in fragments. Roofs and walls trembled beyond those fragments. Only then did I reckon that I'd been on this hill barely a day. It felt much longer. The forty days I'd spent in the city, I could count precisely, in numbers. The time on this hill kept blurring.

Dew had gathered at the edges of the leaves. It sparkled in the morning light. Thirsty, I gathered a few drops on my fingertip. Only a few. I touched them to my lips. Cold and clear.

A few drops of dew couldn't quench my thirst. But in that moment, for the first time, I felt that this hill hadn't abandoned me completely. My stomach was still empty, my throat still dry. But something wasn't leaving me to simply pass through.

I sat back down beneath that shade.

The sunlight was completely blocked now. After the sun I'd taken on my whole body the whole day before, its absence made my body strangely light. That lightness felt unfamiliar.

I didn't know, in that moment, how to receive it. I hadn't come here to be comfortable. I had come here to be punished. And yet overnight, untouched by anyone's hand, shade had come to me.

At first I wanted to reject it. I even thought about going and sitting back out in the sun. But my body wouldn't move. The shade felt strangely good. Its being good made me angry.

I touched one of the leaves. Smooth on top, a little rough underneath. Some kind of scent rubbed off on my fingertips. It was different from the smell of this hill's dry grass. I searched my memory for where I might have smelled it before. Nothing came.

Smelling that scent, I can say now — for a moment, with no context at all, an unfamiliar image flickered past. A seed, fallen on some dry ground, a seed no one knew the name of. I have never seen a scene like that. Not in this body, at least. And yet in that instant the scene passed through me like a memory. I can't explain it. I've decided not to try. Some things stay truer left unexplained.

I tried to hold on to that unfamiliar image. Like water slipping through a fist, it vanished faster the harder I tried to grasp it. Within a few seconds nothing remained. Only a scent, a green light, and an unexplainable sense of familiarity.

I ran my hand along the vine's stem. Each joint was a slightly different thickness. Some places had the hard grain of an old tree; others were as tender as something freshly sprouted. That all these textures had appeared overnight kept striking me as unbelievable.

Out of trained habit, I tried to calculate the vine's age. By its thickness, the stem should have been at least a few years old. But yesterday nothing had stood here at all. The arithmetic didn't work. I had grown up counting one hundred twelve cases. Faced with arithmetic that didn't work, I didn't know what to do. In the end I gave up counting and simply felt the stem with my fingertips.

Past noon, I was still beneath that shade.

My stomach was still empty. My throat still dry. But it was more bearable than yesterday. I found it strange, how much difference one patch of shade could make.

I thought about the Scales again that day. How would it have weighed this shade? Shade itself carries no weight. It isn't stolen, it isn't a lie, it isn't neglect. Nowhere among the items the Scales measures is there a column for shade. And yet that day I felt, for the first time, how much this weightless thing was actually lifting off my body.

I already knew there was much the Scales couldn't measure, even inside that city. What isn't stolen, what isn't struck, what isn't lied — those too carried no weight. But that day, for the first time, I learned that what carries no weight isn't necessarily light. The shade had no weight, and it saved me.

I looked down the hill again.

Far along the path between the fields, very small, I saw a human figure walking. Too far to tell who it was. I watched for a while, trying to tell whether the figure was coming this way, or just heading out to work the fields. It grew smaller and smaller, and soon vanished among the fields.

I have to admit I felt disappointed, in that moment. I had been hoping someone would come find me. And at the same time, I had no idea what I'd say if they did. How would I explain the vine that had grown? How would I explain why I was still here? I had no explanation ready at all.

I moved back beneath the vine. The temperature of the shade stayed strangely constant. However the sun's angle shifted, the leaves seemed to bend slightly toward it, in turn. I watched from beneath it for half a day, trying to tell whether that was real or my imagination. It wasn't imagination. The leaves really were shaping the shade to follow me.

Watching that, I thought — so there really are things in this world that move this precisely for exactly one person. The Scales was precise too. But the Scales' precision was a precision that measured me. This shade's precision was a precision that covered me. I put that difference into words, clearly, for the first time that day.

I was still waiting for the city to fall.

More precisely, using the word "waiting" had begun to feel a little embarrassing now. Yesterday's waiting had had an edge to it. Today's waiting simply remained, out of habit. I looked down at the city. Still there. Roofs, walls, smoke.

That afternoon I had a strange thought. Who had this vine grown for? Not for the city — the city had already lived. So it had grown for me. But was I someone who deserved to receive it? Just the day before, I had climbed this hill resenting the fact that the city lived. That shade should come to someone like that — I still didn't understand it.

Now I know. Not understanding was normal. This shade couldn't be explained by a logic of deserving. That was the Scales' way of doing things. I already knew, in my body, that there was a way other than the Scales' way — I just hadn't yet been able to accept it in my mind.

I reached a hand out past the shade. The sun was hot. I drew my hand back inside the shade. The coolness came immediately. I went back and forth over that line a few times. That one hand could feel heat and coolness by turns felt, strangely, like something I couldn't put down that day.

Crossing that line back and forth, I thought of Trope. She had drawn a line between the city's survival and her own forgiveness. "It was the city that the Scales called void. Not me." I thought of that again, touching the boundary of the shade. There was a line like that too, between what lay inside this vine's shade and what lay outside. Except this line, unlike Trope's, had no condition for crossing it. You only had to move your body.

Far off, a bird called. I listened to it for a long time. It was the first birdsong I'd heard since arriving on this hill. The bird landed briefly on a branch above the vine, looked at me, then flew off again. I followed the direction it flew with my eyes. Toward the city.

The sun began to set again.

I sat under the shade and greeted my second night there. This time I lay down beneath the vine's shade instead of against the rock. The leaves made a sound in the wind. Low and soft.

Listening to that sound, I was able to fall asleep comfortably for the first time since arriving on this hill. I found that strange, and yet I didn't stop it. I was still angry. But beside that anger, something else was growing, very small. I didn't know, then, what it was.

I closed my eyes and went back over what had happened the day before. Shouting at him. The substitute herald's even congratulations. The whole day I'd spent waiting for the city to fall. None of it was erased by this one vine. But it all began to settle at a slightly different weight. I couldn't explain why. It simply felt that way.

I don't want to write that my anger disappeared. It didn't disappear. But that night, I learned for the first time that I could hold another feeling alongside anger at the same time. For three years I had lived as someone who allowed only one feeling at a time. Kind, or angry. Holding it in, or letting it burst. Under this shade, for the first time, I felt two things at once. Still angry, and at the same time, feeling that someone was looking after me.

Starlight came through the leaves in fragments. I began counting the fragments, then gave up. I didn't need to count them. I only needed to look.

I didn't know, that night, whom to thank. I didn't know who had sent this vine. And yet I said one thing, very quietly, where no one could hear.

"…Thank you."

Who those words were meant for, I still don't know exactly. Maybe for the vine. Maybe for whatever had made the vine exist. I was a little surprised at myself, saying it. In forty days — no, in three years — I had never once said something like that so easily.

Before falling asleep, I ran my hand once over one of the vine's leaves. I wanted to remember that feeling. I don't know why I wanted to. I just did.

Falling asleep that night, I didn't reckon how long this shade would last. It wasn't that I'd given up the habit of reckoning. Just that, this once, I chose not to reckon, and simply receive it instead.

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. What the Names Mean

_Previously_ — On the hill, overnight, a vine grew up around the spot where Pheugon had slept and made shade. No one seemed to have planted it. Beneath that shade, Pheugon slept comfortably for the first time.

On the afternoon of the third day, a human figure appeared again on the path through the fields.

This time it didn't vanish. The figure grew larger and larger, and soon I recognized who it was. A walk with a water jug slung over one shoulder. Trope.

I didn't stand. I just watched her come, still sitting where I was.

I felt something strange in that moment. Gladness and shame at once. I wanted to see her again. And at the same time, I didn't want her to see whatever face I was wearing right now. Over these forty days I had felt those two things together many times. Each time, I'd thought it strange. Now I know it's simply how a person's heart works.

Trope stopped for a moment where the field path ended and looked up at the hill. That must have been the first time she saw the vine too. Even from a distance, that green arch would have caught the eye. Watching her stop, I thought — she must be carrying the same question I was.

She only caught her breath after she'd finished climbing the whole hill. Sweat beaded on her forehead. She set down the water jug and looked at the vine. She looked at it for a long time.

"…What is this?" she asked.

"…I don't know." I said. "It was like this yesterday morning."

Trope stepped under the vine and touched a leaf with her hand. The same motion I'd made.

"…Who planted it?"

"…No one. No one's come up here."

She didn't press further. Instead she held out the water jug.

"…The Adjudicator was worried. You hadn't come down in two days."

I took the jug and drank. Water sliding down my throat had never felt so welcome.

We sat side by side beneath the vine's shade.

Neither of us spoke for a while. The city looked small down below. The wind stirred the leaves now and then.

"…The Adjudicator asked again." Trope said. "Whether you'll come back."

"…What did you say?"

"I said I didn't know." she said. "It wasn't a lie. I really didn't know."

I drew up my knees and hugged them.

"…I'm still angry." I said. I said it aloud for the first time.

"…I know." Trope said. "I can tell from your face."

"…I'm sorry." I said. "It's not you I should be angry at."

"…You've never been angry at me." she said. "I'm just the person who brought the water jug."

I laughed at that, a little. Not a real laugh. But something close to one.

I was grateful, in that moment, that she didn't push me. The Adjudicator hadn't either. He hadn't either. In this city, strangely, no one had tried to pull me back until I walked out on my own. I wondered whether that was trained restraint, or something this city had learned in these forty days.

"…The Adjudicator told me to bring you this too."

Trope pulled a sheet of paper from inside her coat. Old, worn paper, its edges a little frayed.

"…What is it?"

"A list of herald names, he said." she said. "A very old one. It turned up from the very back of the proclamation office's storeroom. He said heralds used to be given names for a reason, long ago. Names drawn from an old language, all of them meaningful. No one speaks that language anymore, so no one knows the meanings now. But this list has the meanings written beside them."

I took the paper.

In that moment I didn't know what I was holding. Now I do. I was holding, in my hand, the answer to a question I had never once wondered about in three years. That I'd never once wondered — that itself was strange. Why had I never once wondered what my own name meant?

Thinking about it now, the answer is simple. I had only ever handled names as sound. In the course of reading one hundred twelve cases, I read hundreds of names. Never once did I wonder what any of them meant. A name was a signpost pointing to a person — not a sentence saying something about that person. At least, that's what I'd been taught. But now, this paper in my hand was telling me that was wrong.

I thought of the training grounds. The day Had had stopped three times at his own name. "Why don't you stop?" he'd asked. I suddenly wondered — what had Had's name meant? Why had he stopped at his own name? Maybe he too had never known what his name meant, and his body had simply reacted before his mind did.

I thought of looking for Had's name on that list, and stopped myself. That wasn't mine to do today. Today was my turn.

Trope flipped through the paper.

"…The Adjudicator said he found this yesterday. He was looking for something else — he found it by accident. But the moment he found it, he told me to bring it straight up to you. He didn't say why. I didn't ask."

I could guess, then, why the Adjudicator had done that. He was a man who had waited forty years for this clause. Now that it had finally been invoked, he probably knew exactly what I'd be doing on this hill. He hadn't sent me comfort. He'd sent me this. I thought that was very like him.

Trope ran her finger down the list, searching for the name.

"…Pheugon." she said.

I held my breath.

"…φεύγω." she read the old letters, awkwardly. "It says the meaning here. 'To flee.'"

I heard those words.

For a while I couldn't react at all.

I've gone back over this moment hundreds of times since. Each time I remember it a little differently. At first I remembered it as shock. Later, as relief. Sorting it out now, it was both.

"…Pheugon." I said my own name aloud. For the first time in my life, I heard it as something strange.

"To flee."

And I laughed.

I laughed out loud. Not loudly. But it was unmistakably laughter.

Trope looked at me, startled.

"…Why are you laughing?"

I answered without being able to stop.

"…I've wondered my whole life why I turned out this way. Why I always choose to run, every single time. When the person from the Reckoning wrecked our home, instead of getting angry, I ran into endurance. When I got the Reckoning's sentence, I actually ran. Just now, I didn't go down into the city — I ran up to this hill instead."

In that moment, for the first time, I could see my whole life as one sentence. I'd had that name since I was born. Whether my parents knew its meaning when they chose it, or just liked the sound, I don't know. But I had lived exactly according to that name. Without a single deviation.

"…My name already knew." I said. "It knew from the start that I'd run."

Trope said nothing, just watched me.

"…Aren't you angry?" she asked, carefully. "That it was decided from birth like that?"

I thought about the question.

"…Strangely, no." I said. "I feel like I should be angry, but I'm not. It's almost lighter." I looked at the light coming through the leaves. "All this time I lived without understanding why I'm this way. Carrying something you don't understand and carrying something you understand — the weight is different. The thing itself is the same, but it feels different to hold."

Trope nodded. Not a face that fully understood. But a face trying to.

"…Did the Adjudicator know?" she said. "This meaning."

"…He probably didn't until yesterday." I said. "That's probably why he found it yesterday."

In that moment I imagined the moment the Adjudicator had found this paper and read my name first. A man who'd stood beside this clause for forty years, learning at last that the boy who had finally invoked it was named 'to flee.' What expression had crossed his face? I felt I might never get to ask. And yet, somehow, I felt I already knew.

As my laughter faded, I noticed I had a little water in my eyes.

I couldn't tell whether it was tears of sorrow or tears of laughter. Now I know. Both. For three years I had lived without understanding why I was always a person who ran. Living without that understanding is heavy. A name doesn't remove that weight. But the weight got a name. Once something has a name, at least you know what it is you're carrying.

"…It's strange." Trope said quietly. "I've never seen you laugh before."

"…Neither have I." I said. "Not in this book. Not in my whole life."

Trope looked back down at the paper. Her finger moved slowly down the list. She was clearly looking for her own name.

I didn't stop her.

Her finger stopped.

"…Trope." she read, very quietly. "τροπή."

She didn't read the rest. The paper in her hand trembled slightly.

"…What does it say?" I asked.

She didn't answer.

I looked down at the paper. I read it in her place.

"'Turning.'"

Trope said nothing at all.

I looked at her. She stayed exactly as she was, paper in hand. Her expression didn't move. She didn't seem to blink.

I felt, then, like I should say something. But nothing came to mind. Some silences shouldn't be disturbed. I learned that for the first time that day.

Time passed. The wind stirred the leaves. A bell sounded faintly from the city. Trope still hadn't moved.

Even now I can't say precisely how long that silence lasted. It felt very long to me. She had lived five years not knowing what her own name meant. In those five years, without knowing what her own turning meant, she had checked her weight at the Scales, every single day. And just now, she had learned that her name had been pointing that direction from the very start.

I looked at the side of her face. There were no tears. But the absence of tears looked heavier than tears would have. Some feelings don't come out as tears or laughter. They just stay pooled inside the body.

I set my own laughter and her silence side by side, in my mind, in that moment. Both of us had just had the same weight of feeling suddenly given a name. I let mine out as laughter. She couldn't let hers out at all. I know it wasn't because I was somehow better than her. My name had only confirmed a self I already knew. Her name pointed to a self she had never once let herself be. Confirming is easy. Letting yourself is hard.

A long while later, she finally spoke.

"…Did Mom know?" she said. Her voice was very small. "When she named me. This meaning."

"…I don't know."

"…Does Mom think she's turned, right now?" she said. It sounded like a question to herself. "Or does she think I'm the one turning, right now?"

I couldn't answer that. It wasn't a question that could be answered.

I didn't fully understand, that day, why Trope stayed silent so long. I understand a little more now. I heard my own name and laughed. My name only confirmed a self I already knew. But Trope's name pointed to a self she had never known at all. She had lived believing she was the one pushed aside, the one left behind. And her name had been calling her, from the very start, the one who turns.

The sun began to sink toward the west. The shadows lengthened.

Trope carefully folded the paper again. Instead of putting it back inside her coat, she pressed it into my hand.

"…You keep it." she said.

"…Why?"

"…You're the one who reads things." she said. Then, very briefly, faintly, she smiled. It wasn't a full smile. "I don't have the courage to read this one aloud myself yet."

I took the paper.

Taking it, I understood that the two of us had gone through completely different things that same afternoon. I laughed, and she went silent. I understood, and she was left more confused. And yet both of those responses had come from the same root. We had both, that day, for the first time, looked again at a self we had each thought we already knew.

Trope went down the hill before the sun had fully set. I watched her back until it disappeared among the field paths.

Partway down, she stopped once. She didn't turn around. She just stood there a moment, then walked on again. I didn't ask what that pause meant. She probably couldn't have explained it either.
