That evening, sitting alone beneath the vine, I read the two names on the paper again. To flee. Turning. I found it strange that those two words sat side by side. Someone who fled and someone who turns had passed through the same forty days, in the same city. And in the end, it was six words from the mouth of the one who fled that had saved the city. I still didn't know how to make sense of that.

I don't know who gave us those names. Neither does Trope. Neither did the Adjudicator. What was in the hearts of the people who once spoke that old language, when they named two newborns this way — I may never know, not even in the years ahead. But that night, I came away with one certainty: that these names were not a coincidence. There was no ground at all for that certainty. And still, the certainty stayed.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. Withering

_Previously_ — The Adjudicator found an old list of herald names. Trope brought it up the hill. Pheugon — to flee. Trope — turning. Pheugon laughed for the first time in his life. Trope stayed quiet for a long time.

The moment I opened my eyes, my body knew before I did that something was different.

It was the light. Every day until then, even with my eyes open, there had been shade over my face. Today the light was falling straight onto it. It was too bright.

I sat up.

My body was stiff — the result of three nights spent going back and forth between bare ground and rock. I rolled my neck side to side. It cracked. I nearly laughed at the sound. Yesterday's laughter still seemed to linger somewhere in my body.

The vine had withered.

It looked like it had happened overnight. Every leaf hung downward now. What had been green had turned brown, and in some places, already black. The wide arch had sagged and collapsed down onto the rock. When I touched the stem, there wasn't a trace of moisture left in it.

I couldn't think anything, in that moment. I just stood there and looked at the withered leaves. What had grown in one night had withered in one night too. If you wanted to call that fair, I suppose it was. But I couldn't feel it as fair.

I touched one of the leaves. It broke. It turned to powder between my fingers.

I tried to remember the night before. The vine had been perfectly fine before I fell asleep. I had run my hand over one leaf, said "Thank you" very quietly, and fallen asleep. What had happened in between, I had no idea at all. I didn't remember wind, didn't remember rain. I had simply fallen asleep and woken up, and in between, everything had died.

It bothered me, strangely, that this was the day after Trope had come. The day after that afternoon she'd gone quiet for so long, hearing the meaning of her own name — the vine had died the very next day. I knew there was no connection between the two. And yet I kept placing them side by side, as if one thing had to end before another could begin.

It struck me as strange that I hadn't felt a thing, through that whole night. I hadn't felt anything while it grew, asleep. I hadn't felt anything while it died, either. Both life and death had passed quietly while I slept. That quietness frightened me.

I was angry.

Even writing this sentence, I still surprise myself. Two days before, I had watched an entire city live, and gotten angry. Angry, watching a hundred twenty thousand people survive. And now, watching one vine wither, I was feeling an anger of about the same size.

I looked at the roots again. Where they'd begun in that crack in the rock, only a dry stub of stem remained now. I tried pulling it. It didn't come loose. The roots were still holding on. Only everything that had grown above them had died.

"…Why." I said it aloud. Alone, on the hill, no one there to hear. "Why this."

In that moment I noticed my own voice was exactly the voice I'd used shouting at him near the gate two days before. Same pitch, same tremor. Only the target had changed. Then it had been a person. Now it was a dry stem. I felt ashamed of that. And still my voice didn't quiet down.

I keep hesitating, writing this part. Is it all right to set this down exactly as it happened. Admitting I got angrier over one vine than over the lives of a hundred twenty thousand people — doesn't that make me look far too small a person. But when I first decided to write this book, I made myself one promise. To write what happened at the size it actually happened. To keep that promise, even this shameful part can't be cut.

I looked around. There was no one on the hill but me. No birdsong either. The bird that had briefly landed here yesterday and flown off hadn't come back. Only wind, passing through the dry grass.

I thought of him. The dry well near the gate. It occurred to me that he might still be there. I felt an urge to go to him again, and shout, this time about the vine. To ask why he'd given it and then taken it away. That urge didn't last long. I already knew he wouldn't get angry this time either. And I had no confidence I could bear that silence again.

I sank down where I stood.

I really was angry, in that moment. More angry, watching this one small vine die, than I had been watching the city live. I wanted to deny that. But I couldn't. My body had already reacted first.

I touched the withered leaves one by one. They all crumbled. Two days before, they had been smooth and thick. Now the moment my fingertip touched them, they turned to powder.

I set that powder in my palm and looked at it for a long while. Why did this feel bigger to me than the lives of a hundred twenty thousand people? I asked myself that question, and for the first time, I didn't run from it. I sat with it.

I looked at the spot where Trope had sat yesterday. Where she'd folded the paper and pressed it into my hand. I set that memory beside the dry stem in front of me. Yesterday I'd learned the meaning of my name and laughed. Today the vine had died and I was angry. In one day I felt like a completely different person. But really I was the same person. The one who had laughed learning his name, and the one now breaking apart in front of this vine — both of them carried the name of someone who flees.

I realized then that I had been waiting for something, the whole time I'd been on this hill. First I waited for the city to fall. Then I waited for comfort under the shade. And now I was waiting for this anger to pass. I had always been someone who waits. It was only the direction of the waiting that kept changing.

I didn't move from that spot until noon.

The sun fell over my whole body again. There was no more shade. My throat was dry. There was still a little water left in the jug Trope had left the day before. I didn't drink it. I didn't want to.

Writing this part now, I know how foolish I must have looked that day. Not drinking water that was right there. That wasn't an expression of grief — it was just stubbornness. I think I wanted to punish something. The vine, or myself, or whatever it was that had sent this vine and taken it back — I hadn't decided precisely which.

I gripped the withered stem again. It made a breaking sound. I hated that sound, and kept gripping it anyway.

I thought of Trope's mother. The handprint left on the well's stone rim. A mark that hadn't disappeared even after five years. Trope had said that every time she saw it, it felt like meeting her mother again. Gripping that dry stem, I understood that I too was holding on to some trace of something. Except my trace had lasted only two days, and hadn't been carved into stone. It was simply crumbling apart in my hand.

In that moment I felt a strange envy. Trope's trace could still be touched, even five years later. Mine hadn't lasted a single day. I felt that difference was unfair. And I found myself laughable, thinking it. I was the very person who had just been shouting, days before, that it was unfair for the city to live. Now I was feeling it was unfair that one vine had disappeared so fast. I seemed to have a talent for finding evidence of unfairness in everything in the world.

After a long while like that, I suddenly stopped.

In that moment I felt a strange sense of watching myself from outside. As if I were standing outside my own body, looking down at myself sitting on this hill, gripping a dead vine. And from that vantage, I asked myself. Pheugon. What are you doing right now.

I opened my hand. The broken bits of leaf blew away on the wind.

I got to my feet and looked down at the base of the hill. The city was still there. Roofs, walls, smoke from chimneys. The same view as three days before. Looking at that view, I wondered what those hundred twenty thousand people would say if they knew that, right now, I was falling apart like this over one vine.

Probably no one would understand. I didn't understand it myself. But in that moment I realized something else too. Maybe Trope would understand. For five years she's held on to one handprint left by her mother. To her, that one handprint matters more than the whole city's survival. I had never once thought her strange for it. And here I was doing exactly the same thing, and thinking myself strange for it.

I noticed something for the first time, in that moment. When the city lived, I had gone to shout. I'd gone to find him and poured out thirty minutes. Now that the vine had died, I hadn't gone to find anyone. I had just sat here alone, working the anger down by myself. The same size of anger, and I was handling it in a completely different way.

And that difference was the strangest part of all. I was someone who reacted more strongly to one vine with a few leaves than to a hundred twenty thousand lives. I didn't know whether I should be ashamed of that, or simply accept it as fact.

I sat back down. This time with my back to the dry stem. I didn't want to look at it anymore.

In that moment, for the first time, I saw my three years differently. I had always thought of myself as someone who never did wrong. Someone who never got angry. And yet in two days on this hill, I had fallen apart twice. Once in front of a person, once in front of a dry stem. I wasn't someone who never got angry. I was someone who stockpiled anger. And anger that's been stockpiled eventually breaks out — at any time, over any size of reason at all. I learned that principle in my body, that day.

By afternoon, I simply lay down beside that dry stem.

The sun was hot. I didn't shield myself from it. Only then did I feel, in my whole body, that the shade that had covered me until yesterday was gone. My back stung. My throat burned.

Feeling that heat, I thought: I only had this shade for two days. Not forty days, not three years — barely two days. And watching myself fall apart this badly over its loss, I understood for the first time that the size of my anger didn't match the size of what caused it.

The city had been a matter of a hundred twenty thousand lives. And in front of that, I'd shouted, built arguments, reasoned about fairness. The vine had only been two days of shade. And in front of that, I simply fell apart. No logic at all. Just pain.

I think I dozed off for a while in that heat. When I came to, the sun had already sunk far to the west. I didn't know how long I'd lain there. My throat was badly parched. Only then did I finally reach for the water jug.

The water was lukewarm. I drank it anyway. A few mouthfuls brought a little clarity back.

I realized something strange, in that moment. I had refused water out of anger at a vine. But my body couldn't overcome anger. When you're thirsty enough, you drink water, in the end. I found that sad. I'd thought I was enduring some great sorrow. Really, I was just a person with a body that got thirsty.

I closed my eyes. The sun glowed red through my eyelids.

I think that was the first time I saw the true shape of my anger. I hadn't wanted justice. If it had been justice, the sizes of my anger shouldn't have come out backward like that — bigger for one vine dying than for a hundred twenty thousand people living. I had wanted what was mine. What I couldn't bear was losing something I'd received, something I'd felt was mine. The city was never mine. I only saved that city — I never owned it. But this vine had been there for me, and only me, for two days. It was my shade alone.

When it was gone, I fell apart more than I had watching the city live. That day, for the first time, I noticed that fact myself. Noticing it, at the time, brought no comfort at all.

The wind carried off fragments of dry leaf down the hill. I didn't follow them with my eyes.

Once the sun had fully set, the hilltop grew cold again. I sat there in that spot, with no shade, no blanket. I couldn't even tell anymore whether I was angry because the shade was gone, or just cold.

That night, for the first time, I understood that the anger inside me wasn't only one thing. There was anger three years old, anger accumulated over the forty days, anger that had begun two days before, and today, one very small anger, born of one vine. They were all different angers, and yet inside my body they all boiled at the same temperature. That night I tried to sort them apart, one by one. It didn't go well. Before I could sort them, they were already mixed together.

I drew up my knees and hugged them. Stars began to rise, one by one. The same sky as two nights before. Except tonight there were no leaves left to screen them.

That night, meeting the third night on this hill, I asked myself for the first time. How long am I going to stay here. No answer came. But that I'd asked myself the question at all was, in itself, a little different from before. I was no longer waiting for the city to fall. I was just waiting for something to end. I still didn't know what.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. Doest Thou Well to Be Angry

_Previously_ — The vine withered overnight. Pheugon was angrier than he'd been watching the city live — and he noticed it himself. That night, he no longer waited for the city to fall. He was only waiting for something to end.

I heard footsteps the next morning.

I assumed they were Trope's. But it wasn't the gait of someone carrying a water jug. It was slower, more even.

I looked up.

It was him.

I wasn't surprised, strangely, in that moment. Four days had passed since I'd shouted at him near the gate and walked off. I hadn't once expected, in all that time, that he would come find me. And yet the moment I saw him standing there on the hill, I somehow felt I had already known this moment was coming.

He hadn't walked up from the direction of the city. Exactly which direction he'd come from, I still can't say, even now. He was simply there, at some point. I didn't find that strange. Over these three days I had been learning how to grow used to strange things.

He stood beside the dry vine stem and looked down at it for a moment. He said nothing. Then he came and sat beside me, as if it were nothing at all.

I didn't push him away. I didn't welcome him either.

"…How did you get up here." I asked. My voice had no strength in it.

"…I walked." he said.

I nearly laughed at that answer. I didn't, quite.

I noticed, then, that his clothes were covered in dust. There was dirt on his shoes too. The look of someone who had really walked. I found that strange. He seemed like the kind of person who could simply appear. And yet he'd taken the trouble to walk. I didn't ask why. I didn't ask, but I went on remembering those dirt-covered shoes for a long time after.

We sat a while without speaking.

The city was visible below. Morning sunlight caught brightly on the roofs. A rooster crowed somewhere, faintly.

I looked at the dry stem. He looked at it too.

"…This." I said first. "Did you send this?"

"…I sent nothing." he said.

"Then what?"

He didn't answer. I accepted that silence, this time, as something familiar. A silence with no answer, and a silence that withholds an answer — I'd learned, in the time since, that those were different things. And this silence now was the latter. He wasn't staying quiet because he didn't know the answer. He was waiting for me to reach it myself.

"…You were angry." he said. It was hard to tell whether it was a question or a confirmation.

"…Yesterday?" I asked. "Or the day before?"

"…Both."

I picked up a dry leaf between my fingertips. It crumbled the moment I touched it.

"…I was angry both times." I admitted. "But strangely, today, I'm angrier about the vine dying. More than I was about the city living. I know how strange that is."

"…So you already knew." he said quietly.

"…Yes. I figured it out alone, yesterday. No one told me."

"…Did you tell the Adjudicator." he asked.

"…No."

"…Trope."

"…No. No one." I looked at the dry stem again. "It's not something worth telling anyone. The one who saved a city getting angrier over a dead plant — who do you say that to."

"…You told me."

I stopped at that.

"…You're different." I said. It wasn't an answer I could fully explain, even to myself.

He looked at me for a moment.

There was no judgment in his eyes. No surprise either. I tried to count how many times I'd shouted in front of this person. Provoking him. Getting angry. Nearly crying. Every time, he had looked at me with the same eyes. I wasn't afraid of those eyes anymore.

"Pheugon." he said.

"…Yes."

"Doest thou well to be angry."

I couldn't answer that question right away.

I can still repeat that sentence back exactly, even now. He said it very plainly. Not loudly, not weighted. In the same tone you'd use to ask about today's weather. That made it more frightening, not less. If it had been a rebuke, I could have argued with it. But it was a real question.

"…Which one are you asking about." I asked. "The city living, or the vine dying."

"…Whichever you'd rather answer."

I opened my mouth, then closed it.

"…The city living." I managed at last. "I think that's right. My being angry about that. I lost my family, and that city survived without paying anything for it. I spent three years keeping my own weight low, just enduring. And those people just got a little kinder, and got everything handed back to them. Is it wrong for me to be angry about that? I don't think so."

He nodded. Not agreement, not denial — just a nod.

"…You brought up Trope's mother too." he said. "Near the gate."

"…Yes. I did."

He glanced toward the city, then back at me.

"…Do you still think that, now?"

I thought for a moment.

"…That's changed a little." I said. "Back then I used Trope's mother as evidence for my own anger. Thinking about it now, that wasn't right. Trope's grief is Trope's. It wasn't mine to borrow."

"…When did you realize that."

"…Just now. While I was saying it."

He seemed to smile with his eyes alone, very slightly, at that. I couldn't be sure. I turned my head to look more closely, and by then his face had already returned to normal.

"…What about the vine." he asked.

I couldn't answer.

Before that question, for the first time, I felt my own logic give out. My anger at the city had logic behind it. The logic of unfairness. But my anger at the vine had no logic at all. It was just anger at something of mine being gone. I was feeling both angers at the same size, and yet I could explain one and not the other.

"…Am I strange?" I asked. For the first time, there was pleading in my voice. "This whole time, I thought all my anger had reasons. My family, Trope's mother, the unfairness of this world. But in front of the vine, I can't name a single reason. I'm just angry. I must be a much smaller person than I thought."

"…What do you mean by small." he asked.

"…Someone who gets angry without a reason. Not righteous anger — just greedy anger."

"…I've never divided the two that way." he said.

I looked at him. There was still no judgment on his face.

"…What do you mean?"

"…What you call righteous anger, and anger without a reason. I don't think they split that cleanly. A person's anger usually comes in layers. The reason on top looks the most convincing. But underneath it, other things are there too."

Hearing that, I looked back into my own anger again. Beneath my anger at the city — was there really only outrage at unfairness there? Or was there, somewhere underneath, the same kind of thing I'd felt when I lost the vine — a hurt that what was mine hadn't been recognized? I had no confidence I could answer that question honestly.

"…I don't know." I said. "The vine was just mine. Even though it was only two days. When it was gone, I got angry. Angrier than about the city living. I know that doesn't make sense."

"…Do you think it doesn't make sense."

"…Yes."

"…Why."

I was stuck on that question again.

"…The city is a hundred twenty thousand people. The vine is just a plant. There's no comparison."

"…Whose arithmetic decides there's no comparison." he asked.

At that question I thought, for an instant, of the Scales. The Scales would calculate it that way. That the weight of a hundred twenty thousand people and the weight of one vine can't be compared. But I wasn't the Scales. I was a person. And I already knew that a person's heart doesn't calculate the way the Scales does.

"…It's my arithmetic." I said. My voice grew smaller. "But my heart doesn't calculate that way. My heart feels that two days of shade, given to me, is bigger than a hundred twenty thousand lives. I know that's wrong."

I paused a moment, then went on.

"…For three years I kept my own weight low and believed that made me an accurate person. Like the Scales. But now I see I'm nothing like the Scales at all. The Scales weighs a hundred twenty thousand people and one vine the same way. I don't. I use a different scale for what's mine."

"…Isn't that just what being a person is." he said.

"…Is that supposed to be comfort?"

"…No." he said. "Just a fact."

"…I never said it was wrong." he said.

"Then are you saying it's right?"

"…I never said that either."

I looked at him. The frustration rose in me again.

"Then what is it. I want to know, right now, whether my anger is right or wrong."

He looked at me for a long time.

"…I can't give you that answer." he said. "It's not that I'm withholding it. That answer was never meant to be given as an answer, to begin with."

"…Then how is it given?"

"…By living."

I didn't understand what he meant.

"…What does that mean." I asked. "That I'll know after a few more years?"

"…It could take years. It could happen in a day." he said. "I can't set that time for you. But I know this much. This question isn't one that closes with an answer today. Today is just the day you heard this question, clearly, for the first time."

Hearing that, I felt strangely at ease. I had been raised believing every question had to have an answer. A sentence document had a disposition. The Scales had a number. That you could carry an unanswered question and go on living — I heard that, for the first time, in that moment.

"…So I have to go on carrying this question?"

"…Is that a bad thing."

I couldn't answer that question either.

In that moment I wanted to press him further. Something stopped me. Before that question, I felt for the first time that it was all right not to know the answer. Or rather — it was all right to sit here, in this place, not knowing.

We sat in silence again for a long while.

The city stayed below, exactly as it was. The dry stem lay between us. The wind blew. In that wind, I heard his question again. Doest thou well to be angry.

I repeated that question to myself, again and again. The first time I'd heard it, the sentence had felt like an arrow aimed at me. Each time I heard it again, it sounded slightly different. Now it no longer felt aimed at me — it felt like it was sitting beside me, facing the same direction I was. I learned, that day, that a question could sit with someone too.

I still take this question out sometimes, even now. When I think about being angry that the city lived. When I think about being angry that the vine died. Each time, the answer wavers a little differently. Some days it feels like I was right. Other days, not. I've decided now to accept that very wavering as the answer. Only the Scales has an unwavering answer. I'm not the Scales.

I never did answer that question. Not that day, and not for a long time after. Even now, writing these words, I still don't have a certain answer to it. Some questions have to stay unanswered to remain questions. I may have already wished, in that moment, for this question to go on following me and never leave.

He didn't stand. Neither did I. We just sat there, side by side, with that unanswered question between us.

The sun climbed a little higher. The city's roofs caught the morning light, one by one. Watching that glimmer, I thought of Trope down there somewhere, and the Adjudicator, and the baker, and countless other people whose names I didn't know, all beginning their day. None of them would ever know what conversation had passed on this hill today.

That fact, strangely, didn't weigh on me. Some conversations don't need to be known by anyone. That day, for the first time, I felt that not everything needs to be proven or explained. In front of the Scales, I had always been someone under proof. By numbers, by records, by evidence. Up on this hill, there was nothing to prove. I only had to sit.

The wind stirred the dry stem slightly. A few broken bits of leaf rose into the air, then slowly settled back down. I followed them with my eyes. He didn't. He was still watching me.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. Were You There Before

_Previously_ — He came up the hill to find Pheugon. "Doest thou well to be angry." Pheugon couldn't answer. He gave no answer in his place, either — only, "You'll know that answer by living." The two of them sat a long while without speaking.

The sun was drawing close to noon.

We were still there. He hadn't left yet. I hadn't gone anywhere either. Between us lay the dry stem, an unanswered question, and — strangely — a comfortable silence.

In that silence I thought again about this person. In forty days I'd only seen him a handful of times. Standing beside someone unfairly cornered, in the square. Knowing a person's weight before the Scales did. Never getting angry even when I provoked him on purpose. Even gathering all those pieces together, I couldn't say who this person was. The one thing I could be sure of was that he wasn't from this city. Exactly where he was from, I didn't know.

Fiddling with the dry stem, I suddenly remembered a question I'd been holding back for a long time. I hesitated a long while over whether to say it.

I know now why I'd held that question back so long. It was because I already knew there would be no answer. And still, in the end, I asked it. That day I learned there are questions worth asking even without an answer.

"…Can I ask you something." I said.

"…Go ahead."

I looked toward the city. It was hard to ask, looking straight at him.

"…You." I said. "Have you been in a city like this before?"

He didn't answer.

I kept going. Once I'd started, I couldn't stop.

"What I mean is, is this the first time for you? Being someone who doesn't get angry, someone who knows before the Scales does, someone who sits with a person like this and asks questions? Is this your first time, or has this happened before? In other cities? With other people?"

I poured all that out without pausing to breathe. It felt as if, if I didn't ask now, I might never ask again.

He looked at me.

And smiled.

I still don't feel confident describing that smile precisely, even now. Nowhere in this book had I ever seen him smile before. I'd known he was someone who never got angry. That he was also someone who never smiled — I hadn't known that either, until that moment. When he smiled, for a second I wondered if I'd asked something wrong. But it wasn't a mocking smile. It wasn't teasing, either. I never fully worked out, that day, what that smile really was.

"…That's an interesting question." he said.

"…I didn't ask to be interesting."

"…I know."

He kept smiling, watching me. I didn't look away. There was something in that smile. I searched his face for a long while, trying to find what it was. It wasn't only pleasure. There was something like a very old weariness mixed into it too. And yet that weariness didn't feel like exhaustion — it felt like the kind of weariness only someone who has endured something for a very long time can carry.

In that moment, for the first time, I wondered. How much silence had this person passed through, to arrive at this one smile. I didn't know the answer. I still don't. But I could tell that this one smile had revealed, just barely, the weight of everything he'd swallowed down until now.

"…You're not going to answer, are you." I asked.

"…If I" he said, still smiling a little, "answer that question, what happened on this hill won't stay on this hill."

I didn't understand what he meant.

"…What does that mean?"

"…Some questions" he said, "give you the next question once you hear the answer. And answering that one gives you another. You're knocking on the first door right now."

"…So you won't open it?"

"…Not today." he said.

"…Then someday?"

"…That's not for me to decide." he said.

"…Then who decides?"

He gave no answer to that question either. In that silence, I felt a third kind of silence — different from the other two I'd learned from him before.

That was the first time I met this third silence. It wasn't a silence that knows the answer and withholds it. It wasn't a silence waiting for me to reach it myself, either. It was simply a silence that said, now is not the time. I couldn't find the right words to explain it then. I still can't, fully, now. But before that silence, I felt two things at once — that the answer to this question surely existed somewhere, and that I wasn't the one meant to hear it today.

I thought I should have been angry at that answer. Strangely, I wasn't.

I didn't know why, then. I know a little more now. There were several questions in this book that never got answered. Why the clause never activated. Who wrote the clause. Why he never got angry. Before all of those questions, I'd always felt only frustration. But before this question, something else came first. In that moment I felt certain he was hiding something. And that certainty — that he was hiding something — was itself proof that something was there.

"…I think that's the answer." I said. "You not answering."

He looked at me. A little of the smile still remained.

"…Think whatever you like."

"…Just one more, then." I said. "Have you seen this city before, or is this the first time?"

"…Same question." he said. "Just dressed differently."

I couldn't argue with that. He was right.

"…Then let me ask it differently." I said. "I thought this was the first forty days this city had ever had to face. That the clause had activated for the first time too. The Adjudicator waited forty years for it. But you. Is this your first time too?"

He was quiet for a moment.

"…I" he said, "make a distinction between saying something is the first time, and saying it isn't familiar."

I turned that sentence over several times.

"…What's the difference?"

"…Whether something's the first time is a matter of time." he said. "Whether you've been through it before or not. Familiarity is a different matter. There are things you can go through many times and never grow familiar with."

"…So which is this? How many times has it been? Are you used to it, or not?"

"…Same question again." he said. This time he didn't smile. But there was still gentleness left in his voice. "You've put a third set of clothes on it."

I felt a little embarrassed at that. I didn't give up, though.

I held on to what he'd said.

"…So are you used to it? Things like this?"

He didn't answer. Instead he looked toward the city. The roofs down below were shining in the sunlight.

"…Pheugon." he said. It was the tone of someone changing the subject. "Do you know what that city is doing right now?"

"…I don't know. I'm up here."

"…Living." he said. "It's just living. It lived yesterday, it's living today, it'll live tomorrow. That's the most important thing there is."

In that moment I knew he was changing the subject. I didn't press him on it. I already knew I wasn't going to get an answer. But in that moment, I also felt that the way he changed the subject wasn't a retreat. It was closer to a hand, gesturing to take me somewhere else.

We went quiet again.

I never heard the answer to that question, that day. I still haven't heard it. Even now, writing this, I don't know what city he was in before, how many cities he's watched through forty days like these, whether this really was the first time.

And yet I've never been able to let go of that question, since that day. I've held on to it the whole time I've been writing this book. The thought that someday, in some other city, someone else passing through their own forty days, might go through something very close to what happened in this one. That thought unsettled me. And, strangely, it comforted me too.

I saw Trope, far off, climbing the hill. She had the water jug slung over her shoulder again, today too. He saw her, and stood without a word.

"…I'll come again." he said.

"…When?"

"…When you need me."

He left with those words and walked down the hill. I watched, as he passed Trope, the two of them bow their heads briefly to each other. Then he kept walking, and I never did see which direction he vanished in.

Trope only really looked at me once she'd finished climbing all the way to the top.

"…That person just now — do you know him?" she asked. She was still out of breath. "As he passed me, he nodded, and it was a face I'd never seen before, but somehow it felt familiar."

I stopped for a moment at that.

I didn't know, then, what to tell Trope. How do you explain a person you don't fully understand yourself? In the end I chose the most honest answer I had.

"…I don't really know him either." I said. "But he's helped me the whole forty days."

Trope didn't press further. Instead she sat down beside me and unwrapped her bundle.

"…The Adjudicator said you really have to eat something now." she said. Two loaves of bread and a few boiled potatoes came out.

I took them. My hands were shaking. I only truly admitted, then, how hungry I was.

We sat side by side next to the dry stem and shared the bread.

We were quiet for a long while. A bell sounded faintly from the city. It was ringing noon.

"…What did you two talk about earlier?" Trope asked again. This time she was asking about the content, not the person.

I stopped, halfway through splitting a potato in two.

"…I asked him." I said. "Whether he'd been in a city like this before."

"…What did he say?"

"…He smiled." I said. "And didn't answer."

Trope listened to that quietly.

"…That's strange." she said. "I've never seen someone smile and dodge a question at the same time."

Hearing her say that, I felt reconfirmed in what I'd just seen — that it really had been strange. In forty days I'd watched this person never get angry. I'd watched him never smile, either. And today, just once, both of those broke at the same time. I don't think that was a coincidence. I still don't.

"…I think it's strange too." I said. "But it's better than getting angry."

Trope laughed a little at that.

We sat quietly again. The sun climbed fully overhead. There was still no shade. The dry stem lay between us, casting not even a shadow.

"…What are you going to do with this now?" Trope asked, pointing at the dry stem.

"…I don't know."

"…Are you going to pull it out?"

"…No." I said. "I'll just leave it."

I couldn't explain, then, why I answered that way. I understand a little now. I wanted to leave that stem behind as evidence. Evidence of what, I didn't know exactly. I only wanted whoever came up this hill again someday to know that there had once been shade here.

Trope nodded. She didn't ask anything more.

"…The Adjudicator asked again." she said, a while later. "Whether you'll come back."

"…What did you say?"

"…I answered differently this time." Trope said. "I told him you'd be back soon."

I looked at her.

"…I never said that."

"…I know." she said. "But I wanted to say it."

I had no answer to that. I still hadn't decided when I'd go down. And Trope had already decided the answer for me. I should have felt burdened by that. Strangely, I didn't.

As the sun began sinking toward the west, Trope left the water jug and the remaining bread beside me and stood.

"…I'll come tomorrow too." she said.

"…You don't have to."

"…I know." she said. "But I will."

She went down the hill. I watched her back the whole way. Partway down the field path, she stopped once again, then walked on.

I could recognize that pause now. It wasn't a pause to check anything. It was just her, taking one more moment to hold me — left behind on this hill — in her heart, before walking on.

I set the question he'd left me and the pause Trope had left me side by side, and looked at them together. Neither one demanded anything of me. Both were simply waiting for me to move on my own. I wondered how long that waiting could go on. I didn't know the answer. But that day, for the first time, I felt I didn't want to test how long that waiting could last, any longer.

Alone again on the hill, I opened the water jug and drank. It was lukewarm. But this time I didn't want to refuse it.

The sun sank completely. Stars began rising, one by one. I sat with my back against the dry stem. I remembered counting stars in this same spot a few nights before, and giving up. I didn't count them today either.

That night I kept going back over his smiling face. The old weariness inside that smile. I still didn't know what that weariness had endured. But since seeing it, I began to see him a little differently. Until then I had thought of him only as some strange person who had appeared in this city. Now he seemed like someone who had passed through something even before this city. What he had passed through, I still didn't know. But knowing that he had passed through something at all was enough to make me see him differently.

That night, for the first time, I pictured concretely the day I would go down this hill. Whether it would be days away, or tomorrow, I didn't decide. But that the day was coming — for the first time, in that moment, that felt certain.

(Continued in Chapter 48.)

Chapter 48. The Way Down

_Previously_ — Pheugon asked him, "Have you been in a city like this before?" For the first time, he smiled and dodged the question. He left with the words, "I'll come again. When you need me." Trope said she'd come up every day. That night, for the first time, Pheugon felt certain that the day he'd go down the hill was coming.

I spent a few more days on that hill.

I didn't count exactly how many. Writing this book, I know how strange that is. I was a person who counted three days, counted forty days, counted one hundred twelve cases. And yet I didn't count those days. I never decided not to count them, either. At some point, without my noticing, the habit of marking off days on my fingers had simply stopped.

I still can't pin down exactly when that started. Maybe it was from the day the vine grew. Or maybe from the day he smiled. Either way, from that day on, I stopped being someone who counted time.

Trope came up every day. She'd leave the water jug and bread, sit a while, then go back down. Some days she talked a lot. Other days she just sat beside me in silence and left. The Adjudicator never came up, not once. Instead he sent short messages through Trope. "Take your time." I turned those words over many times. There was no urging in them at all.

One day Trope brought up the substitute herald. He'd already left for another city, she said. Before leaving, he'd left one line. "This case is going to sit strangely in the record." Trope said she didn't know what that meant. I didn't say that I did.

One day, no one came at all. I spent that whole day sitting beside the dry stem, thinking about those small white points that had once looked like they might become buds. Whatever they might have become, they had withered along with everything else, before becoming anything at all. I still hadn't decided, even that day, whether that was a sad thing or not.

I knew, the moment I opened my eyes that morning.

I couldn't have said exactly what I knew, then. My body simply moved first. I sat up. The dry stem was still there. Withered black, its roots still lodged in the rock crack.

I looked at that stem for a long while.

I've rewritten this scene several times. In one draft I wrote that I cried in front of it. In another, that I laughed. Neither was true. The truth is, I did nothing at all. I just looked. For a long time.

I reached out and gripped the base of the dry stem once. It made a breaking sound. That sound no longer bothered me.

"…Thank you." I said. The same words I'd said, that first time, under its shade. But the words that day and these words today were different words. I still can't explain the difference. I only know that it is a difference.

I stood.

My body swayed. I hadn't eaten properly in days. I stood a moment, steadying myself. The city lay below. The moment I'd first been washed up at the well forty days before, the first night on this hill waiting for the city to fall, and now — these three moments overlapped all at once.

I decided nothing, in that moment. Writing this book, I've felt the temptation, many times, to write "I decided, then, to..." It would have made for a cleaner sentence. But it isn't true. I didn't decide anything. My body simply stood, and my legs simply began to walk. The decision followed later, little by little, along the way.

I gathered my things. There wasn't much. A few clothes, an empty water jug, and —

I stopped there.

There was a folded paper tucked inside my coat. Old, its edges a little worn. I took it out and unfolded it.

Two sentences, written in my own hand.

Original: This one never explained himself.
Precedent: This one never repented.

I haven't once thrown this paper away, since the day I copied it out in the room of records. Not on the day I believed it would be evidence enough to bring down the Scales. Not on the day I learned that evidence would overturn nothing at all. Why I never threw it away, I didn't fully know myself, even then.

I looked at that paper for a long time.

Never explained himself. Never repented.

Mena had been someone who, in some city, had watched the difference between those two lines. While that difference quietly reshaped verdicts in other cities, one after another, Mena had probably never known it. Or she had known, and been unable to do anything about it. I still don't know which of those two is the sadder thing.

I thought about leaving this paper up on the hill. It was evidence that had already become useless inside this city. The Scales' conclusion had been right. This paper had overturned nothing. And still, I folded it back up and put it inside my coat.

I couldn't explain, that day, why I did that. I still can't, fully. I only know that I had become someone who knew that this person's — Mena's — sentence had become something else entirely, passing through someone else's hands. And someone who knows a thing like that, I learned that day, cannot leave that knowledge lying anywhere behind.

Here, for the first time, I have something to set straight about this book.

I am writing these words right now.

I have been writing this from the very first line of this book. And yet, through forty-seven chapters, I never once set that fact plainly down on the page. I only ever wrote "I know," "I didn't know then," "now I know a little more." I never once revealed who stood behind those sentences.

I asked myself why I'm writing this so late. The answer is simple. I felt I could only admit I was writing this once I had come all the way down this last hill of the book. Right now, before I've even finished coming down this hill, I set it down here. Because I wanted to write it before it was too late.

Why am I writing this.

Mena was someone who kept records. Someone who belonged to no city, who moved between many, writing down what people said. One sentence of hers, passing through a few hands, was turned into something entirely different. She couldn't stop it. Either she had already left that city, or she had already let go of that sentence — either way, she wasn't in a position to protect her own words.

I was afraid of my own sentences ending up the same way. So I decided to set down these forty days with my own hand. In case someone, copying this story out later, turned "he was angry to the end" into "he repented in the end." Or the reverse — turned "he raged to the end" into "in the end he understood it all."

I am neither. I am still walking, somewhere between the two.

That's why I'm keeping this paper. What Mena couldn't protect, I want to protect in her place. And yet I know — keeping this one sheet of paper in my coat won't restore their proper meaning to the rest of Mena's sentences, scattered somewhere across other cities. That wasn't something I could do today. But there was one thing I could do today. At least this one page in my hand — I could keep it safe.

I don't know who will read this book. Maybe no one will. I write it anyway. I never wanted to answer someone the way the supervisor once answered me — "because that's the rule." I wanted to leave behind what happened, instead of the rule. At the size it actually happened, neither adding nor taking away. That was the one proclamation I found I could still make, after enduring these forty days.

I started down the hill.

The field path was the same one I'd first seen that night, forty days before, going out to bring Trope back. It had been dark then; I'd never really seen the shape of the road. Now it was daylight, and the path lay open before me. A narrow dirt track, half-buried in grass. A path farmers must have walked, once, long ago.

I followed it down.

Writing this sentence, I find it strange that I can say precisely where I'm walking now. For the past three years I had always known exactly where I was supposed to go. The proclamation office. The training grounds. The Reckoning. There was always a fixed destination. But on this path down the hill, there was no fixed place at all. I was simply walking downward, toward where the city was.

My body still had no strength. Every few steps I stopped briefly to catch my breath. Each time, I looked back. The hilltop grew smaller, little by little. The dry stem couldn't be seen from here anymore.

The sun was hot. Dry dirt crumbled under my feet. Somewhere, insects kept up a low, steady chirring. I caught myself listening to each of those sounds, one by one. For forty days, and for a few more days on this hill, I had spent most of my time listening only to the sounds inside myself. The sound anger made. The sound a question made. On this road, for the first time in a long while, I was hearing the world outside.

Walking, I thought of many faces.

The four who had sat at our table, and then five. Had's face, turning back as he packed his things and walked out. "Why don't you stop?" The supervisor's face, asking, "Do you have some connection to the Reckoning?" Trope's face at the well, asking, "You're not from here, are you?" The Adjudicator's face, saying, "That isn't the face of someone afraid." The substitute herald's face, saying, "Congratulations. I think you did the right thing." And the face, a few mornings before, that had smiled and dodged my question.

I started counting those faces one by one, and stopped myself. I had decided I was no longer someone who counts. But a habit worn into the body doesn't disappear easily. I caught myself trying to count again a few more times, and stopped each time. Stopping, too, I learned on that road, takes its own kind of training.

I thought, too, of his dirt-covered shoes — the ones from the day he'd said he'd walked all the way up. He could have simply appeared, and yet every time, he'd come with dust on his shoes. I noticed dust gathering on my own shoes now too. It felt strange, as if it placed him and me, somehow, on the same road. It was only a brief feeling, but I wanted to hold on to it.

Halfway down, I stopped and turned to look back up at the hill.

The vine couldn't be seen from here either. Too small, or too far. I only knew that something remained up there. There are things you know are there even when you can't see them.

In that moment I thought of the Forty-Day Clause. That one line, sitting inside the Scales' code, never once invoked. No one had ever thought to strike it out. Even though it looked useless, someone had left it there, inside the text, all along. I understood that impulse a little now. Some things need to be there, even unused.

I thought of the Adjudicator. In forty years, he had never once seen this clause invoked. And yet, he'd told me, he had never once suggested striking it from the text. I understood those forty years a little now, too. Someone who waits for something doesn't give up the waiting itself, even if they never live to see it used.

In that moment I thought of Trope's mother too. The handprint left on the well's stone rim. A trace that hadn't faded in five years. Walking that road, I confirmed once more that waiting settles into different shapes for different people. For the Adjudicator, one line in a clause. For Trope, a handprint left in stone. For me, one sheet of paper folded inside my coat.

I started walking again.

The city drew closer, little by little. The outline of the wall sharpened. A few threads of smoke rose among the rooftops. Someone was cooking a meal today too.

Watching that smoke, I thought again of everyone inside those walls — Trope, the Adjudicator, the baker, a hundred twenty thousand people whose names I didn't know, living out their day. How many of them knew that a vine had grown on that hill, and withered? Probably none of them.

Strangely, that didn't weigh on me. I'd already learned this, back in Chapter 46. Some things don't need to be known by anyone. That day, on that hill, I'd learned to simply sit without needing to prove anything. Today I was learning to simply walk, having risen from that spot, without needing to prove anything either.

I thought of the Scales. It was probably still turning somewhere in that city, right now. Someone's theft today, someone's restraint today, calculated without error. The Scales would still not be wrong. And I was still walking a place the Scales could never measure.

I reached a point where the gate came into view. Still a long way off. People moving in and out looked like nothing more than small points from here. I stopped again. The dry well beside the gate came into sight. The spot where he'd said he'd walked here from. No one there now.

I looked at that well for a long while. Whether I would meet him there again someday, or in some other city, at some other well, I didn't know. I'll come again. When you need me. I still hadn't decided whether to take those words as a real promise, or just as comfort.

I thought, suddenly, of my name again, and Trope's. To flee. Turning. For three years I had fled. For forty days I had fled. And on this hill, I had fled a few days more. And yet now, on this road, I was doing something that was neither fleeing nor turning. I was simply walking. I still don't know, even now, which of the two it's closer to.

The path curved in among the fields. I stopped, briefly, at that bend.

I thought for a very long time about how to end this book, here. I considered writing the scene of my arrival in the city. Someone recognizing me in front of the gate. Trope running out. The Adjudicator taking my hand. I wrote scenes like that several times. I deleted them all.

It isn't that I deleted them because I felt I had no right to write them. It isn't that I deleted them because I couldn't clearly remember whether they'd actually happened. I knew that this book had to end in a question, not an answer. I didn't want to invent the answer.

When I was eleven, I asked the proclamation supervisor, "But why?" He only answered, "Because that's the rule." I didn't like that answer. I didn't know then that it wasn't really an answer at all. I know now. Some questions have no rule, and no answer. There is only the person who keeps walking, carrying the question.

In the first chapter of this book I wrote that I'd wondered why the Scales never speaks its own verdict aloud. I wrote that it was only much later that I came to think about why a person had been placed there instead, and who had placed them there, and why. Even reaching this last chapter, I still haven't found that answer in full. But I know, now, what it feels like to stand in that place.

Right now, I am standing at that bend.

Mena's paper is inside my coat. Behind me is the withered vine. Ahead of me is the city. I don't know whether I'm ready to go back into that city. I don't know what I'll say when I see Trope again. I don't have an answer, yet, to his question — Doest thou well to be angry.

I am walking, carrying all of this, unknown. For three years I believed that doing something without knowing was the most frightening thing in the world. That's why I always calculated. Always counted. Always confirmed. Right now I am walking without calculating anything at all. And strangely, it isn't frightening.

I started walking again.

The path kept leading downward. The city lay at its end. I hadn't yet seen the end of that path.

Even after finishing this book, I am still on that path. Whether I reached the city or not, I've chosen not to write here. That isn't me hiding something. I learned, writing this book, that some stories must not end in arrival.

The wind blew across the fields. The smell of dry earth came with it, and, very faintly, the smell of someone cooking, carried from the direction of the city. I walked on, breathing that smell in.

My stomach was hungry again. My throat was dry again. This time I didn't turn that hunger and thirst into hurrying. I'd walk a few steps, stop to rest, and walk again. Whenever the thought came that someone might be waiting for me, I found myself, instead, slowing my steps. I couldn't explain why, then. I understand a little now — I wanted to walk this road itself a little longer.

I walk.

And I am still, even now, standing before that question.

Was I right to be angry.

(The End.)

The Forty-Day Clause

Johan Choi
